# Sūrah Yūnus (Chapter 10) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Makkan Chapter Named After a Prophet Whose People Were Saved**

Sūrah Yūnus is the tenth chapter of the Qur'an, consisting of one hundred and nine verses. It belongs, in the judgment of the great majority of commentators, to the Makkan period — and, more precisely, to a phase of that period in which the confrontation between the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ and the leadership of Quraysh had hardened into a settled pattern of ridicule, demand, and denial. A few verses have been suggested by some authorities to be Madinan (v. 40 and vv. 94–96 are named in this connection), and some scholars place the sūrah after the Night Journey and Ascension; but the atmosphere of the whole is unmistakably Makkan. There is no legislation of inheritance here, no regulation of warfare, no detailed ritual law. What there is instead is the fundamental argument of Islam conducted at full intensity: Who made the heavens and the earth? To whom do you return? What is the standing of the messenger who recites this Book? And what exactly are you waiting for?

The sūrah takes its name from a single verse near the end, v. 98, which mentions the people of Jonah — Yūnus ibn Mattā, peace be upon him. That single mention is placed with deliberate art. The whole sūrah has been recounting communities that refused their prophets and were destroyed: the generations of v. 13, the people of Noah in vv. 71–73, the messengers after him in v. 74, Pharaoh and his court in vv. 75–92. Then, at the very end of that catalogue of ruin, one town appears that broke the pattern:

> *"Why has not a single town believed and benefitted from its belief, other than the people of Jonah? When they believed, We removed from them the punishment of disgrace in the life of this world, and We granted them enjoyment for a while."* (Qur'an 10:98)

The naming of the sūrah after that exception is itself the sūrah's argument in miniature. The Makkans are being told: the door has not yet closed. Ruin is not fate; it is a consequence, and consequences can be averted by a change of heart made in time. A chapter that could have been named after Noah's flood or Pharaoh's drowning is instead named after the one people who repented and lived.

**The Family of the Alif–Lām–Rā Sūrahs**

Yūnus opens the sequence of sūrahs that begin with the disconnected letters *Alif. Lām. Rā.* — chapters 10, 11, 12, 14, and 15, with chapter 13 opening *Alif. Lām. Mīm. Rā.* Each of these openings is immediately followed by a statement about the Book: *"These are the signs of the Wise Book"* (10:1), *"a Book whose signs have been made firm"* (11:1), *"These are the signs of the clear Book"* (12:1). The pattern tells the attentive reader what the whole family of sūrahs is about. These chapters are a sustained meditation on revelation itself: its source, its authenticity, its reception, and the historical record of what happens to nations that receive it.

This matters for understanding Yūnus. The sūrah is not a miscellany. It is an extended case for the divine origin of the Qur'an and the reasonableness of accepting it, argued from several directions at once: from the order of the cosmos (vv. 3–6, 31, 61, 67), from the moral psychology of the deniers themselves (vv. 12, 21–23), from the inimitability of the text (v. 38), from the universality of prophecy (v. 47), and from the recorded fate of earlier peoples (vv. 13, 71–92, 102–103).

**The Central Accusation and the Central Reply**

The Makkan leadership had two accusations, and the sūrah answers both. The first was that Muhammad ﷺ was *sāḥir mubīn*, "a manifest sorcerer" (v. 2) — that is, that the extraordinary effect of the Qur'an upon its hearers was a species of enchantment. The second was *iftarāhu*, "he has fabricated it" (v. 38) — that the text was his own composition dressed in divine claims.

The sūrah's reply is patient and layered. To the charge of fabrication it offers, first, a biographical argument: *"Indeed, I tarried among you for a lifetime before it"* (v. 16) — forty years of a life lived openly among the very people now accusing him, a life in which he was nicknamed *al-Amīn*, the Trustworthy. Second, a logical argument: a man who could invent revelation could also amend it on demand, yet the Prophet's answer to the demand for a more congenial Qur'an is *"It is not for me to alter it of my own accord"* (v. 15) — an inability that is itself evidence of an external source. Third, an open challenge: *"Then bring a sūrah like it"* (v. 38). And fourth, a diagnosis of motive: *"Nay, but they deny that whose knowledge they cannot comprehend"* (v. 39) — rejection preceding examination, which is the signature of prejudice rather than inquiry.

**The Psychology of Denial: The Sūrah's Most Original Contribution**

Perhaps the most striking thread in Sūrah Yūnus is its portrait of the inner life of the person who denies God while knowing Him. This is not caricature; it is close observation, and it is recognizable in every century.

The sūrah says that when catastrophe strikes, the denier prays — *"lying on his side, or sitting, or standing"* (v. 12) — and that when the ship is about to founder he calls upon God *"devoting religion entirely to Him"* (v. 22). The disbelief, in other words, is not a settled metaphysical conclusion. It is a mood that survives only in comfort. Under pressure it collapses instantly into the monotheism it denied, and after rescue it reassembles itself as though nothing had happened: *"he carries on as if he had not called upon Us"* (v. 12).

This observation grounds one of the sūrah's key claims about human nature: that faith in one God is the substratum of the human being, and unbelief is a layer laid over it. The verse in Sūrat al-Rūm names this substratum *fiṭrah*:

> *"So set thy face toward religion as a ḥanīf — the fiṭrah of God upon which He originated mankind."* (Qur'an 30:30)

Sūrah Yūnus ends, significantly, with that very command addressed to the Prophet: *"Set thy face toward the religion as a ḥanīf"* (v. 105). The circle closes: the sūrah that diagnosed a buried nature commands, at its end, the uncovering of it.

**Conjecture Versus Knowledge**

A second thread, running through vv. 36, 66, and elsewhere, is the Qur'an's assault on *ẓann* — conjecture, supposition, the opinion held without warrant.

> *"And most of them follow naught but conjecture. Truly conjecture does not avail against the truth in the least."* (Qur'an 10:36)

The polemical target is idolatry inherited by custom rather than reached by evidence. But the epistemological principle is general, and it is one of the Qur'an's permanent contributions to human thought: that in matters of ultimate consequence, guesswork is not a permissible foundation. What our fathers did, what most people say, what feels comfortable — none of these is *ʿilm*, knowledge. A religion that repeatedly rebukes its opponents for reasoning badly has implicitly committed itself to reasoning well.

**The Sovereignty of God and the Freedom of Man Held Together**

Sūrah Yūnus is also one of the Qur'an's most important chapters for the theology of guidance. Within a few pages it affirms both that no soul believes except by God's leave (v. 100) and that compulsion in belief is neither God's method nor the Prophet's mandate (v. 99); both that God guides whom He wills (v. 25) and that whoever is guided is guided for his own soul's benefit (v. 108); both that some hearts are sealed (v. 74) and that God wrongs no one in the least (v. 44).

The classical schools debated the balance of these statements at length, and the essential Sunni resolution — articulated by al-Ashʿarī, refined by al-Ghazzālī, and pressed with particular force by Ibn Taymiyyah — is that divine sovereignty is the condition of human action rather than its rival. God creates the capacity, the circumstance, and the moment; the servant genuinely chooses within them and is genuinely answerable for the choice. The sūrah's own formula for the human side of the ledger is uncompromising:

> *"Truly God does not wrong human beings in the least, but rather human beings wrong themselves."* (Qur'an 10:44)

**The Structure of the Sūrah**

The sūrah moves through recognizable movements. Verses 1–10 establish the Book, the Creator, the Return, and the two destinies. Verses 11–24 dissect the psychology of the deniers and close with the great parable of the perishing garden. Verses 25–36 present the Abode of Peace, the courtroom of the Hereafter where false gods disown their worshippers, and the argument from providence. Verses 37–56 defend the Qur'an's authenticity and answer the mocking demand for immediate punishment. Verses 57–70 turn tender — the Qur'an as *shifāʾ*, cure; the friends of God who neither fear nor grieve; the refutation of the claim that God has taken a child. Verses 71–93 recount Noah, the messengers after him, and at length Moses and Pharaoh. Verses 94–109 conclude with the sealed hearts, the people of Jonah, the impossibility of compulsion in faith, and the Prophet's final marching orders: *"And follow that which has been revealed unto thee, and be patient till God renders judgment."*

**The Sūrah's Address to the Present Reader**

It would be a mistake to read this chapter as a period piece about seventh-century Makkah. Its subject is a permanent human posture: the person who is comfortable, who is "content with the life of this world and feels secure therein" (v. 7), who defers the ultimate questions because nothing at present compels him to face them. That posture is more available today than it has ever been. Modern life is remarkably good at removing the conditions that used to force the question — the storm at sea, the untreatable illness, the famine year. The sūrah's answer is not to wish those conditions back but to make the reflection voluntary: to look at the sun and moon (v. 5), the alternation of night and day (v. 6), the provision that arrives daily from a source no one manufactures (v. 31), and to conclude in security what most people only concede in terror.

What follows is an expanded, verse-by-verse commentary on the entire sūrah. Each verse is treated as a complete and independent discussion, drawing on the Arabic of the text, the classical exegetical tradition (al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī, al-Bayḍāwī and others), the authenticated words of the Prophet ﷺ with their sources named, and the reasoning that connects each passage to the lived experience of the reader.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:1

> **Alif. Lām. Rā. These are the signs of the Wise Book.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Letters and a Deliberate Silence**

The sūrah opens with three letters of the Arabic alphabet pronounced separately: *alif, lām, rā*. They are not a word. They carry no lexical meaning in Arabic, and no reader of the seventh century would have heard them as anything but the bare names of letters — the sounds a child learns before he learns speech.

Twenty-nine sūrahs of the Qur'an open in this manner with what the tradition calls *al-ḥurūf al-muqaṭṭaʿah*, the disconnected letters. Al-Ṭabarī, who is normally the most exhaustive of commentators, gathers the opinions on them and then transmits with evident approval the position of a group of the earliest authorities: these are among the matters whose full meaning God has retained with Himself. Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq is reported to have said, "In every Book there is a secret, and the secret of God in the Qur'an is the openings of the sūrahs." Ibn Kathīr, surveying the same material, judges that the soundest course is to affirm the letters as revelation and to decline to force upon them a meaning the text does not supply.

**The Interpretations the Tradition Did Offer**

That reticence did not prevent careful proposals. Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have read certain of these letter-groups as abbreviations of divine names or attributes — *alif* for *Allāh*, *lām* for *Laṭīf* (the Subtle), *rā* for *Raḥīm* (the Merciful) — though the reports vary and the exegetes treated them as suggestions rather than settled readings. Others held that they are the names by which the sūrahs themselves are known, or that they are oaths sworn by the letters of the language.

The interpretation with the widest scholarly acceptance, however, is structural, and it is the one that best explains why the letters are almost always followed immediately by a mention of the Book — as here: *Alif. Lām. Rā. These are the signs of the Wise Book.* The letters are the raw material of Arabic. They are common property. Every poet of Quraysh, every orator at ʿUkāẓ, every accuser who called the Prophet a sorcerer had precisely the same twenty-eight letters at his disposal. The Qur'an begins by laying that material on the table, and then says: *these* are the signs of the Wise Book. The juxtaposition is an argument. From these letters you make your speech; from these same letters comes this. If the difference lies not in the materials, where does it lie?

**Why This Argument Is Stronger Than It First Appears**

Consider an analogy from ordinary experience. Two people are given identical boxes of paints and identical canvases. One produces a competent picture; the other produces a work that people still travel to see a thousand years later. The materials do not explain the difference — the source does. The Qur'an's challenge in v. 38 of this very sūrah, *"Then bring a sūrah like it,"* only carries force because the raw materials were never in dispute. No one accused the Prophet of using a secret vocabulary. He used the market Arabic of Makkah, and the result was a text that his most gifted opponents, who had every motive of honour and politics to answer it, could not match.

This is why the disconnected letters are not a mystical embellishment but a rhetorical opening move. They function like a challenge laid down before the argument begins.

**The Word *Āyāt*: Signs, Not Merely Sentences**

*Tilka āyātu al-kitābi al-ḥakīm* — "These are the signs of the Wise Book." The word *āyah* (plural *āyāt*) is one of the Qur'an's most important terms, and English "verse" flattens it badly. Its root sense is a mark, a token, an indication — something that points beyond itself. The same word is used for the natural world:

> *"Surely in the creation of the heavens and the earth, and the variation of the night and the day, are signs (āyāt) for possessors of intellect."* (Qur'an 3:190)

And for the miracles given to prophets, and for the units of the revealed text. The single word links three things: the cosmos, the miracle, and the scripture. All three are signposts, and all three point in the same direction. A verse of the Qur'an is therefore not primarily an item of information; it is a marker planted in the reader's path to redirect his attention toward God.

**What "Wise" Means When Applied to a Book**

*Al-ḥakīm* comes from the root *ḥ-k-m*, which generates a remarkable cluster of words: *ḥikmah* (wisdom), *ḥukm* (judgment, ruling), *ḥākim* (judge), *iḥkām* (making something firm and sound), *muḥkam* (unambiguous, firmly established), and even *ḥakamah*, the bridle-bit that restrains a horse from bolting. That last derivation is the key to the whole family: at root, *ḥ-k-m* means to restrain something from disorder. Wisdom restrains action from folly; judgment restrains dispute from chaos; a firm structure restrains a building from collapse.

The commentators exploit the whole range. Al-Rāzī and al-Kāshānī note that *ḥakīm* here can mean the Book that contains wisdom; the Book that is itself sound and unambiguous in its construction (*muḥkam*, from the same root — see 3:7 and 11:1); and the Book that judges, that renders *ḥukm* between disputing parties. Ibn Kathīr emphasises the sense of *muḥkam*: a Book made firm, in which there is no incoherence.

There is also a well-recognised rhetorical figure at work. Strictly, wisdom is an attribute of a person, not of a text; a book does not deliberate. To call the Book "wise" is to transfer the attribute from the Author to the work, in the way that we call a document "authoritative" when what we mean is that its issuer has authority. The Book is wise because it comes from the Wise (*al-Ḥakīm* is one of God's names, occurring scores of times in the Qur'an, most often paired with *al-ʿAlīm*, the Knowing).

**Which Book Is Meant?**

Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Zamakhsharī record the view of some that *the Book* here refers to this particular sūrah. The great majority, however — al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, al-Kalbī, and the author of *Jalālayn* among them — take it to refer to the Qur'an as a whole. The majority view is stronger. The sūrahs beginning with these letters consistently follow them with a reference to the total revelation, and the argument being mounted throughout Yūnus concerns the authenticity of the Qur'an as such, not of one chapter.

**The Practical Lesson of an Unexplained Opening**

There is a discipline embedded in these three letters that the modern reader particularly needs. The very first thing the sūrah does is present something that cannot be fully explained. The believing reader's response is *āmannā bihi*, "we believe in it" — a phrase the Qur'an itself supplies for exactly this situation:

> *"...those firmly rooted in knowledge say, 'We believe in it; all is from our Lord.'"* (Qur'an 3:7)

This is not an invitation to abandon reason. It is training in a precise and rational humility: the recognition that a finite mind receiving communication from an infinite source will inevitably encounter material it cannot exhaust. A student who abandons a science the first time he meets a result he cannot yet derive will never learn the science. The disconnected letters stand at the threshold of the sūrah as a small examination in intellectual maturity, and the whole chapter that follows — which will accuse the deniers of rejecting *"that whose knowledge they cannot comprehend"* (v. 39) — is, in a sense, an extended commentary on how badly that examination can be failed.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:2

> **Is it a marvel for mankind that We have sent revelation unto a man from among themselves, [saying], "Warn mankind and give glad tidings to those who believe that they shall have a station of sincerity with their Lord"? The disbelievers say, "Surely this is a manifest sorcerer."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Objection Behind the Verse**

The commentators preserve the occasion that prompted this verse. When the Prophet ﷺ presented himself to Quraysh as God's Messenger, their reaction was not primarily theological but social. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Maybudī record their retort: *"Could God not find anyone to send but the orphan of Abū Ṭālib's household?"*

The sneer is worth pausing over, because it exposes the real objection. It was not that God cannot speak to man; the Arabs had inherited stories of Abraham, of Moses, of prophets and soothsayers. The objection was that God had spoken to *this* man — a man they knew, a man without wealth, without a father, without a following, who had grown up in their own streets and married a widow and traded modestly and sat among them at the Kaʿbah. Prophethood, they assumed, ought to arrive with the trappings of status. The Qur'an records the demand elsewhere in blunt terms:

> *"And they said, 'Why was this Qur'an not sent down upon a great man of the two towns?'"* (Qur'an 43:31)

**"A Marvel" — Examining the Astonishment**

*A-kāna li-l-nāsi ʿajaban* — "Is it a marvel for mankind...?" The word *ʿajab* denotes astonishment at something anomalous, something that violates expectation. The verse poses a question that is really a refutation: what, exactly, is anomalous here?

The logic is worth drawing out, because it is one of the Qur'an's recurring arguments. If a message is to be delivered to human beings, it must be delivered in a form human beings can receive. A messenger from among the angels would have been unintelligible as a model — and the Qur'an says so directly:

> *"Say: Had there been angels walking about on the earth in peace, We would have sent down upon them an angel from Heaven as a messenger."* (Qur'an 17:95)

A message that must be *lived* as well as heard requires a messenger who can live it. The Prophet's humanity is not a defect in his credentials; it is the precondition of his usefulness. He must sleep, so that his followers learn how a believer sleeps. He must be hungry, wronged, bereaved, and afraid, so that the religion he brings is demonstrably practicable by beings who are hungry, wronged, bereaved and afraid. An angelic messenger could have delivered information; only a human messenger can deliver an example.

**"From Among Themselves" — The Argument From Familiarity**

*Rajulin minhum* — "a man from among themselves." The phrase that Quraysh treated as the weakness of the claim is presented by the Qur'an as its strength, and later in this same sūrah the point is made explicit: *"Indeed, I tarried among you for a lifetime before it"* (v. 16). A stranger's claim cannot be checked. A neighbour's claim can. Quraysh had four decades of data on Muhammad ﷺ, and their own name for him was *al-Amīn*, the Trustworthy. The verse quietly places them in a corner: either the man they had certified as truthful for forty years turned liar on the single subject of God, or he was telling the truth.

**The Two Halves of the Mission: Warning and Glad Tidings**

The content of the revelation is summarised in two verbs: *andhir* — "warn" — and *bashshir* — "give glad tidings." This pairing defines prophetic office throughout the Qur'an (see 4:165; 17:105; 2:213), and its order here is instructive. Warning comes first because it is the more urgent; a man asleep in a burning house must be woken before he is offered breakfast.

*Nadhīr*, from *n-dh-r*, is a warner who gives notice of impending danger in time for it to be avoided. The Arabs used the word for the scout who ran ahead of the tribe to report an approaching raid. The Prophet ﷺ used exactly this image of himself in a hadith recorded by al-Bukhārī:

> *"My likeness and the likeness of what God has sent me with is that of a man who came to a people and said, 'O people, I have seen the army with my own eyes, and I am the naked warner, so save yourselves!'"* (al-Bukhārī 6482; Muslim 2283)

The "naked warner" of pre-Islamic Arabia was the scout who stripped off his garment and waved it to signal that the danger was real and immediate — a man does not humiliate himself in public for a rumour. The self-description tells us how the Prophet understood his own warning: not as condemnation but as rescue.

*Bashshir*, from *b-sh-r*, is linguistically vivid. The root also gives *bashrah*, the skin — and *bishārah* is news so good that it visibly changes the complexion of the face that hears it. Good news, in Arabic, is news you can see arriving.

**"A Station of Sincerity" — The Meaning of *Qadam Ṣidq***

The glad tidings are given a precise content: *anna lahum qadama ṣidqin ʿinda rabbihim*, "that they shall have a station of sincerity with their Lord." Literally, *qadam ṣidq* is "a foot of truthfulness" — an idiom, and the commentators offer three main readings.

Al-Bayḍāwī takes it as precedence and high standing: the foot is what one stands on, so a "true foot" is a firm and honourable footing, a rank genuinely earned rather than claimed. Al-Rāzī reads it as the reward itself, already secured with God, waiting. Ibn Kathīr, al-Maybudī, and al-Rāzī also transmit the interpretation of Ibn ʿAbbās and Qatādah that it refers to the intercession of the Prophet ﷺ on the Day of Resurrection — a "foot" already planted on the believers' behalf in the court of judgment.

All three readings share a structure worth noticing. *Ṣidq*, truthfulness, is here an attribute not of speech but of position. A "true" footing is one that will not shift when weight is put on it. In this world people build their standing on things that are literally untrue — reputations larger than achievements, wealth that is borrowed, positions that depend on an employer's goodwill. The believer is promised standing that is *ṣidq*: it corresponds exactly to reality, and therefore it cannot be embarrassed by exposure.

**The Charge of Sorcery and Why It Was Chosen**

The verse closes with the response of the deniers: *inna hādhā la-sāḥirun mubīn*, "Surely this is a manifest sorcerer." Some readings of the text have *siḥrun mubīn*, "manifest sorcery," in which case *this* refers to the Qur'an rather than to the Prophet; al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī and al-Nasafī record both.

Why sorcery? The charge is more revealing than a charge of madness or fraud would have been. Sorcery, in Arabian usage, meant an effect that was real but illegitimately produced. Nobody calls a man a sorcerer because his words are dull. The accusation is an involuntary confession: the Qur'an was doing something to its hearers that they could not explain and could not resist. Al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah's famous verdict, preserved in the sīrah and reflected in the Qur'an's own account of him in Sūrat al-Muddaththir, was that the recitation had "a sweetness and a beauty" and that they had to say *something* — and "sorcery" was the least damaging thing available.

The same accusation was levelled at Moses in this very sūrah: *"Surely this is manifest sorcery!"* (v. 76). The repetition across centuries is part of the Qur'an's argument. When rejection of a divine message reaches for the same word in Egypt and in Makkah, the word is telling us less about the message than about the mechanism of rejection.

**What This Verse Asks of the Reader Today**

The modern form of Quraysh's objection is not "God could not speak to an orphan" but "revelation could not come through a historical human being at all — such things belong to a pre-scientific imagination." The verse's reply still holds. The question is not whether the *channel* is impressive but whether the *message* is true; and the way to test that is not to be astonished at the messenger's ordinariness but to examine what he brought. The verse's word for the wrong response — *ʿajab*, astonishment — is precisely the reaction that substitutes an emotion for an investigation.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:3

> **Truly your Lord is God, Who created the heavens and the earth in six days, then mounted the Throne, directing the affair. There is no intercessor, save by His Leave. That is God, your Lord; so worship Him! Will you not remember?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From the Messenger to the One Who Sent Him**

Having answered the objection to the messenger, the sūrah moves immediately to the subject of the message. The transition is deliberate: an argument about a prophet is only worth having if there is a God who sends prophets. So the verse begins at the foundation — *inna rabbakumu Allāh*, "Truly your Lord is God" — and then supplies, in a single sweeping sentence, the credentials of the One being named: He created, He established His sovereignty, He governs, He alone authorises intercession.

Note the pronoun. *Your* Lord. The verse does not argue for a distant deity in the abstract; it identifies the God who already stands in a relationship to the addressee, whether the addressee acknowledges it or not.

**"Six Days" — What the Qur'an Is and Is Not Saying**

*Khalaqa al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍa fī sittati ayyām.* The statement recurs seven times in the Qur'an (7:54; 10:3; 11:7; 25:59; 32:4; 50:38; 57:4), and the commentators addressed the obvious question early: what is a "day" before the existence of the sun that measures days?

The classical answer was that *yawm* in Arabic denotes a period, not necessarily a solar rotation, and that the Qur'an itself uses the word with radically different scales:

> *"The angels and the Spirit ascend unto Him in a day whose measure is fifty thousand years."* (Qur'an 70:4)

> *"And truly a day with thy Lord is as a thousand years of that which you reckon."* (Qur'an 22:47)

Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have held that the days of creation were of God's reckoning, each equal to a thousand years of ours. Al-Ṭabarī records this and related views without insisting on a figure. What the tradition consistently refuses is the notion that the six days were needed. God's creative command is *kun fa-yakūn*, "Be, and it is" (2:117). Al-Rāzī draws the natural conclusion: since instantaneous creation was fully within divine power, the sequencing is a deliberate teaching. It establishes that the universe has a history — an order of before and after, of process and development — and it models for human beings the value of measured, staged work over impulsive haste. A God who could have made everything in an instant and chose instead to build it in phases has said something about the dignity of process.

**"Then Mounted the Throne" — The Classical Position on a Difficult Phrase**

*Thumma istawā ʿalā al-ʿarsh.* This clause is among the most discussed in Islamic theology, and the sūrah's readers should know how the tradition handled it.

The verb *istawā* in Arabic can mean to be level or even; to be complete; and, with the preposition *ʿalā*, to mount, ascend, or settle upon. The *ʿarsh*, Throne, is described in the Qur'an as the greatest of created things, encompassing the heavens and the earth (2:255).

The earliest generations affirmed the statement without asking how. The most famous transmitted response is that of Imām Mālik ibn Anas (d. 179/795), recorded in numerous sources, when a questioner asked him how God mounted the Throne:

> *"The mounting is known; the how is unknown; belief in it is obligatory; and asking about it is an innovation."*

Later theologians of the Ashʿarī and Māturīdī schools generally read the phrase figuratively, as an assertion of God's sovereign command — *istawā* understood as *istawlā*, took full control — precisely in order to guard against anthropomorphism, since the Qur'an's own governing principle is *"There is nothing like unto Him"* (42:11). Ibn Taymiyyah and those following him argued for affirming the attribute as revealed, with the crucial reservation that its modality is unknown and that no created likeness may be imagined. Both camps agreed on the two boundary conditions: God is not confined by His creation, and no image drawn from thrones and kings may be transferred to Him.

For the ordinary reader, the theologically safe and spiritually fruitful reading is the one that the verse itself immediately supplies. The clause is not left hanging; it is followed at once by *yudabbiru al-amr*.

**"Directing the Affair" — Sovereignty That Is Never Absent**

*Yudabbiru al-amr* — "directing the affair." The verb comes from *dubur*, the back or rear end of a thing; to *tadbīr* something is to consider its outcome, its "back end," and to arrange matters so that they arrive there. It is the word one would use for a general planning a campaign to its conclusion, or an estate manager running a property so that every part serves the whole.

This phrase is the Qur'an's decisive answer to deism. The pagan Arabs, and many since, were willing to concede a creator who made the world and withdrew. The Qur'an concedes no such thing. The same God who created is, at this moment, managing: allocating rain, timing deaths, moving the affairs of nations, answering prayers. The clause recurs in this sūrah at v. 31 as part of the argument from providence, and elsewhere at 13:2 and 32:5.

Al-Ṭabarī glosses it as God's control over all affairs, and the sūrah develops the practical consequence: if God is actively governing, then history is not drift, and the delay in punishing the wrongdoers (v. 11) is a policy rather than an absence.

**"No Intercessor Save by His Leave" — Dismantling the Makkan Theology**

*Mā min shafīʿin illā min baʿdi idhnih.* This is the pivot of the verse, and it strikes at the exact point where Makkan religion was vulnerable.

The Makkans did not deny God. They asserted, as v. 18 of this sūrah reports, that their idols were *shufaʿāʾunā ʿinda Allāh*, "our intercessors with God." Their model was drawn from human courts: a distant king, approached through courtiers who have his ear. Access is bought through the courtiers; the king's decisions can be influenced by those with standing.

The verse dismantles this by inserting a single condition: intercession exists, but only *by His leave*. That condition destroys the entire economy the Makkans had built. If the intercessor can only speak when permitted, then he brings no independent leverage; nothing is gained by cultivating him behind the King's back. The identical logic governs the Verse of the Throne:

> *"Who is there that may intercede with Him save by His leave?"* (Qur'an 2:255)

> *"Say: Unto God belongs intercession altogether."* (Qur'an 39:44)

Islam therefore does not deny intercession — the hadith literature is explicit that the Prophet ﷺ will intercede on the Day of Judgment, and the great hadith of the intercession in al-Bukhārī and Muslim describes humanity going from prophet to prophet until they reach Muhammad ﷺ, who prostrates beneath the Throne and is told: *"Raise your head, ask and you will be given, intercede and your intercession will be accepted"* (al-Bukhārī 4712; Muslim 194). What Islam denies is intercession as *influence* — as a lever by which a creature can move the Creator. Permission flows downward from God; it is never leverage applied upward.

**The Psychology Behind Idolatry's Appeal**

Why did the Makkans want intermediaries at all, when they already acknowledged Allāh? The answer, then and now, is that a mediated god is a manageable god. Intermediaries can be bargained with. A stone can be fed, dressed, circumambulated, and satisfied; and once satisfied, it makes no further moral demand on your conduct at home or in the market. Direct accountability to a God who sees everything and cannot be bought is a far more disturbing arrangement.

The modern reader should not assume immunity here. The intermediaries change form — the belief that a donation offsets a dishonesty, that ritual observance purchases exemption from ethics, that some category of person will "sort it out" on one's behalf — but the underlying wish is identical: to keep God at a distance where He can be negotiated with. This verse closes that distance.

**"That Is God, Your Lord; So Worship Him!"**

*Dhālikumu Allāhu rabbukum fa-ʿbudūh.* The demonstrative *dhālikum* points back to everything just said: the Creator of the heavens and earth, the Sovereign upon the Throne, the Manager of all affairs, the sole Authoriser of intercession. *That* is your Lord.

The commentators note that this phrase recurs in the Qur'an (6:102; 10:32; 35:13; 39:6; 40:62, 64) and is always adjacent to some tangible demonstration of God's dealing with creation. The pattern is an argument in miniature: first evidence, then identification, then the conclusion — *so worship Him*.

Note that worship is presented as the *conclusion of an argument*, not as an arbitrary imposition. The reasoning is: the One who originated you, sustains you, governs your circumstances and holds your outcome is the only being to whom absolute devotion is a rational response. *Jalālayn* observes that the phrase calls attention to God's *ulūhiyyah* (divinity, His right to be worshipped) alongside His *rubūbiyyah* (lordship, His creating and sustaining), and Ibn Kathīr draws the classical conclusion: acknowledging the second without the first is precisely the incoherence of which the Makkans were guilty. They conceded that God made them, and then knelt to something else.

**"Will You Not Remember?" — The Verse's Closing Appeal**

*A-fa-lā tadhakkarūn.* The verb is from *dh-k-r*, to remember, to be mindful — and its choice is precise. The verse does not ask, "Will you not learn?" as though the information were new. It asks, "Will you not *remember*?" — because on the Qur'an's account this knowledge is not foreign to the human being. It is buried, and revelation excavates it.

Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Kāshānī understand the phrase as an appeal to *tafakkur*, reflection, noting that even a minimal degree of honest reflection turns the attention toward God's lordship. That is a modest and realistic claim. The verse does not demand a metaphysical proof; it asks for a moment of undistracted attention. And this is exactly what modern life is engineered to prevent. A person can now pass an entire day without a single unfilled interval — the commute has a podcast, the meal has a screen, the walk has music, the moment before sleep has a feed. The faculty this verse appeals to needs silence to operate. *Will you not remember?* is, in practice, an invitation to switch something off.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:4

> **Unto Him is your return all together; God's Promise is true. Verily He originates creation, then He brings it back, that He may recompense with justice those who believe and perform righteous deeds. As for the disbelievers, theirs shall be a drink of boiling liquid and a painful punishment for having disbelieved.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Symmetry of Origin and Return**

Al-Rāzī makes a fine structural observation here: the previous verse began with the *origin* of creation, so this verse begins with the *return* to God. The two form a single arc, and Islamic thought gave the arc a technical vocabulary — *al-mabdaʾ* (the Origin) and *al-maʿād* (the Return). The Qur'an states the pair repeatedly: *"He originates creation, then brings it back"* (10:34; 27:64; 30:11, 27).

*Ilayhi marjiʿukum jamīʿan* — "Unto Him is your return, all together." The word *marjiʿ* is a noun of place and time from *r-j-ʿ*, to return: it means both the act of returning and the destination returned to. And the small word *jamīʿan*, "all together," carries a warning that the Makkan nobility would have felt. In this world people are separated into ranks — the chief and the servant, the creditor and the debtor. The return is *jamīʿan*: everyone, on the same day, in the same assembly, on the same terms.

**Why the Argument From Origin to Return Is Rationally Compelling**

The verse does not merely assert resurrection; it argues for it, and the argument is compressed into the phrase *innahu yabdaʾu al-khalqa thumma yuʿīduh* — "Verily He originates creation, then He brings it back."

The logic is *a fortiori*: whoever can do the harder thing can do the easier. Bringing something into existence from nothing is a greater act than restoring something that has already existed. The Qur'an makes this reasoning explicit elsewhere:

> *"And He it is Who originates creation, then brings it back, and that is easier for Him."* (Qur'an 30:27)

> *"Does man not consider that We created him from a drop, and behold, he is an open adversary? And he sets forth for Us a parable and forgets his own creation, saying, 'Who revives these bones when they are decayed?' Say: He will revive them Who brought them forth the first time, and He knows every creation."* (Qur'an 36:77–79)

The counter-intuition being addressed is real. To the natural mind, the decomposition of a body seems irreversible in a way that its original formation does not, because we witness the second happening constantly and never witness the first being undone. The Qur'an's reply is that the appearance of difficulty is an artefact of our perspective. For a power that does not work with tools and does not tire, "harder" and "easier" as we experience them do not apply.

**"That He May Recompense With Justice" — The Moral Necessity of the Return**

*Li-yajziya alladhīna āmanū wa-ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāti bi-l-qisṭ.* Here the verse gives the *purpose* of the return, and the reason is moral rather than merely metaphysical. Resurrection exists so that recompense can be delivered *bi-l-qisṭ*, with equity.

This is one of the strongest arguments the Qur'an offers, and it deserves to be stated plainly. Every human being possesses an ineradicable sense that wrongs ought to be righted. We do not merely dislike injustice; we experience it as a violation of how things *should* be. Yet the observable world does not deliver on that expectation. Tyrants die comfortable in old age. Children are murdered and no one is ever charged. Whole populations are ground down by systems whose architects are never identified. If death is final, then the moral intuition that has driven every legal system, every reform movement, and every act of conscience in human history is a systematic delusion — a demand built into us for which no reality corresponds.

The Qur'an's position is that the demand is not a delusion but a signpost, and that the docket is not closed but adjourned. Justice is coming; the court date is fixed.

**The Word *Qisṭ* and Its Precision**

*Qisṭ* is worth distinguishing from its near-synonym *ʿadl*. Both are translated "justice," but *qisṭ* carries the sense of an allotted portion given in full measure — it is related to *qisṭās*, the balance-scale. Its opposite, from the same root in a different form, is *qāsiṭ*, one who deviates from the just share. So *bi-l-qisṭ* means recompense in which every party receives exactly the portion due, neither clipped nor inflated. Later in this sūrah the same standard is applied to the judgment scene: *"And judgment shall be rendered between them with justice, and they will not be wronged"* (vv. 47, 54).

Notice also what is being recompensed: *āmanū wa-ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāt*, "believe and perform righteous deeds." The pairing occurs some seventy times in the Qur'an, and its consistency is doctrinally significant. Faith without works is not the Qur'an's picture of a saved person, and works without faith lack the orientation that makes them worship. Belief is the root; action is the fruit; the Qur'an almost never mentions one without the other.

**The Drink of Boiling Liquid: On Reading Qur'anic Descriptions of Punishment**

*Sharābun min ḥamīmin wa-ʿadhābun alīmun bi-mā kānū yakfurūn.* *Ḥamīm* denotes water heated to its extremity — scalding, boiling. The image recurs across the Qur'an (6:70; 37:67; 56:42, 54; 44:48).

Three observations help the reader receive such passages rightly.

First, the imagery is chosen for its intelligibility, not its arbitrariness. A drink is what one turns to in thirst for relief; to be given boiling water is to have the very instrument of relief become the instrument of torment. The picture encodes the logic of the punishment: what was sought as comfort turns to harm.

Second, the verse attaches the punishment to a cause and repeats it for emphasis: *bi-mā kānū yakfurūn*, "for having disbelieved." Qur'anic punishment is never presented as divine temper. It is consequence, and this sūrah will state the principle with unusual clarity: *"Truly God does not wrong human beings in the least, but rather human beings wrong themselves"* (v. 44).

Third, the placement is pedagogical. Warning is set immediately beside promise, in a single verse, in a fixed proportion. Islam's spiritual method is *bayna al-khawf wa-l-rajāʾ*, between fear and hope. Fear alone produces despair, and despair produces paralysis; hope alone produces presumption, and presumption produces laziness. The classical scholars, Ibn al-Qayyim among them, likened the two to the wings of a bird: with one alone, the bird spins on the ground.

**The Meaning of *Kufr* — Ingratitude Before It Is Denial**

The final word deserves attention because English "disbelief" obscures it. The root *k-f-r* means, at base, to cover or conceal. An Arab farmer was called a *kāfir* because he buries the seed under earth; night is *kāfir* because it covers the world. From this root comes the Qur'anic *kufr*: the covering over of a truth that is, at some level, known — and also the covering over of a benefit received, which is why the same root supplies the ordinary Arabic word for ingratitude.

This is why the Qur'an so often opposes *kufr* not to faith but to thankfulness:

> *"So remember Me; I will remember you. And give thanks to Me and do not be ungrateful (lā takfurūn)."* (Qur'an 2:152)

Understood this way, the punishment described here is not for an intellectual error honestly arrived at. It is for the deliberate burial of a recognised truth and the systematic refusal to acknowledge a benefactor from whom one accepts benefits daily. That is a moral posture, not a cognitive accident — and it is precisely the posture this sūrah will describe in v. 12, in the man who prays in his sickness and forgets in his health.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:5

> **He it is Who made the sun a radiance, and the moon a light, and determined for it stations, that you might know the number of years and the reckoning [of time]. God did not create these, save in truth. He expounds the signs for a people who know.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument Moves Outdoors**

Having established the Creator, the Throne, the governance and the Return, the sūrah now does something characteristic of the Qur'an: it stops arguing abstractly and points at the sky. The Makkan audience did not need to be persuaded to look up. They were desert people who navigated by stars, timed their pilgrimages and markets by lunar months, and knew the heavens with a practical intimacy that most modern city-dwellers, whose skies are washed out by streetlight, have entirely lost.

**Two Different Words for Two Different Luminaries**

The precision here has attracted attention for centuries. The sun is *ḍiyāʾ*; the moon is *nūr*. The Qur'an does not use the words interchangeably, and the distinction holds wherever the pair occurs:

> *"And made the moon therein a light (nūran), and made the sun a lamp (sirājan)."* (Qur'an 71:16)

> *"And placed therein a lamp (sirājan) and a moon giving light (munīran)."* (Qur'an 25:61)

*Ḍiyāʾ*, from the root *ḍ-w-ʾ*, denotes brilliance that is intrinsic and usually accompanied by heat — the light of a fire or a blazing lamp. *Nūr*, from *n-w-r*, denotes light in the general sense of illumination, gentler, and in Arabic usage frequently light that falls upon a thing rather than blazing from it.

The classical commentators noticed and explained this. Al-Bayḍāwī states plainly that the moon is called *nūr* because it receives its luminosity from the sun. Ibn Kathīr similarly distinguishes the sun's own burning radiance from the moon's borrowed illumination. This is a matter of internal Qur'anic diction and classical exegesis, not a retrofitted modern claim, and it is a good example of how carefully the Book selects its terms.

**"Determined for It Stations" — Measurement as a Sign of Design**

*Wa-qaddarahu manāzil.* The verb *qaddara* is from *q-d-r*, the root of *qadar*, divine measuring-out or decree. Its basic sense is to determine a precise quantity — to measure, to apportion, to set exactly.

*Manāzil* are "stations" or lodging-places, from *nazala*, to descend or alight. The Arabs mapped twenty-eight lunar mansions, one for each night of the moon's visible cycle, and used them to organise agriculture, travel and weather-lore. The Qur'an refers to the same phenomenon in Sūrah Yā Sīn:

> *"And the moon: We have determined for it stations, till it returns like an aged palm-frond."* (Qur'an 36:39)

That closing image — the thin, curved, yellowed old date-branch — is one of the most exact visual descriptions of a waning crescent in any literature.

The theological point of *qaddara* is that the cosmos runs on measurement. The moon does not wander; it keeps an appointment. Regularity is the signature of intention, because randomness does not produce timetables. The Qur'an makes the inference explicit two verses later in Sūrah Yā Sīn: *"The sun is not able to overtake the moon, nor does the night outstrip the day; each swims in an orbit"* (36:40).

**Why God Gave Humanity a Clock in the Sky**

*Li-taʿlamū ʿadada al-sinīna wa-l-ḥisāb* — "that you might know the number of years and the reckoning of time." Here the verse states a *purpose*, and the purpose is human.

Consider what would be impossible without a public, unfalsifiable, universally visible calendar. No contract could specify a due date. No debt could be repaid at an agreed term. No caravan could arrange to meet another. No pilgrimage could gather a scattered people at one place at one time. No historian could date an event. No farmer could plant to a season. Every civilisation on earth built its calendar by looking at these two bodies, and every civilisation had to.

The Qur'an builds worship itself upon this astronomy. The fast begins and ends with the sighting of the moon; the pilgrimage is fixed to lunar months; the five daily prayers are timed by the sun's position — dawn, midday decline, afternoon shadow-length, sunset, nightfall. The Prophet ﷺ tied the community's ritual life to what any illiterate shepherd could verify with his own eyes:

> *"Fast when you see it and break your fast when you see it; and if it is obscured from you, then complete thirty days."* (al-Bukhārī 1909; Muslim 1081)

There is a quiet democratic wisdom in this. A religion whose sacred times depend on an instrument that only priests can read gives the priests control over the religion. A religion timed by the sky belongs to everyone.

**"Save in Truth" — Against a Purposeless Universe**

*Mā khalaqa Allāhu dhālika illā bi-l-ḥaqq.* The commentators — *Jalālayn*, al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī — explain *bi-l-ḥaqq* here to mean that these creations possess reality and purpose and were not made in vain. The contrast is with *bāṭil*, futility, and the Qur'an poses it directly:

> *"And We did not create the heaven and the earth and what is between them in vain (bāṭilan). That is the assumption of those who disbelieve."* (Qur'an 38:27)

> *"Our Lord, You did not create this in vain; glory be to You!"* (Qur'an 3:191)

This is one of the great dividing questions, and it has not aged. Modern secular thought has largely settled on the answer that the universe is a brute fact with no intrinsic purpose, and that meaning is a private construction laid over meaninglessness. The Qur'an's counter-claim is that purposelessness is an *assumption* — the verse in Sūrah Ṣād calls it exactly that, *ẓann*, the same word this sūrah attacks in vv. 36 and 66 — and that the observable order of the thing argues the other way. A system of interlocking regularities precise enough to build a civilisation upon is a strange sort of accident.

**Two Audiences, Two Words: "A People Who Know"**

*Yufaṣṣilu al-āyāti li-qawmin yaʿlamūn* — "He expounds the signs for a people who know." The verb *yufaṣṣil*, from *f-ṣ-l*, means to separate into distinct parts, to detail, to set out item by item. The signs are not dumped in a heap; they are articulated.

But the expounding is *li-qawmin yaʿlamūn*, for people who know. The next verse will end with a different qualifier: *li-qawmin yattaqūn*, "for a people who are reverent" (v. 6); elsewhere in this sūrah we find *li-qawmin yatafakkarūn*, who reflect (v. 24), and *li-qawmin yasmaʿūn*, who hear (v. 67). The variation is not decorative. The Qur'an is telling us that the same sign lands differently depending on the receiver.

The sun rises identically for everyone. Whether it functions as a *sign* depends on the state of the observer. A person with knowledge sees mechanism and infers a Designer; a person with reverence sees transience and infers accountability; a person who is heedless sees only the light by which to do business. The datum is constant; the reading is not.

**A Practical Reflection: Recovering the Sky**

There is a particular loss in modern life that this verse quietly diagnoses. Our relationship with the sun and moon has been mediated almost out of existence. We know the date from a device, the time from a screen, the weather from an app. Most people in a city cannot say what phase the moon is in tonight. The instruments God placed overhead to be read by every human being have been replaced by instruments that require nothing of us but a glance.

The Islamic calendar's insistence on physical moon-sighting, and the daily prayer's insistence on solar timing, are among the few remaining structures that force a believer to look up. The verse suggests this is not incidental. A person who steps outside to find the crescent has done something that a person who consults an app has not: he has renewed, for a moment, the relationship that this verse describes.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:6

> **Surely in the variation of the night and the day and whatsoever God has created in the heavens and on the earth are signs for a people who are reverent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From the Bodies to Their Motion**

The previous verse pointed at the sun and the moon as objects; this one points at the *rhythm* they produce. *Ikhtilāf al-layl wa-l-nahār* — literally "the difference," or the succession and alternation, of night and day.

The root *kh-l-f* means to come after, to succeed, to take the place of another — the same root that gives *khalīfah*, the successor or vicegerent (a word this sūrah will use in v. 14). So *ikhtilāf al-layl wa-l-nahār* is not only "the difference between" but "the taking-turns of" night and day: each steps into the place the other vacates, endlessly, without collision and without failure. The Qur'an uses the phrase repeatedly as a proof (2:164; 3:190; 23:80; 45:5).

**Why an Everyday Fact Counts as Evidence**

Someone might object that nothing is more ordinary than sunrise. That objection is precisely what the verse is designed to overcome, and its force depends on a psychological fact the Qur'an understands well: familiarity destroys perception. We stop seeing what we always see.

Yet consider what the alternation actually accomplishes. It regulates the sleep cycle of nearly every complex organism on earth. It divides labour from rest so that human beings can sustain effort without collapse. It makes agriculture possible by cycling temperature. It gives the species a shared, involuntary unit of time — the day — which is the basis of memory, planning, and history itself. And it does all this by a mechanism so stable that we can predict sunrise in a given city a thousand years hence to the minute.

An engineer who saw a machine of this reliability would ask who built it. We do not ask, only because we were born inside the machine.

**The Second Half: Everything Else, Too**

*Wa-mā khalaqa Allāhu fī al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍ* — "and whatsoever God has created in the heavens and on the earth." The clause is deliberately unbounded. Having named a specific sign, the verse throws the category open: *everything* qualifies.

This is the Qur'anic vision of a world densely populated with evidence. There is no privileged class of holy objects that carry meaning while the rest of matter is inert. A fig, a bee, a spider's house, an ant's warning to its colony, iron, milk, the human embryo, the mountains as pegs — the Qur'an takes its evidence from wherever it lies. Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Rāzī observe that what all created things share, and what makes each of them a sign, is *finitude*: each is limited, dependent, and passing, and therefore each points beyond itself to what is unlimited, independent and enduring.

**"For a People Who Are Reverent" — Why Taqwā Is the Organ of Sight Here**

The verse's qualifier changes from the previous one. There, the signs were expounded for *a people who know*; here they are signs *li-qawmin yattaqūn*, for a people who are reverent — who possess *taqwā*.

*Taqwā* comes from *w-q-y*, to guard, shield, protect oneself. A *wiqāyah* is a protective covering. So *taqwā* is not raw fear; it is *protective alertness* — the state of a person who is aware that harm is possible and who moves accordingly.

The classical definition given by ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib, widely transmitted in works of spirituality, captures it: *"Taqwā is fear of the Majestic, action according to what has been revealed, contentment with little, and preparation for the day of departure."* And the famous exchange with Ubayy ibn Kaʿb makes the meaning vivid. Asked what *taqwā* is, he replied, "Have you ever walked a path full of thorns?" The questioner said yes. "What did you do?" "I gathered up my garment and picked my way carefully." Ubayy said: *"That is taqwā."*

This is the analogy that unlocks the concept, and it is worth extending into modern experience. Consider a person driving at night on a mountain road in rain. He is not paralysed with terror — if he were, he could not drive at all. He is *awake*: hands on the wheel, speed moderated, eyes scanning, aware that the margin for error is thin. He is not enjoying the drive less; he is simply not pretending the road is a car park. That combination — full functioning plus continuous awareness of real consequences — is *taqwā*.

Now the verse's logic becomes clear. Why do signs appear to the reverent in particular? Because *taqwā* is precisely the state of attentiveness in which signs can register. The distracted driver misses the road sign not because it is small but because he is not looking. *Jalālayn* and Ājurrūmī-influenced commentators gloss the reverent here as those mindful of God and of their final outcome; al-Rāzī adds that this attitude leads them to reflect deeply on the nature of things. Mindfulness of outcome produces attention to evidence.

**The Transience Written Into the Rhythm**

The commentators draw one further meaning from the alternation of night and day that is easy to miss. Al-Bayḍāwī, al-Rāzī and Ājībah note that the ebb and flow of light and darkness itself teaches impermanence. Every day that dawns is subtracted from a fixed allowance. The same rhythm that makes life workable is also the mechanism by which life is spent.

This is why the Qur'an can treat the passage of time as an argument, and why an entire sūrah swears by it:

> *"By time! Truly mankind is in loss — save those who believe, and perform righteous deeds, and counsel one another to truth, and counsel one another to patience."* (Qur'an 103:1–3)

Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī is reported to have said: *"O son of Adam, you are nothing but a number of days; whenever a day passes, part of you has gone."* The night and day are not a neutral backdrop to our lives. They are the meter running.

**A Poet's Summary and the Verse's Final Force**

The commentary tradition preserves, in connection with this verse, a couplet by the poet Abū al-ʿAtāhiyah (d. 213/828):

> *"And in every thing there is a sign of Him, indicating that He is One."*

The line states the verse's whole doctrine in a breath. The Qur'an's evidential method is not to demand that the reader accept an argument he cannot check. It is to ask him to attend properly to what he already sees every day of his life — and to point out that the person who is genuinely awake to consequences is the person most likely to notice.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:7

> **Truly those who anticipate not the meeting with Us, and who are content with the life of this world and feel secure therein, and who are heedless of Our signs,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Clinical Diagnosis in Three Symptoms**

The previous verse described those for whom the world is full of signs. This verse describes the opposite type, and it does so with the precision of a physician listing symptoms. Three clauses, and each names a distinct condition of the soul: they do not anticipate the meeting; they are content with this world and feel secure in it; they are heedless of the signs.

The order is causal, not random. Each condition produces the next.

**First Symptom: Not Anticipating the Meeting**

*Alladhīna lā yarjūna liqāʾanā.* The verb *rajā* means to hope for, to expect, to look forward to. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Ṭūsī explain the phrase as describing people who reject the idea of a Hereafter altogether. Al-Rāzī gives it a warmer reading: to *anticipate* the meeting with God is to desire His recompense and mercy, and al-Qurṭubī connects it to the supreme hope of the beatific vision, alluded to in *"Faces that Day will be radiant, gazing upon their Lord"* (75:22–23).

So the failure named here is a failure of *hope* before it is a failure of belief. These are people for whom the meeting with God is not an object of longing. Whether they deny it outright or simply never think about it, functionally it plays no part in their inner life.

The Qur'an regards this as the decisive variable in a human life, because everything downstream depends on it. A person's behaviour is governed less by what he professes than by what he *expects*. A student who genuinely expects an examination studies; one who does not, does not — regardless of what he says about examinations in general.

**Second Symptom: Contentment and False Security**

*Wa-raḍū bi-l-ḥayāti al-dunyā wa-ṭmaʾannū bihā.* Two verbs, and each is significant.

*Raḍū* — "they are content, satisfied, pleased." This is not the word for enjoyment; it is the word for *acceptance as sufficient*. It is what one says of a man who is offered a settlement and takes it: he is content with it, meaning he asks for nothing further. These people have been offered this world and have accepted it as the full payment for their existence.

*Iṭmaʾannū* — "they feel secure, tranquil, settled." The root *ṭ-m-ʾ-n* denotes coming to rest, being at ease, the heart's stillness. It is, remarkably, a positive word elsewhere in the Qur'an: *"Truly in the remembrance of God do hearts find rest (taṭmaʾinnu al-qulūb)"* (13:28), and *"O soul at peace (al-nafs al-muṭmaʾinnah), return unto thy Lord"* (89:27–28).

The Qur'an is therefore making a pointed statement: the human heart is built to come to rest somewhere. Rest is not optional. The only question is *where*. The tragedy described here is not that these people rest, but that they have anchored in a harbour that will not hold — they have made their peace with something that is itself passing away. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī gloss the state as directing all one's aspirations toward this world and its transient trappings, and al-Kalbī adds: *acting as though they will remain in it forever*.

**The Word *Dunyā* and What It Actually Means**

*Al-ḥayāt al-dunyā* is usually rendered "the life of this world," but the Arabic says more. *Dunyā* is a superlative feminine adjective from *d-n-w*, and it carries two senses simultaneously: "the nearest" and "the lowest."

Both are exact. This life is the *nearest* — it is what is immediately present to the senses, the thing we are standing in. And it is the *lowest* — of lesser worth than what follows. The Qur'an never names this world by a neutral term; every time it says *al-dunyā*, it has already made an evaluation.

The two senses together explain the phenomenon the verse describes. What is nearest is naturally the most vivid. A small object held close to the eye blocks out a mountain. The error of the *dunyā*-content is not that they see this world — everyone does — but that they let its proximity determine their estimate of its size.

The Prophet ﷺ used exactly this proportion:

> *"By God, this world compared to the Hereafter is nothing but as one of you dipping his finger in the sea — let him see what he brings back."* (Muslim 2858, from al-Mustawrid ibn Shaddād)

The image is masterful. The finger does come back wet — this world is not nothing, and Islam does not teach that it is. But the ratio of the drop to the ocean is the ratio being misjudged.

**Third Symptom: Heedlessness of the Signs**

*Wa-lladhīna hum ʿan āyātinā ghāfilūn.* The third condition follows from the second. *Ghaflah* is heedlessness — not ignorance, but inattention. A *ghāfil* is not someone who lacks the information; he is someone whose attention is elsewhere.

Al-Bayḍāwī observes that this heedlessness arises *bi-mā kānū yaksibūn*, "on account of that which they used to earn" — their absorption in acquisition. This is a precise causal claim, and it is verifiable in ordinary life. Attention is finite. It is the one resource that cannot be expanded by effort. A man whose attention is fully committed to earning, competing, and consuming has none left over, and the signs that surround him become, functionally, invisible.

The Qur'an's most severe statement of this condition is worth setting alongside the verse:

> *"They know the outward of the life of this world, but of the Hereafter they are heedless."* (Qur'an 30:7)

That verse grants such people a genuine expertise. They *know* things — markets, systems, techniques, the outward surface of the world. The deficiency is not intelligence. It is that the whole of a considerable intelligence has been deployed on the surface layer.

**Why Comfort Is Spiritually Dangerous**

This verse identifies something modern religious discourse often avoids: that ease is a greater threat to faith than hardship. The suffering man asks the ultimate questions because he is forced to. The comfortable man is under no such compulsion, and the entire apparatus of modern life is arranged to ensure he never is.

The Prophet ﷺ named this danger explicitly:

> *"By God, it is not poverty that I fear for you, but I fear that the world will be spread out before you as it was spread out before those before you, and that you will compete for it as they competed for it, and it will destroy you as it destroyed them."* (al-Bukhārī 3158; Muslim 2961, from ʿAmr ibn ʿAwf)

Note the structure of the warning: what destroys is not possession but *competition* — the state in which the world becomes the measure by which one ranks oneself against others. That is precisely *raḍū bihā wa-ṭmaʾannū bihā*: having settled on this world as the arena in which one's life will be scored.

**The Diagnosis Applied Today**

It is worth being concrete, because this verse can be read as being about seventh-century pagans and thereby escaped.

Consider a person of the present day who would describe himself as a believer. He affirms the Hereafter if asked. But: he has not thought about death in months; his sense of whether his life is going well is determined entirely by income, health, and the regard of colleagues; a reversal in any of those produces genuine despair, while a lapse in prayer produces mild discomfort at most; and he passes each day inside a stream of content that leaves no gap in which reflection might occur.

That person satisfies all three clauses of this verse. He does not *deny* the meeting; he simply does not *anticipate* it. He is content with the *dunyā* and settled in it. And he is *ghāfil* — not from lack of information, but from lack of available attention.

The verse's diagnosis is not aimed only at atheists. It is aimed at a way of living, and it is entirely possible to hold orthodox beliefs while living exactly this way. That is what makes it uncomfortable, and useful.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:8

> **it is they whose refuge shall be the Fire for that which they used to earn.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verdict That Completes the Diagnosis**

The previous verse listed the symptoms; this verse gives the prognosis. Grammatically the two form a single sentence — a long conditional subject followed at last by its predicate — and the delay is itself rhetorically effective. The listener is held through three clauses of description before the outcome lands.

**The Bitter Irony of *Maʾwā***

*Ulāʾika maʾwāhumu al-nār* — "it is they whose refuge shall be the Fire."

The word *maʾwā* is the heart of this verse. It comes from *a-w-y*, to take shelter, to seek a place of return. A *maʾwā* is where one goes to be safe — a den for an animal, a home for a traveller, a harbour for a ship. The Qur'an uses it for Paradise as well: *jannat al-maʾwā*, "the Garden of Refuge" (53:15).

To pair this word with *al-nār*, the Fire, is a deliberate and devastating irony. The place they will retreat to is the thing they should be fleeing. And the irony is not gratuitous; it is exactly appropriate to the condition described in v. 7. Those people *sought refuge* in the *dunyā*. They looked for security (*iṭmaʾnān*) in a place that could not provide it. The outcome preserves the structure of the choice: you wanted a shelter that was not God; here is a shelter that is not God.

Compare the parallel image in Sūrat al-ʿAnkabūt:

> *"The likeness of those who take protectors apart from God is the likeness of the spider that takes a house — and truly the frailest of houses is the house of the spider, if they but knew."* (Qur'an 29:41)

The spider's web is a genuine house. It is also, structurally, nothing. That is the *maʾwā* of anyone who anchors in the passing world.

**"For That Which They Used to Earn" — The Language of Wages**

*Bi-mā kānū yaksibūn.* The verb *kasaba* is the ordinary Arabic word for earning — what a merchant does, what a labourer does. Its use here is one of the Qur'an's most important moral metaphors.

The metaphor asserts three things at once. First, *agency*: earnings are not accidents; a wage is the result of work done. Second, *accumulation*: earning happens incrementally, transaction by transaction, day by day — no one earns a fortune in a moment. Third, *proportion*: what one receives corresponds to what one did.

The continuous past construction *kānū yaksibūn*, "they used to earn," reinforces the second point. This is habitual action over a lifetime, not a single catastrophic sin. The Qur'an is describing a career, not an accident.

And the metaphor cuts both ways, which is why it is fair. The same root governs reward:

> *"For it is what it has earned (mā kasabat), and against it is what it has perpetrated."* (Qur'an 2:286)

**Consequence Rather Than Retribution**

A recurring modern objection to Qur'anic eschatology runs: how can finite wrongdoing merit unending punishment? The objection deserves a serious answer, and the vocabulary of this verse contains part of it.

The verse does not say the Fire is *imposed* on them; it says the Fire is their *maʾwā* — the place they end up, the natural terminus of the trajectory described in v. 7. On this reading, the Hereafter is less a sentencing than a disclosure: the removal of the material scaffolding that concealed, in this life, what a soul had actually become. A person who spent seventy years training himself to be unable to bear the remembrance of God is not being arbitrarily excluded from God's presence; he has become the sort of creature for whom that presence is unbearable.

The tradition also holds open considerations of mercy that the plainest reading might obscure. The Fire is repeatedly described as containing people who will be removed from it: the hadith of the intercession in al-Bukhārī and Muslim describes God bringing out of the Fire those in whose hearts was faith *"the weight of the smallest atom"* (al-Bukhārī 7439; Muslim 193). And this very sūrah, at v. 52, uses the word *khuld* for the punishment — a word which Ibn Qayyim al-Jawziyyah argued can denote a long but finite duration, a position he set out at length in *Shifāʾ al-ʿalīl*.

None of this softens the warning. It clarifies its structure: the warning is real, the mercy is real, and both are held in the same hand.

**The Practical Weight of Two Verses Read Together**

Verses 7 and 8 together form one of the Qur'an's most efficient moral instruments, because they permit a person to audit himself without waiting for the verdict.

The audit is simple. Ask: what am I actually earning? Not what do I intend, or believe, or say — what is the daily accumulation? Where does my attention go when it is not compelled? What news would genuinely devastate me, and what news would merely trouble me? A person's real theology is revealed by the second question far more reliably than by any statement of creed.

The mercy in the verse's placement is that it comes *now*, while the earning is still in progress. A statement of accounts delivered mid-year is not a punishment; it is an opportunity. That is exactly why the Qur'an describes the Fire to people who are not yet in it.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:9

> **Truly those who believe and perform righteous deeds, their Lord guides them by their faith. They shall have rivers running below them in Gardens of bliss.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Deliberate Symmetry**

Two verses described the trajectory of denial and its terminus. Now, with the same grammatical shape — a described subject followed by its outcome — the Qur'an gives the opposite trajectory. This pairing is one of the Book's most consistent structural habits, and it is a teaching device: the mind grasps a category best when it is placed beside its opposite.

Notice the exact correspondences. There: *lā yarjūna liqāʾanā*, they do not anticipate the meeting; here: *āmanū*, they believed. There: heedless of the signs; here: *ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāt*, they acted on them. There: *maʾwāhumu al-nār*, the Fire is their refuge; here: *jannāt al-naʿīm*, Gardens of bliss. Point for point, the two verses are a mirror.

**"Guides Them by Their Faith" — The Verse's Central Puzzle**

*Yahdīhim rabbuhum bi-īmānihim.* This phrase repays careful thought, because at first glance it seems circular. If they already have faith, they are already guided — what further guidance is being given?

The commentators offer several complementary readings.

Al-Qurṭubī's answer is that God *increases them in guidance*. Faith is not a terminus but a starting capital, and God adds to it. This reading is strongly supported by the Qur'an's own statements:

> *"And those who are guided, He increases them in guidance and gives them their reverence."* (Qur'an 47:17)

> *"He it is Who sent down tranquillity into the hearts of the believers, that they might increase in faith along with their faith."* (Qur'an 48:4)

*Jalālayn* offers a second reading: God will grant them a *light* by which they will be guided on the Day of Resurrection. This connects the phrase to the eschatological scene:

> *"On the Day when you see the believing men and believing women, their light running before them and on their right..."* (Qur'an 57:12)

> *"Our Lord, perfect for us our light and forgive us."* (Qur'an 66:8)

On this reading, faith held in this world becomes, quite literally, illumination in the next — the internal made external.

Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī offer a third: He guides them *to Paradise*, faith being the credential by which they are conducted there.

**Faith as a Faculty of Perception**

There is a further sense, implied by the preposition *bi-* ("by" their faith), that deserves drawing out, because it names something believers actually experience.

*Bi-īmānihim* can be read instrumentally: faith is the *means* by which the guidance operates. On this understanding, faith is not merely a set of propositions assented to; it is a capacity that changes what a person can perceive and how he weighs what he perceives.

Two people face an identical setback — a business failure, an illness, a bereavement. One experiences it as meaningless damage. The other experiences the same event as containing a summons, a discipline, possibly a mercy. Nothing external differs. The difference lies in the interpretive faculty brought to the event, and that faculty is *īmān*.

This is why the Prophet ﷺ could describe the believer's condition as uniformly advantageous:

> *"Amazing is the affair of the believer! All of his affair is good, and that is for no one but the believer. If ease comes to him he is grateful, and that is good for him; and if hardship comes to him he is patient, and that is good for him."* (Muslim 2999, from Ṣuhayb)

The hadith does not claim the believer suffers less. It claims that faith converts both outcomes into gain. That is guidance *by* faith.

**The Cumulative Logic of Guidance**

There is also a mechanism here that is observable in ordinary life and worth stating plainly, because it makes the verse's promise intelligible rather than merely comforting.

Guidance is cumulative because acting on what you know makes you fit to receive more. A student who does the exercises is ready for the next chapter; one who does not is not, regardless of how much he wishes to advance. The same principle governs the moral life: a person who acts on the light he has develops the capacity for more light, while a person who ignores it loses even what he had. The Qur'an states the negative version of this law in the very next movement of many of its arguments — and states the positive version as a divine promise in the hadith qudsī of drawing near:

> *"My servant continues to draw near to Me with voluntary works until I love him; and when I love him, I become his hearing with which he hears, his sight with which he sees, his hand with which he strikes, and his foot with which he walks."* (al-Bukhārī 6502, from Abū Hurayrah)

Whatever the depths of that hadith, its plain structure is the one this verse describes: action taken on the basis of faith results in a transformation of the faculties themselves.

**"Rivers Running Below Them" — Reading the Description of Paradise**

*Tajrī min taḥtihimu al-anhāru fī jannāti al-naʿīm.* The phrase "rivers running below them" occurs in this exact form (with *taḥtihim*, "below them," rather than the more common *taḥtihā*, "below it") in only a few places (see also 7:43).

Al-Rāzī notes the implication: the inhabitants are seated on raised couches, with the water flowing beneath them — as in *"upon raised couches"* (88:13). The picture is of elevation and repose, not of labour beside a stream.

Some reflections on why the imagery is what it is:

*Jannah* itself means a garden so densely planted that its ground is hidden — from *j-n-n*, to cover or conceal, the same root as *janīn* (foetus, hidden in the womb) and *jinn* (the concealed creatures). Paradise is named for shade and covering, which to a desert people meant everything.

The rivers matter for the same reason. In a landscape where water is scarce, unpredictable, and worth fighting over, a *running* river is the image of security itself: not a cistern that can empty, but a supply that keeps arriving.

And *naʿīm*, from *n-ʿ-m*, is not merely pleasure but *sustained ease and favour*; the same root gives *niʿmah*, a blessing, and *anʿām*, livestock — wealth on the hoof. "Gardens of *naʿīm*" are gardens of continuous, effortless bounty.

The commentators were careful to insist, however, that these descriptions are indications rather than exhaustive depictions. The hadith qudsī is decisive:

> *"I have prepared for My righteous servants what no eye has seen, no ear has heard, and what has never occurred to the heart of a human being."* (al-Bukhārī 3244; Muslim 2824, from Abū Hurayrah)

The imagery is a language of correspondence, not a catalogue. It is how the unimaginable is made addressable to imaginations formed in a desert — and, for that matter, in a city.

**The Order of the Terms: Belief, Action, Guidance, Reward**

Finally, notice the sequence the verse establishes: they believed; they acted; God guided them further; they arrive at the Gardens. Reward is placed at the end of a chain, not at the beginning, and every link is necessary.

This has a practical implication that the sūrah will develop later in its treatment of the *awliyāʾ*, the friends of God (vv. 62–64): the Islamic path is not a single transaction — a moment of profession followed by a lifetime of coasting. It is a course of development in which each stage earns access to the next. That is demanding. It is also hopeful, because it means that whoever is willing to begin at the first link has the whole chain available to him.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:10
