> **Their supplication therein shall be, "Glory be to Thee, O God!" And therein their greeting shall be, "Peace." And the conclusion of their supplication shall be, "Praise be to God, Lord of the worlds!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**What Do People Say in Paradise?**

Having described where the believers arrive, the sūrah now describes what they *do* there — and the answer is startling in its simplicity. They speak. Three utterances are named: a glorification at the beginning, a greeting exchanged among them, and a praise at the end.

This is one of the most quietly instructive verses in the Qur'an about the nature of felicity. Paradise has been described in terms of gardens and rivers, and the reader might expect the next detail to concern consumption. Instead we are told about *speech* — and specifically about worship. The inhabitants of the Garden are still praising God, not because they are commanded to, but because that is what the state produces.

**"Supplication" That Is Not Asking**

*Daʿwāhum fīhā subḥānaka allāhumma.* The word *daʿwā* comes from *d-ʿ-w*, to call, and normally denotes petition — asking God for something.

But the commentators — al-Rāzī, al-Shawkānī, al-Ṭūsī, al-Zamakhsharī and others — note that *supplication* cannot mean petition here, because Paradise is a place where nothing is lacking. There is nothing left to ask for. They therefore explain *daʿwā* in this context as *tamjīd wa-thanāʾ*: magnification and praise. The calling-upon of God continues, but stripped of need.

This is a profound observation about the nature of prayer. In this world, most human calling upon God is mixed with request; we come to Him partly because we want something. In the Garden that motive is entirely absent, and yet the calling remains. What is left when need is removed turns out to be the purest form of the thing: love that asks for nothing because it has everything.

Ibn al-Qayyim and others in the ascetic tradition made this the criterion of sincerity: *would you still call on Him if you needed nothing?* This verse gives the answer that the perfected soul gives.

**"Glory Be to Thee" — The Meaning of *Subḥān***

*Subḥānaka allāhumma.* The root *s-b-ḥ* means to swim, to travel swiftly, to move freely through a medium — and in its intensive form, *tasbīḥ*, it means to declare God far removed from every deficiency, error, and unworthy attribution. To say *subḥān Allāh* is to say: God is beyond that.

The word is therefore fundamentally *negative* in logical form, and this is deliberate. Human beings cannot positively comprehend the divine essence; but they can, accurately and truthfully, negate from it everything unworthy. *Tasbīḥ* is the mind's honest movement of clearing away — removing from one's conception of God the injustice, the limitation, the fatigue, the partiality, and the resemblance to created things that the imagination keeps trying to smuggle in. It is the verbal form of *"There is nothing like unto Him"* (42:11).

That it is the *first* word spoken in Paradise is significant. The soul that has arrived begins by declaring the transcendence of the One it has arrived to.

**"Peace" — The Greeting That Is Also a Name**

*Wa-taḥiyyatuhum fīhā salām.* Their greeting is *salām*.

Three layers of meaning converge in this word.

First, it is God's own name. *Al-Salām* is listed among the divine names, and the Qur'an names Him so directly: *"He is God, other than Whom there is no god, the Sovereign, the Holy, the Peace (al-Salām), the Granter of Security..."* (59:23). To greet with *salām* is thus to invoke God Himself upon the one greeted.

Second, it is the condition of the place. The Qur'an calls Paradise *dār al-salām*, the Abode of Peace — as this very sūrah will say fifteen verses later: *"And God calls unto the Abode of Peace"* (10:25). Its people are described as *"engulfed in peace"* (19:62) and hearing *"no vain talk therein, nor any sin — only the saying: Peace, Peace"* (56:25–26).

Third, and most striking, it is what God Himself says to them:

> *"'Peace!' — a word from a Merciful Lord."* (Qur'an 36:58)

**The Continuity Between This World's Greeting and the Next**

Ājībah and al-Zamakhsharī note the continuity: the greeting Muslims exchange in this world is the greeting of the people of Paradise. That continuity is a piece of deliberate spiritual training, and it is worth appreciating how unusual the greeting is.

Most human greetings are inquiries about status ("How are you?"), assertions of time ("Good morning"), or acknowledgements of presence ("Hello"). *Al-salāmu ʿalaykum* is a *guarantee*: peace be upon you — from me, you are safe. The Prophet ﷺ made the connection between the word and the security explicit:

> *"The Muslim is the one from whose tongue and hand the Muslims are safe."* (al-Bukhārī 10; Muslim 40, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr)

And he tied the greeting directly to the entry into the Garden:

> *"You will not enter Paradise until you believe, and you will not believe until you love one another. Shall I not tell you of something which, if you do it, you will love one another? Spread the greeting of peace among yourselves."* (Muslim 54, from Abū Hurayrah)

The hadith describes a causal chain: greeting produces affection, affection produces the quality of faith that permits entry. A verse describing what people say in Paradise is thus not a piece of remote scenery; it is instructions for what to say this afternoon.

**"The Conclusion of Their Supplication" — Ending as It Began**

*Wa-ākhiru daʿwāhum ani al-ḥamdu lillāhi rabbi al-ʿālamīn.*

The final utterance is the opening line of the Qur'an itself — the first verse of al-Fātiḥah after the basmalah. The Book that begins with *"Praise be to God, Lord of the worlds"* describes the redeemed as ending with the same words. Revelation opens where Paradise closes.

*Ḥamd* is praise that combines two elements: acknowledgement of excellence *and* gratitude for benefit received. It differs from *madḥ*, which can be offered to anything admirable regardless of relationship, and from *shukr*, which responds to a favour but need not extol the giver's intrinsic worth. *Ḥamd* does both at once, which is why it is the word used of God.

And *rabb al-ʿālamīn* — Lord of all the worlds, all the categories of created being — closes the circle of the sūrah's opening argument. The verse that began the chapter established God as Creator and Governor; the inhabitants of the Garden confirm the same description as their last word.

**The Structure of the Verse as a Model of Worship**

Read as a whole, the verse gives a complete liturgical shape: *tasbīḥ* (declaring God free of imperfection), *salām* (peace among the worshippers), *ḥamd* (praise and thanks). Transcendence, community, gratitude.

This is, in fact, the shape of the Muslim's daily practice. The prayer opens with *Allāhu akbar* and the glorification; it is filled with *tasbīḥ* in bowing and prostration; it concludes with *taslīm* — turning right and left and saying *al-salāmu ʿalaykum* to those beside you — and the recommended remembrances afterwards are thirty-three *subḥān Allāh*, thirty-three *al-ḥamdu lillāh*, and thirty-four *Allāhu akbar*, as taught in the hadith of Abū Hurayrah:

> *"Whoever glorifies God after every prayer thirty-three times, and praises God thirty-three times, and magnifies God thirty-three times — that is ninety-nine — and says, to complete the hundred: 'There is no god but God alone, without partner; His is the dominion and His is the praise, and He has power over all things' — his sins will be forgiven even if they are like the foam of the sea."* (Muslim 597)

The verse therefore reveals something about ritual that is easy to miss. The forms of worship practised in this world are not arbitrary religious duties imposed for a season. They are rehearsals. What the believer does now, imperfectly and against distraction, is what the people of the Garden do perfectly and without effort. Prayer is not preparation *for* Paradise in the sense of a fee paid; it is preparation in the sense of *training in the activity itself*.

**A Note on What Is Absent**

It is worth observing what the verse does *not* say. There is no mention here of what the inhabitants receive, consume, or possess. Elsewhere the Qur'an describes those things vividly; here it does not.

The omission makes a point. The deepest pleasure of the Garden, on the Qur'an's account, is not the fruit or the rivers but the relationship — and the sūrah will say this outright at v. 26: the righteous have *al-ḥusnā* (the most beautiful reward) *wa-ziyādah*, "and more besides," which the majority of commentators, on the strength of a hadith recorded by al-Qurṭubī and others, identify as the vision of God. The gardens are the setting. The *salām* from the Lord is the event.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:11

> **Were God to hasten evil for mankind, even as they would hasten the good, their appointed term would have been concluded. But We leave those who anticipate not the meeting with Us to wander confused in their rebellion.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Taunt Behind the Verse**

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Zamakhsharī record the occasion: this verse responds to the Makkans' notorious challenge, preserved in the Qur'an's own words:

> *"And when they said, 'O God, if this be the truth from Thee, then rain down stones upon us from the sky, or bring us a painful punishment.'"* (Qur'an 8:32)

The speaker, according to the sīrah, was al-Naḍr ibn al-Ḥārith. The logic of the taunt is familiar in every age: *if your God is real and I am guilty, let Him strike me now.* And when nothing happens, the taunter treats the silence as proof.

**The Argument the Verse Makes**

*Wa-law yuʿajjilu Allāhu li-l-nāsi al-sharra istiʿjālahum bi-l-khayri la-quḍiya ilayhim ajaluhum.*

The Arabic sets up a precise comparison. People habitually rush God for the *good*: they want the answer to the prayer today, the provision this month, the relief immediately. The verse says: suppose God answered your requests for *harm* with the same speed with which you demand your good — *their appointed term would have been concluded*. That is, they would already be dead.

The reasoning is quietly devastating. The taunter is asking for speed, and the verse points out that he is asking for the one thing that, if granted, would end him. He does not want speed. He wants the *appearance* of divine absence to continue, so that he can point to it.

*Jalālayn* and al-Zamakhsharī gloss *la-quḍiya ilayhim ajaluhum* as: they would have been destroyed. And al-Ṭabarī draws the further point that the request is granted to no one precisely because God's manner is not to respond to human impatience.

**Two Kinds of Haste: The Root *ʿ-j-l***

The verse turns on the root *ʿ-j-l*, which generates *ʿajila* (to hasten), *ʿajala* (haste), and *al-ʿājilah* (the fleeting present life). The Qur'an's judgment on human haste is explicit:

> *"Man was created of haste. I shall show you My signs, so do not ask Me to hasten."* (Qur'an 21:37)

> *"And man supplicates for evil as he supplicates for good; and man is ever hasty."* (Qur'an 17:11)

That second verse is the exact companion to ours. Human beings routinely pray for things that would harm them, with complete sincerity, because their knowledge of consequences is short. A person prays for the job that would have destroyed his family, for the marriage that would have crushed him, for the money that would have made him unbearable. If prayer operated as a vending machine, the human race would have consumed itself long ago.

This is why unanswered prayer is not evidence against God's responsiveness. It may be the clearest evidence of it. The Prophet ﷺ taught that the supplication is never wasted:

> *"There is no Muslim who supplicates with a supplication in which there is no sin and no severing of kinship, but that God gives him one of three things: either He hastens for him his request, or He stores it up for him in the Hereafter, or He turns away from him an evil equivalent to it."* (Aḥmad, *Musnad* 11133, from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī; graded sound by the hadith scholars)

**The Wisdom of Delay**

Al-Ṭabāṭabāʾī and others explain the divine policy here as *ḥikmah*, wisdom: God grants respite deliberately. The Qur'an elsewhere makes the policy explicit and even warns against misreading it:

> *"And let not those who disbelieve suppose that the respite We grant them is good for their souls. We only grant them respite that they may increase in sin."* (Qur'an 3:178)

> *"So leave Me with those who deny this discourse; We shall lead them on step by step whence they know not. And I shall grant them respite; truly My scheme is firm."* (Qur'an 68:44–45)

There is a hadith that states the principle with memorable force:

> *"Truly God gives respite to the wrongdoer, until, when He seizes him, He does not let him go."* Then he recited: *"And such is the seizing of thy Lord when He seizes the towns while they are doing wrong; truly His seizing is painful, severe."* (al-Bukhārī 4686; Muslim 2583, from Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī)

But delay is not only a mechanism of accumulation for the guilty; it is also mercy. Every year of respite is a year in which repentance remains possible. The Makkans who mocked in year five of the mission included men who became, in year twenty, companions of the Prophet ﷺ — Khālid ibn al-Walīd, ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, Abū Sufyān. Had the stones fallen when requested, that history would not exist. The taunter is, without knowing it, asking God to cancel his own last chance.

**"To Wander Confused in Their Rebellion"**

*Fa-nadharu alladhīna lā yarjūna liqāʾanā fī ṭughyānihim yaʿmahūn.*

The description repeats, word for word, a phrase from Sūrat al-Baqarah's portrait of the hypocrites: *"and He leaves them in their rebellion, wandering confused"* (2:15).

*Ṭughyān* is from *ṭ-gh-y*, to overflow, to exceed the bound. It is used of floodwater breaking its banks (*"When the water overflowed [ṭaghā], We carried you in the sailing ship"* — 69:11) and, morally, of a person who has burst the limits set for him. The Qur'an's archetypal *ṭāghī* is Pharaoh — *"Go to Pharaoh; truly he has transgressed all bounds (ṭaghā)"* (20:24) — and Pharaoh's story occupies the second half of this very sūrah.

*Yaʿmahūn* is more specific still. The root *ʿ-m-h* denotes a particular kind of blindness — not the blindness of a man who cannot see, but the disoriented staggering of one who has lost his bearings and moves without direction. The Arabs used it of a camel wandering in a wilderness with no track to follow.

The picture is therefore exact: a person who has broken through every limit and is now staggering in the open, with nothing to steer by. Freedom from all constraint has produced not liberation but disorientation.

**Being "Left" as the Severest Punishment**

The word that should arrest the reader is *nadhar* — "We leave them." God does not strike; He withdraws. And in the Qur'an's moral universe, this is the more serious outcome.

Immediate punishment is a form of communication. It interrupts, it corrects, it implies that the relationship is still active. To be left alone — to sin and encounter no resistance, to transgress and prosper, to feel nothing at all where one used to feel unease — is the withdrawal of that communication.

Consider the human parallel. A father who corrects his son is engaged with him. A father who has stopped correcting, who watches in silence and says nothing, has reached a more frightening stage. The son may experience the silence as freedom. It is the opposite.

The Prophet ﷺ described this state:

> *"When you see God giving a servant of this world what he loves while he persists in disobedience, that is only a gradual enticement (istidrāj)."* Then he recited: *"So when they forgot what they had been reminded of, We opened unto them the gates of all things, until, when they exulted in what they were given, We seized them suddenly, and behold, they were in despair."* (Aḥmad, *Musnad* 17311, from ʿUqbah ibn ʿĀmir; the verse is 6:44)

**The Practical Test This Verse Offers**

The verse gives the reader a diagnostic he can apply to himself today, and it is more useful than most.

The question is not "am I being punished?" It is: *do I still feel resistance?* Does wrongdoing still cost me something internally — a discomfort, a reluctance, a difficulty in prayer afterwards? If it does, the relationship is live; the correction is functioning. The state described in this verse is the one in which that resistance has gone quiet, and the person mistakes the quiet for permission.

Ibn ʿAṭāʾ Allāh al-Iskandarī put it in one line in his *Ḥikam*: *"Whoever finds no grief at his disobedience — let him weep for the death of his heart."* The Qur'an's phrase for that condition is *fī ṭughyānihim yaʿmahūn*.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:12

> **And when harm afflicts man, he calls upon Us, [lying] on his side, or sitting, or standing. Then, when We remove his affliction from him, he carries on as if he had not called upon Us concerning the harm that had befallen him. Thus is that which they used to do made to seem fair unto the prodigal.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**One of the Qur'an's Most Precise Portraits of the Human Being**

This verse and the two that follow it (vv. 21–23 develop the same theme) constitute the sūrah's psychological centrepiece. What is described here is not a Makkan peculiarity. It is a pattern so universal that every reader can locate himself in it.

*Wa-idhā massa al-insāna al-ḍurru daʿānā li-janbihi aw qāʿidan aw qāʾiman.* The word *massa*, "touches," is gentler than "strikes" — the Qur'an is not describing catastrophe only, but any contact with harm. And the subject is *al-insān*, "the human being," in the generic sense. This is a statement about the species.

**The Three Postures: A Portrait Drawn From Life**

The three positions named — *li-janbihi* (on his side), *qāʿidan* (sitting), *qāʾiman* (standing) — are among the most vivid details in the sūrah, and they work on several levels at once.

Most obviously, they describe a sick man's progression. He begins bedridden, lying on his side, calling on God. As he improves he can sit up, and he is still calling. Then he can stand — and, by the verse's account, that is roughly when the calling stops.

They also indicate totality: *in every posture, at every moment*. There is no position in which he is not praying. The affliction has produced, temporarily, exactly the continuous God-consciousness that religion asks for and that he could never sustain in health.

And there is a third resonance, noticed by the commentators. These are the three postures of the ritual prayer as taught for the sick — standing, sitting, lying on the side — in the famous instruction to ʿImrān ibn Ḥuṣayn:

> *"Pray standing; if you cannot, then sitting; if you cannot, then on your side."* (al-Bukhārī 1117)

The order is reversed in our verse, and the reversal is telling. In the hadith the believer descends through the postures as illness compels him, still praying. In the verse the man ascends through them as health returns, and stops praying at the top.

**"He Carries On As If He Had Not Called Upon Us"**

*Marra ka-an lam yadʿunā ilā ḍurrin massah.* The verb *marra* means to pass by, to walk on, to continue on one's way — it is the word for a man walking past something without stopping.

The image is of someone stepping over the site of an intense experience as though it were not there. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Ṭabāṭabāʾī gloss the state as forgetting both the affliction and the help, and returning to old habits of heedlessness and caprice.

What is being described is not a change of belief. The man does not, on recovering, become an atheist. He simply *walks past* — the entire episode, the prayers, the promises, the tears, are filed away as belonging to a state he is no longer in.

Anyone who has been seriously ill or in serious danger knows exactly how this works. In the hospital corridor at three in the morning, a person's priorities rearrange themselves entirely, and the rearrangement feels permanent — one is certain that from now on everything will be different. Six weeks after discharge, the rearrangement has almost entirely dissolved. The verse's genius is in the word *marra*: it happens not by decision but by drift.

**Why the Amnesia Happens: Four Mechanisms**

The Qur'an states the fact; the reader can profit from understanding the mechanism, and it is not mysterious.

*Attribution shifts.* In the crisis, help came from an unknown quarter and was received as help. Afterwards, the mind rebuilds a causal story that leaves God out: the medicine worked, the surgeon was skilled, the market recovered. Every one of those is true, and each is a secondary cause; the reconstruction quietly substitutes the instrument for the Giver.

*Fear is not stored.* Human memory keeps facts but does not keep intensities. One can recall being terrified without being terrified. Since the crisis-faith was powered by the intensity, and the intensity is unrepeatable in memory, the faith it powered has nothing left to run on.

*Comfort restores the illusion of control.* This is what v. 7 called *iṭmaʾnān*, false security. In health, the ordinary machinery of life resumes and gives the impression of self-sufficiency. The dependence was always total; it merely became visible for a while.

*Habit outlives insight.* The old routines are still standing, and routines are stronger than resolutions. Unless the crisis-resolution is converted into a *structure* — a fixed prayer, a scheduled charity, a standing commitment — it will be overwritten by the pre-existing pattern within weeks.

The practical remedy follows directly from the diagnosis: whatever a person resolves during hardship should be written down and institutionalised *before* the hardship lifts. Resolutions that depend on remembering a feeling will fail. Resolutions embedded in a calendar and a standing order may survive.

**"Made to Seem Fair" — The Mechanism of Self-Deception**

*Kadhālika zuyyina li-l-musrifīna mā kānū yaʿmalūn.*

The verb *zuyyina* is passive: it "was beautified" or "made to seem fair." The Qur'an uses this construction repeatedly and, significantly, attributes the beautification to different agents in different places: to Satan (*"Satan made their deeds seem fair to them"* — 8:48; 16:63), to the self (*"his soul made the killing of his brother seem fair to him"* — 5:30), and, in the widest sense, to God as the one who permits the psychological law to operate (*"Beautified for mankind is the love of desires..."* — 3:14).

The passive construction leaves the agent unnamed, which is exactly right, because in lived experience one cannot tell where the beautification came from. That is what makes it effective. Nobody experiences self-deception *as* self-deception; one experiences it as a perfectly reasonable assessment.

This is the most dangerous condition the Qur'an describes, because it is invisible from the inside. A person who knows he is doing wrong retains the possibility of repentance. A person whose wrong has been *beautified* has lost the alarm. The Qur'an's most chilling formulation of this is:

> *"Say: Shall We inform you of the greatest losers in respect of works? They are those whose effort goes astray in the life of this world, while they reckon that they are doing good work."* (Qur'an 18:103–104)

**The *Musrif*: Al-Rāzī's Definition and Its Force**

The verse names the type: *al-musrifīn*, the prodigal. The root *s-r-f* means to exceed the proper measure — extravagance, waste, going beyond the bound. English "prodigal" catches it well: the prodigal son wasted his inheritance.

Al-Rāzī's comment on this word is one of the finest passages in his commentary, and the source preserves it:

> *"A 'prodigal' person is one who expends much wealth for a lowly purpose. It is well known that the pleasures and delights of this world are very lowly in comparison to the felicities of the Abode of the next life. God has given man the senses, an intellect, understanding, and strength in order to acquire these majestic felicities. But whoever exhausts these noble tools in order to gain those lowly, bodily delights has expended many majestic things in order to gain lowly and vile things. Such a person is surely one of the prodigal."*

This redefinition is worth dwelling on. We normally use "extravagance" of money. Al-Rāzī applies it to *faculties*. A human being has been issued with an intellect capable of grasping the structure of the cosmos, a memory capable of holding a book, a will capable of sustained sacrifice, a heart capable of loving what it cannot see. To spend that equipment on the accumulation of comforts is a mismatch of means to ends so severe that it constitutes waste in the strict sense.

Put in modern terms: it is like using a research telescope to check whether the neighbours are home. Nothing forbidden has occurred. But something extraordinary has been consumed to achieve something trivial, and the waste is real.

**Reading the Verse Without Despair**

It would be a misreading to conclude that prayer under affliction is worthless. It is not. The Qur'an records God answering exactly such prayers throughout its narratives, including the prayer of Jonah from the belly of the fish — the prophet after whom this sūrah is named (21:87–88).

The verse's target is not the crisis-prayer but the *sequel*. God does not reject the desperate man's call; He answers it, as the verse plainly says — *"then, when We remove his affliction from him."* What the verse indicts is what the man does afterwards.

The instruction implicit in this is therefore hopeful and practical: pray in the crisis, by all means. But treat the answered prayer as the beginning of an obligation rather than the closing of a file. The Qur'an's word for the right sequel is *shukr*, gratitude — and gratitude in Islamic usage is not a feeling but a *use*: to be grateful for a restored body is to employ it in what pleases the One who restored it.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:13

> **We have indeed destroyed generations before you when they did wrong, and their messengers brought them clear proofs, but they would not believe. Thus do We recompense the guilty people.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Psychology to History**

The previous verse examined the individual soul; this one widens the lens to civilisations. The Qur'an characteristically alternates between these two scales, and the alternation is itself an argument: the same law that governs one man's ingratitude governs the fate of nations, because a nation is a large number of men.

*Wa-la-qad ahlaknā al-qurūna min qablikum lammā ẓalamū.*

**The Word *Qurūn*: Generations, or Whole Civilisations**

*Qurūn* (singular *qarn*) is usually translated "generations," but the term is broader. Its root *q-r-n* means to join or couple; a *qarn* is a cohort of people joined by living in the same period. *Jalālayn* glosses it here as previous religious communities, and Ibn ʿAṭiyyah names examples: the people of Noah, the people of Hūd.

The Qur'an's list of such peoples is long and, importantly, was partly verifiable by its first audience. ʿĀd in the sands of the Aḥqāf, Thamūd in the rock-cut dwellings of al-Ḥijr, the cities of Lot by the Dead Sea — these lay on the trade routes the Makkan merchants used twice a year. The Qur'an says so:

> *"And you pass by them in the morning and at night. Will you not then understand?"* (Qur'an 37:137–138)

This is not an appeal to legend. It is an appeal to ruins the audience had personally driven past.

**The Precise Cause: *Lammā Ẓalamū***

The verse identifies the trigger with a temporal particle: *lammā ẓalamū* — "when they did wrong." The destruction is not attributed to divine caprice, nor to disbelief in the abstract, but to *ẓulm*.

*Ẓulm* is one of the Qur'an's key ethical terms. Its root sense is to put something in a place that is not its place — hence darkness (*ẓulmah*) is the placing of night where light should be. Ethically, it covers every misplacement: giving to the wrong recipient, withholding from the right one, assigning worship to what does not deserve it.

The commentators give it two applications here. Al-Baghawī and al-Kalbī read *ẓalamū* as ascribing partners to God, on the strength of Luqmān's warning to his son:

> *"O my son, do not ascribe partners to God; truly ascribing partners is a tremendous wrong (ẓulm ʿaẓīm)."* (Qur'an 31:13)

Al-Bayḍāwī reads it as denying the truth. Both are instances of misplacement — putting the creature in the Creator's place, or putting falsehood in truth's place.

**The Aggravating Condition: They Had Been Warned**

*Wa-jāʾathum rusuluhum bi-l-bayyināt.* This clause is essential to the verse's justice, and the Qur'an never omits it. Destruction never falls on the uninformed.

*Bayyināt* — "clear proofs," from *b-y-n*, to be distinct, evident, separated out. A *bayyinah* is evidence that makes a matter unmistakable. The word implies that the case put to those nations was not obscure or ambiguous.

The Qur'an states the underlying principle as an absolute rule:

> *"And never do We punish until We have sent a messenger."* (Qur'an 17:15)

And this sūrah restates it in universal terms at v. 47: *"For every community there is a messenger."* The system described is therefore not arbitrary. Warning precedes consequence, always, without exception. And the warning is *bayyinah* — sufficient to establish the case for a reasonable person.

**"But They Would Not Believe" — The Language of Refusal**

*Wa-mā kānū li-yuʾminū.* The Arabic construction here (*mā kāna li-* plus the subjunctive) is stronger than a simple past negative. It expresses something like: *it was not in them to believe; they were not such as would believe.*

This is not a statement that they were prevented. It is a statement about settled disposition. Verse 74 of this sūrah says the same thing about the peoples after Noah: *"But it was not for them to believe in that which they had denied beforehand."* Al-Bayḍāwī explains that phrase precisely: they had already habituated themselves to denial, and the habit closed them off to the possibility of accepting guidance when it came.

This is a psychologically accurate account of how conviction hardens. Positions taken publicly become identity; identity resists evidence; and after enough repetitions, the capacity to be persuaded atrophies. It is not that such a person examines the proof and finds it wanting. It is that the examination no longer takes place.

**"Thus Do We Recompense the Guilty People" — Law, Not Anecdote**

*Kadhālika najzī al-qawma al-mujrimīn.* The identical clause appears at 46:25. Its function is to convert a historical report into a *principle*.

*Mujrim* comes from *j-r-m*, whose original sense is to cut or sever — *jarama* is used of cutting fruit from a tree. A *mujrim* is one who has severed himself: from the truth, from his obligations, from the community of the guided. Ibn Kathīr and others gloss the term here as the disbelievers.

The present-tense verb *najzī* ("We recompense") is significant. The verse does not say "thus did We recompense" but "thus do We" — an ongoing practice, a standing policy. History is being presented as data from which a law can be induced.

**Is This a Falsifiable Claim? On Reading Qur'anic History**

A thoughtful modern reader may ask whether the claim is testable. Civilisations have collapsed for many reasons: climate shift, plague, invasion, resource exhaustion, institutional decay. Does the Qur'an really assert that God individually smites the wicked?

Two things should be said.

First, the Qur'an does describe direct, miraculous destructions — the flood, the shout, the wind, the overturning of the cities. It presents these as historical interventions, and a believer accepts them as such.

Second, and just as importantly, the Qur'an itself frames these events as instances of a *general law*, and states the law in terms that are entirely intelligible in ordinary historical categories:

> *"And when We desire to destroy a town, We command its affluent ones, and they commit iniquity therein; thus the Word comes due against it, and We destroy it utterly."* (Qur'an 17:16)

Read that carefully. The mechanism named is *internal*: the corruption of the elite. Affluence, then iniquity, then collapse. That is a sequence historians have documented from Rome to the present without any appeal to the supernatural. The Qur'an's claim is not that God bypasses causation but that God authored the causation — that societies which permit systemic injustice are running against the grain of the created order and will, in due course, splinter.

Ibn Khaldūn, writing his *Muqaddimah* in the fourteenth century, built an entire theory of civilisational cycles on precisely this Qur'anic foundation: group solidarity (*ʿaṣabiyyah*) produces conquest, conquest produces luxury, luxury dissolves solidarity, and dissolution invites the next cycle. He regarded himself as describing the operation of divine custom, *sunnat Allāh*, not replacing it.

**The Address: "Before *You*"**

The verse says *min qablikum* — "before *you*." The second-person plural is aimed at the Makkans, and through them at every subsequent audience.

The implication is direct: you are not a special case. You are a *qarn* like the ones before you, subject to the same law, currently in the phase where the messenger has come and the proofs have been presented. What happens next is the part still under your control.

This is the sūrah's characteristic move, and it is the reason the chapter is named after the people of Jonah. The catalogue of destroyed nations is not offered to induce fatalism. It is offered as a set of case studies, ending with one town that read the case studies in time.

**What a Society Should Take From This Today**

The verse invites communal self-examination, which is harder than individual self-examination because the responsibility is diffused.

The indicators the Qur'an gives are consistent across its historical passages: the concentration of wealth and its use to buy exemption from law; the mockery of those who counsel restraint; the treatment of the poor as an irritant rather than a claim; sexual and commercial practices that dissolve trust; and, above all, the loss of the capacity to hear criticism. That last is what the verse means by *mā kānū li-yuʾminū* — a society that has lost the ability to be persuaded by evidence against its own interest.

None of this requires a supernatural mechanism to be terrifying. The Qur'an's point is that it does not require one, and that the God who set up the mechanism will not be argued out of it.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:14

> **Then We made you vicegerents upon the earth after them, that We might observe how you behave.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Succession of Peoples as a Divine Appointment**

The previous verse described nations destroyed; this verse describes what happened to the vacated space. *Thumma jaʿalnākum khalāʾifa fī al-arḍi min baʿdihim* — "Then We made you vicegerents upon the earth after them."

The historical observation is one that every civilisation resists making about itself: *you are living on someone else's site*. The Makkans inhabited a peninsula strewn with the remains of ʿĀd and Thamūd. Every modern nation likewise occupies ground that has been occupied before and will be occupied again. The verse names this fact and gives it a theological character: the succession is not a matter of who happened to survive. It is an appointment.

**The Meaning of *Khalāʾif***

*Khalāʾif* is a plural of *khalīfah*, from the root *kh-l-f*, to come after, to succeed, to take the place of another. The same root gave us *ikhtilāf al-layl wa-l-nahār* in v. 6 — the taking-turns of night and day.

The Qur'an uses *khalīfah* in several senses, and the commentators distinguish them (see the discussion at 2:30).

In the broadest sense, it means simply *successor*: this generation succeeds the last. That is clearly the primary sense here, given *min baʿdihim*, "after them."

In the deeper sense established at 2:30 — *"I am placing upon the earth a vicegerent"* — it denotes humanity's appointment as God's representative on earth, entrusted with authority that is not its own. The Qur'an makes the trust explicit at 6:165: *"And He it is Who appointed you vicegerents of the earth, and raised some of you above others in degrees, that He may try you in what He has given you."*

The theological content of the term is precise, and it cuts in two directions simultaneously. A *khalīfah* has real authority — this is not a doctrine of human insignificance. But a *khalīfah*'s authority is *delegated*, exercised on behalf of another, according to that other's instructions, and subject to review. A regional governor who begins to imagine the province is his own property has misunderstood his position; and the misunderstanding does not change the fact.

**Vicegerency and the Natural World**

This concept has become urgently relevant in ways the classical commentators could not have anticipated. Human beings now possess technical capacities — over climate, genome, and species survival — that make the question of the *terms* of our authority a practical one.

The Qur'anic framework gives a clear answer: the earth is held in trust, not in freehold. The Prophet ﷺ applied the principle to the smallest details:

> *"If the Hour comes upon one of you while he has a palm-shoot in his hand, let him plant it if he is able to do so before he rises."* (al-Bukhārī, *al-Adab al-Mufrad* 479; Aḥmad 12902, from Anas)

The instruction is deliberately absurd on utilitarian grounds — a tree planted at the end of the world will never bear fruit. Its point is that cultivation is intrinsically right, independent of the return. That is precisely the ethic of a steward rather than an owner.

**"That We Might Observe How You Behave" — The Theological Problem**

*Li-nanẓura kayfa taʿmalūn.* Read literally, this raises an obvious difficulty: does God need to *watch* in order to find out? That would contradict the Qur'an's insistence on complete divine knowledge, including of what has not yet happened — indeed, this very sūrah says at v. 61 that *"not a mote's weight evades thy Lord."*

The commentators addressed this squarely, and the source preserves their reasoning.

Al-Kalbī explains *li-nanẓura* as meaning "in order for it to be made manifest in existence." That is, the purpose of the trial is not the acquisition of information by God but the *actualisation* of what was known — bringing potential into fact.

Al-Ālūsī notes that *kayfa* ("how") in the expression *kayfa taʿmalūn* functions, by the norms of Arabic rhetoric, as "how it is" — so the phrase means something like: "in order for it to be manifest in existence how it is that you will behave."

Putting these together: God knows the outcome; the trial makes the outcome *real*. There is a genuine difference between a person who *would* be honest under pressure and a person who *has been* honest under pressure — not in God's knowledge, but in fact. The second has actually done something; the first has only a disposition. Merit belongs to acts, and acts require occasions.

An analogy: a teacher may know with total confidence how a particular student will perform in an examination. The examination is not held to inform the teacher. It is held so that the performance exists — so that there is something to be assessed, recorded, and rewarded, and so that the student himself learns what he is.

**The Divine Perspective Expressed in Human Terms**

There is a further point about Qur'anic language here that helps with many similar passages. The Qur'an frequently speaks of God in terms drawn from human relations — He hears, He sees, He comes, He is pleased, He tests to see. This is not naivety; it is the necessary condition of communication. A message addressed to human beings must use human categories, and the Qur'an supplies its own governing corrective in a single clause:

> *"There is nothing like unto Him, and He is the Hearing, the Seeing."* (Qur'an 42:11)

That verse is the interpretive key. It affirms the attributes and denies the likeness in the same breath. Applied here: God's "observing" is real, but it is not the observing of one who was previously uninformed.

**The Trial Is the Purpose, Not an Interruption of It**

There is a large implication in this short clause that shapes the whole Islamic understanding of life.

If the purpose of vicegerency is *li-nanẓura kayfa taʿmalūn*, then testing is not an unfortunate feature of an otherwise pleasant arrangement; it is the arrangement. The Qur'an says so explicitly:

> *"Blessed is He in Whose Hand is sovereignty, and He has power over all things — Who created death and life to test you as to which of you is best in deed."* (Qur'an 67:1–2)

> *"Truly We created man from a drop of mingled fluid, to test him; and We made him hearing, seeing."* (Qur'an 76:2)

This reframes the ordinary complaint that life is difficult. Difficulty is not evidence that something has gone wrong with the design. A student who complains that the examination contains questions has misunderstood what an examination is.

Note also what is being tested: *kayfa taʿmalūn* — *how* you act, not how much. Sūrat al-Mulk uses the phrase *aḥsanu ʿamalan*, "best in deed," and al-Fuḍayl ibn ʿIyāḍ's famous gloss is that the best deed is *"the most sincere and the most correct"* — sincere meaning done for God alone, correct meaning done in accordance with the guidance. Quantity is not the criterion.

**Two Warnings Folded Into an Honour**

The verse is, on its surface, a statement of dignity: you have been made vicegerents. But its placement, immediately after the destruction of the previous tenants, converts the honour into a warning.

The first warning is about *tenure*. You were made successors to people who were removed. The office is not permanent, and the previous holders lost it for cause.

The second is about *scrutiny*. The appointment comes with an explicit purpose clause — you are here to be observed. There is no unmonitored period.

The Prophet ﷺ stated both dimensions in a single hadith:

> *"Truly the world is sweet and green, and God has appointed you as successors (mustakhlifukum) in it, to see how you will act. So beware of the world, and beware of women."* (Muslim 2742, from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī)

The wording is almost a citation of our verse. Note that the Prophet ﷺ does not deny the sweetness of the world; he affirms it. The danger is not that the world is bad, but that it is *sweet* — attractive enough to make a steward forget he is a steward.

**Living as a Successor**

What does this look like in practice? The Qur'anic concept of vicegerency, taken seriously, produces a specific relationship to everything one holds.

Money is held in trust, so its acquisition is subject to law and its expenditure to accountability. Authority — over employees, students, children, a household — is delegated, and the Prophet ﷺ said: *"Every one of you is a shepherd, and every one of you is responsible for his flock"* (al-Bukhārī 893; Muslim 1829). Talent is issued equipment, and to bury it is the waste that al-Rāzī called *isrāf* in the previous verse's commentary. Even the body is not property: *"Your body has a right over you"* (al-Bukhārī 1975).

The unifying idea is simple and demanding. Nothing a person has is *his*, in the sense of being at his unconditional disposal. Everything is his in the sense of being his *responsibility*. The generations before us held the same lease on the same terms, and the record of what happened when they forgot it is the subject of the previous verse.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:15

> **And when Our signs are recited unto them as clear proofs, those who anticipate not the meeting with Us say, "Bring a Quran other than this, or alter it." Say, "It is not for me to alter it of my own accord. I follow only that which is revealed unto me. Truly I fear, should I disobey my Lord, the punishment of a tremendous day."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Most Revealing Demand in the Sūrah**

Of all the objections raised against the Prophet ﷺ, this is the one that most exposes the objectors. They do not say *this Qur'an is false*. They say: *bring a different one, or change this one*.

The commentators are unanimous about the motive. Al-Maybudī, al-Ṭūsī, al-Wāḥidī and al-Zamakhsharī all record that the Qur'an's condemnation of their deities was the main provocation. The demand was, in effect: *keep the religion, drop the clause about our gods.* Other reports add that they wanted the threats of punishment removed and the promises retained.

Notice what this concedes. A man who believes a text is fabricated does not ask its author to revise it; he dismisses it. Asking for amendments is an implicit acknowledgement that the text has authority worth capturing. They wanted the prestige of revelation with the content negotiated.

**Two Requests, One Answer**

The demand has two parts — *iʾti bi-qurʾānin ghayri hādhā aw baddilhu*, "bring a Qur'an other than this, or alter it" — and the Prophet's reply appears to address only the second: *"It is not for me to alter it of my own accord."*

Al-Rāzī notices the apparent gap and resolves it with a neat piece of reasoning preserved in the source: by denying the possibility of changing even a word of the existing Qur'an, the reply covers the larger request *a fortiori*. If he cannot alter one syllable, he certainly cannot produce a whole new scripture. The lesser impossibility establishes the greater.

**"Of My Own Accord" — The Phrase That Settles Authorship**

*Mā yakūnu lī an ubaddilahu min tilqāʾi nafsī.*

The expression *min tilqāʾi nafsī* means "from myself," "on my own initiative." *Tilqāʾ* is from *l-q-y*, to meet or encounter, and the phrase denotes something arising from one's own side rather than being received from elsewhere.

Al-Rāzī draws out the argumentative force. This inability is *itself evidence*. Consider: if the Prophet ﷺ were the author, the demand would have been trivially easy to satisfy — and enormously profitable. He was under boycott, his followers were being tortured, his tribe had turned against him, and the leadership of Makkah was offering, according to the sīrah, wealth, kingship, and marriage in exchange for compromise. A revision would have ended the crisis in an afternoon.

He could not do it. Not "would not" as a matter of stubbornness, but *could not* as a matter of the thing's nature: *mā yakūnu lī*, "it is not for me." One does not amend a message one did not compose.

**The Involuntary Character of Revelation**

This connects to a broader pattern that the Qur'an itself makes visible, and which constitutes one of the strongest internal arguments for its origin. The revelation was not under the Prophet's control, and repeatedly said things that no author in his position would have chosen to say.

It rebuked him publicly. Sūrat ʿAbasa opens: *"He frowned and turned away, because the blind man came to him"* (80:1–2) — a correction of the Prophet ﷺ for a moment of inattention, permanently recorded in the scripture he was preaching.

It withheld itself when he most needed it. In the incident of the slander against ʿĀʾishah, the revelation was silent for roughly a month while the Prophet ﷺ suffered acutely and his household was in agony — a delay no author would inflict on himself.

It answered questions he had not asked and left unanswered questions he had. The Qur'an describes the pressure directly:

> *"And when Our signs are recited unto them as clear proofs..."* — and elsewhere: *"And thou wast not expecting that the Book would be conveyed unto thee, but it is a mercy from thy Lord."* (Qur'an 28:86)

And it warned him in the sternest terms against any tampering:

> *"And had he fabricated against Us any sayings, We would surely have seized him by the right hand, then severed his life-artery, and none of you could have protected him."* (Qur'an 69:44–47)

That passage is decisive. A forger does not insert into his forgery a threat against forgery.

**"I Follow Only That Which Is Revealed Unto Me"**

*In attabiʿu illā mā yūḥā ilayya.* The restrictive construction (*in ... illā*, "I do not ... except") makes the statement exclusive: nothing but revelation.

The same formula appears at 6:50 and, most famously, in the description of the Prophet's speech:

> *"Nor does he speak from caprice. It is naught but a revelation revealed."* (Qur'an 53:3–4)

The word *waḥy* itself is instructive. Its root *w-ḥ-y* denotes rapid, subtle communication — a signal passed swiftly and privately. The Qur'an uses it of God's communication to the bee (16:68) and of a suggestion passed between conspirators (6:112). Its common thread is transmission from an external source into a receiver.

The Prophet's status here is therefore that of a *transmitter*, and this is fundamental to Islamic theology. He is not the source of the revelation; he is not divine; he does not co-author. This is why Islam has never treated the Prophet ﷺ as an object of worship, and why he himself forbade the exaggeration that had overtaken earlier communities:

> *"Do not extol me as the Christians extolled the son of Mary. I am only a servant, so say: the servant of God, and His Messenger."* (al-Bukhārī 3445, from ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb)

**"Truly I Fear ... the Punishment of a Tremendous Day"**

*Innī akhāfu in ʿaṣaytu rabbī ʿadhāba yawmin ʿaẓīm.*

This closing clause is remarkable and is easily passed over. The Prophet ﷺ — the recipient of revelation, the most honoured of creation — is instructed to declare, in public, that he fears punishment if he disobeys.

Several things follow.

*It establishes that the law binds its bearer.* The messenger is not exempt from the message. Compare the Prophet's own declaration to his family:

> *"O Fāṭimah, daughter of Muhammad, ask me for what you will of my wealth — I cannot avail you anything against God."* (al-Bukhārī 2753; Muslim 206)

*It removes the possibility of negotiation on religious terms.* The Makkans were treating the Qur'an as the Prophet's property, negotiable like any other asset. The reply relocates the whole matter: he is not the owner, he is under orders, and he is himself answerable.

*It models the correct motive for integrity under pressure.* The reason given for refusing is not pride, or stubbornness, or the strength of his convictions. It is fear of God. That is a more durable foundation than any of the others, because pride collapses when humiliated, stubbornness tires, and convictions can be argued away — but accountability to God does not depend on the state of one's morale.

**The Permanent Relevance: Every Age Asks for a Different Qur'an**

This verse describes a demand that has never gone out of fashion, and it is worth naming its modern forms honestly.

Every era encounters aspects of revelation that conflict with its assumptions, and every era produces people who propose that those aspects be reinterpreted out of existence — not on the basis of philological or legal evidence, which is the legitimate work of scholarship, but on the basis that the age finds them uncongenial. The demand is precisely *baddilhu*: keep the text, adjust the meaning until it agrees with us.

The verse does not forbid interpretation. The entire Islamic legal tradition is interpretation, and the tradition developed sophisticated tools — the study of context, of abrogation, of the general and the specific, of the objectives of the law — precisely in order to apply a fixed text to changing circumstances. What the verse forbids is the reversal of authority: making the text answerable to the reader's preferences rather than the reader answerable to the text.

The distinction is testable by a simple question. Does the proposed reading emerge from the evidence, or was it decided in advance and the evidence recruited afterwards? Every honest scholar knows the difference, and so, usually, does everyone else.

**The Underlying Principle**

What makes revelation valuable is exactly what makes it uncomfortable: that it comes from outside. A text that could be adjusted to suit its audience would be a mirror, and one does not need a mirror to know what one already thinks.

The Prophet's answer — *"It is not for me to alter it"* — is therefore not a refusal to serve his people. It is the only way of serving them that is worth anything. A physician who adjusts the diagnosis to please the patient has stopped being a physician.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:16

> **Say, "Had God willed, I would not have recited it unto you; nor would He have made it known to you. Indeed, I tarried among you for a lifetime before it. Do you not understand?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument From a Life Lived in Public**

The previous verse established that the Prophet ﷺ could not alter the revelation. This verse presses the point from a different direction — from biography.

*Fa-qad labithtu fīkum ʿumuran min qablih* — "Indeed, I tarried among you for a lifetime before it." The word *ʿumur* means a lifespan, an extended period of life. The Prophet ﷺ received the first revelation at forty. Forty years is not a phase; it is most of a life by the standards of the time.

Al-Kāshānī and al-Zamakhsharī draw out the implication: he was born and raised in Makkah, and by the time the Qur'an came he was a thoroughly known quantity — a man with a public record of honesty and trustworthiness.

**"Al-Amīn" — The Testimony They Gave Him Themselves**

The most powerful part of this argument is that the character reference was issued by the accusers.

The Makkans called him *al-Amīn*, the Trustworthy, before the revelation began. The name was not honorific flattery; it was functional. They deposited valuables with him. Ibn Kathīr and the sīrah literature record that even during the years of open hostility, Makkans continued to leave goods in his keeping — which is why, on the night of the emigration, ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib had to remain behind in Makkah to return the deposits their owners had entrusted to the very man they were trying to kill.

The most celebrated instance of this reputation is the arbitration over the Black Stone some five years before the revelation, recorded in Ibn Isḥāq. The clans of Quraysh, rebuilding the Kaʿbah, came to blows over which of them would have the honour of setting the stone in place. They agreed to accept the judgment of the next man to enter the sanctuary. When Muhammad ﷺ appeared, they exclaimed, "It is al-Amīn; we are content with him." He spread out a cloak, placed the stone upon it, and had a representative of each clan lift a corner — then set the stone himself. A community does not hand a dispute that has brought it to the edge of civil war to a man it considers unreliable.

Ibn Kathīr also cites the testimony of Jaʿfar ibn Abī Ṭālib before the Negus of Abyssinia, addressing a foreign monarch about a man his own people were persecuting:

> *"God sent a messenger to us whose truthfulness, lineage, and trustworthiness we all know."*

**The Logical Trap**

The argument this verse constructs is a dilemma, and it is airtight in its own terms.

For forty years, on every subject — commerce, disputes, personal dealings — this man was truthful. Then, at forty, he began to make statements about God.

Either he was truthful then and is truthful now, or he was truthful for forty years and then began to lie on the single subject that could bring him nothing but persecution.

The second option is psychologically incoherent. Habitual deceivers do not lie for the first time at forty, and when they do lie, they lie for gain. What did this claim bring him? The loss of his tribe's protection, a three-year boycott in which his clan ate leaves, the deaths under strain of his wife and his uncle in a single year, exile from his birthplace, and years of war. The Qur'an elsewhere frames the same point as a challenge to reason: *"I have tarried among you a lifetime before it — do you not then understand?"*

The verse's closing question, *a-fa-lā taʿqilūn*, is exactly this. The commentators — al-Bayḍāwī, *Jalālayn*, al-Maybudī — paraphrase it: given that you know I am not a liar, why do you not reflect until you realise that this Qur'an is from God?

**"Had God Willed, I Would Not Have Recited It"**

The verse's first clause is a separate argument, and it addresses the deeper question of agency.

*Law shāʾa Allāhu mā talawtuhu ʿalaykum wa-lā adrākum bih.* Had God willed otherwise, none of this would have reached you. The recitation is not the Prophet's initiative; the whole transaction proceeds from divine will.

*Wa-lā adrākum bih* is a subtle phrase — literally, "nor would He have made you aware of it." The verb *adrā* is causative of *darā*, to know: God caused you to know. The knowledge arrived from outside; nobody in Makkah worked it out.

This is why the argument matters beyond its immediate context. The Prophet ﷺ is presented throughout as a *conduit*. Even the timing of the revelation was not his: the pause in revelation early in the mission (*fatrat al-waḥy*) caused him deep distress, and the sūrah revealed to end it — *al-Ḍuḥā* — opens by swearing that his Lord had neither forsaken him nor despised him (93:3). A man who could summon revelation would not spend weeks in anguish waiting for it.

**Why the Illiteracy of the Prophet Matters Here**

The Qur'an elsewhere makes an argument closely related to this verse:

> *"And thou didst not recite before it any book, nor didst thou inscribe one with thy right hand; then the falsifiers would have doubted."* (Qur'an 29:48)

The point is not that illiteracy is a virtue. The point is evidential: a man with no scribal training, no access to libraries, no history of literary production, in a town with no scriptural schools, produced a text that reshaped the Arabic language and has been the standard of its eloquence for fourteen centuries. The absence of a plausible route from the man to the book is part of the case for the book.

**The Broader Principle: Character as Evidence**

There is a lesson here that extends beyond the specific claim about Muhammad ﷺ, and it is worth stating because it shapes how Muslims are expected to live.

The Qur'an is content to rest part of its case on the *observed character* of its messenger. That is a striking thing for a scripture to do. It means that the credibility of the message and the credibility of the messenger were understood to be linked — and, by extension, that the conduct of those who carry the message continues to affect how the message is received.

This is why the Prophet ﷺ placed such emphasis on ordinary honesty:

> *"Truthfulness leads to righteousness, and righteousness leads to Paradise. A man keeps telling the truth until he is recorded with God as a truthful one. And falsehood leads to wickedness, and wickedness leads to the Fire. A man keeps lying until he is recorded with God as a liar."* (al-Bukhārī 6094; Muslim 2607, from Ibn Masʿūd)

Note the mechanism the hadith describes: a person is not classified as truthful by a single act but by a sustained record, *ḥattā yuktaba* — "until he is written down" as such. That is precisely what the Makkans had done with Muhammad ﷺ over forty years. Their own filing system had already labelled him *al-Amīn*, and this verse simply asks them to read their own file.

**A Question Worth Asking Oneself**

If a person's forty-year record were placed before those who know him best — colleagues, neighbours, family — what would it establish? The verse's argument only works because the answer in the Prophet's case was unambiguous and publicly known.

The lesson is not merely apologetic. Islam has always spread more effectively through conduct than through argument; the historical expansion of the religion into East Africa, the Indian Ocean world, and Southeast Asia was carried substantially by merchants whose reliability was itself the argument. This verse is the theological root of that history: *I tarried among you for a lifetime before it — do you not understand?*

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:17

> **And who does greater wrong than one who fabricates a lie against God or denies His signs? Surely the guilty will not prosper.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Rhetorical Question as an Instrument of Argument**

*Fa-man aẓlamu mimmani iftarā ʿalā Allāhi kadhiban aw kadhdhaba bi-āyātih.*

The construction *man aẓlamu* — "who does greater wrong?" — is one of the Qur'an's recurring formulas (see 6:21, 93, 144; 7:37; 11:18; 18:15; 29:68; 61:7). It is a rhetorical question in the strict sense: it does not seek information but forces the listener to complete the reasoning himself and arrive at the answer *no one*.

Al-Rāzī reads this verse as a direct continuation of the Prophet's defence in vv. 15–16. If the Prophet had fabricated revelation, no one would have done a greater wrong — and the wrong would have fallen most heavily on himself. That is the first half of the argument.

The second half is the turn: since the Prophet is truthful and merely conveys what he receives, the description in this verse applies not to him but to those rejecting him. They are *the guilty*. Al-Rāzī states this explicitly: the deniers are the ones who have wronged themselves.

**Two Crimes Named, and Why They Belong Together**

The verse identifies two offences:

*Iftirāʾ ʿalā Allāh* — fabricating against God. The verb *iftarā* comes from *f-r-y*, whose root sense is to cut or split leather — hence to fabricate in the literal manufacturing sense, to cut and stitch something together. To *iftarā* against God is to manufacture a claim and attach His name to it.

*Takdhīb bi-āyātihi* — denying His signs. The intensive form *kadhdhaba* means not merely to disbelieve but to actively declare false.

These are opposite errors in one respect and identical in another. The first *adds* to God's speech what He did not say; the second *subtracts* from it what He did. Both are misrepresentations of divine communication, and both, on the Qur'an's reckoning, are the gravest category of wrongdoing.

Why the gravest? Because the harm scales with the authority invoked. To lie in one's own name misleads those who trust the speaker. To lie in God's name misleads everyone who trusts God — and worse, it corrupts the very channel by which humanity is guided. A false claim attributed to God does not merely misinform; it poisons the source.

**Fabricating Against God in Everyday Religious Life**

It would be comfortable to read this verse as concerning only self-proclaimed prophets. The classical scholars did not read it so narrowly, and their broader reading is more useful.

To speak about God's law without knowledge falls under this heading. The Qur'an makes the connection explicitly:

> *"And do not say, concerning what your tongues falsely describe, 'This is lawful and this is forbidden,' so as to fabricate a lie against God. Truly those who fabricate lies against God will not prosper."* (Qur'an 16:116)

That verse ends with almost exactly the phrase our verse ends with. The context there is precisely the arbitrary dietary rulings of the Arabs, which Sūrah Yūnus itself addresses at vv. 59–60: *"Then you made some of it forbidden and some lawful. Say: Has God granted you leave, or do you fabricate against God?"*

The offence, then, includes: declaring forbidden what God permitted; declaring permitted what God forbade; attributing to the religion customs it does not contain; and issuing confident religious rulings without the knowledge to support them.

This last is a live danger in an age when anyone can broadcast. The gravity that the tradition attached to it is reflected in the practice of the early scholars. Mālik ibn Anas was asked forty-eight questions and answered only sixteen, saying to the rest, *lā adrī* — "I do not know." Ibn Abī Laylā said he met a hundred and twenty Companions of the Prophet ﷺ in Madinah, and *"there was not one of them who was asked a question but that he wished his brother would suffice him in answering it."* The reluctance was not modesty as a social grace. It was fear of this verse.

**The Second Offence: Denying the Signs**

*Kadhdhaba bi-āyātih.* Recall from v. 1 that *āyāt* covers three things at once: the verses of scripture, the miracles given to prophets, and the signs in creation.

So *takdhīb* — denial — can take three corresponding forms. One can deny the revealed text. One can deny the prophetic evidence. And one can look at the ordered cosmos described in vv. 5–6 and declare that it indicates nothing.

The Qur'an consistently treats the third as a form of dishonesty rather than an innocent conclusion, because it holds that the evidence is sufficient and the refusal is motivated. Sūrat al-Naml puts it starkly:

> *"And they rejected them, out of wrongdoing and arrogance, though their souls were convinced of them."* (Qur'an 27:14)

That is a claim about internal states, and it is the same claim that this sūrah makes about the men who prayed in the storm and forgot on the shore (vv. 12, 22–23). The Qur'an's charge against its opponents is not that they examined the evidence and were unpersuaded; it is that they knew and covered it — which, as noted at v. 4, is the root meaning of *kufr* itself.

**"Surely the Guilty Will Not Prosper"**

*Innahu lā yufliḥu al-mujrimūn.*

The verb *aflaḥa* is worth examining, because it carries an agricultural image that transforms the sentence. The root *f-l-ḥ* means to split or cleave — a *fallāḥ* is a ploughman, one who splits the soil. *Falāḥ*, success, is therefore originally the farmer's success: the breaking of the ground, the planting, the patient waiting, and finally the harvest.

This is the word called out from every minaret five times a day: *ḥayya ʿalā al-falāḥ*, "come to success."

The agricultural sense reframes the verse's claim. Success, in the Qur'anic sense, is not a windfall; it is a yield. It requires that something be planted, that the planting be sound, and that time be allowed to pass. And this is why the *mujrim* cannot achieve it: whatever he appears to gather, he has not planted anything that can produce a harvest. He may accumulate — the Qur'an never denies that wrongdoers accumulate — but accumulation is not *falāḥ*.

**Defining "Prosperity" Properly**

The obvious objection is empirical: liars do prosper. Regimes built on systematic falsehood have lasted for generations. Frauds die wealthy.

The verse's answer is contained in the definition of the term. *Falāḥ* is the final yield, assessed at harvest, and the harvest in the Qur'anic frame is not in this life. The Qur'an frequently pairs apparent worldly success with ultimate loss:

> *"Let it not deceive thee, the going to and fro of those who disbelieve in the land. A brief enjoyment; then their refuge is Hell — an evil resting place."* (Qur'an 3:196–197)

But there is also a claim about *this* life that is worth taking seriously, because it is observable. A person who has built his position on falsehood must maintain the falsehood, and maintenance is expensive. He cannot be known. He must monitor what he has said to whom. He cannot receive praise without knowing it is misdirected, which corrodes the pleasure of praise. And he cannot rest, because exposure is always one accident away.

*Falāḥ*, by contrast, includes something that fraud structurally cannot deliver: the capacity to be at ease with oneself. That is why the same root that gives "success" gives "ploughing." Both are slow, both are unglamorous, and both produce something real.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:18

> **They worship apart from God that which neither harms them nor benefits them. And they say, "These are our intercessors with God." Say, "Would you inform God about something in the heavens or on the earth that He does not know? Glory be to Him and exalted is He above the partners they ascribe!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Anatomy of Makkan Idolatry**

This verse gives us the clearest statement in the sūrah of what the Makkans actually believed, and it is important to get this right, because the popular picture is inaccurate.

The Makkans were not atheists, and they were not simple-minded people who thought stone objects were gods. Al-Qurṭubī explains their theology: they believed the idols of the pre-Islamic pantheon — al-Lāt, al-ʿUzzā, Manāt and the rest — would intercede with God to arrange the affairs of their livelihood in this world.

They believed in Allāh. The Qur'an records this repeatedly, including later in this very sūrah:

> *"Say: Who provides for you from Heaven and earth? ... They will say, 'God.'"* (Qur'an 10:31)

> *"And if you asked them who created the heavens and the earth, they would surely say, 'God.'"* (Qur'an 31:25)

Their idols were not rivals to God; they were, in their own understanding, *access*. The Qur'an quotes their self-justification directly in another sūrah:

> *"We only worship them that they may bring us nearer to God in nearness."* (Qur'an 39:3)

Understanding this changes what the verse is arguing against. The target is not the crude belief that a rock made the world. It is the sophisticated belief that a rock can lobby the One who did.

**"Neither Harms Them Nor Benefits Them" — The Empirical Test**

*Mā lā yaḍurruhum wa-lā yanfaʿuhum.*

Before any theological argument, the Qur'an applies a test that requires no learning at all: *what does it actually do?*

Ājībah and al-Qurṭubī note that these are lifeless objects fashioned by human hands, and the Qur'an elsewhere presses the point with almost mocking directness:

> *"Do you worship what you carve, when God created you and what you make?"* (Qur'an 37:95–96)

> *"They have feet with which they walk not, or hands with which they grasp not, or eyes with which they see not, or ears with which they hear not."* (Qur'an 7:195)

The most famous dramatisation of this argument is Abraham's in Sūrat al-Anbiyāʾ. Having smashed the idols and left the axe on the shoulder of the largest, he tells his accusers to ask the surviving idol what happened. They reply that it cannot speak — and he has them:

> *"He said: Do you then worship, apart from God, that which does not benefit you at all, nor harm you?"* (Qur'an 21:66)

The criterion of harm and benefit is deliberately chosen. It is the criterion by which human beings actually organise their lives. We take seriously what can affect us. To worship what demonstrably cannot is not merely a theological error; it is a failure of ordinary practical reasoning.

**The Intercession Claim and Its Refutation**

The Makkans' reply to the empirical test was the intercession claim: *hāʾulāʾi shufaʿāʾunā ʿinda Allāh*. The idols do not act; they *petition*.

Verse 3 of this sūrah already answered this in principle — *"There is no intercessor, save by His Leave."* Here the answer takes a different form: a question so unexpected that it reorients the entire dispute.

**"Would You Inform God of Something He Does Not Know?"**

*A-tunabbiʾūna Allāha bi-mā lā yaʿlamu fī al-samāwāti wa-lā fī al-arḍ.*

This is one of the most elegant refutations in the Qur'an, and its logic deserves to be unpacked slowly.

You claim these beings hold the office of intercessor with God. An office implies an appointment; an appointment implies an appointing authority. Yet God, who knows everything in the heavens and the earth, has no knowledge of any such appointment. He has issued no such commission.

So who did? If you are telling God about an intercessor He does not know of, you are, in effect, *informing* Him — claiming to possess information about His own court that He lacks.

The absurdity is total, and it is derived entirely from the idolaters' own premises. They agreed that God is the supreme knower. The argument simply holds them to it.

Al-Zamakhsharī and others note the same move at 2:255 (*"He knows what lies before them and what is after them"*) and at 58:7 (*"God knows whatsoever is in the heavens and the earth"*). The Qur'an repeatedly makes divine omniscience do argumentative work, not merely doctrinal work.

**The Positive Doctrine: Intercession Exists, but Belongs to God**

It is essential not to over-read the verse. The Qur'an does not deny intercession as such. It says:

> *"Say: Unto God belongs intercession altogether."* (Qur'an 39:44)

Intercession is real. What the Qur'an insists is that it is a *possession* of God, dispensed by His permission, and not a lever available to creatures. Al-Qurṭubī and the majority of commentators affirm that God may grant the power of intercession to whom He wills — the Prophet ﷺ, and others — and the hadith literature is unambiguous about this. The great intercession hadith describes the Prophet ﷺ prostrating beneath the Throne on the Day of Judgment and being told:

> *"Raise your head; ask, and you shall be given; intercede, and your intercession shall be accepted."* (al-Bukhārī 7440; Muslim 193)

The difference between the Islamic doctrine and the Makkan one can be stated in a sentence. In the Makkan model, intercession flows *upward*: the client cultivates the intermediary, and the intermediary applies pressure to the King. In the Islamic model, intercession flows *downward*: God, out of His mercy, permits a servant to speak, and the permission is the gift.

This is why direct access is the norm in Islam and mediation the exception. There is no priesthood; there is no sacrament that requires a functionary; the ordinary Muslim addresses God directly, in his own language, at any hour. The Qur'an's most tender statement of this is:

> *"And when My servants ask thee about Me — truly I am near. I answer the call of the caller when he calls upon Me."* (Qur'an 2:186)

Note that even here, where a question is directed *to the Prophet*, God does not say "tell them I am near." He drops the intermediary mid-sentence: *innī qarīb*, "truly I am near."

**Modern Idols: Extending the Category Honestly**

The verse's structure permits a legitimate extension, and the tradition made it. Al-Maybudī observes that a "partner" is anything that can be worshipped besides God, and the Qur'an itself names desire as an object of worship:

> *"Hast thou seen the one who takes his caprice as his god?"* (Qur'an 25:43; cf. 45:23)

The test the verse supplies — *does it harm or benefit?* — can be applied to any candidate. And the results are instructive, because modern substitutes fail the test in the same way the stone ones did, though less obviously.

Consider what people actually rely on for security: savings, status, professional position, the approval of a group. Each of these has *some* power, unlike a stone; that is what makes them harder to see clearly. But none of them can extend a life by an hour, none can accompany a person into the grave, and each is subject to forces entirely outside the holder's control — a market, an employer, an illness, a change of fashion. To treat any of them as *the* source of security is to make the Makkan error in a modern idiom: attributing to a contingent thing a power it structurally lacks.

The Qur'anic corrective is not to despise these things. It is to relocate them: they are means, granted and withdrawn by God, and useful in exactly that capacity.

**"Glory Be to Him and Exalted Is He"**

*Subḥānahu wa-taʿālā ʿammā yushrikūn.*

The verse ends where argument ends and worship begins. Ibn Kathīr describes this formula as a declaration of God's utter transcendence and incomparability.

Two words: *subḥānahu*, He is free of every deficiency (the sense of *tasbīḥ* explored at v. 10), and *taʿālā*, He is exalted high above. The preposition *ʿan* — "above/away from" — governs *what they associate*: God is above the whole business of having partners.

There is a lesson in this ending. Having refuted the error rationally, the verse does not stop at refutation. It moves to *tanzīh* — the declaration of transcendence — because the aim of the argument was never merely to win it. The aim was to restore the reader to a correct posture before God. An argument about God that leaves the arguer unchanged has failed even when it succeeds.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:19

> **Mankind was but one community, then they differed. And if not for a Word that had preceded from thy Lord, judgment would have been made between them concerning that wherein they differed.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse About the Origin of Religious Division**

This short verse addresses one of the oldest questions a thoughtful person asks: if there is one God and one truth, why are there so many religions?

*Wa-mā kāna al-nāsu illā ummatan wāḥidatan fa-khtalafū.* "Mankind was but one community, then they differed."

The statement has two parts: an original unity, and a subsequent divergence. Both need examination.

**What Was the Original Unity?**

The commentators offer several accounts, and the fuller discussion is at 2:213 — *"Mankind was one community; then God sent the prophets as bearers of glad tidings and as warners..."*

One reading, transmitted from Ibn ʿAbbās, is that humanity was united upon guidance in the earliest period — from Adam through the generations before the flood — and then fell into idolatry, after which prophets were sent to restore what had been lost.

Another reading, also transmitted early, is that humanity was united in *misguidance* or in a natural, undifferentiated state, and that revelation introduced the distinction between belief and denial.

A third, favoured by many, is that the unity was the unity of *fiṭrah* — the innate disposition toward the recognition of one God with which every human being is born. This sūrah's later command, *"Set thy face toward the religion as a ḥanīf"* (v. 105), points in the same direction, as does the well-known hadith:

> *"Every child is born upon the fiṭrah; it is his parents who make him a Jew, a Christian, or a Magian."* (al-Bukhārī 1358; Muslim 2658, from Abū Hurayrah)

The source notes that although the commentators differ on details, what they differed over can generally be said to pertain to particular understandings of revelation. That is the crucial observation, and it directs us to the right reading of the second clause.

**"Then They Differed" — The Nature of the Divergence**

The Qur'an's position on religious difference is more specific than a general lament about disunity. Its consistent claim is that divergence arose *after* knowledge, not from a lack of it:

> *"And they did not differ until after knowledge had come to them, out of envy among themselves."* (Qur'an 45:17)

> *"Nor did those who were given the Book differ except after knowledge had come to them, out of envy among themselves."* (Qur'an 3:19)

This sūrah says the same of the Children of Israel at v. 93: *"They differed not till knowledge came unto them."*

The diagnosis is therefore moral rather than epistemic. The Qur'an does not primarily attribute religious division to honest confusion or to the genuine difficulty of ultimate questions — though it acknowledges that people can be misled (see v. 100 of this sūrah). It attributes the *primary* divisions to *baghyan baynahum*, mutual envy and rivalry: the appropriation of religion by interest.

This is historically recognisable. Doctrinal splits have very often tracked political, ethnic, and economic fault lines with remarkable precision. Communities discover theological reasons for divisions that already existed on other grounds, and priesthoods develop material interests in maintaining distinctions.
