The Qur'an's claim is not that every believer in every tradition is dishonest; it is that the *institutional* history of division is not innocent.

**"A Word That Had Preceded From Thy Lord"**

*Wa-law-lā kalimatun sabaqat min rabbika la-quḍiya baynahum fīmā fīhi yakhtalifūn.*

*Kalimah* here means a decree, a settled determination. The commentators — Ājībah, al-Bayḍāwī, *Jalālayn* — explain the preceding Word as God's decision to defer judgment between the disputing parties until the Day of Resurrection.

Without that decree, judgment would have been immediate. Truth and falsehood would have been separated on the spot, publicly and unmistakably, with each party's claim adjudicated in real time.

**Why the Deferral Is a Mercy, and Why It Is a Trial**

Consider what immediate judgment would mean in practice. Every false doctrine would be refuted by visible catastrophe. Every truth-claim would be verified by demonstration. Religious disagreement would be settled the way a dispute about the weather is settled: by looking outside.

That world would have three features, and each explains why it is not the world we are in.

First, faith would be impossible in the Qur'anic sense. *Īmān* involves *al-ghayb*, the unseen — *"who believe in the unseen"* (2:3) is the first quality of the God-conscious in Sūrat al-Baqarah. Belief in what has been demonstrated in front of you is not faith; it is observation. The Qur'an is explicit that faith accepted only after the removal of doubt is worthless — this sūrah says so of Pharaoh at v. 91 and of the deniers at v. 97.

Second, moral choice would collapse into calculation. If wrongdoing were immediately and visibly punished, no one would choose rightly *because it is right*; they would choose rightly because it is prudent. The entire moral dimension of human life depends on a gap between the act and its consequence.

Third, there would be no room for repentance, growth, or the slow turning of a soul. The deferral is what makes the story of the people of Jonah possible (v. 98). A community that had already been judged could not have repented.

The Qur'an names this same deferral elsewhere as the reason the world continues at all:

> *"And if God were to take mankind to task for their wrongdoing, He would not leave upon it a single creature; but He grants them respite until a term appointed."* (Qur'an 16:61)

**How a Believer Should Hold Religious Difference**

The verse has practical consequences for how Muslims relate to disagreement, and they are worth stating.

It means that the resolution of religious dispute is not, in the end, a human assignment. God has explicitly reserved final judgment. This sūrah repeats the principle at v. 93 — *"Thy Lord will surely judge between them on the Day of Resurrection concerning that wherein they differed"* — and it recurs across the Qur'an (2:113; 22:69; 39:46).

That does not mean truth is indeterminate or that argument is pointless; the Qur'an argues constantly, and this sūrah is one long argument. It means that the *verdict* belongs to God and that human beings are responsible for conveying, not for coercing. The sūrah will make this explicit near its close: *"Wouldst thou compel men till they become believers?"* (v. 99), and *"I am not a guardian over you"* (v. 108).

The distinction is between conviction and compulsion. A Muslim may hold, and argue, that his religion is true. What he may not do is arrogate to himself the office that this verse says God has reserved for the Day of Resurrection.

**The Address to Muslims Themselves**

There is a final application that the verse's placement does not permit us to avoid. The pattern it describes — one community, then difference — has operated within the Muslim community as thoroughly as anywhere else.

The Prophet ﷺ warned that it would:

> *"You will follow the ways of those before you, hand-span by hand-span and arm-length by arm-length, until if they entered the hole of a lizard you would enter it too."* They asked: *"The Jews and the Christians?"* He said: *"Who else?"* (al-Bukhārī 7320; Muslim 2669, from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī)

And the Qur'an addresses the community directly:

> *"And hold fast, all together, to the rope of God, and do not become divided."* (Qur'an 3:103)

The Qur'anic diagnosis of division as *baghy* — mutual rivalry — is at least as applicable to sectarian conflict among Muslims as to the divisions between religions. A community that reads this verse as being about other people has read it badly.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:20

> **And they say, "Why has no sign been sent down unto him from his Lord?" Say, "Truly the Unseen belongs to God alone. So wait! Truly I am among those waiting along with you."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Demand for a Miracle**

*Wa-yaqūlūna law-lā unzila ʿalayhi āyatun min rabbih.* The Makkans wanted a *sign* — meaning, in their usage, a spectacular physical miracle of the kind attributed to earlier prophets.

The Qur'an records the specific menu of their demands elsewhere, and it is worth quoting in full because it shows how the demand escalated:

> *"And they said, 'We will not believe thee until thou makest a spring gush forth for us from the earth, or hast a garden of date palms and grapevines and makest rivers gush forth abundantly therein, or makest the sky fall upon us in pieces as thou hast claimed, or bringest God and the angels before us, or hast a house of gold, or ascendest into the sky — and we will not believe thy ascent until thou bringest down to us a book we can read.' Say: Glory be to my Lord! Am I aught but a human messenger?"* (Qur'an 17:90–93)

The escalation exposes the demand. It is not a request for evidence; it is a series of moving goalposts. Al-Zamakhsharī observes in connection with our verse that they were insisting on a sign conforming to their own requests of v. 15 — that is, a sign of their own specification.

**Why the Qur'an Refuses This Demand**

The refusal is principled, and the Qur'an gives its reasons in several places.

First, signs *had* been given. The Qur'an itself is presented as the standing miracle — the sūrah calls its verses *āyāt* precisely because they are signs. The Qur'an makes the argument directly:

> *"Does it not suffice them that We have sent down unto thee the Book that is recited unto them? Truly in that is a mercy and a reminder for a people who believe."* (Qur'an 29:51)

Second, the Qur'an states that compelling signs had been withheld deliberately because of what happened to earlier peoples who received them:

> *"And naught prevented Us from sending signs, save that those of old denied them."* (Qur'an 17:59)

This is a sobering principle. A miracle that is rejected raises the stakes catastrophically: the peoples of Thamūd and of Pharaoh saw signs and denied them, and the denial after demonstration brought immediate destruction. Withholding the requested sign was therefore an act of restraint toward a people who had shown they would deny it.

Third — and this is the deepest reason — coercive evidence is incompatible with the purpose of the test. This sūrah will state it at v. 99: *"Wouldst thou compel men till they become believers?"* A sign that removes the possibility of refusal removes the meaning of acceptance.

**"The Unseen Belongs to God Alone"**

*Fa-qul innamā al-ghaybu lillāh.* The reply does not argue with the demand; it relocates it.

*Al-ghayb* — the Unseen — is one of the Qur'an's foundational categories. Its root *gh-y-b* means to be absent, hidden from perception. The *ghayb* comprises everything beyond human sensory and inferential access: God's essence, the angels, the decree, the hour of death, the events of the Hereafter, and — crucially here — the timing and manner of divine acts.

The Qur'an's most emphatic statement of exclusive divine possession of the Unseen is:

> *"And with Him are the keys of the Unseen; none knows them but He."* (Qur'an 6:59)

Al-Zamakhsharī explains the force of the reply in this context: the Prophet is once again told to state his passive relationship to revelation. Signs come from the Unseen; the Unseen is God's; therefore the decision to send a sign is not the Prophet's to make. He is being asked to authorise something he has no authority over.

**The Prophet Does Not Know the Unseen — and Says So**

This point is doctrinally significant and worth stating plainly, because popular religiosity in many places has blurred it.

The Qur'an has the Prophet ﷺ declare his own limits repeatedly:

> *"Say: I do not say to you that with me are the treasuries of God, nor do I know the Unseen, nor do I say to you that I am an angel. I follow only what is revealed to me."* (Qur'an 6:50)

> *"Say: I have no power to bring benefit or harm to myself, save as God wills. Had I knowledge of the Unseen, I would have amassed much good, and no evil would have touched me."* (Qur'an 7:188)

This sūrah repeats the same disclaimer at v. 49: *"I have no power over what harm or benefit may come to me, save as God wills."*

The Prophet ﷺ knew of the Unseen exactly what God disclosed to him, and no more. That is precisely what a messenger is. The distinction preserves both his dignity and God's exclusivity.

**"So Wait! I Am Among Those Waiting"**

*Fa-ntaẓirū innī maʿakum mina al-muntaẓirīn.*

This closing instruction is rhetorically remarkable, and both al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Jarīr's reading make its force clear. The paraphrase preserved in the source runs: "Wait, then, for the sign you seek; I too am among those waiting along with you for God's Judgment between us, so that the one following truth may triumph over the one following falsehood."

The command *intaẓirū* is what Arabic grammarians call an imperative of challenge (*amr li-l-taḥaddī*). It is not a polite suggestion. It says: very well — let us both wait and see how this turns out.

The confidence in this is extraordinary when one considers the circumstances. The speaker is an isolated man in a hostile city, with a handful of followers, several of them slaves being tortured, no army, no wealth, and no political protection. The people he is addressing control the sanctuary, the trade, and the tribal alliances of the entire region. And he is told to say: *wait — I am waiting too.*

Within twenty-three years, that wait resolved. Makkah opened to him without a battle; the idols of the Kaʿbah were cleared; and the men who had demanded signs stood in the courtyard of the sanctuary hearing their conqueror ask what they expected of him.

**The Discipline of Waiting**

There is a deeper spiritual instruction embedded in this reply, and it applies far beyond its historical setting.

The Prophet ﷺ is not told to force the outcome, to produce something impressive, or to defend himself further. He is told to *wait*, and to say that he is waiting. The Arabic *intiẓār* is more than passive delay; it is expectant waiting — waiting for something one is confident will come.

This is why the Prophet ﷺ described it as an act of worship:

> *"Waiting for relief is worship."* (al-Tirmidhī 3571 and others, from Ibn ʿAbbās; the hadith is transmitted with variation in its chains, but its meaning is corroborated by the Qur'an's own repeated commands to await God's judgment)

And the sūrah closes with the same instruction in fuller form: *"And be patient till God renders judgment, and He is the best of judges"* (v. 109). The chapter that begins by telling the deniers to wait ends by telling the believer to wait — the same posture, but with entirely different expectations of what the waiting will produce.

**Applying This: The Modern Demand for a Sign**

The demand for a decisive, compelling, undeniable sign has not disappeared; it has changed vocabulary. It appears now as: *if God exists, why does He not simply make Himself evident?* Why the ambiguity? Why permit honest people to reach opposite conclusions?

The Qur'anic answer is contained in this verse and in v. 99. Overwhelming evidence would produce compliance, and compliance is not what is wanted. What is wanted is *īmān* — a free movement of trust toward a God who has provided sufficient evidence without providing coercive evidence. The evidence in the Qur'an's view is genuinely sufficient: the ordered cosmos of vv. 5–6, the providence of v. 31, the moral structure of the conscience, the text itself. What it is not is *irresistible*.

The person who says "I would believe if God gave me a sign" should be honest with himself about what happened to the people who received them. The Qur'an's historical record — and this sūrah is largely a recital of it — is that signs convince those already willing to be convinced and harden the rest. The people of Pharaoh saw the staff become a serpent, the sea part, and the plagues arrive on schedule (vv. 75–92). It changed nothing.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:21

> **When We make men taste mercy after hardship has befallen them, behold, they have a plot regarding Our signs. Say, "God is swifter in plotting." Surely Our messengers record whatsoever you plot.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Historical Occasion: Seven Years of Drought**

Although this verse states a general law of human behaviour, the commentators attach it to a specific episode. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī record that the Makkans suffered a severe drought lasting seven years. In their desperation they came to the Prophet ﷺ — the very man they were persecuting — and asked him to pray for rain.

He did. Al-Ṭabarī, *Jalālayn*, and al-Zamakhsharī record that God sent abundant rain, described in this verse as *raḥmah*, mercy.

And then, as Ibn Kathīr and al-Zamakhsharī note, they returned to exactly what they had been doing before: rejecting and mocking the signs.

This is the pattern of v. 12 reproduced at the scale of a whole city. There it was one man on his sickbed; here it is a population under famine. The behaviour is identical.

**"Taste" — A Deliberately Chosen Verb**

*Idhā adhaqnā al-nāsa raḥmatan.* The verb *adhāqa* is causative of *dhāqa*, to taste.

Tasting is a peculiar kind of experience, and the choice of verb is precise. A taste is small, immediate, and sensory. One does not taste in the abstract; one tastes *this*, now, on the tongue. And a taste is a sample, not a meal.

The Qur'an uses this verb for both mercy and punishment (*"Taste the punishment everlasting"* — v. 52 of this sūrah), and in both cases it conveys direct, unmediated, personal experience. The rain after seven years of drought was not a theological proposition. It was water on cracked ground, and grass, and animals that did not die, and children who ate. It was *tasted*.

Which makes the response all the more damning.

**"Behold, They Have a Plot"**

*Idhā lahum makrun fī āyātinā.* The particle *idhā* here is what the grammarians call *idhā al-fujāʾiyyah*, the *idhā* of surprise — "and lo!", "and suddenly!" It marks an abrupt and unexpected turn. God gives; and suddenly, scheming.

*Makr* is the word to examine. Its root *m-k-r* denotes a covert manoeuvre — a plan executed by indirection, designed to produce an effect the target does not see coming. In Arabic it is not automatically negative; a *makr* is simply a stratagem, and its moral quality depends on its purpose.

What was their *makr* "regarding Our signs"? Ibn Kathīr and al-Zamakhsharī explain it as continued rejection and mockery. In practice this would have included: attributing the rain to natural cycles or to the stars (the pre-Islamic Arabs commonly credited rain to the setting of particular constellations, a practice the Prophet ﷺ explicitly condemned); circulating alternative explanations to prevent the event from functioning as evidence; and reasserting the authority of the idols they had abandoned during the crisis.

The essential move in every case is the same: to *disarm the sign*. Not to refute it — one cannot refute rain — but to construct a frame in which it means nothing.

**Attributing the Blessing to Anything But God**

The Prophet ﷺ addressed exactly this behaviour in a well-known hadith:

> He led the dawn prayer after a night of rain and said: *"Do you know what your Lord has said?"* They said: *"God and His Messenger know best."* He said: *"He said: This morning some of My servants became believers in Me and some disbelievers. As for the one who said, 'We have been given rain by the bounty of God and His mercy,' he is a believer in Me and a disbeliever in the stars. And as for the one who said, 'We have been given rain by such-and-such a star,' he is a disbeliever in Me and a believer in the stars."* (al-Bukhārī 846; Muslim 71, from Zayd ibn Khālid al-Juhanī)

The hadith is not a denial of meteorology. It is a statement about *attribution*. The rain has causes; the question is whether the chain of causes is understood to terminate in God or in itself. Modern versions of the same manoeuvre are readily available: every blessing can be assigned entirely to the market, the medicine, the algorithm, one's own foresight — and thereby stripped of any claim on gratitude.

**"God Is Swifter in Plotting"**

*Quli Allāhu asraʿu makran.*

This is a form of Qur'anic expression that requires care. God is described elsewhere as the best of plotters:

> *"And they plotted, and God plotted; and God is the best of plotters."* (Qur'an 3:54)

> *"And when those who disbelieve plotted against thee, to imprison thee or kill thee or expel thee — they plotted, and God plotted, and God is the best of plotters."* (Qur'an 8:30)

The theologians explain this usage carefully. Attributing *makr* to God is what the rhetoricians call *mushākalah* — matching the vocabulary of the opponent for effect. It does not mean God deceives. It means that the schemers' plans are met by a counter-arrangement they cannot see, which is precisely what they were attempting against Him.

Al-Bayḍāwī glosses God's plotting here as His punishment; more broadly, it refers to His control over the final outcome of things, against which human contrivance is futile.

The word *asraʿu*, "swifter," adds a temporal dimension. Their scheme takes planning, coordination, and time. God's response is immediate — indeed, it is in place before the scheme is conceived, since the scheme itself occurs within a reality He governs.

The Qur'an's most vivid illustration of this is in Sūrat al-Ṭāriq:

> *"Truly they are devising a plot, and I am devising a plan. So respite the disbelievers; grant them respite for a while."* (Qur'an 86:15–17)

**The Historical Demonstration**

The most striking historical illustration is the one referred to in 8:30, quoted above. The chiefs of Quraysh met in the *dār al-nadwah* and settled on a plan: a young man from each clan would strike the Prophet ﷺ simultaneously, so that the blood-claim would be distributed across all the clans and Banū Hāshim could not retaliate. It was, in purely political terms, an excellent plan.

That same night the Prophet ﷺ left Makkah. Their plan, executed exactly as designed, produced the emigration to Madinah — the event from which Islam's calendar is dated and from which the community's political existence began. Their *makr* was not defeated; it was *used*.

**"Our Messengers Record Whatsoever You Plot"**

*Inna rusulanā yaktubūna mā tamkurūn.*

*Rusul* here does not mean prophets. *Jalālayn*, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī and al-Zamakhsharī identify them as the angels charged with recording human deeds — the *kirāman kātibīn* of Sūrat al-Infiṭār:

> *"And truly over you are keepers — noble, writing — who know whatever you do."* (Qur'an 82:10–12)

> *"He utters not a word but that with him is a watcher ready."* (Qur'an 50:18)

The detail that matters here is *what* is recorded: not the deed but the *plot*. Scheming is an internal, invisible activity. The whole point of a *makr* is that it is unobserved. The verse says that the unobserved is being written down.

This closes a loophole in ordinary moral reasoning. Most people restrain their public conduct and consider their private intentions unaccountable. The verse says the record includes the planning stage — the conversation held in confidence, the calculation never voiced, the arrangement designed to appear as something else.

**The Practical Weight of Being Recorded**

The doctrine of the recording angels has a specific psychological function, and it is worth naming.

Human beings behave differently when observed. This is one of the most robust findings of ordinary experience and of social science alike; the mere image of eyes on a wall measurably increases honesty in unsupervised payment boxes. Islam's response to this feature of our psychology is not to install surveillance but to teach a fact: *you are already observed, always, including in the places you believe are private*.

The Prophet ﷺ made this the definition of religion's highest station. When Gabriel asked him about *iḥsān*, excellence, he replied:

> *"That you worship God as though you see Him; for if you do not see Him, truly He sees you."* (al-Bukhārī 50; Muslim 8, from ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb)

The clause that does the work is the second. The first — worshipping as though you see Him — is a lofty spiritual state few sustain. The second is simply a fact, available to anyone: *He sees you*. A person who genuinely internalises that one fact will not need to be supervised.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:22

> **He it is Who carries you over land and sea, even when you are sailing in ships, till, when they sail with them upon a favorable wind, and rejoice therein, there comes upon them a violent gale, and the waves come at them from every side, and they think they shall be encompassed by them. They call upon God, devoting religion entirely to Him: "If Thou savest us from this, we shall surely be among the thankful!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Parable Made of Salt Water**

Al-Rāzī observes that this verse functions as the concrete illustration of v. 21. That verse stated the principle — mercy after hardship, followed by scheming — but gave no example. Here the example arrives, and it is the most cinematic passage in the sūrah.

The Qur'an chooses a storm at sea, and the choice is apt for reasons that are worth spelling out. Of all the situations available to a seventh-century Arab, none removes human agency so completely. On land, a man in danger can run, hide, fight, or negotiate. On a wooden ship in a gale, there is nothing to do. The sea is the one place where the illusion of self-sufficiency cannot be maintained even for a minute.

**The Narrative Technique: A Shift in Person**

Something remarkable happens in the grammar of this verse, and readers of the Arabic have always noticed it.

The verse opens in the second person: *huwa alladhī yusayyirukum* — "He it is Who carries *you*." It continues: *kuntum fī al-fulk* — "when *you* are in ships."

Then, mid-sentence, it switches to the third person: *wa-jarayna bihim* — "and they sail with *them*"; *farihū bihā* — "*they* rejoice therein"; *jāʾathā rīḥun ʿāṣif* — the gale comes upon *them*.

The rhetoricians call this *iltifāt*, the turn. Its effect here is precise and powerful. The addressee is drawn in personally — *you* are on this ship — and then, at the exact moment the wind turns favourable and the passengers begin to enjoy themselves, the camera pulls back and they become *them*. The reader is shifted from participant to observer, watching the scene unfold from outside.

The effect is to make the reader see what the passengers cannot. They are congratulating themselves on a fine voyage. We are watching from a distance, and we know what is coming.

**The False Security of a Favourable Wind**

*Jarayna bihim bi-rīḥin ṭayyibatin wa-fariḥū bihā.* A good wind, and they *rejoice*.

The verb *fariḥa* denotes exuberant delight — and the Qur'an consistently marks it as the emotion of those who have forgotten their situation. Qārūn *rejoiced* in his treasure (28:76); the deniers *rejoice* in what they have of knowledge (40:83). It is the joy of someone who thinks the conditions of his happiness are secure.

There is a hard truth here about the psychology of comfort, and it connects directly to v. 7. What makes the passengers vulnerable is not the storm; the storm is external and unavoidable. What makes them vulnerable is that the good wind had already persuaded them that nothing was required of them. Twenty minutes earlier, they were in exactly the same relationship of total dependence on God as they were during the gale. The only difference was that they could not see it.

**The Storm: Three Escalating Details**

The Qur'an builds the crisis in three strokes, and each raises the pressure.

*Jāʾathā rīḥun ʿāṣif* — a violent gale comes upon them. *ʿĀṣif* is from *ʿ-ṣ-f*, to blow fiercely; the word is used of a wind that strips and scatters.

*Wa-jāʾahumu al-mawju min kulli makān* — "and the waves come at them from every place." This is the detail that closes off hope. A threat from one direction can be steered against. A threat from every direction cannot.

*Wa-ẓannū annahum uḥīṭa bihim* — "and they think that they are encompassed." The verb *uḥīṭa*, passive of *aḥāṭa*, means to be surrounded, enclosed, with no gap. It is the word used for a siege. The Arabs used *uḥīṭa bihi* idiomatically for a man who is finished — destruction has closed around him.

**"Devoting Religion Entirely to Him" — The Instant Conversion**

*Daʿawu Allāha mukhliṣīna lahu al-dīn.*

Here is the sūrah's central claim about human nature, stated as an observation rather than an argument. Under sufficient pressure, the polytheist becomes a monotheist. Not gradually, not after reflection — *instantly*.

*Mukhliṣīna lahu al-dīn* is precisely the phrase Islam uses for its own ideal of pure worship. *Ikhlāṣ*, from *kh-l-ṣ*, means to make something pure, unmixed, free of adulterants — the same root gives *khāliṣ*, unalloyed gold. To devote religion *entirely* to Him is to have no other addressee in one's prayer.

Al-Zamakhsharī notes that this refers to their promise not to associate partners with God. And the astonishing thing is that these are the same men who, on shore, insisted that al-Lāt and al-ʿUzzā were their intercessors (v. 18). In the storm, nobody prays to the intercessors. The Qur'an says so in almost identical terms elsewhere:

> *"And when a wave covers them like awnings, they call upon God, devoting religion entirely to Him. But when He delivers them safely to land, some of them are lukewarm."* (Qur'an 31:32)

> *"And when they board a ship, they call upon God, devoting religion entirely to Him; but when He delivers them safely to land, behold, they ascribe partners."* (Qur'an 29:65)

**What This Proves About Idolatry**

The evidential force of this observation is considerable, and it is the reason the Qur'an returns to it three times.

If polytheism were a genuine conviction — a sincerely held theory about how the divine world is organised — it would survive a crisis. Real convictions do; people have died for them. Instead it evaporates the moment it is tested. The intercessors, so essential on a normal Tuesday in Makkah, are not even mentioned when the ship is going down.

The conclusion the Qur'an draws is that idolatry is not a belief at all but a *convenience* — a socially maintained arrangement that persists only while nothing depends on it. Beneath it, at all times, lies the *fiṭrah*: the direct, unmediated recognition of one God, which surfaces the instant the social scaffolding is removed.

Which means, in turn, that the moment of terror is not a moment of *conversion*. It is a moment of *disclosure*. Nothing new is created; something permanent is uncovered.

**The Promise: "We Shall Surely Be Among the Thankful"**

*La-in anjaytanā min hādhihi la-nakūnanna mina al-shākirīn.*

The Arabic is emphatic three times over: the conditional particle *la-in*, the emphatic *la-* on the response, and the heavy emphatic ending *-anna* on the verb. This is a vow made with the maximum available force of the language.

And its content is instructive. They do not promise wealth, sacrifice, or temple-building. They promise *gratitude* — *shukr*. Even in extremity, the human being knows what the appropriate response to rescue is.

The tragedy, of course, is the next verse. But it is worth noting before we get there that the promise itself was correct. They identified the right obligation. What failed was not their moral perception but their memory.

**Reading This Verse in a World Without Sailing Ships**

The specific scene is dated; the situation is not. Everyone eventually finds himself in a version of this ship.

It is the phone call from the hospital. It is the moment the aircraft drops and does not immediately recover. It is the accident that happens too fast to respond to. It is the reading of a test result. In each case the machinery of ordinary control — plans, savings, competence, connections — is revealed to be irrelevant to the thing at hand, and what emerges from a person in those seconds is, as this verse says, direct and unmediated prayer.

The verse's implicit question is worth putting to oneself deliberately, in calm weather: *the relationship with God that appears in that moment — is it available to me now?* Because the God addressed in the storm is the same God who is present on the calm sea. The difference is entirely in the passenger.

The Prophet ﷺ gave the practical instruction that follows from this:

> *"Know God in times of ease, and He will know you in times of hardship."* (al-Tirmidhī 2516; Aḥmad 2804, from Ibn ʿAbbās)

That single sentence is the correct response to this verse. The relationship established in the good wind is what one has to draw on in the gale.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:23

> **Then when He saves them, behold, they behave tyrannically upon the earth without right. O mankind! Your tyranny is only against yourselves—the enjoyment of the life of this world. Then unto Us shall be your return, whereupon We shall inform you of that which you used to do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Rescue and Its Immediate Sequel**

*Fa-lammā anjāhum idhā hum yabghūna fī al-arḍi bi-ghayri al-ḥaqq.*

God does save them. This should be noted before anything else: the prayer of the panicking polytheist is answered. The Qur'an does not say that God refused the desperate call of people who had spent their lives worshipping stones. He rescued them.

And then — *idhā*, the particle of abruptness again, exactly as in v. 21 — the moment their feet touch dry ground, they begin to *transgress*.

Notice how fast this is. Verse 22 ended with a triple-emphatic vow of gratitude. Verse 23 begins with the vow already broken. The Qur'an does not even narrate an interval.

**"Tyranny" — The Meaning of *Baghy***

*Yabghūna fī al-arḍ* — from *b-gh-y*, whose basic sense is to seek, to desire, and by extension to seek beyond one's right, to overreach, to oppress. *Baghy* in Qur'anic usage covers aggression, insolence, and the transgression of limits. Al-Baghawī and al-Zamakhsharī gloss the behaviour here as transgressing God's command and acting corruptly.

The qualifying phrase *bi-ghayri al-ḥaqq*, "without right," is technically important. It distinguishes *baghy* from the legitimate exercise of power. There is such a thing as authority rightly held and rightly used; the previous verses on vicegerency (v. 14) presuppose it. *Baghy* is what authority becomes when it is detached from truth and answerable to nothing.

**Why Rescue Produces Arrogance**

The psychological sequence here is worth thinking through, because it seems paradoxical. One would expect a near-death experience to produce humility. The verse says it produces the opposite.

The mechanism, on reflection, is not obscure. The survivor tells himself a story about his survival, and the story almost always centres on himself: *I kept my head; I made the right call; I was strong enough; I got through it.* The rescue that was received as a gift in the water becomes, on land, an achievement. And an achievement generates entitlement.

There is a second mechanism, subtler. Having been terrified, a person needs to re-establish his sense of control, and the fastest way to feel powerful is to exercise power over someone weaker. Humiliation frequently produces domination rather than humility. The Qur'an's placement of *baghy* immediately after rescue captures something real about how human beings process fear.

**"Your Tyranny Is Only Against Yourselves"**

*Yā ayyuhā al-nāsu innamā baghyukum ʿalā anfusikum.*

The address changes here. Until now the verse has been narrating in the third person; now, abruptly, it turns and addresses humanity directly: *O mankind!* The rhetorical effect is that of a speaker breaking off a story to look the audience in the eye.

And the statement made is the sūrah's most important ethical claim: your aggression lands on *you*.

This is not a moral platitude. It is a structural claim about how wrongdoing works, and the Qur'an makes it repeatedly:

> *"Truly God does not wrong human beings in the least, but rather human beings wrong themselves."* (Qur'an 10:44 — this same sūrah)

> *"Whoever does righteousness, it is for his own soul; and whoever does evil, it is against it."* (Qur'an 41:46)

> *"And they wronged Us not, but they used to wrong themselves."* (Qur'an 2:57)

**How Wrongdoing Injures the Wrongdoer**

It is worth making the claim concrete, because stated abstractly it can sound like consolation offered to victims rather than a description of reality.

*It deforms the agent.* Every act of oppression requires a self-justification, and self-justifications are not discarded after use; they become part of how the person sees the world. A man who cheats must adopt the view that the other party deserved it, or that everyone does it, or that rules are for the naive. Having adopted that view, he now lives inside it. He has not merely done a bad thing; he has become a person who believes something false about human beings.

*It destroys trust, which is the wrongdoer's own infrastructure.* The oppressor must eventually live in a world of people who cannot be trusted, because he has trained himself to expect from others what he practises himself.

*It removes the possibility of rest.* The Prophet ﷺ defined righteousness by exactly this criterion:

> *"Righteousness is good character, and sin is what wavers in your breast and which you would hate for people to find out about."* (Muslim 2553, from al-Nawwās ibn Simʿān)

*And it is answerable.* This is the decisive point, and the verse states it in its final clause.

**"The Enjoyment of the Life of This World"**

*Matāʿa al-ḥayāti al-dunyā.* The commentators note that this phrase can be read in two ways depending on the vowelling, and both readings are illuminating.

Read as an accusative of specification, the sense is: your transgression profits you only *the enjoyment of this world's life* — a brief material gain, and nothing beyond.

Read as the predicate of the sentence, as the source notes, the rendering becomes: "Your tyranny against yourselves is only the enjoyment of the life of this world" — that is, the whole enterprise amounts to nothing more than a temporary pleasure.

Either way the point is the accounting. *Matāʿ* is a significant word: it denotes goods, provisions, something one makes use of for a period — from *m-t-ʿ*, to enjoy or benefit temporarily. The Qur'an consistently applies it to this world:

> *"And the life of this world is naught but the enjoyment of delusion (matāʿ al-ghurūr)."* (Qur'an 3:185)

So the exchange being described is: a permanent deformation of the soul and a debt owed at judgment, purchased with a temporary convenience. Put in commercial terms, which the Makkan merchants would have understood immediately, it is a catastrophic trade.

**"Then Unto Us Shall Be Your Return"**

*Thumma ilaynā marjiʿukum fa-nunabbiʾukum bi-mā kuntum taʿmalūn.*

The verse ends by naming the settlement date. The word *marjiʿ* — the place of return — echoes v. 4 exactly, binding the psychological analysis of this passage to the eschatological framework established at the sūrah's opening.

And the verb chosen for what happens then is *nunabbiʾukum*: "We shall *inform* you."

This is a striking choice, and it recurs across the Qur'an (5:105; 6:60; 9:94; 62:8). The Day of Judgment is described not primarily as a day of punishment but as a day of *information*. The dominant experience will be finding out.

Finding out what? What one actually did — as distinct from what one told oneself one was doing. This is the moment when the self-justifications examined above are stripped away and the acts are seen bare, in the light in which God saw them all along. The Qur'an describes it as the presentation of a complete record:

> *"And the Book will be laid open, and thou wilt see the guilty fearful of what is in it. They will say, 'Woe unto us! What a Book is this! It leaves out nothing small or great, but has enumerated it.' And they will find all that they did present before them, and thy Lord wrongs no one."* (Qur'an 18:49)

**The Whole Sequence in Summary**

Verses 21–23 form a single unit, and read together they trace a complete cycle: hardship, prayer, rescue, forgetting, transgression, and finally the reckoning.

The cycle is exact and it is universal. What breaks it is the one thing the passengers promised and did not deliver: *shukr*, gratitude — understood in Islam not as a warm feeling but as the deliberate use of a restored blessing in the service of the One who restored it.

The verse's final clause is the reason the cycle matters. If the return were not real, the cycle would be merely sad. Because the return is real, the cycle is a trajectory, and its endpoint is a conversation in which one is informed, in complete detail, of exactly what one did.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:24

> **The parable of the life of this world is that of water which We send down from the sky: the earth's vegetation, from which men and cattle eat, mingles with it till, when the earth takes on its luster and is adorned, and its inhabitants think they have gained mastery over it, Our Command comes upon it by night or by day, whereupon We make it a mown field, as if it had not flourished the day before! Thus do We expound the signs for a people who reflect.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Why a Parable at This Point**

The preceding verses described people who transgress after rescue and settle for *matāʿ al-ḥayāt al-dunyā*, "the enjoyment of the life of this world" (v. 23). This verse asks the obvious follow-up question: what exactly is that enjoyment worth?

Rather than answer with an argument, the Qur'an answers with a picture. Ibn Jubayr and al-Kalbī note that the parable is laid down to draw attention to the evanescent nature of this world, and al-Rāzī observes that it is aimed at those deluded by it who have no concern for the Hereafter.

A parable does something an argument cannot. An argument informs the mind; an image lodges in the memory and colours perception afterwards. Once you have seen a flattened field where a garden stood, you cannot look at a garden in quite the same way.

**The Cycle Described**

The Qur'an builds the picture in careful stages, and each stage corresponds to a phase of a human life or a civilisation.

*Rain descends.* *Kamāʾin anzalnāhu min al-samāʾ* — water from the sky, arriving from outside, unearned by anything below.

*Vegetation mingles.* *Fa-khtalaṭa bihi nabātu al-arḍ* — the verb *ikhtalaṭa* means to intermingle thoroughly, and it captures the density of growth after rain in an arid land: species tangled together, ground disappearing under green.

*Men and cattle eat.* *Mimmā yaʾkulu al-nāsu wa-l-anʿām.* Real benefit is derived. The Qur'an does not despise the world's goods; it acknowledges that people and animals genuinely live on this.

*The earth is adorned.* *Ḥattā idhā akhadhati al-arḍu zukhrufahā wa-zzayyanat.* Two words for beauty: *zukhruf* (ornament, gilding — the same word gives its name to Sūrah 43) and *zayyanat* (she adorned herself). Al-Rāzī notes the delight this brings to the inhabitants, who see the multiple ways they can benefit from it.

There is an unmistakable personification here. The earth *puts on her finery*. The image is of a bride dressed for a wedding — which makes what follows all the more shocking.

*The illusion of mastery.* *Wa-ẓanna ahluhā annahum qādirūna ʿalayhā* — "and its inhabitants think they have gained mastery over it."

**The Turning Point: The Word *Qādirūna***

This clause is the hinge of the parable, and it identifies the exact moment at which the situation becomes dangerous.

The catastrophe does not arrive when the crop is planted, nor when it grows, nor when it is enjoyed. It arrives when the inhabitants *think they are in control of it*. The verb is *ẓanna* — the Qur'an's word for supposition (see vv. 36 and 66 of this sūrah) — and the object of the supposition is *qudrah*, power.

The delusion is precise. They did not send the rain. They did not design the seed, or the soil chemistry, or the sequence by which a green shoot converts light into matter. They tilled and waited. Yet the visible result stands on their land, and possession slides imperceptibly into authorship.

This is the same error as v. 23's, transposed from the moral to the economic register. There, the survivor took credit for his survival; here, the farmer takes credit for the harvest.

**"Our Command Comes Upon It by Night or by Day"**

*Atāhā amrunā laylan aw nahāran.*

The commentators identify the *amr* as some sudden destructive event — al-Kalbī, al-Nasafī, al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī suggest a violent wind, a torrent, or a blast. Al-Thaʿlabī notes that *by night or by day* means simply: at any moment.

Two details deserve attention.

First, the timing is unspecified precisely because unpredictability is the point. If disaster came only at night, one could stay awake. If only in winter, one could prepare. *Laylan aw nahāran* forecloses all such management.

Second, the destruction is described as *amrunā* — "Our Command." It is not an accident, a system failure, or bad luck. In the Qur'anic frame, the same governance (*yudabbiru al-amr*, v. 3) that arranged the rain arranges the wind. Blessing and its removal come from one hand.

**"A Mown Field, as if It Had Not Flourished the Day Before"**

*Fa-jaʿalnāhā ḥaṣīdan ka-an lam taghna bi-l-ams.*

*Ḥaṣīd* is harvested stubble — a field cut level. The image is of total flatness where there had been height and colour.

But the phrase that does the real work is *ka-an lam taghna bi-l-ams*: "as if it had not flourished yesterday." The verb *ghaniya* means to be rich, to thrive, to be well-off — and here, of vegetation, to be lush.

The devastating word is *bi-l-ams*, "yesterday." Not a decade ago. Not in some remote past. *Yesterday* the field was at its most beautiful; today the ground shows no evidence it was ever there.

This is the parable's psychological sting, and it corresponds to real experience with uncomfortable precision. What is most striking about ruin is not that it happens but how completely it erases the memory of what preceded it. A dynasty falls and within a generation the streets are named after someone else. A company that dominated an industry is a footnote in five years. A man dies and his desk is cleared by Tuesday.

Al-Rāzī draws the moral, and the source preserves his words:

> *"Such is the one who invests his heart in the pleasures of this world and its delights: when these things part from him, his sorrow becomes immense, and he grieves for them."*

**The Parallel Parable in Sūrat al-Kahf**

The Qur'an gives a closely related version at 18:45, and reading them together sharpens both:

> *"And set forth for them the parable of the life of this world: it is as water We send down from the sky; the vegetation of the earth mingles with it; then it becomes dry stubble scattered by the winds. And God is Powerful over all things."* (Qur'an 18:45)

The Kahf version is more compressed, and it adds the detail of scattering by wind — not merely cut down but dispersed, so that even the stubble does not remain in place. The version here in Yūnus adds what Kahf omits: the psychological middle stage, the moment of imagined mastery, which is the moral centre of the story.

**What the Parable Is Not Saying**

It would be a serious misreading to take this as a call to withdraw from the world, and the Qur'an guards against that reading in the parable itself.

Note that the vegetation is explicitly described as food for people and livestock. It is *real* benefit, divinely provided, and its consumption is not criticised. The rain is a mercy. Nothing in the parable suggests that farming, prosperity, or enjoyment is wrong.

What is criticised is a specific error: mistaking the temporary for the permanent, and mistaking stewardship for ownership. Islam's classical position on this is not asceticism but proportion, and the Qur'an states it in a single balanced instruction:

> *"But seek, through what God has given thee, the Abode of the Hereafter; and forget not thy portion of this world."* (Qur'an 28:77)

The Prophet ﷺ gave the practical formulation:

> *"Be in this world as though you were a stranger or a wayfarer."* And Ibn ʿUmar used to say: *"When you reach the evening, do not expect the morning; and when you reach the morning, do not expect the evening. Take from your health for your illness, and from your life for your death."* (al-Bukhārī 6416)

A wayfarer is not indifferent to the road. He uses the inn, eats the meal, and is grateful for good weather. He simply does not redecorate the room.

**"For a People Who Reflect"**

*Kadhālika nufaṣṣilu al-āyāti li-qawmin yatafakkarūn.*

This closing formula varies through the sūrah — for people who *know* (v. 5), who are *reverent* (v. 6), who *hear* (v. 67) — and here it is *yatafakkarūn*, who reflect.

*Tafakkur* is deliberate, sustained mental engagement: turning a matter over. The choice of this quality for this verse is exact, because a parable *requires* it. An image left unexamined is merely a pretty picture. Its meaning has to be extracted by the reader's own work — which is why parables teach more durably than statements. What you work out yourself, you retain.

The early Muslims treated *tafakkur* as a formal spiritual practice. Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī is reported to have said: *"An hour's reflection is better than a night's worship."* The point is not to disparage worship but to note that reflection is what converts information into conviction.

**A Reflection for the Modern Reader**

The parable's imagery is agricultural, but its application is not limited to farming, and the modern reader can supply his own instances without difficulty.

Consider any structure that currently seems solid: a career, a market position, a national economy, a state of health. Each was built with real effort. Each produces real benefit. And each is, on inspection, dependent on conditions entirely outside anyone's control — a diagnosis, a policy change, a technological shift, a war on another continent.

The parable does not ask us to stop building. It asks us to hold the building without the sentence *"they think they have gained mastery over it."* That single clause is the difference between a person whose life is destroyed by reversal and one who is grieved by it but not destroyed — because he never mistook the garden for his own creation, and therefore does not lose himself when the field is mown.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:25

> **And God calls unto the Abode of Peace, and guides whomsoever He will unto a straight path.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer to the Parable**

Immediately after the picture of a world that flowers and is flattened, this verse offers the alternative. The commentators make the connection explicit: the opposite of the impermanent and illusory life described in vv. 23–24 is the *dār al-salām*, the Abode of Peace, which is the *dār al-qarār*, the Abode of Permanence (40:39).

The structure of the sūrah's argument is thus complete at this point. It has diagnosed the problem (attachment to the transient), demonstrated it (the parable), and now names the alternative — and, crucially, presents it not as an abstract possibility but as an *invitation already issued*.

**"God Calls" — The Initiative Is His**

*Wa-Allāhu yadʿū ilā dāri al-salām.*

Take the grammar seriously: God is the subject of the verb. He is the caller.

This inverts the natural assumption. Most religious language, and most religious feeling, imagines human beings searching for God — climbing toward Him, seeking access, petitioning for entry. This verse describes God calling *out* to human beings.

Al-Ṭabāṭabāʾī understands the calling to be the very process by which human beings come into existence and are thereby placed within reach of felicity — that is, the summons is built into the fact of being created at all. On this reading, existence itself is the invitation.

The Qur'an elsewhere describes this divine initiative in terms that are almost startling in their warmth:

> *"Say: O My servants who have transgressed against their own souls, despair not of God's Mercy. Truly God forgives all sins."* (Qur'an 39:53)

And the hadith qudsī:

> *"Whoever draws near to Me by a hand-span, I draw near to him by an arm's length; whoever draws near to Me by an arm's length, I draw near to him by two arms' length; and whoever comes to Me walking, I come to him running."* (al-Bukhārī 7405; Muslim 2675, from Abū Hurayrah)

The proportions in that hadith are not decorative. They describe an asymmetry: the servant's movement is small and God's response is disproportionate. This verse is the same doctrine in a single clause.

**The Universality of the Call**

Al-Zamakhsharī's comment on this verse is preserved in the source and deserves to be quoted, because it resolves the tension that the second half of the verse raises:

> *"He calls all people to the Abode of Peace, but only those who are guided enter it."*

The call is universal; the entry is not. And this distinction is essential to Islamic theology, because it separates two things that are often confused: God's *will of command* (what He invites and commands all people to do) and His *will of decree* (what He determines will actually occur).

The Qur'an is unambiguous that God does not desire disbelief for His servants:

> *"He is not pleased with disbelief for His servants; and if you are thankful, He is pleased with that for you."* (Qur'an 39:7)

So the summons is genuine and it is addressed to everyone. No one is uninvited.

**"The Abode of Peace" — Unpacking *Dār al-Salām***

The name given to the destination is remarkably chosen. Not the Abode of Pleasure, or Reward, or Beauty — though the Qur'an uses all those images elsewhere. The Abode of *Peace*.

Three layers give the name its depth.

*Salām is a name of God.* Ājībah and Ibn Ḥayyān note that because "Peace" is one of God's names (see 59:23), *dār al-salām* is where God "is" — the Abode of Peace is God's own house, so to speak, named after Him.

*Salām connotes safety.* Many commentators — Ibn Ḥayyān, al-Kāshānī, al-Qushayrī, al-Thaʿlabī, al-Zamakhsharī — draw attention to the link between *salām* (peace) and *salāmah* (safety, soundness, freedom from defect). The inhabitants are at peace because they are *safe*: safe from perishing, from loss, from fear, from the possibility of ending.

*Salām is what is spoken there.* As v. 10 of this sūrah established, the greeting of the Garden is *salām*, and it is what God Himself says to its people (36:58).

**Why "Peace" Is the Right Name for Ultimate Happiness**

There is a piece of practical wisdom in this naming that is worth drawing out, because it corrects a common misconception about Paradise.

Human beings, asked to imagine perfect happiness, tend to imagine *intensity*: pleasure at maximum, continuously. But intensity is not sustainable and, more importantly, it is not what people actually long for when they examine themselves honestly.

What is missing from ordinary life is not pleasure — pleasure is abundantly available — but *rest*. The absence of the low-grade continuous anxiety that accompanies every good thing we possess: the awareness that health can fail, that relationships end, that positions are lost, that we are all on a clock. Every joy in this world is accompanied by an unspoken clause: *this will not last.*

*Dār al-salām* is the name for a condition in which that clause has been struck out. The Qur'an describes its inhabitants in exactly these terms:

> *"No fatigue shall touch them therein, nor shall they be expelled from it."* (Qur'an 15:48)

> *"Therein they will not taste death, save the first death."* (Qur'an 44:56)

And this sūrah's own description of the friends of God — *"no fear shall come upon them, nor shall they grieve"* (v. 62) — names precisely the two temporal anxieties: fear (about the future) and grief (about the past). Remove both, and what remains is *salām*.

**"Guides Whomsoever He Will" — The Theological Question**

*Wa-yahdī man yashāʾu ilā ṣirāṭin mustaqīm.*

This clause raises the question that has occupied Muslim theologians for fourteen centuries: if God guides whom He wills, in what sense is anyone responsible?

The commentators addressed it directly, and the answer preserved in the source is careful. Al-Ālūsī and al-Zamakhsharī hold that although God wishes for everyone to receive guidance — since He is not pleased with disbelief for His servants (39:7) — His *guiding whomsoever He will* means that He grants guidance to those of His servants who He knows will benefit by it. In this sense, God's will is predicated upon His wisdom.

This is the crucial qualification. Divine will is not arbitrary preference; it is the operation of perfect wisdom upon perfect knowledge. God is not choosing at random. He is acting on complete knowledge of each soul — including the knowledge of who is disposed to receive.

The Qur'an supports this reading by repeatedly linking guidance and misguidance to prior human orientation:

> *"And God does not lead astray any people after He has guided them, until He makes clear to them that of which they should be wary."* (Qur'an 9:115)

> *"So when they deviated, God caused their hearts to deviate."* (Qur'an 61:5)

> *"And as for those who are guided, He increases them in guidance."* (Qur'an 47:17)

The pattern in each case is: a human movement, then a divine response that confirms and amplifies it. Guidance is a gift, but it is a gift given to the outstretched hand.

**The Straight Path**

*Ṣirāṭ mustaqīm.* The phrase every Muslim recites at least seventeen times a day in al-Fātiḥah.

*Ṣirāṭ* is a broad, paved, public road — the word is a borrowing from Latin *strata* (whence English "street"), which is itself significant: this is not a private mountain track for spiritual specialists but a highway. *Mustaqīm* means straight, upright, direct.

The virtue of a straight road is that it is the shortest distance and that it does not require constant navigation. Ibn Masʿūd's illustration, recorded in the collections, is the classic one: the Prophet ﷺ drew a straight line and said *"This is the path of God,"* then drew lines branching off it and said *"These are paths, upon each of which is a devil calling to it,"* and recited: *"And this is My path, straight, so follow it, and follow not the [other] ways, lest they separate you from His way"* (Qur'an 6:153; the report is in Aḥmad 4142 and al-Nasāʾī).

**Reading the Verse as an Invitation**

The verse's most important feature is its tense. God *calls* — present, continuous.

This is not a record of an invitation issued in the past to someone else. The call is being made now, to whoever is reading. And the two halves of the verse divide the responsibility with precision: the *call* is God's initiative, universal and unconditional; the *guidance* is God's gift, given to those disposed to receive it.

What remains to the human being is the disposition — the turning toward the voice. This sūrah will state the human side of it in its final movement: *"Whosoever is rightly guided is only rightly guided for the sake of his own soul"* (v. 108).

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:26

> **Unto those who are virtuous shall be that which is most beautiful and more besides. Neither darkness nor abasement shall come over their faces. It is they who are the inhabitants of the Garden; they shall abide therein.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reward of *Iḥsān***

*Li-lladhīna aḥsanū al-ḥusnā wa-ziyādah.*

The Arabic is built on a single root repeated: *aḥsanū* (those who did *iḥsān*) and *al-ḥusnā* (the most beautiful). The reward is named with the same word as the deed. Those who did beautifully receive the beautiful — the correspondence is built into the language.

*Iḥsān* is one of Islam's key terms, and it means more than "doing good." Its root *ḥ-s-n* denotes beauty and excellence. *Iḥsān* is therefore doing something *well* — with mastery, care, and completion — as distinct from merely doing it. The Prophet ﷺ gave the term its definitive religious sense in the hadith of Gabriel:

> *"That you worship God as though you see Him; for if you do not see Him, truly He sees you."* (al-Bukhārī 50; Muslim 8, from ʿUmar)

And he extended it to the whole of conduct, including to animals:

> *"Truly God has prescribed excellence (iḥsān) in all things. So if you kill, kill well; and if you slaughter, slaughter well. Let each of you sharpen his blade and spare suffering to the animal he slaughters."* (Muslim 1955, from Shaddād ibn Aws)

That hadith is worth pausing on, because it shows how far the concept reaches. *Iḥsān* is not confined to prayer. It governs the quality of every act — including the least glamorous, performed where no one is watching.

**"And More Besides" — The Most Discussed Word in the Verse**

*Wa-ziyādah.* "And an increase."

The majority of commentators — *Jalālayn*, al-Qurṭubī, al-Qummī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Thaʿlabī — understand *al-ḥusnā* as Paradise and *ziyādah* as the vision of God in the Hereafter, alluded to in 75:23. The interpretation rests on an explicit hadith, which the source preserves:

> When the Prophet ﷺ was asked about the words *"Unto those who are virtuous shall be that which is most beautiful and more besides,"* he said: *"When the inhabitants of Paradise enter Paradise and the inhabitants of the Fire enter the Fire, a caller will call out, 'O inhabitants of Paradise! You have a promise from God that He wishes to fulfil for you.' They will reply, 'And what can that be? Has God not already made heavy our scales, whitened our faces, caused us to enter Paradise, and saved us from the Fire?' Then He will remove the veil, and they will look upon Him. By God, God will give them nothing more beloved to them, nor anything cooler to their eyes, than the vision of Him."* (recorded by Muslim 181, from Ṣuhayb; cited by al-Qurṭubī in his commentary on this verse)

**Why the Vision Is the Real Reward**

The hadith's structure is worth examining, because it makes a theological point through narrative.

The inhabitants of Paradise are asked what more they could want, and their answer is a list of everything one might have supposed constituted salvation: the scales weighted, the faces whitened, entry granted, the Fire escaped. In other words: *we already have everything.*

And then something is given that exceeds all of it — so far that the hadith says nothing they have received is more beloved to them.

The theological implication is that Paradise, in the fullest Islamic understanding, is not fundamentally a place of gardens and rivers. It is a place where the veil is removed. The gardens are the setting; the encounter is the substance. Ibn al-Qayyim developed this at length: the deepest human longing is not for pleasure but for the Beloved, and the highest pleasure of Paradise is the one that answers that longing directly.

This is why the Qur'an describes the corresponding deprivation of the deniers in exactly these terms:

> *"Nay! Truly on that Day they shall be veiled from their Lord."* (Qur'an 83:15)

The punishment named there is not fire. It is the veil. And by symmetry, the reward named here is its removal.

**The Doctrine of the Beatific Vision in Sunni Theology**

Since this is a point on which Muslim schools have differed, it is worth noting the positions.

The Sunni majority — Ashʿarī, Māturīdī, and the traditionist schools — affirm that the believers will see God in the Hereafter, in a manner without modality, without direction, and without comparison to created seeing. Their primary evidence is 75:22–23:

> *"Faces that Day will be radiant, gazing upon their Lord."*

together with the hadith recorded in both Ṣaḥīḥs:

> *"You will see your Lord as you see this moon, having no difficulty in seeing it."* (al-Bukhārī 554; Muslim 633, from Jarīr ibn ʿAbd Allāh)

The Muʿtazilah denied the vision, arguing that sight requires spatial location and God is not in space. The Sunni response, given by al-Ashʿarī and refined by later theologians, was that the vision is affirmed as revealed while its modality is denied to be like created vision — the same *bi-lā kayf* principle applied to *istawā* in v. 3.

The source also notes that Sufis have held the vision to be possible in this life for those who attain spiritual perfection — a position that the mainstream theologians generally restricted, on the strength of the Prophet's ﷺ report and Moses' request at 7:143, to the Hereafter.

**"Neither Darkness Nor Abasement Shall Come Over Their Faces"**

*Wa-lā yarhaqu wujūhahum qatarun wa-lā dhillah.*

*Qatar* is the dark dust or smoke-grime that settles on a face; *dhillah* is humiliation, abasement. The verb *yarhaqu* means to overtake and cover, to overwhelm.

The Qur'an consistently uses the face as the register on which a soul's condition is displayed. This is psychologically astute — the face is where shame, fear, and joy become visible whether one wishes it or not.

The contrast is drawn explicitly at 75:22–24 and at 80:38–41:

> *"Some faces that Day will be bright, laughing, rejoicing. And other faces that Day will have dust upon them, darkness covering them."* (Qur'an 80:38–41)

And the next verse of our sūrah supplies the counterpart image: the faces of the deniers *"as if covered with dark patches of night"* (v. 27).

The radiance promised here is therefore not cosmetic. It is the outward form of an inward state. And the commentators connect it directly to the *ziyādah*: their faces are radiant *because* they are gazing upon their Lord (75:22–23). The light is reflected.

**"They Shall Abide Therein"**

*Ulāʾika aṣḥābu al-jannati hum fīhā khālidūn.*

The word *aṣḥāb* is worth a note. It means companions or associates, from *ṣ-ḥ-b*, and it implies belonging rather than mere presence. They are not visitors to the Garden; they are its people. The same construction is used of the Fire in the next verse, with the same implication of belonging — which supports the reading offered at v. 8, that the Hereafter discloses what a soul had become rather than assigning it somewhere arbitrary.

*Khālidūn* — abiding, remaining. The root *kh-l-d* denotes long duration. As noted in the commentary on v. 52 of this sūrah, Ibn Qayyim al-Jawziyyah argued that this root can denote a very long but finite period, and applied that argument to the punishment of the Fire. Notably, the scholars who took that position did *not* apply it to Paradise, whose permanence is affirmed by explicit texts:

> *"...a gift uninterrupted."* (Qur'an 11:108)

**The Ethical Weight of *Iḥsān* in Daily Life**

Finally, it is worth returning to the deed that earns all this, because the verse does not say *"those who believed"* — it says *"those who did beautifully."*

*Iḥsān* is a standard applied to the *quality* of what one does. In practice, this means: the work completed properly when no one will inspect it; the repair done thoroughly rather than adequately; the debt repaid before it is demanded; the apology given fully rather than partially; the kindness extended without the recipient's knowledge.

The Prophet ﷺ tied this quality to God's love directly:

> *"Truly God loves, when one of you does a work, that he do it with excellence (an yutqinahu)."* (al-Bayhaqī, *Shuʿab al-Īmān*; widely transmitted with supporting evidence)

A religion that promises the vision of God to those who slaughter an animal kindly and finish their work properly has said something profound about where the spiritual life actually takes place.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:27

> **And as for those who commit evil deeds, the recompense of an evil is one like it, and abasement shall overcome them. There will be none to protect them from God. [It will be] as if their faces are covered with dark patches of night. It is they who are the inhabitants of the Fire; they shall abide therein.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Mirror Verse**

This verse is constructed as the exact negative image of the one before it, clause for clause, and the correspondences are deliberate.

There: *those who did beautifully*; here: *those who earned evil deeds*. There: *the most beautiful and more besides*; here: *the recompense of an evil is one like it* — with no *ziyādah*. There: *neither darkness nor abasement*; here: *abasement shall overcome them* and their faces are *covered with patches of night*. There: *inhabitants of the Garden*; here: *inhabitants of the Fire*.

The parallelism is a teaching device, but one asymmetry within it carries the verse's most important theological content.

**The Asymmetry of Divine Justice**

Read the two verses again with attention to proportion.

The virtuous receive *al-ḥusnā wa-ziyādah* — the beautiful reward, *and more besides*. The reward exceeds the deed.

Those who earn evil receive *jazāʾu sayyiʾatin bi-mithlihā* — the recompense of an evil is *one like it*. Exactly equivalent. No increase.

This asymmetry is not incidental; it is a stated principle of the Qur'an, and it is expressed with mathematical precision elsewhere:

> *"Whoever brings a good deed shall have ten times the like thereof; and whoever brings an evil deed shall be recompensed only with the like thereof, and they shall not be wronged."* (Qur'an 6:160)

And the hadith qudsī makes the ratio even more generous:

> *"God has written down the good deeds and the evil deeds. Then He explained it: whoever intends a good deed and does not do it, God writes it down as one complete good deed; and if he intends it and does it, God writes it down as ten to seven hundred times over. And whoever intends an evil deed and does not do it, God writes it down as one complete good deed; and if he intends it and does it, God writes it down as one single evil deed."* (al-Bukhārī 6491; Muslim 131, from Ibn ʿAbbās)

The accounting system described is deliberately unbalanced in the servant's favour at every point: good intentions count even unfulfilled; good deeds multiply; abandoned bad intentions convert into credits; and bad deeds are recorded singly. Al-Rāzī notes in connection with our verse that the recompense is in just proportion, in accordance with God's justice and wisdom (see 42:40).

So mercy exceeds justice, and this verse's restraint — *one like it* — is itself a form of that mercy.

**"Those Who Earned Evil Deeds"**

*Alladhīna kasabū al-sayyiʾāt.* The verb is *kasaba*, the commercial term for earning, as at v. 8. Wrongdoing is again described as a wage: something worked for, accumulated, and eventually paid out.

*Sayyiʾah* is from *s-w-ʾ*, to be bad or evil, and it is worth noting that the same root gives the verb used for the effect on the face: something that *makes bad* an appearance. The Qur'an frequently plays on this: *"So when the promise of the Hereafter comes, [We will send your enemies] to disgrace your faces (li-yasūʾū wujūhakum)"* (17:7).

**"Abasement Shall Overcome Them"**

*Wa-tarhaquhum dhillah.* The same verb *rahiqa* used in the previous verse — to overtake, to cover over — but now with *dhillah*, abasement, as its subject rather than its negation.

*Dhull* is the opposite of *ʿizz*, might and honour. The Qur'an will say fifteen verses later, at v. 65, that *"might belongs altogether to God"*, which explains why abasement is the specific penalty here: those who sought honour from sources other than God are left with none, because there was none to be had there.

The Qur'an describes the posture of *dhillah* on that Day with painful vividness:

> *"And thou wilt see them exposed to it, humbled by abasement, looking with furtive glances."* (Qur'an 42:45)

That detail — *looking with furtive glances*, from the corner of the eye — is the physical posture of a person who cannot bear to look directly at what is in front of him.

**"There Will Be None to Protect Them From God"**

*Mā lahum mina Allāhi min ʿāṣim.*

*ʿĀṣim* is a protector, one who prevents or shields, from *ʿ-ṣ-m*, to hold fast and protect. Al-Zamakhsharī glosses the clause: none will be able to save them from God's wrath and punishment.

This clause completes the demolition of the intercession theology addressed at vv. 3 and 18. In this world, protection is a universally available commodity: one can be shielded by a tribe, a patron, a lawyer, an insurance policy, a passport, a bank balance. Every one of these is a mechanism for placing something between oneself and a consequence.

On that Day, the verse says, there is no such mechanism. Every protective structure human beings construct depends on the existence of an outside — a jurisdiction the authority does not reach, an official who can be influenced, a resource the other party lacks. Before the Sovereign of the Day of Judgment, there is no outside.

The Qur'an dramatises the failure of every protective relationship:

> *"On the Day when a man will flee from his brother, and his mother and his father, and his wife and his children — every man among them, that Day, will have a concern to occupy him."* (Qur'an 80:34–37)

The same word *ʿāṣim* appears in the story of Noah's son, who says he will take refuge on a mountain that will protect him from the water, and is told:

> *"There is no protector (ʿāṣim) today from God's command, save for him on whom He has mercy."* (Qur'an 11:43)

That exception is the whole of the matter. There is one protection, and it is not *from* God but *with* Him.

**"Faces Covered With Dark Patches of Night"**

*Ka-annamā ughshiyat wujūhuhum qiṭaʿan mina al-layli muẓliman.*

The image is unusual and worth appreciating as literature. Not "their faces were dark," but "as if pieces of night, black night, had been laid over their faces." *Qiṭaʿ* means cut portions, slices, fragments — as though the darkness were a material substance that could be cut and applied.

The image conveys something a simple adjective could not: that the darkness is *external and imposed*, covering a face that is still there underneath. It is a veiling. And the veiling of the face corresponds exactly to the veiling from God described at 83:15 — *"Nay! Truly on that Day they shall be veiled from their Lord."*

Where the virtuous gaze on their Lord and their faces shine with what they see, these are covered on both sides.

The Qur'an's fullest treatment of this imagery is at 3:106:

> *"On the Day when some faces will be whitened and some faces will be blackened."*

**Why the Punishment Fits: The Internal Logic**

A reader may still ask why the outcome should be so severe, and the verse's own vocabulary suggests the answer.

Every element of the punishment is the natural completion of something the person chose. He *earned* (*kasaba*) — so he is paid. He sought honour where there was none — so he is abased. He relied on protectors who had no authority — so he stands unprotected. He turned away from the light — so he is covered in darkness. He belonged, in his loyalties and habits, to the Fire — so he is named among its *aṣḥāb*, its people.

At no point does the verse describe God as inflicting something foreign to what the person had made of himself. This is why the sūrah can say, seventeen verses later and without any sense of contradiction:

> *"Truly God does not wrong human beings in the least, but rather human beings wrong themselves."* (Qur'an 10:44)

**The Verse Read as a Warning Rather Than a Verdict**

The final and most important observation concerns *when* this verse is being delivered.

It is being recited to people who are still alive. The whole point of describing the covered faces is that the faces of the audience are not yet covered. A verdict announced after the trial is information; a verdict announced before it is an opportunity.

This is why Qur'anic descriptions of punishment are never merely descriptive. They are placed in the hands of people who still have time, and they are almost always followed — as here, in the verses to come — by renewed invitation. The Qur'an's method is to show a person the destination of the road he is on while he is still able to change roads.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:28

> **On the Day when We shall gather them all together, then We shall say to those who ascribed partners unto God, "Stop there! You and your partners!" Then We shall separate them, and their partners will say, "It was not us whom you worshipped.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Courtroom Scene**

The sūrah now stages a scene. Having described the two destinations in vv. 26–27, it takes the reader back to the moment before the verdict and lets him watch the proceedings.

*Wa-yawma naḥshuruhum jamīʿan.* The verb *ḥashara* means to gather, to drive together into one place — it is used of assembling an army or herding a scattered flock. The Day of Judgment is *yawm al-ḥashr*, the Day of Gathering, and the Qur'an's descriptions emphasise the totality: *jamīʿan*, all of them, with none omitted and none exempt.

Ājībah notes that *them all together* may include both the inhabitants of Paradise and the inhabitants of the Fire; al-Maybudī and al-Qushayrī restrict it to the inhabitants of the Fire — the deniers and the objects of their worship. The scene that follows concerns the second group.

**"Stop There! You and Your Partners!"**

*Makānakum antum wa-shurakāʾukum.*

The Arabic is a terse command: literally "your place!" — meaning *stay where you are, do not move.* It is the tone of a court officer freezing the parties in position while the matter is examined.

There is something chilling in the grammar. *Antum wa-shurakāʾukum*: "you *and your partners*." The possessive is retained. Even here, at the moment of exposure, the deities are referred to as *theirs* — the gods they made, the gods they maintained, the gods whose ownership they never questioned. The pronoun quietly indicts: these were never God's partners; they were *yours*.

**"Then We Shall Separate Them"**

*Fa-zayyalnā baynahum.* The verb is from *z-y-l*, to remove, to set apart. In this world, the worshipper and his object of worship were bound together in a relationship the worshipper experienced as intimate and reliable. Here that bond is cut.

The separation is the first act of the proceedings, and it is significant that it precedes the testimony. Before anything is said, the alliance is dissolved. Whatever the worshippers expected of their gods on this Day — support, advocacy, at minimum solidarity — is removed before the conversation begins.

**"It Was Not Us Whom You Worshipped"**

*Wa-qāla shurakāʾuhum mā kuntum iyyānā taʿbudūn.*

The disowning is the heart of the scene, and it deserves close attention because it recurs throughout the Qur'an as a fixed feature of the Judgment.

The idols speak. The Qur'an addresses the obvious objection — that stones cannot talk — with a principle stated in Sūrat Fuṣṣilat:

> *"When they come upon it, their hearing, their eyes, and their skins will bear witness against them for what they used to do. And they will say to their skins, 'Why did you bear witness against us?' They will reply, 'God, Who makes all things speak, made us speak. He created you the first time, and unto Him you shall be returned.'"* (Qur'an 41:20–21)

Al-Shawkānī and others note the principle: whatever is mute in this world may be given speech on that Day. The One who gave speech to speaking things can give it to anything.

**Why the Denial Is Technically True**

The disowning statement — *"It was not us whom you worshipped"* — is worth examining, because it is not merely a betrayal. In an important sense it is *accurate*.

The stone never asked to be worshipped. It received no benefit from the worship, gave no instructions, made no promises, and, as v. 29 will say, *"We were heedless of your worship."* The relationship the worshipper believed himself to be in was entirely one-sided. He was, in reality, worshipping a projection of his own making.

This is the deepest point in the passage, and it applies far beyond stone idols. The Qur'an is describing a general feature of false devotion: the object never actually receives the devotion. A man who devotes his life to a career is not in a relationship with the career; the career is not aware of him and will replace him within a month of his death. A man who devotes himself to the approval of a public is worshipping something that does not know his name.

The Qur'an makes the same point about Satan's disowning:

> *"And Satan will say, when the matter has been decided: 'Truly God promised you a promise of truth; and I promised you, then failed you. I had no authority over you, except that I called you and you responded to me. So blame me not, but blame yourselves. I cannot help you, nor can you help me.'"* (Qur'an 14:22)

Note the crucial clause: *"I had no authority over you, except that I called you and you responded."* The whole force of the temptation lay in the response. The tempter supplied nothing but a suggestion.

**What Counts as a "Partner"**

Al-Maybudī's observation, preserved in the source, opens the category usefully: a "partner" is anything or any being that can be worshipped other than God. He notes that partners may be physical idols, human beings (al-Nasafī, al-Rāzī), or even one's own caprices, which are often taken as gods.

The Qur'an itself makes the last extension explicit:

> *"Hast thou seen the one who takes his caprice as his god? Wouldst thou then be a guardian over him?"* (Qur'an 25:43)

> *"Hast thou seen the one who takes his caprice as his god, and God has led him astray despite knowledge?"* (Qur'an 45:23)

The source also cites the Persian mystic ʿAyn al-Quḍāt (d. 525/1131):

> *"O dear friend! If you want the beauty of the secret of self-knowledge displayed to you, then let go of habit-worship, for habit-worship is idol-worship."*

The phrase *habit-worship* is precise and uncomfortable. Much of what governs a life is not chosen at all but simply repeated: the routines, assumptions, and loyalties one has never examined. A habit that determines your conduct more reliably than your convictions do is functioning, in the Qur'anic sense, as a *rabb*.

**Following Others as a Form of Association**

There is a further application that the Qur'an treats with particular seriousness: the worship of authority.

The Qur'an describes the People of the Book as having *"taken their rabbis and their monks as lords apart from God"* (9:31). When ʿAdī ibn Ḥātim, a former Christian, objected to the Prophet ﷺ that they did not worship them, he replied:
