> *"Did they not make lawful for you what God had forbidden, and you made it lawful; and forbid you what God had made lawful, and you forbade it?"* He said: *"Yes."* He said: *"That was your worship of them."* (al-Tirmidhī 3095, from ʿAdī ibn Ḥātim)

The definition given here is behavioural, not ritual. To accept another's determination of right and wrong *over* God's, knowingly, is the substance of the matter — whatever one calls it. This is why the scene in our verse is not a quaint episode about Arabian paganism. It is a general description of what happens on the Day of Judgment to every allegiance that displaced God.

**The Loneliness of the Scene**

There is a psychological dimension to this passage that is easy to overlook.

Idolatry, in every form, is partly a search for company in the universe — for a presence that attends to you, that can be approached, that stands between you and a cosmos that would otherwise be silent. What the scene discloses is that this company was never there. The worshipper stands in the assembly beside the beings he served, and they turn to him and say: *we do not know you.*

The Qur'an's alternative is not that the human being should stand alone but that he should have attached himself to the One who is genuinely present, genuinely attentive, and genuinely near:

> *"And when My servants ask thee about Me — truly I am near. I answer the call of the caller when he calls upon Me."* (Qur'an 2:186)

> *"And We are nearer to him than his jugular vein."* (Qur'an 50:16)

**A Question to Take From the Verse**

The scene supplies a diagnostic that can be applied now, while there is time to act on it. It is this: *what am I relying on that will not acknowledge me?*

The test is not what a person says he believes but what he turns to under pressure, what he cannot imagine living without, and what he would sacrifice a religious obligation to protect. Whatever occupies that position is, functionally, a *sharīk*. And the verse describes what it will say when asked.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:29

> **God suffices as a Witness between you and us. We were heedless of your worship."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Sentence of the Disowning**

The speech begun in the previous verse continues. The false objects of worship, having denied that they were ever the recipients of the worship offered them, now do two further things: they call God to witness, and they state their own condition — total unawareness.

**"God Suffices as a Witness Between You and Us"**

*Fa-kafā billāhi shahīdan baynanā wa-baynakum.*

There is a bitter irony in this line. The idols — objects that were set up precisely to mediate between human beings and God — now appeal directly to God against the very people who set them up.

The formula *kafā billāhi shahīdan* ("God suffices as a witness") is a standing Qur'anic expression (see 4:79, 166; 13:43; 17:96; 29:52; 48:28). It is the invocation of the ultimate testimony: the witness who was present at everything, whose memory is perfect, and who cannot be impeached.

Its use here has a specific force. In this world, disputes are settled by evidence, and evidence can be manufactured, lost, or contested. The idols invoke a witness before whom no such manoeuvring is possible. They are, in effect, saying: *let the record speak.*

**"We Were Heedless of Your Worship"**

*In kunnā ʿan ʿibādatikum la-ghāfilīn.*

The word is *ghāfilīn* — heedless, unaware, oblivious. It is the same word used of human beings in v. 7 of this sūrah: *"and who are heedless of Our signs."*

The symmetry is exact and devastating. In this world, the worshippers were heedless of God's signs while attending devotedly to their idols. On that Day, they learn that their idols were heedless of them the entire time.

Consider what this means concretely. Generations of ritual: the circumambulations, the sacrifices, the offerings laid at the base of a stone, the vows made in crisis, the naming of children after deities, the pilgrimages, the festivals, the family traditions passed down for centuries. And the recipient of all of it registered *nothing*. Not one prayer was heard. Not one offering was received. The entire structure was, from the other side, silence.

**The Terrifying Possibility of Wasted Worship**

This is the point at which the verse stops being about pagans and becomes uncomfortable for everyone.

The Qur'an raises elsewhere the possibility of religious effort that yields nothing, and it does so in some of its most severe language:

> *"Say: Shall We inform you of the greatest losers in respect of works? They are those whose effort goes astray in the life of this world, while they reckon that they are doing good work."* (Qur'an 18:103–104)

> *"And We shall turn to whatever deeds they did, and make them as scattered dust."* (Qur'an 25:23)

> *"Some faces that Day will be humbled, labouring, weary, burning in a scorching fire."* (Qur'an 88:2–4)

That last passage is particularly striking: *ʿāmilatun nāṣibah* — "labouring, weary." These are people who *worked*. The Qur'an is not describing the lazy but the industrious, whose industry was misdirected.

The Prophet ﷺ named the mechanism by which this happens in a hadith of great severity:

> *"Shall I not tell you of what I fear for you more than the false messiah?"* They said: *"Yes."* He said: *"Hidden idolatry (al-shirk al-khafī): that a man stands to pray and beautifies his prayer because he sees another man looking at him."* (Ibn Mājah 4204; Aḥmad 11252, from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī)

And in the hadith of the first three to be judged — the martyr, the scholar, and the philanthropist — each is confronted with an act of manifest religious achievement and told:

> *"You have lied. Rather, you did it so that it would be said..."* — and each is dragged on his face into the Fire. (Muslim 1905, from Abū Hurayrah)

The connection to our verse is direct. In each case, the deed was offered to an audience that could not receive it — the good opinion of people who will themselves be preoccupied on that Day, and who were, all along, *ghāfilīn* about the worshipper's inner life.

**Ikhlāṣ as the Only Protection**

The remedy the tradition prescribes is the one this sūrah has already named at v. 22: *ikhlāṣ*, the purification of intent, making the act unmixed.

The definition given by the early scholars is precise. Al-Fuḍayl ibn ʿIyāḍ (d. 187/803), commenting on the phrase *"best in deed"* in 67:2, said:

> *"The most sincere and the most correct. If a deed is sincere but not correct, it is not accepted; and if it is correct but not sincere, it is not accepted — until it is both sincere and correct. Sincere means that it is for God alone; correct means that it is in accordance with the Sunnah."*

Two conditions, both necessary. And *ikhlāṣ* is difficult in a specific way: it cannot be achieved by effort at the moment of action, because attention to one's own sincerity is itself a form of self-regard. The classical solution was structural rather than psychological — conceal what can be concealed. The Prophet ﷺ listed among the seven whom God shades on the Day when there is no shade:

> *"...a man who gives charity and conceals it, so that his left hand does not know what his right hand has spent."* (al-Bukhārī 660; Muslim 1031, from Abū Hurayrah)

**Why God's Witness Cuts Both Ways**

The invocation of God as witness in this verse is used against the idolaters. But the same principle is, for the believer, one of the most consoling facts in the religion.

The deed done in secret is *not* unwitnessed. The kindness no one saw, the patience no one credited, the temptation refused in an empty room, the money given without acknowledgement, the years of care given to a parent or a disabled child with no audience whatsoever — all of it is recorded by the Witness who suffices.

This is precisely why the Islamic tradition places such weight on private worship. The night prayer, performed when everyone is asleep, is the paradigm case: it has no possible audience. The Qur'an praises exactly this:

> *"Their sides forsake their beds, calling upon their Lord in fear and hope, and they spend of what We have provided them. No soul knows what comfort of the eyes is kept hidden for them, as a reward for what they used to do."* (Qur'an 32:16–17)

*No soul knows.* The reward for the hidden deed is itself hidden.

**Putting the Two Verses Together**

Verses 28 and 29 form one speech, and read together they describe the total collapse of a false relationship: the partner is separated from the worshipper, denies the worship, calls God to witness, and confesses complete unawareness.

What the passage leaves standing, by contrast, is the shape of the true relationship. It is one in which the One worshipped is *aware* — the Hearing, the Knowing, as v. 65 will say; near, as 2:186 says; a Witness to every hidden act. The Qur'an's case against idolatry, in the end, is not merely that it is forbidden. It is that it does not work, because there is nobody there.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:30

> **There and then every soul shall experience that which it did in the past, and they shall be brought back unto God, their true Master, and that which they used to fabricate will forsake them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Moment of Full Disclosure**

*Hunālika tablū kullu nafsin mā aslafat.*

*Hunālika* means "there" — at that place, at that point. Al-Kāshānī notes it refers to the very place in which each soul is gathered before God, the scene set in v. 28. The commentator al-Baqāʿī observes that the verse can be read as speaking first of both the obedient and the disobedient, and then narrowing to those who ascribed partners.

**"Every Soul Shall Experience" — The Verb *Tablū***

The verb chosen is remarkable. *Tablū* is from *b-l-w*, the root of *balāʾ* (trial) and *ibtilāʾ* (testing). Its basic sense is to test something by putting it to use — to try a thing out and discover its quality.

So the phrase does not mean merely "every soul will be shown what it did." It means something closer to: *every soul will test out, will taste, will come to know by experience what it sent ahead.* The deeds are not read from a list; they are *encountered*.

Some readings of the text have *tatlū*, "shall recite/follow," which yields a related sense: each soul will read out or follow up what it sent forward. Both readings point to the same reality — direct, personal confrontation with one's own record.

**"That Which It Did in the Past" — The Metaphor of Sending Ahead**

*Mā aslafat.* The verb *aslafa* means to send forward, to advance — and in commercial Arabic, to pay in advance for goods to be delivered later (*salaf* is a forward payment). The related Qur'anic phrase is *mā qaddamat*, "what it sent forward" (see 2:110; 59:18; 75:13).

The metaphor reframes the entire moral life. Deeds are not events that happen and vanish; they are *consignments dispatched ahead of the traveller*. A person is, at every moment, shipping something to an address he will himself arrive at later.

This image transforms how one thinks about ordinary days. The Qur'an gives the practical instruction:

> *"O you who believe, be mindful of God, and let every soul look to what it has sent forward for tomorrow."* (Qur'an 59:18)

And the Prophet ﷺ described the arrival of the consignment:

> *"Three things follow the deceased: his family, his wealth, and his deeds. Two return and one remains: his family and his wealth return, and his deeds remain."* (al-Bukhārī 6514; Muslim 2960, from Anas)

**"Their True Master" — The Meaning of *Mawlāhum al-Ḥaqq***

*Wa-ruddū ilā Allāhi mawlāhumu al-ḥaqq.*

*Mawlā* is a rich Arabic word. From *w-l-y*, to be near, it denotes a relationship of protection and loyalty and can mean master, patron, protector, ally, or client, depending on direction. The word is used in Islamic law for both parties in the relationship of manumission — the freed slave and the one who freed him.

The adjective *al-ḥaqq* — the True, the Real — is doing critical work. God is their *real* master, as opposed to the masters they claimed. The word *ḥaqq* in Arabic means both "true" (as opposed to false) and "real" (as opposed to illusory) and "rightful" (as opposed to usurped). All three senses apply. The idols were not merely false claimants; they were never in the relationship at all.

Al-Qushayrī offers a striking reading of this return, preserved in the source: when those who have lost their souls are brought back to God, they find nothing but that they are distant from Him and therefore rejected — because they devoted their lives to "gods" other than God, who in reality is their true Master.

The tragedy is in the word *ruddū*, "they were returned." A return implies a place of origin. They were always His; they were made by Him, sustained by Him, and owned by Him throughout the years in which they attributed themselves to another. The return is not a change of ownership. It is the correction of a long-standing error of paperwork.

**"That Which They Used to Fabricate Will Forsake Them"**

*Wa-ḍalla ʿanhum mā kānū yaftarūn.*

The verb *ḍalla* is the same root as *ḍalāl*, misguidance — and its basic sense is to be lost, to go astray, to disappear from view. Used here of the fabrications, it means they *vanish*, they wander off, they fail to be found when needed.

There is a fine irony in the choice of verb. In this life, the fabrications *misled* them (*aḍallahum*); on that Day, the fabrications are the ones who *get lost*.

What are the *muftarayāt*, the fabrications? They include the deities themselves, the doctrines invented about them, the claims that this was what the ancestors practised, and the assurances that everything would be fine. The Qur'an's word *iftirāʾ* — as at v. 17 of this sūrah — carries the sense of manufacturing something and attaching God's name to it.

**Why False Structures Fail Precisely When Needed**

There is a general principle in this clause worth extracting, because it is verifiable in ordinary life long before the Day of Judgment.

Every false support has an identical property: it works under normal conditions and fails under load. That is, in fact, the definition of a false support. A structure that cannot bear weight may look identical to one that can, right up until the weight is applied.

This is why the Qur'an describes the deniers' works with images of substances that seem solid and are not:

> *"And those who disbelieve — their deeds are like a mirage in a desert, which the thirsty man supposes to be water, until, when he comes to it, he finds it to be nothing."* (Qur'an 24:39)

The mirage is not a hallucination. It is a real optical phenomenon that reliably appears at a distance and reliably disappears on approach. That is exactly the behaviour this verse attributes to the fabrications: present while distant, absent on arrival.

**The Practical Test Available Now**

The useful application of this verse is that the failure mode can be tested in advance, and one need not wait for the Day of Judgment to run the test.

Everyone has already experienced small versions of *ḍalla ʿanhum mā kānū yaftarūn*. The friendship that evaporated when it became inconvenient. The professional network that stopped returning calls after the redundancy. The confidence in one's own health that lasted exactly until the scan. The certainty that there would be time.

Each of these is a rehearsal, and each is offered as information rather than as cruelty. The verse describes the final, comprehensive instance of a pattern that life has already demonstrated repeatedly on a smaller scale. Whoever has noticed the pattern has been given the substance of the warning without having to pay its full price.

**And What Does Not Forsake**

The verse's structure invites the obvious counter-question: what *does not* forsake?

The Qur'an's answer is consistent. What remains is *al-bāqiyāt al-ṣāliḥāt*, the enduring good deeds:

> *"Wealth and children are the adornment of the life of this world; but the enduring good deeds are better with thy Lord in reward and better in hope."* (Qur'an 18:46)

And the relationship with the One named in this very verse as *mawlāhum al-ḥaqq*. The word for those who take God as their *mawlā* is *awliyāʾ* — the friends of God, whom this sūrah will describe in a few pages:

> *"Behold! Truly the friends of God, no fear shall come upon them, nor shall they grieve."* (Qur'an 10:62)

The same root, *w-l-y*, on both sides. He is their *mawlā*; they are His *awliyāʾ*. That relationship is the one thing in the verse that does not go missing.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:31

> **Say, "Who provides for you from Heaven and earth? Who has power over hearing and sight? And who brings forth the living from the dead, and brings forth the dead from the living, and who directs the affair?" They will say, "God." So say, "Will you not, then, be reverent?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument From What They Already Admit**

The sūrah now shifts from describing the Judgment to making a case in the present tense, and the method it uses is the most effective in any debate: it builds entirely on premises the opponent already accepts.

Four questions are put. Al-Rāzī notes that the answer — *"God"* — is what the Makkans themselves will give, because His agency comes immediately to mind when these particular questions are asked. Ājībah and al-Kāshānī make the same observation, and al-Rāzī adds the historical explanation: a number of them had some form of belief in Allāh as the head of their pantheon.

The Qur'an records this concession repeatedly:

> *"And if thou askest them who created the heavens and the earth and made subject the sun and the moon, they will surely say, 'God.' How then are they deluded?"* (Qur'an 29:61)

> *"And if thou askest them who sends down water from the sky and revives thereby the earth after its death, they will surely say, 'God.' Say: Praise be to God! But most of them do not understand."* (Qur'an 29:63)

> *"And if thou askest them who created them, they will surely say, 'God.' How then are they turned away?"* (Qur'an 43:87)

The strategy is worth naming because it is a model of how the Qur'an argues. It does not begin by demanding that the opponent abandon his position. It begins by identifying what he already believes and showing him that his own beliefs, followed consistently, land him somewhere other than where he is standing.

**First Question: Provision From Heaven and Earth**

*Man yarzuqukum mina al-samāʾi wa-l-arḍ.*

Al-Baghawī and al-Ṭūsī understand "from heaven and earth" as a reference to rain and vegetation respectively; al-Qurṭubī notes that such provision can be both bodily and spiritual.

*Rizq* — provision — is a broader concept in Qur'anic usage than income. It covers everything a creature receives that sustains it: food, water, air, health, knowledge, companionship, and the opportunity to earn.

The question is designed to trace a chain. Where does bread come from? A bakery. Where does flour come from? Wheat. Where does wheat come from? Soil, seed, sunlight, rain. Who arranged that soil should be fertile, that seed should contain the coded instructions for a plant, that water should fall from the sky at intervals compatible with growth?

At no point in the chain does human agency do more than *arrange* what it did not make. The farmer does not manufacture fertility; he cooperates with it. The Qur'an makes the point with pointed directness:

> *"Have you considered what you sow? Is it you who cause it to grow, or are We the Grower? If We willed, We could make it broken debris, and you would remain in wonder."* (Qur'an 56:63–65)

> *"Have you considered the water you drink? Is it you who sent it down from the clouds, or is it We who send it?"* (Qur'an 56:68–69)

**Second Question: Hearing and Sight**

*Am man yamliku al-samʿa wa-l-abṣār.*

Al-Baghawī and al-Ṭūsī gloss this: who gives you hearing and sight? The Qur'an refers to the endowment elsewhere: *"And We endowed them with hearing, sight, and hearts"* (46:26; cf. 23:78; 32:9; 67:23).

The verb is *yamliku* — who *owns*, who has power and disposal over. This is more pointed than "who gave." Not only were these faculties conferred; they remain in the giver's possession. They can be withdrawn.

Anyone who has known someone lose their sight or hearing understands the force of this. These are not permanent attributes of a person; they are held on terms. And notice how the question is framed to expose the ordinary human relationship to them: nobody thanks anyone for their eyesight on a normal morning. It is only when the faculty is threatened that it is perceived as a gift at all.

The Qur'an makes the threat explicit as a way of restoring perception:

> *"Say: Have you considered, if God should take away your hearing and your sight and seal your hearts — what god other than God could restore them to you?"* (Qur'an 6:46)

There is a reason hearing is consistently mentioned before sight throughout the Qur'an — some thirty times in this order. The classical scholars offered several explanations: hearing develops first in the infant; hearing operates in darkness and behind obstacles, so it is the more universal faculty; and hearing is the sense by which revelation is received, which is why the deniers are described as deaf rather than blind in vv. 42–43 of this very sūrah.

**Third Question: Life From Death, Death From Life**

*Wa-man yukhriju al-ḥayya mina al-mayyiti wa-yukhriju al-mayyita mina al-ḥayy.*

The Qur'an poses the same question at 30:19 and 6:95.

The commentators understood this both literally and figuratively. Literally: the living plant from the dead seed, the living chick from the inert egg, the living child from the fluid that is not itself alive; and conversely, the dead egg from the living bird, the corpse from the living man. Figuratively, and this reading is well attested from Ibn ʿAbbās: the believer from the disbeliever and the disbeliever from the believer — Abraham from Āzar, and the sons of Noah and Lot from their prophetic fathers.

Both readings serve the same argument. The transition between life and death, in either direction, is the one boundary human technology has never crossed. We can preserve life, extend it, and end it. We cannot originate it. Every living cell in existence descends from another living cell; nobody has ever made one from inert material.

The Qur'an builds its case for resurrection on precisely this observation, and the analogy of dead earth revived by rain is its favourite illustration:

> *"And thou seest the earth barren; but when We send down water upon it, it stirs and swells and puts forth every lovely kind. That is because God is the Truth, and because He gives life to the dead, and because He is Powerful over all things."* (Qur'an 22:5–6)

**Fourth Question: Who Directs the Affair?**

*Wa-man yudabbiru al-amr.*

This is the phrase from v. 3 returning, and its reappearance is not accidental. The sūrah's opening description of God — *"then mounted the Throne, directing the affair"* — is now presented as something the opponents themselves would concede.

*Tadbīr*, as discussed at v. 3, is management with a view to outcomes. The question asks: who is running this? Who allocates, who times, who coordinates?

The cumulative force of the four questions is that they cover the whole field: sustenance (what enters you), perception (how you know the world), life and death (the boundaries of existence), and governance (the arrangement of events). If God is the answer to all four, there is nothing left for another deity to do.

**"They Will Say: God" — The Confession That Convicts**

*Fa-sa-yaqūlūna Allāh.*

The Qur'an predicts the answer, and the prediction is itself a challenge: *you will say this — and I am telling you in advance that you will.*

What makes the concession so damaging to their position is its completeness. They are not conceding that God is one among several powers. They are conceding that He alone provides, alone owns the faculties, alone controls life and death, and alone governs. Having conceded all that, what function remains for al-Lāt, al-ʿUzzā, and Manāt?

This is the incoherence the Qur'an exposes throughout: an intact monotheism at the level of *rubūbiyyah* (lordship — who made and runs the world) combined with polytheism at the level of *ulūhiyyah* (divinity — who is to be worshipped). *Jalālayn* and Ibn Kathīr both note at v. 3 that these two must go together. The Makkans accepted the premise and refused the conclusion.

**"Will You Not, Then, Be Reverent?"**

*Fa-qul a-fa-lā tattaqūn.*

The verse ends where the argument leads: with *taqwā*.

Note that the conclusion is *not* "will you not then believe?" — they already believe in the sense of assenting to the propositions. The conclusion is behavioural. Given that you know all this, will you not *act* like people who know it?

This is the sūrah's characteristic move, and it is repeated with variation: *"Will you not remember?"* (v. 3), *"Do you not understand?"* (v. 16), *"How do you judge?"* (v. 35), *"Will you not, then, be reverent?"* Each is a demand that belief be brought into contact with conduct.

The gap between the two is the sūrah's real subject. Its diagnosis is not that people do not know about God, but that knowing has been insulated from doing. And *taqwā*, as explored at v. 6, is precisely the state in which the insulation is removed: the awareness of consequences actually governs the driving.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:32

> **That is God, your Lord, the Truth. And what is there beyond truth save error? How, then, are you turned away?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Conclusion of the Preceding Argument**

The four questions of v. 31 ended in a confession: *"They will say, 'God.'"* This verse draws the conclusion from that confession, and it does so with the same demonstrative phrase used at v. 3: *dhālikumu Allāhu rabbukum*, "That is God, your Lord."

The commentators note that this phrase appears at several points in the Qur'an (6:102; 10:3, 32; 35:13; 39:6; 40:62, 64) and is always adjacent to some tangible demonstration of God's dealing with creation. Here the demonstration was the fourfold argument from provision, perception, life, and governance. *That* — the One who does all that — is your Lord.

**The Addition of *Al-Ḥaqq***

What is new in this verse is the appended term: *al-ḥaqq*. That is God, your Lord — *the Truth*, or *the Real*.

*Ḥaqq* is one of the most important words in the Qur'anic vocabulary, and English has no single equivalent. Its root *ḥ-q-q* denotes what is firmly established, fixed, unshakeable. From it derive *ḥaqīqah* (reality), *ḥuqūq* (rights, what is due), *ḥaqqa* (to be established as true), and *istaḥaqqa* (to deserve, to have a rightful claim).

So *al-Ḥaqq*, as a divine name, carries several senses simultaneously:

*The Real* — that which exists in the fullest sense, whose existence is not derived or contingent. Everything else exists on loan; God exists absolutely. The Qur'an makes this ontological point in one of its most compressed statements: *"Everything is perishing save His Face"* (28:88).

*The True* — that whose reality corresponds to what is claimed of Him, in contrast to the "gods" whose claimed status has no corresponding reality.

*The Rightful* — the one with a genuine claim, in contrast to usurped claims. This connects to the phrase in v. 30: *mawlāhumu al-ḥaqq*, "their true Master."

**"What Is There Beyond Truth Save Error?"**

*Fa-mādhā baʿda al-ḥaqqi illā al-ḍalāl.*

This is the logical pivot of the verse, and it states a principle of striking simplicity: there is no third category.

The Arabic is a rhetorical question that admits only one answer. Once you have identified the truth, everything that departs from it is *ḍalāl* — error, straying, being lost. There is no intermediate position, no partial truth that is not, in the respect in which it departs, error.

This is the law of excluded middle applied to the most consequential question a human being faces. And it is worth appreciating how uncomfortable this is for the modern reader, because the dominant intellectual temperament of our age prefers to avoid exactly this kind of statement. The instinct is to say that everyone has a piece of the truth, that these are matters of perspective, that certainty is arrogance.

The Qur'an does not accommodate that instinct on the fundamental question. Either God is one, or He is not. Either the universe is governed and purposeful, or it is not. Either there is a return and a reckoning, or there is not. These are not matters on which a position can be half-right in the way that an approximate measurement is half-right.

**Where the Qur'an *Does* Allow Uncertainty**

It is important to state the limits of this principle carefully, because it is easily abused.

The Qur'an's insistence on a binary applies to the foundational realities: God's oneness, prophecy, the resurrection. On these it permits no middle ground.

It does *not* impose the same binary on matters of legal interpretation, jurisprudential detail, or contested questions of application. The Islamic scholarly tradition was, from its earliest generations, remarkably tolerant of disagreement in these areas — and the Prophet ﷺ established the principle explicitly:

> *"When a judge exercises judgment and gets it right, he has two rewards; and when he exercises judgment and gets it wrong, he has one reward."* (al-Bukhārī 7352; Muslim 1716, from ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ)

A tradition that rewards a sincere scholar for a wrong conclusion is not one that treats every disagreement as truth versus error. The distinction the tradition drew was between *uṣūl* (foundations, where the answer is one) and *furūʿ* (branches, where qualified diligence is honoured even when it errs).

Reading v. 32 as licensing intolerance of every difference of opinion would therefore be a misreading of both the verse and the tradition that received it.

**Why There Is No Neutral Ground on the Central Question**

The verse's logic can be defended on its own terms, and it is worth doing so, because it is often dismissed as mere dogmatism.

Consider the practical situation. A person either orders his life on the assumption that he will be answerable to God or he does not. There is no third option, because *not deciding* is itself a decision to live as though the answer were no. Suspension of judgment is available in physics; it is not available in conduct, because conduct proceeds regardless.

This is why the Qur'an treats indifference as a position rather than an abstention. The heedless of v. 7 are not neutral parties awaiting more information. They have already chosen a way of living, and the choice has consequences whether or not it was consciously made.

**"How, Then, Are You Turned Away?"**

*Fa-annā tuṣrafūn.*

The verb *ṣarafa* means to turn something away, to divert, to redirect. The passive voice — *tuṣrafūn*, "you are turned away" — is significant.

The passive suggests that the diversion is not entirely self-generated. Something is turning them. And the Qur'an's answer, given throughout this sūrah, is a combination of factors: inherited custom (v. 78, *"that which we found our fathers following"*), self-interest, the beautification of one's deeds (v. 12), and the whispering of Satan.

But the passive also carries a note of bewilderment. The question is not "why do you turn away?" but "how are you turned away?" — as though the speaker is genuinely puzzled at the mechanism. You have just conceded that God provides, owns your senses, controls life and death, and governs everything. Having conceded that, *how* does anything succeed in diverting you?

It is the tone of someone watching a person walk past an open door.

**The Modern Form of Being "Turned Away"**

The diversion is rarely a decision. This is the insight in the passive voice, and it is worth applying honestly.

Almost nobody sits down and decides to live as though God does not matter. What happens is that a person is *turned* — by the accumulation of a thousand small pressures, none of which announces itself as a challenge to faith: the schedule that leaves no time, the peer group in which religion is faintly embarrassing, the entertainment that fills every silence, the professional culture with its own implicit creed about what is worth wanting.

None of these makes an argument. They do not need to. They simply occupy the space in which the question would otherwise arise, and after twenty years the person finds himself somewhere he never chose to go.

The Qur'an's response to this is not primarily argumentative but structural. The five daily prayers exist, in part, as an interruption of exactly this drift: a fixed, non-negotiable turning-back inserted into the day at intervals short enough that the diversion cannot accumulate unchecked. The verse asks *how are you turned away*; the prayer is the mechanism by which one is turned back.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:33

> **Thus is the Word of thy Lord realized against those who are iniquitous: they will not believe.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse About Divine Foreknowledge**

*Kadhālika ḥaqqat kalimatu rabbika ʿalā alladhīna fasaqū annahum lā yuʾminūn.*

This verse addresses one of the hardest questions in theology: what does it mean for God's word to be *realised against* a person before he has finished his life?

The verb *ḥaqqat* is from the same root as *ḥaqq* in the previous verse: to become established, to be confirmed as true, to fall due. A *kalimah* (word) that *ḥaqqat* against someone is a determination that has come into effect — like a judgment that has been entered.

**The Order of Cause and Effect**

Everything depends on reading the verse's sequence correctly, and the Arabic makes the order explicit.

The word is realised *ʿalā alladhīna fasaqū* — "against those who *acted iniquitously*." The verb *fasaqū* is in the past tense, and it *precedes* the realisation of the word.

So the order is: they committed *fisq*; and *therefore* the word came due against them; and its content is that they will not believe.

This is not a decree issued in eternity that forced them into disbelief. It is a determination that took effect *in consequence of* a course of conduct they had already adopted. The Qur'an states this causal order elsewhere with unusual clarity:

> *"So when they deviated, God caused their hearts to deviate."* (Qur'an 61:5)

Note the sequence: their deviation first, then God's confirming of it. And:

> *"And God does not lead astray any people after He has guided them, until He makes clear to them that of which they should be wary."* (Qur'an 9:115)

Again: guidance offered, clarity provided, and only then, upon rejection, the withdrawal.

**The Meaning of *Fisq***

The term used is not *kufr* (disbelief) but *fisq*. The distinction matters.

*F-s-q* has a vivid original sense: the Arabs said *fasaqat al-ruṭabah* of a ripe date when it *burst out of its skin*. A *fāsiq* is therefore one who has broken out of the covering that contained him — who has left the boundary, departed from obedience, stepped outside the frame.

The word denotes an act of exit. And this is precisely why it is the right word here. What is being described is not an intellectual failure to be convinced; it is a *departure* — a deliberate stepping out from a known boundary. The Qur'an uses the same word of Satan: *"He was of the jinn, and he departed (fasaqa) from the command of his Lord"* (18:50). Satan's problem was never a failure of information.

**How Sin Destroys the Capacity to Believe**

The verse asserts a causal relationship between wrongdoing and the loss of the ability to believe. This is not arbitrary; it describes a mechanism that is observable.

The Qur'an names the mechanism directly:

> *"Nay, but that which they used to earn has rusted upon their hearts."* (Qur'an 83:14)

The word is *rān* — a stain or corrosion that accumulates on a surface. The Prophet ﷺ explained it:

> *"Truly, when the servant commits a sin, a black spot is placed upon his heart. If he desists, seeks forgiveness, and repents, his heart is polished clean. But if he returns, it is increased until it overcomes his heart — and that is the rust (rān) which God has mentioned."* (al-Tirmidhī 3334; Ibn Mājah 4244, from Abū Hurayrah)

The mechanism described is incremental and — crucially — reversible at each stage, until it is not. Each sin leaves a mark; repentance removes it; repetition without repentance accumulates the marks until the surface is no longer capable of reflecting anything.

The psychological version of this is entirely familiar. A person who does something he knows to be wrong feels resistance the first time and less the tenth. What has changed is not the act but the actor's capacity to perceive it. Conscience is a faculty, and faculties atrophy with disuse in exactly the way muscles do.

And once the faculty is gone, the person is not lying when he says he sees nothing wrong. He genuinely does not. That is the state this verse names.

**Belief Is Not Purely Intellectual**

This verse thus makes a claim that runs against a common modern assumption: that belief is a matter of evidence assessed by a neutral intellect, and that moral conduct is a separate department.

The Qur'an's position is that the two are entangled. A person's willingness to accept a truth depends heavily on what accepting it would require of him. This is why the sūrah says of the deniers that they *"deny that whose knowledge they cannot comprehend"* (v. 39) — rejection before examination — and why it describes them as knowing and covering (the sense of *kufr* discussed at v. 4).

The Qur'an is explicit that unwillingness precedes unbelief:

> *"And they rejected them, out of wrongdoing and arrogance, though their souls were convinced of them."* (Qur'an 27:14)

> *"Nay, but man desires to continue in his licentiousness. He asks: When is the Day of Resurrection?"* (Qur'an 75:5–6)

That second passage is a piece of psychological analysis in two lines. The question about the Resurrection looks like a philosophical inquiry. The Qur'an says it is generated by something else entirely: the desire to continue.

**Does This Verse Remove Hope?**

A reader might conclude from this verse that some people are simply written off. Two considerations correct that reading.

First, the verse does not identify *who* these people are, and no human being has access to that determination. The Qur'an nowhere permits anyone to declare a living person beyond guidance. Indeed, the sūrah goes out of its way at v. 40 to note that among the Makkans were *"those who believe in it"* and *"those who do not"* — and the commentators (Ājībah, al-Bayḍāwī, *Jalālayn*, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī) explain the first group as those who had *not yet* believed but whom God knew would come to believe later.

The Prophet ﷺ prayed for the guidance of his worst persecutors, and several of them became Muslims. ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, who set out to kill him, became the second caliph. The Prophet ﷺ had prayed: *"O God, strengthen Islam with the dearer of the two ʿUmars to You"* (al-Tirmidhī 3681). One does not pray for a person one has classified as beyond hope.

Second, and decisively, the door of repentance is described as open until two specific moments:

> *"Truly God accepts the repentance of His servant so long as the death-rattle has not reached his throat."* (al-Tirmidhī 3537; Ibn Mājah 4253, from Ibn ʿUmar)

> *"Whoever repents before the sun rises from its place of setting, God turns to him in forgiveness."* (Muslim 2703, from Abū Hurayrah)

So the determination described in this verse is real, but it is God's knowledge of an outcome, not a human classification available for use. This sūrah itself will demonstrate the point in its most famous verse: the people of Jonah, who had every mark of a doomed community, believed at the last moment and were spared (v. 98).

**What the Verse Asks of the Reader**

The correct response to this verse is not speculation about who is sealed. It is urgency about oneself.

The mechanism it describes — *fisq* leading to the loss of the capacity for faith — is one to which everyone is subject, and it operates gradually. Its early stages are barely perceptible: a slight dulling, a prayer performed without attention, a wrong done without the discomfort it used to produce.

The tradition's response to that dulling is immediate and specific: *istighfār*, seeking forgiveness, performed constantly rather than reserved for major failures. The Prophet ﷺ, who had no sins to seek forgiveness for, said:

> *"By God, I seek God's forgiveness and turn to Him in repentance more than seventy times a day."* (al-Bukhārī 6307, from Abū Hurayrah)

If the sinless man polished the surface seventy times daily, the appropriate frequency for the rest is not less.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:34

> **Say, "Is there any among your partners who originates creation, then brings it back?" Say, "God originates creation, then brings it back. How, then, are you perverted?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument Resumed, With a Sharper Edge**

The sūrah returns to the cross-examination it began at v. 31, and it does so with a question that goes to the very definition of divinity.

*Qul hal min shurakāʾikum man yabdaʾu al-khalqa thumma yuʿīduh.*

The question is not "did your gods make anything?" — which they would have denied, since they conceded creation to Allāh. The question is designed to make the concession explicit and then draw the conclusion the Makkans were avoiding.

**The Two Acts That Define Divinity**

The question pairs two acts: *yabdaʾu al-khalq* (originates creation) and *thumma yuʿīduhu* (then brings it back). This is the *mabdaʾ* and *maʿād* pair introduced at v. 4.

These two acts are chosen because together they encompass the entire arc of existence. To originate is to bring something into being from nothing; to bring back is to restore it after it has ceased. Whoever can do both has complete authority over being itself — the power to start it and the power to restart it.

Nothing else counts as divinity. A being that can influence events within an existing world, however impressively, is a creature operating inside a system it did not make. The question therefore establishes a criterion, and the criterion is one no candidate but God can meet.

**The Question Answers Itself — So It Is Answered Twice**

Notice the structure: *Say* ... question ... *Say* ... answer.

The Prophet ﷺ is instructed to pose the question and then to supply the answer himself. Why not wait for their reply?

Because there is no reply. The question is what logicians call a *reductio*: it has only one possible answer, and that answer destroys the position of the one being asked. Their silence *is* the answer, and the second *Say* simply states aloud what their silence has conceded.

There is a rhetorical dignity in this construction. It does not humiliate the opponent by forcing him to say it. It says it for him and moves on.

**"How, Then, Are You Perverted?"**

*Fa-annā tuʾfakūn.*

The verb *afaka* means to turn something away from its proper direction, to invert, to pervert. From it comes *ifk*, a lie — the word used in the Qur'an for the great slander against ʿĀʾishah (24:11) — because a lie is a statement turned away from reality. And it is used of the overturned cities of Lot's people, *al-muʾtafikāt* (69:9), which were literally inverted.

So the question is: *how are you turned upside down?*

This is more forceful than v. 32's *fa-annā tuṣrafūn* ("how are you turned away?"). To be turned away is to be diverted from a direction. To be *maʾfūk* is to be inverted — to have the natural order of things reversed within you.

And the inversion is precise. The natural order is: recognise the Creator, worship the Creator. The Makkans had inverted it: recognise the Creator, worship the created. They had put the bottom on top.

**Why the Argument From Origination Is So Central**

The Qur'an returns to origination and restoration constantly, and it is worth understanding why this particular argument carries so much weight.

Every human being lives inside a chain of dependence. You exist because of parents who existed because of theirs. You eat what grew because of processes you did not design. You breathe air whose composition you did not arrange. Every element of your existence was received.

The question the Qur'an keeps asking is simply: *where does the chain terminate?* It cannot regress infinitely, because an infinite chain of borrowers with no lender explains nothing. Something must exist that did not receive its existence. That something is what the word "God" means.

The classical Muslim theologians developed this into the argument from contingency (*dalīl al-imkān*), and Ibn Sīnā gave it its most rigorous form: whatever is possible-in-itself requires a cause; the totality of possible things therefore requires a cause outside itself; that cause must be necessary-in-itself. Al-Ghazzālī, though critical of much of Ibn Sīnā's metaphysics, pressed a related argument from temporal origination: whatever begins to exist has a cause of its beginning; the universe began; therefore it has a cause.

The Qur'an's own version is not technical. It simply asks the question in a form that anyone can follow: *can any of these things you worship start something from nothing?*

**The Argument Applied to Contemporary Substitutes**

The candidates have changed since Makkah, and it is worth putting the same question to the modern ones honestly, because the exercise is clarifying.

Can any natural process originate itself? A physical law describes how matter behaves; it does not explain why there is matter to behave, or why the law holds. Can any human institution restore the dead? Medicine can delay death and, within narrow windows, reverse the process of dying; it has never restored anyone from decomposition. Can any ideology bring back what it destroyed? The twentieth century's political religions promised to remake humanity and could not repair a single one of the lives they consumed.

The Qur'anic criterion — origination and restoration — remains as discriminating as it was. Nothing available to human beings meets it. That is the point of asking.

**The Necessity of the Second Act**

There is a reason the question includes *"then brings it back"* rather than stopping at origination.

A deity who created and then withdrew — the god of the deists — would satisfy the first half of the criterion and fail the second. And such a god, whatever else may be said of him, generates no obligations. If God started the universe and left, there is nothing to worship and nothing to fear.

The inclusion of the return is therefore what converts a metaphysical proposition into a moral one. It is the difference between a first cause and a Lord. The Qur'an consistently refuses to argue for the first without the second, which is why *maʿād* — the return — appears in almost every passage where creation is mentioned, including v. 4 of this sūrah and its immediate sequel here.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:35

> **Say, "Is there any among your partners who guides unto Truth?" Say, "God guides unto Truth. Is one who guides unto Truth worthier to be followed, or one who cannot guide unless he be guided? What ails you? How do you judge?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Physical Origin to Spiritual Direction**

Al-Baqāʿī makes a precise structural observation about this pair of verses: v. 34 addresses the physical nature of people's origin and return, while v. 35 addresses the *spiritual* dimension. Creation and resurrection concern where we come from and where we go bodily; guidance concerns how we find our way in between.

*Qul hal min shurakāʾikum man yahdī ilā al-ḥaqq.*

The structure is identical to v. 34 — question, then answer supplied — but the criterion has changed. Now the test is not power over existence but the ability to direct a soul toward truth.

**Guidance as an Exclusively Divine Capacity**

The commentators are clear that *hidāyah ilā al-ḥaqq* in its ultimate sense belongs to God alone. Al-Ālūsī, *Jalālayn*, and Rūzbihān al-Baqlī all take *"one who guides unto Truth"* as referring to God; al-Ḥasan and al-Thaʿlabī gloss the *ḥaqq* here as God Himself, as revelation, and as the correct path. The Shiite commentator al-Qummī, the source notes, sees in the phrase an allusion to the guidance offered by the Prophet and the Imams.

The Qur'an distinguishes two kinds of guidance, and the distinction resolves what would otherwise be a contradiction.

*Hidāyat al-dalālah* — guidance in the sense of showing the way, pointing out the road. This is available to prophets, teachers, parents, and books. The Qur'an attributes it to the Prophet ﷺ directly:

> *"And truly thou dost guide unto a straight path."* (Qur'an 42:52)

*Hidāyat al-tawfīq* — guidance in the sense of actually causing the heart to accept and follow. This belongs to God alone, and the Qur'an denies it even to the Prophet ﷺ in the plainest terms:

> *"Truly thou canst not guide whomsoever thou lovest; rather God guides whomsoever He will."* (Qur'an 28:56)

That verse was revealed, according to the commentators, concerning Abū Ṭālib — the uncle who protected the Prophet ﷺ for a lifetime at enormous personal cost, whom the Prophet ﷺ loved deeply, and who died without accepting the message. If the Messenger of God, at the deathbed of the man who had shielded him from the world, could not produce faith in a heart, then faith is not producible by any human agency.

**"Or One Who Cannot Guide Unless He Be Guided?"**

*Am man lā yahiddī illā an yuhdā.*

This is the sharpest clause in the verse, and al-Kalbī's gloss is exactly right: it refers to the futility of following those who cannot guide themselves — if they cannot even find their own way, they certainly cannot lead others.

The Arabic *lā yahiddī* (a contracted form of *yahtadī*) means "does not find his own way." The image is of a blind man appointed as a guide.

Applied to the idols, the point is almost comically literal. The stone had to be *carried* to its position. It had to be washed by its worshippers, protected from theft, and repaired when damaged. When Abraham smashed them, they could not defend themselves; when the Prophet ﷺ entered Makkah and struck them with his staff, they simply fell over. An object that must be transported by its devotees is not in a position to lead them anywhere.

**The Principle Extended: Whom Should One Follow?**

The verse establishes a general criterion for authority, and it is one of the most practically useful principles in the sūrah: *the guide must himself be guided*.

This is why Islam attached such importance to the character and knowledge of those from whom religion is taken. The classical scholars of hadith developed an entire science, *al-jarḥ wa-l-taʿdīl*, dedicated to evaluating the reliability of transmitters — because a chain of transmission is only as sound as the people in it. Ibn Sīrīn's famous statement captures the principle:

> *"Truly this knowledge is religion, so look carefully at from whom you take your religion."* (transmitted by Muslim in the introduction to his *Ṣaḥīḥ*)

The criterion applies with special force in an age when religious authority can be assumed by anyone with a broadcast channel and a confident manner. The question this verse supplies is: *is this person himself guided?* Not: is he articulate, popular, or in agreement with me. Does his own life bear the marks of the thing he is teaching?

The Qur'an issues the corresponding warning to teachers:

> *"Do you enjoin righteousness upon people and forget yourselves, while you recite the Book? Will you not then understand?"* (Qur'an 2:44)

**"What Ails You? How Do You Judge?"**

*Fa-mā lakum kayfa taḥkumūn.*

The verse closes with two short questions that are less argument than exclamation. *Mā lakum* — "what is the matter with you?" — is the Arabic idiom for astonishment at someone's behaviour. *Kayfa taḥkumūn* — "how do you judge?" — questions the very process by which they reached their conclusion.

The verb *taḥkumūn* is from *ḥ-k-m*, the root discussed at v. 1: judgment, ruling, the restraining of chaos. The irony is pointed. The sūrah opened by calling the Qur'an *al-kitāb al-ḥakīm*, the Wise Book — the Book characterised by sound judgment. Here the opponents are asked how *their* judgment operates, and the implication is that it does not operate at all: they have reached a conclusion by a process that is not judgment in any recognisable sense.

**The Sūrah's Recurring Complaint: Bad Reasoning**

It is worth stepping back to notice how much of this sūrah is devoted to the *quality of reasoning* rather than to assertion.

Verse 16: *"Do you not understand?"* Verse 3: *"Will you not remember?"* Verse 31: *"Will you not then be reverent?"* Verse 32: *"How, then, are you turned away?"* Verse 34: *"How, then, are you perverted?"* Verse 35: *"What ails you? How do you judge?"* Verse 36 will name the disease: *ẓann*, conjecture. Verse 39 will name its mechanism: rejecting what one has not troubled to understand.

The cumulative effect is a portrait of a group of people who are not stupid — the Makkans were sophisticated traders and superb poets — but whose reasoning has been disabled in one specific domain by interest and inheritance.

This is worth noting because it establishes something about the Qur'an's own posture. A book that repeatedly demands better reasoning from its opponents has staked its claim on the ground of reason. It has not asked to be exempted from scrutiny; it has complained that scrutiny is not being applied.

**A Note on Following One's Own Judgment**

There is a final application that the verse invites, and it is uncomfortable.

The person who follows no external guide at all — who takes his own judgment as sufficient — falls under the same criterion. Is he guided? By what? A person navigating by his own instincts is not free of a guide; he has appointed one, and it is the most interested party available.

This is the meaning of the Qur'an's warning about taking one's caprice as a god (25:43; 45:23), examined at v. 28. It is not primarily about hedonism. It is about the epistemological problem of being one's own final authority on questions where one has a stake in the answer.

The Qur'an's alternative is not the surrender of reason but its correct employment: use your judgment to evaluate the guide, and then follow the guide where your judgment cannot reach. That is what any rational person does with a physician, an engineer, or a map. This verse simply asks that the same procedure be applied to the most consequential domain of all — and that the guide chosen be one who is, himself, guided.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:36

> **And most of them follow naught but conjecture. Truly conjecture does not avail against the truth in the least. Truly God knows what they do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Diagnosis Named**

After six verses of exposing bad reasoning, the sūrah finally names the disease.

*Wa-mā yattabiʿu aktharuhum illā ẓannā.* "Most of them follow naught but conjecture."

This is one of the Qur'an's most important epistemological statements, and it establishes a principle that reaches far beyond its immediate polemical target.

**The Meaning of *Ẓann***

*Ẓ-n-n* is a subtle root. In classical Arabic, *ẓann* covers a range from mere supposition to strong probability, and it can occasionally carry a positive sense — *"those who know with certainty (yaẓunnūna) that they will meet their Lord"* (2:46), where the context and the strength of the conviction give it the force of certainty.

But its dominant Qur'anic usage is negative, and it denotes belief that is *not grounded in evidence sufficient to warrant it*. It is the opinion one has picked up rather than established: what one assumes, supposes, imagines, or has always heard.

The contrast term is *ʿilm*, knowledge — belief that is grounded, verified, warranted.

**"Conjecture Does Not Avail Against the Truth in the Least"**

*Inna al-ẓanna lā yughnī mina al-ḥaqqi shayʾā.*

The verb *aghnā* means to suffice, to substitute for, to be of avail. The statement is that supposition cannot do the work of truth — cannot stand in for it, cannot substitute for it, cannot produce its effects.

This is a claim about how reality operates, and it is worth stating plainly because it is easy to dismiss as an obvious point.

Consider a bridge. An engineer's confident belief that it will hold does not make it hold. The bridge holds or fails according to properties entirely independent of anyone's confidence. Belief has no causal power over the material world.

The Qur'an's application of this to religion is direct: what you *suppose* about God, the Hereafter, and the moral status of your actions has no effect whatsoever on what is actually the case. Sincerity of conviction is not a substitute for accuracy. A man who sincerely believes the water is shallow drowns exactly as thoroughly as one who knew and jumped anyway.

**Why This Rules Out "Believing What Works for You"**

This verse constitutes the Qur'an's rejection of a position that is now widespread: that religious belief is a matter of what one finds meaningful, helpful, or resonant.

On that view, the truth of a belief is not the relevant criterion; its usefulness to the believer is. Religion becomes a resource for wellbeing rather than a claim about how things are.

The Qur'an will not permit this framing, and the reason is contained in this verse. If there *is* a God, a return, and a reckoning, then those are facts, and facts are indifferent to what anyone finds helpful. And if there is not, then no amount of finding it helpful will make it so. Either way, the question of usefulness is downstream of the question of truth, and cannot substitute for it.

This is why the Qur'an argues rather than merely inspires. A text offering comfort would not need to establish anything. This sūrah spends six verses demanding better reasoning precisely because it takes itself to be making truth-claims.

**Where Conjecture Is Forbidden and Where It Is Permitted**

The scholars drew an important distinction here, and it prevents the principle from being applied absurdly.

*In matters of ʿaqīdah* — the foundational beliefs about God, prophecy, and the Hereafter — certainty is required, and *ẓann* is insufficient. This is the domain of the present verse.

*In matters of practical judgment* — legal rulings derived from evidence, medical decisions, the assessment of testimony — *ẓann* in the sense of well-grounded probability is not merely permitted but unavoidable. The entire structure of Islamic jurisprudence operates on *ghalabat al-ẓann*, preponderant probability, because certainty is rarely available in applied questions. A judge rules on the balance of evidence; a jurist derives on the basis of the strongest indication.

The distinction is between conjecture *offered as a substitute for available knowledge*, which this verse condemns, and reasoned probability *employed where certainty is unattainable*, which the tradition requires.

**Conjecture About People**

There is a further application that the Qur'an itself makes explicit, and it is one of the most practically consequential ethical teachings in the Book:

> *"O you who believe, avoid much conjecture (ẓann); truly some conjecture is sin. And do not spy, nor backbite one another. Would any of you like to eat the flesh of his dead brother? You would abhor it."* (Qur'an 49:12)

The Prophet ﷺ made the same point in the strongest terms:

> *"Beware of conjecture, for conjecture is the most untruthful of speech."* (al-Bukhārī 6064; Muslim 2563, from Abū Hurayrah)

The linkage between the two verses is instructive. Conjecture about metaphysical questions and conjecture about people's motives are the same intellectual failure applied to different objects: belief adopted without warrant, and then acted upon as though warranted.

And the interpersonal version is where most people actually commit the offence, daily. We construct entire accounts of why someone said what they said, what they meant by it, what they think of us — from evidence that would not survive a moment's examination. We then respond to the construction rather than to the person. Most social conflict is built out of *ẓann*.

**"Truly God Knows What They Do"**

*Inna Allāha ʿalīmun bimā yafʿalūn.*

Ājībah and al-Ālūsī understand this closing clause as a warning: God is fully aware of their actions and of their rejection of the truth.

But there is a further point in the juxtaposition. The verse has just described people operating on the basis of *ẓann* — supposition, guesswork, unverified belief. It ends by contrasting them with One who possesses *ʿilm*, knowledge, and who knows exactly what they are doing.

The contrast is structural. Human beings guess; God knows. And the guessing is being observed by the knowing.

This has a consoling dimension as well as a warning one. When others conjecture wrongly about you — when reputation is damaged by supposition, when motives are misread, when a false account of you circulates and cannot be corrected — there exists a Knower whose assessment is not based on *ẓann*. The Qur'an offers this consolation directly to those slandered:

> *"Truly those who came with the slander are a group among you. Deem it not an evil for you; rather it is good for you. Unto every man among them is what he has earned of sin."* (Qur'an 24:11)

**The Practical Discipline This Verse Requires**

The verse asks for a habit of mind that is genuinely difficult: the willingness to distinguish, in one's own thinking, between what one *knows* and what one *supposes*.

This is harder than it sounds, because the two feel identical from the inside. A supposition held for twenty years feels exactly like a fact. The only way to tell them apart is to ask, deliberately: *how do I know this? What is the evidence, and did I ever examine it?*

The early Muslim scholars institutionalised this discipline in their willingness to say *lā adrī*, "I do not know." Mālik ibn Anas's practice of answering only what he was sure of, mentioned in the commentary on v. 17, is the applied form of this verse. So is the formula that scholars appended to their opinions: *wa-Allāhu aʿlam*, "and God knows best" — a standing acknowledgement that the speaker has offered his best reasoning and that certainty rests elsewhere.

A tradition whose greatest scholars said "I do not know" as a matter of routine has understood this verse. A culture in which everyone has a confident opinion on everything has not.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:37

> **This Quran could not have been fabricated [by anyone] apart from God; rather, it is a confirmation of that which came before it, and an elaboration of the Book in which there is no doubt, from the Lord of the worlds.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Returning to the Central Claim**

The sūrah now returns to the argument it began at vv. 15–16: the Qur'an is not the Prophet's composition. Having spent the intervening verses demolishing the alternative theology, it comes back to the text itself.

*Wa-mā kāna hādhā al-qurʾānu an yuftarā min dūni Allāh.*

The construction *mā kāna ... an* expresses impossibility rather than mere non-occurrence — "it could not be that." The claim is not "this happens not to be a fabrication" but "this could not have been fabricated."

**Three Arguments Compressed Into One Verse**

The verse offers three distinct grounds, and it is worth separating them.

*The argument from inimitability.* Implicit in *mā kāna ... an yuftarā*, and made explicit in the next verse's challenge: no one could produce this. The Qur'an rests this on a public, falsifiable challenge that has stood for fourteen centuries.

*The argument from confirmation.* *Taṣdīqa alladhī bayna yadayh* — "a confirmation of that which came before it." *Jalālayn*, al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabāṭabāʾī and al-Thaʿlabī explain this as the previously revealed scriptures, the Torah and the Gospel.

*The argument from elaboration.* *Wa-tafṣīla al-kitāb* — an unfolding, a detailing, of the Book.

**"Confirmation of That Which Came Before"**

The claim of continuity is one of Islam's most distinctive features, and it is stated throughout the Qur'an (2:41, 89, 91, 97, 101; 3:3, 81; 5:48; 6:92; 35:31; 46:30).

The Qur'an does not present itself as a new religion. It presents itself as the restoration and completion of one message delivered repeatedly across history:

> *"He has ordained for you of religion what He enjoined upon Noah, and what We have revealed to thee, and what We enjoined upon Abraham and Moses and Jesus: that you uphold the religion and do not become divided therein."* (Qur'an 42:13)

> *"Say: We believe in God and what has been sent down to us, and what was sent down to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and the Tribes, and what was given to Moses and Jesus, and what was given to the prophets from their Lord. We make no distinction between any of them, and to Him we submit."* (Qur'an 2:136)

This claim of continuity is itself an argument. A fabricator inventing a religion in seventh-century Arabia would have had strong incentives to claim novelty and to disparage the older, established traditions whose adherents were rivals. Instead, the Qur'an binds itself to them, affirms their prophets, adopts their sacred history, and stakes its own credibility on being recognisably continuous with what preceded it. This sūrah goes so far as to invite verification from them:

> *"So if thou art in doubt concerning that which We have sent down unto thee, ask those who recite the Book before thee."* (Qur'an 10:94)

That is not the move of someone concealing his sources. It is the move of someone confident that the older texts corroborate him.

**What "Confirmation" Does and Does Not Mean**

The Islamic position is precise, and it needs stating, because it is often misunderstood in both directions.

The Qur'an affirms that God sent the Torah to Moses and the Gospel to Jesus, that these were genuine revelations, and that their essential message was the same as the Qur'an's. It does *not* affirm that the texts presently in circulation are those revelations intact. It states that alteration occurred:

> *"Among those who are Jews are those who distort words from their places."* (Qur'an 4:46)

> *"So woe to those who write the Book with their own hands, then say, 'This is from God,' that they may purchase thereby a small gain."* (Qur'an 2:79)

The relationship is therefore one of confirmation *and* correction: confirming the core (one God, prophecy, moral accountability, resurrection) and correcting what was added or lost. The Qur'an describes itself in Sūrat al-Māʾidah as *muhayminan ʿalayh* — a guardian and criterion over the previous scriptures (5:48).

**"An Elaboration of the Book"**

*Wa-tafṣīla al-kitābi lā rayba fīhi min rabbi al-ʿālamīn.*

*Tafṣīl* is from *f-ṣ-l*, to separate into parts — the same root used at v. 5 (*yufaṣṣilu al-āyāt*, "He expounds the signs"). It means detailed exposition, setting out item by item.

The source notes an interesting interpretive point: while *"the Book in which there is no doubt"* is usually understood as a reference to the Qur'an itself (as at 2:2), here Ājībah, *Jalālayn*, al-Nīsābūrī and al-Ṭabarī understand it to refer to the laws and beliefs that God has ordained for Muslims to follow.

On this reading, the Qur'an is the detailed unfolding of a body of divine guidance — the specification of what had previously been given in more general form.

**Why Detail Matters**

There is a practical wisdom in *tafṣīl* that is easy to overlook.

General principles are easy to affirm and impossible to apply. Everyone agrees that justice is good, that one should be honest, that the vulnerable should be protected. These affirmations cost nothing and change little, because the disagreement always lies in the specification: *what counts as justice in this dispute? What does honesty require when the truth will harm someone? What exactly is owed to the poor?*

The Qur'an insists on descending to detail. It specifies shares of inheritance to the fraction. It regulates the terms of a loan and requires that it be written down — the longest verse in the Qur'an (2:282) is about documenting debt. It sets the rules of divorce, the waiting periods, the maintenance obligations, the treatment of orphans' property.

This descent into particulars has been criticised as legalism. It is better understood as seriousness. A principle that is never specified is a principle that will never be enforced, and the parties who benefit from vagueness are always the powerful.

**"From the Lord of the Worlds"**

The verse closes by naming the source: *min rabbi al-ʿālamīn*.

The phrase is the second verse of al-Fātiḥah. *ʿĀlamīn* is the plural of *ʿālam*, a "world" in the sense of a whole category or order of created being — the world of humans, of angels, of jinn, of animals, and whatever else exists.

The phrase does two things here.

It establishes *universality*. This is not a tribal deity endorsing a tribal scripture. The Lord of *all* the worlds is the source, which means the message is addressed to all of them — hence the sūrah's repeated *yā ayyuhā al-nās*, "O mankind" (vv. 23, 57, 104, 108), rather than "O Quraysh."

And it establishes *authority*. The word *rabb* means not merely owner but the one who nurtures a thing to its completion — a *rabb* of a garden is one who tends it to fruit. A message from the *rabb* of the worlds is therefore, by definition, a message aimed at bringing its recipients to their proper completion. That is what a scripture from such a source would be *for*.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:38

> **Or do they say, "He has fabricated it"? Say, "Then bring a sūrah like it, and call upon whomsoever you can apart from God, if you are truthful."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Challenge**

The previous verse asserted that the Qur'an could not have been fabricated. This verse converts the assertion into a test.

*Am yaqūlūna iftarāh. Qul faʾtū bi-sūratin mithlihi wa-dʿū mani istaṭaʿtum min dūni Allāhi in kuntum ṣādiqīn.*

The source paraphrases the argument: if you claim the Qur'an is imitable, then seek the help of whomever you can apart from God; and if you are truthful in claiming the Prophet fabricated it, you will be able to produce a sūrah like it.

**The Structure of a Fair Test**

What makes this challenge remarkable is how it is constructed. Consider its terms.

*The bar is set low.* Not the whole Qur'an — a single sūrah. And the shortest sūrahs are three verses. Al-Baghawī suggests *"a sūrah like it"* means a work similar to the entire Qur'an, but Ājībah, al-Rāzī, al-Thaʿlabī and al-Zamakhsharī take the more likely reading: one similar to another sūrah in eloquence, order, and beauty of meaning.

*Collaboration is permitted.* *Wa-dʿū mani istaṭaʿtum* — "call upon whomsoever you can." Assemble every poet in Arabia. Take as long as you need. The Qur'an came in fragments over twenty-three years to an unlettered man under conditions of persecution; you may work in comfort, in committee, with unlimited time.

*The criterion is public.* The judges are the Arabs themselves — the most linguistically discriminating audience in history, who ranked poems and hung the best on the Kaʿbah, who could detect a false vowel at a hundred paces, and who had every conceivable motive to succeed.

*The challenge is repeated and escalating.* The Qur'an issues it in graduated form across its chapters: bring ten fabricated sūrahs (11:13); bring one sūrah (2:23; 10:38); and finally, the most sweeping:

> *"Say: If mankind and jinn gathered together to produce the like of this Qur'an, they could not produce the like of it, even if they were helpers of one another."* (Qur'an 17:88)

**Why the Challenge Was Not Met**

The historical fact is that it was not answered, and the reasons are worth examining because the failure is more significant than it first appears.

The Makkans had every incentive. They were losing sons, splitting families, and eventually fighting battles over this text. Producing three verses that matched it would have ended the movement immediately and at no cost. Instead they tried boycott, torture, assassination plots, and open war — expensive, dangerous options, all of them chosen over a literary exercise that would have been free.

They had every capability. Arabic poetry at that moment was at a peak that has not been surpassed. The *muʿallaqāt* were composed within living memory. Imruʾ al-Qays, Zuhayr, Labīd, ʿAntarah — this was a culture whose highest art form was language and whose champions were poets.

And their own experts conceded the point privately. The sīrah preserves the verdict of al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah, sent by Quraysh to formulate an official line on the Qur'an. He said:

> *"By God, there is a sweetness to it, and a beauty upon it. Its lower part is abundant with fruit and its upper part is laden. It rises above and nothing rises above it, and it crushes what is beneath it."*

He then advised them to call it sorcery — not because he believed it, but because it was the accusation most likely to keep people away.

**The Nature of Qur'anic Inimitability**

The classical scholars developed a whole discipline, *iʿjāz al-Qurʾān*, to analyse what exactly could not be matched. Several strands emerged.

*Naẓm* — composition and structure. Al-Jurjānī (d. 471/1078), in *Dalāʾil al-Iʿjāz*, argued that the inimitability lies not in individual words but in the arrangement: the precise ordering of elements such that no substitution improves the sense.

*Genre.* The Qur'an belongs to no existing Arabic category. It is not *shiʿr* (poetry — it has no metre), not *sajʿ* (rhymed prose — its rhythms are irregular and subordinate to meaning), and not ordinary prose. The Arabs, who had exhaustive taxonomies for verbal art, had no shelf for it.

*Sustained level.* Any writer produces peaks and troughs. The Qur'an maintains its register across 114 chapters, over twenty-three years, across subjects as varied as metaphysics, law, narrative, and rhetoric, delivered extempore in response to unpredictable events.

*Content.* Predictions that came true, information about earlier peoples unavailable in the Arabian context, and — most tellingly — a text produced under maximum political pressure that never bends to that pressure, and repeatedly says things its supposed author would have had every reason to omit (as discussed at v. 15).

**How the Challenge Stands Today**

The challenge has never been withdrawn, and it has never been met, though attempts have been made. Musaylimah, the false prophet of the Yamāmah, produced imitations that survive in the historical literature chiefly as objects of ridicule. Later attempts have generally been pastiches, which is itself instructive: to imitate, one must first have a model, and the existence of the model is the point at issue.

An objection sometimes raised is that literary quality is subjective, and therefore the challenge is unfalsifiable. The objection has some force, but less than it appears. Literary judgment is not arbitrary; it is a trained competence, and communities of competent judges agree far more than they disagree. And in this case the judges most qualified to assess the claim, and most motivated to reject it, are the ones whose failure to answer is the historical datum.

**Why "If You Are Truthful" Is the Right Ending**

*In kuntum ṣādiqīn.*

The challenge is framed as a test of *their* honesty, not of the Qur'an's authenticity — and this is a sharp rhetorical move.

They have made a claim: *he fabricated it*. That claim entails a prediction: *therefore it is the sort of thing a man can fabricate*. The challenge simply asks them to make good on their own prediction. If they cannot, their claim was not a considered judgment but an evasion.

The verse thus turns the burden around. It does not ask them to disprove revelation; it asks them to substantiate the alternative explanation *they themselves proposed*. And that is the correct structure for any such dispute: whoever offers an alternative account of a phenomenon is responsible for showing that his account can produce the phenomenon.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:39

> **Nay, but they deny that whose knowledge they cannot comprehend and whose interpretation has not yet come to them. Even so did those who were before them deny. So behold how the wrongdoers fared in the end.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Psychology of Premature Rejection**

Having issued the challenge and received no answer, the sūrah turns to explain *why* the rejection happened. And the explanation is not that they examined the Qur'an and found it wanting.

*Bal kadhdhabū bi-mā lam yuḥīṭū bi-ʿilmih.*

Al-Nasafī's gloss, preserved in the source, is exact: as soon as the disbelievers heard the Qur'an, they were quick to deny it without first understanding its message or reflecting deeply upon its meanings.

The verb *yuḥīṭū* is significant. From *ḥ-w-ṭ*, to surround or encompass — the same root as *uḥīṭa bihim* in v. 22, the waves closing in. To *encompass* something with knowledge is to have comprehended it fully, to have gone around it.

So the charge is: they rejected what they had not yet gone around. The verdict came before the examination.

**"Whose Interpretation Has Not Yet Come to Them"**

*Wa-lammā yaʾtihim taʾwīluh.*

*Taʾwīl* is from *a-w-l*, to return to an origin. It means the ultimate meaning or the eventual outcome of something — what a matter *comes to*. The Qur'an uses it both for the interpretation of a dream (Joseph's *taʾwīl al-aḥādīth*) and for the final realisation of a prophecy:

> *"Do they await anything but its taʾwīl? On the Day its taʾwīl comes, those who forgot it before will say, 'The messengers of our Lord did indeed bring the truth.'"* (Qur'an 7:53)

The particle *lammā* means "not yet" — implying that the *taʾwīl* is coming, in due course.

So the clause identifies a second failure alongside the first. They rejected what they had not understood, *and* they rejected it before the events that would confirm it had occurred. They passed judgment early on both counts: before comprehension, and before the evidence was complete.

**"People Are Enemies of That of Which They Are Ignorant"**

The source preserves a saying attributed to ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib that has become proverbial in Arabic:

> *"People are enemies of that of which they are ignorant."*

The observation is acute, and it explains something that mere stupidity cannot. Hostility to the unfamiliar is not primarily an intellectual failure; it is a defensive reflex. What one does not understand cannot be controlled, may contain a threat, and — most importantly — implies one's own inadequacy. Rejecting it removes all three problems at once.

This is why the reflex operates most strongly in people with the most invested in their existing understanding. The Makkan elite were not ignorant men. They were the most successful and sophisticated people in Arabia, and that was precisely the difficulty: a claim that required them to reconsider everything was more threatening to them than to a slave with nothing to lose. Which is why the early converts were disproportionately the young, the poor, and the enslaved — people whose existing understanding was not load-bearing.

**The Intellectual Vice Being Named**

It is worth being precise about what this verse condemns, because it is not disagreement.
