The Qur'an does not say: *they examined it carefully and reached the wrong conclusion*. It says they denied *what they had not encompassed with knowledge*. The vice is not error but *premature closure* — reaching a verdict before doing the work.

This is a vice everyone can recognise in others and almost no one detects in himself. Its symptoms are familiar: forming a settled opinion of a book from a summary of it; dismissing a position on the basis of its worst advocates; knowing in advance which conclusions one will find persuasive; treating the source of a claim as sufficient grounds for evaluating it.

The honest alternative is not credulity. It is the willingness to say: *I have not looked into this sufficiently to have an opinion.* The Qur'an's standing instruction is:

> *"And pursue not that of which thou hast no knowledge. Truly hearing, sight, and the heart — each of these will be questioned."* (Qur'an 17:36)

That verse is a complete epistemological ethic in one line, and it makes the faculties themselves answerable: not only will you be asked what you concluded, but how you used the equipment for concluding.

**"Even So Did Those Who Were Before Them Deny"**

*Kadhālika kadhdhaba alladhīna min qablihim.*

The pattern is not new. Ibn Kathīr identifies *those before them* as previous human communities, and the sūrah has already listed the mechanism at v. 13 and will illustrate it at length in the narratives of vv. 71–92.

The value of this observation is that it converts a local dispute into a recognisable historical type. The Makkans presumably thought of themselves as making a considered response to a novel situation. The Qur'an tells them they are performing a script — one that has been performed by every community that received a prophet, with the same lines and the same outcome.

The same charge of sorcery, as noted at v. 2, was thrown at Moses (v. 76 of this sūrah). The same appeal to ancestral practice appears at v. 78. The same demand for miracles appears at v. 20. Nothing they were doing was original, and the Qur'an's method of showing them this is to lay their behaviour alongside its precedents.

**"So Behold How the Wrongdoers Fared in the End"**

*Fa-nẓur kayfa kāna ʿāqibatu al-ẓālimīn.*

The imperative *unẓur* — "look!" — recurs throughout the Qur'an in exactly this construction (3:137; 7:84–86, 103; 10:73; 12:109; 16:36; 27:14, 51, 69; 29:20; 30:9, 42; 35:44; 37:73; 40:21, 82; 43:25; 47:10). The source lists these parallels, and the sheer number is itself a datum: the Qur'an regards the inspection of historical outcomes as a primary method of moral instruction.

*ʿĀqibah* means the end, the outcome, what a thing comes to — from *ʿ-q-b*, to follow after. The command is to evaluate a course of action by where it terminated, not by how it appeared at the time.

This is an important methodological principle. In the middle of a process, evidence is ambiguous: the tyrant looks powerful, the honest man looks foolish, the movement looks doomed. Judgments made at the midpoint are unreliable. The Qur'an directs attention to the *ʿāqibah* because that is where the reliable data is.

Ibn Kathīr's comment on the phrase is that it is meant to draw the deniers' attention to the fact that they too are heading toward destruction for rejecting God's message — the outcome of the earlier cases being a preview of their own.

**The Method of Learning From History**

Two things are required for this verse's instruction to work, and both are demanding.

The first is *looking*. The Qur'an says *unẓur* because the information is available but generally not consulted. Most people know the outcomes of history in outline and never derive anything from them, because knowing a fact and using it are different operations.

The second is *applying the lesson to oneself*. This is the harder step. Every generation reads the record of previous collapses with the settled conviction that its own case is different — that its circumstances are unprecedented, its technology decisive, its institutions robust. The Makkans, standing in a peninsula full of ruins, believed exactly this.

The verse's placement is therefore pointed. It comes immediately after a challenge they could not meet, and it says, in effect: *this is the stage of the script you are at now. You know how the previous performances ended. Look.*

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:40

> **Among them are those who believe in it, and among them are those who do not believe in it, and thy Lord knows best the workers of corruption.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Division Within a Single Audience**

After several verses treating the Makkan opposition as a bloc, this verse breaks the bloc apart.

*Wa-minhum man yuʾminu bihi wa-minhum man lā yuʾminu bih.*

The commentators — Ājībah, al-Bayḍāwī, *Jalālayn*, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī — offer a striking reading. *Those who believe in it* refers to people in Makkah who were, at that moment, rejecting the message, but whom God knew would come to believe later. *Those who do not believe in it* are those who never would.

On this reading, the present tense conceals a temporal distinction. From the outside, both groups looked identical in the year the verse was revealed: both were opponents. God's knowledge distinguishes them.

The source also notes that *in it* can refer to the Prophet rather than to the Qur'an, in which case it would read "believe in him."

**The Consequence: No Human Being Can Sort the Two**

This is the verse's most important practical implication, and it should govern how Muslims deal with those who reject the message.

If the two groups are indistinguishable to observation and distinguished only by divine foreknowledge, then any human attempt to sort them is guesswork — *ẓann*, which the sūrah has just condemned at v. 36.

The history of the first Muslim generation is a series of demonstrations of this point.

ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb set out with a drawn sword to kill the Prophet ﷺ. Within the hour he had accepted Islam, and he became the second caliph, under whom the community expanded from Arabia to Persia and Egypt.

Khālid ibn al-Walīd commanded the cavalry that inflicted the Muslims' worst defeat at Uḥud, killing many Companions. He accepted Islam six years later and became the most successful general in Muslim history; the Prophet ﷺ called him *sayf min suyūf Allāh*, a sword among God's swords.

ʿIkrimah ibn Abī Jahl, son of the man who led the persecution and died at Badr fighting Islam, spent years as an implacable enemy and died as a Muslim martyr at the Battle of Yarmūk.

Hind bint ʿUtbah mutilated the body of the Prophet's uncle Ḥamzah at Uḥud. She accepted Islam at the conquest of Makkah.

Every one of these people, at the moment of this verse's revelation, would have appeared to any reasonable Muslim observer to belong permanently to the second category.

**"Thy Lord Knows Best the Workers of Corruption"**

*Wa-rabbuka aʿlamu bi-l-mufsidīn.*

The clause is a reservation of judgment to God, and its wording is precise in two ways.

First, *aʿlam* — "knows best," the comparative. It implies that others know something, but God knows better. Human assessment is not worthless; it is simply not final.

Second, the category named is *mufsidūn*, workers of corruption — not *kāfirūn*, disbelievers. The shift is significant. *Fasād* is from *f-s-d*, to become rotten or spoiled, and *ifsād* is the active causing of corruption: the spreading of harm through a social body.

Why this term rather than "disbelievers"? Because the verse is not describing an internal state but a public activity. The people whom God knows best are those who are actively working to corrupt — not those who are privately unconvinced. The Qur'an's severest condemnations are consistently reserved for *fasād fī al-arḍ*, corruption in the land:

> *"And when he turns away, he strives throughout the land to cause corruption therein and to destroy crops and lineage; and God loves not corruption."* (Qur'an 2:205)

> *"And do not cause corruption upon the earth after its reformation."* (Qur'an 7:56)

**The Practical Ethic That Follows**

Two duties follow from this verse, and they pull in different directions, which is why holding both is difficult.

The first is *sustained invitation*. Since one cannot know who is in which category, the message must be offered to everyone, indefinitely, without writing anyone off. The Prophet's ﷺ conduct is the model here. Al-Ṭāʾif rejected him and set its children to stone him until his sandals filled with blood; when the angel of the mountains offered to crush the town between two mountains, he replied:

> *"Rather, I hope that God will bring forth from their loins those who will worship God alone, associating nothing with Him."* (al-Bukhārī 3231; Muslim 1795, from ʿĀʾishah)

That is precisely the reasoning of this verse applied in the worst possible circumstances: *among them are those who believe in it* — perhaps not this generation, but their children.

The second duty is *realism about active harm*. The verse does not counsel naivety. Some people are, in fact, working corruption, and the community is entitled to protect itself from what they do. What the verse forbids is the pretence of knowing who is beyond hope.

The distinction is between *conduct*, which can be judged, and *destiny*, which cannot. A person's actions may be opposed while his soul is left to God.

**The Prohibition on Declaring Others Damned**

The tradition took this principle very seriously, and it is worth stating because its violation has caused enormous harm in Muslim history.

The Prophet ﷺ warned:

> *"Whoever says to his brother, 'O disbeliever,' it returns upon one of them."* (al-Bukhārī 6104; Muslim 60, from Ibn ʿUmar)

And in a hadith of extraordinary severity:

> There were two men among the Children of Israel, one of whom was diligent in worship and the other sinful. The diligent one kept seeing the other sinning and saying, "Stop." One day he found him at a sin and said, "By God, God will not forgive you." God said: *"Who is it that swears by Me that I will not forgive so-and-so? I have forgiven him, and I have invalidated your deeds."* (Abū Dāwūd 4901, from Abū Hurayrah)

The offence in that story is not the correction; it is the presumption of knowing the verdict. The worshipper had appointed himself to an office this verse reserves for God.

**The Verse as a Source of Hope**

There is a final reading of this verse that is a comfort rather than a warning, and it should not be missed.

Anyone who has prayed for a family member's guidance for years without visible result is in exactly the position of the Muslims to whom this verse was revealed. They were looking at people who seemed permanently closed, and they were told: *among them are those who believe in it.*

The Prophet ﷺ prayed for his uncle Abū Ṭālib and did not see the answer he wanted. He prayed for ʿUmar and did. He could not have told in advance which prayer would take which form — and neither can anyone else. What the verse establishes is that the appearance of closure is not the same as closure, and that the file is not closed by anyone's assessment, including one's own.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:41

> **And if they deny thee, say, "Unto me, my deeds, and unto you, your deeds. You are quit of that which I do, and I am quit of that which you do."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Instruction for What to Do After Rejection**

The preceding verses have established that some will believe and some will not. This verse gives the Prophet ﷺ — and every believer after him — the correct posture toward those who do not.

*Wa-in kadhdhabūka fa-qul lī ʿamalī wa-lakum ʿamalukum.*

Al-Ṭabarī's gloss is preserved in the source and is worth quoting in full:

> *"O Muhammad, if these idolaters disbelieve in you and reject from you that which you have brought to them, then say, 'O people! Unto me are my religion and deeds and unto you are your religion and deeds. Your actions shall not harm me, nor shall mine harm you. All those who perform actions will only be recompensed for the acts that they have performed.'"*

**The Doctrine of Individual Accountability**

The theological foundation of this verse is one of the Qur'an's most consistent teachings: no soul bears another's burden.

> *"And no bearer of burdens shall bear the burden of another."* (Qur'an 6:164; 17:15; 35:18; 39:7; 53:38)

> *"And that man shall have nothing but what he strives for."* (Qur'an 53:39)

This principle has enormous consequences, and it is worth spelling them out because it distinguishes Islam sharply from several alternatives.

It rules out inherited guilt. No one is born accountable for an ancestor's sin. The Qur'anic account of Adam's error is that Adam repented and was forgiven (2:37; 20:122); no residue was transmitted.

It rules out collective punishment. A person is not answerable for what his tribe, nation, or co-religionists have done. The Qur'an states this even of the People of the Book, whom it criticises at length: *"They are not all alike"* (3:113).

And it rules out vicarious salvation. No one else's righteousness can be transferred to your account. This is why the Prophet ﷺ told his own daughter that he could not avail her against God (al-Bukhārī 2753).

**"You Are Quit of That Which I Do"**

*Antum barīʾūna mimmā aʿmalu wa-anā barīʾun mimmā taʿmalūn.*

*Barīʾ* is from *b-r-ʾ*, to be free, clear, absolved. The word is used in Arabic for being cleared of a debt or an accusation, and it gives *barāʾah*, immunity or acquittal — the name of the ninth sūrah.

Notice that the disclaiming runs in both directions, and the *other* party is mentioned first. "*You* are quit of what I do" — before "and I am quit of what you do."

This ordering is a courtesy, and it is also a claim. It says: I do not hold you responsible for my choices. Whatever I do, you bear no part of it. That is the fair version of the separation, and it must be extended before it can be claimed.

**What This Verse Is Not**

It is important to read this verse in context, because in isolation it can be made to mean something the sūrah does not intend.

It is *not* an instruction to abandon the mission. The Prophet ﷺ continued calling to Islam for the remainder of his life, including to the very people addressed here. The sūrah's final verse commands him to *follow what is revealed and be patient* (v. 109) — not to withdraw.

It is *not* religious relativism. The sūrah has just said, four verses earlier, that *"what is there beyond truth save error?"* (v. 32). This verse does not concede that both parties are equally right; it concedes that both parties are separately answerable.

It is *not* a licence for indifference to injustice. The prohibition of *fasād*, corruption in the land, is a communal obligation, and the Qur'an's commands to enjoin right and forbid wrong are not cancelled by this verse.

What it *is*: a rule about the limits of responsibility for another's belief. Having conveyed the message clearly, one is not accountable for its acceptance, and one need not carry the burden of another adult's free decision.

**Sūrat al-Kāfirūn as the Parallel**

The classic parallel is the sūrah revealed in Makkah in response to a specific proposal from Quraysh: that the Prophet ﷺ worship their gods for a year and they worship his for a year.

> *"Say: O disbelievers! I worship not what you worship, nor do you worship what I worship. Nor shall I worship what you worship, nor will you worship what I worship. Unto you your religion, and unto me my religion."* (Qur'an 109:1–6)

The final line, *lakum dīnukum wa-liya dīn*, is the same structure as our verse. And its context establishes the boundary precisely: it was revealed to *refuse a compromise*, not to endorse pluralism about truth. The separation is a refusal to merge, delivered without hostility.

**The Psychological Wisdom of the Instruction**

There is a piece of practical wisdom here that anyone who has tried to persuade a resistant person will recognise.

The greatest source of exhaustion in calling others to a truth is the assumption of responsibility for the outcome. A person who believes he must produce belief in someone else will inevitably escalate: he will argue longer, press harder, resort to emotional pressure, and eventually damage the relationship — and none of it will work, because belief is not produced by pressure.

The verse relieves that burden. The task is to convey; the outcome belongs to God, as v. 35 established (*"Truly thou canst not guide whomsoever thou lovest"*). Having conveyed, one is released.

This release is what makes long-term relationships with those who disagree sustainable. A parent whose adult child has left the religion, a Muslim in a family that does not share his faith, a person whose closest friend regards his beliefs as nonsense — each has to find a way to continue in relationship without either capitulating or waging a permanent campaign. This verse describes exactly that position: clarity about the difference, courtesy in maintaining it, and no assumption of responsibility for the other's soul.

**How the Separation Should Sound**

Tone matters, and the Qur'an is specific about it elsewhere:

> *"Call unto the way of thy Lord with wisdom and beautiful exhortation, and dispute with them in the way that is best."* (Qur'an 16:125)

> *"And do not dispute with the People of the Book save in the fairest way."* (Qur'an 29:46)

> *"And had thou been rough and hard-hearted, they would have scattered from around thee."* (Qur'an 3:159)

The formula in our verse is notable for what it lacks: there is no insult, no curse, no prediction of the opponent's fate. It is a clean statement of separated accounts. The Qur'an even forbids abusing the objects of others' devotion, on grounds of consequence:

> *"And revile not those whom they call upon apart from God, lest they revile God in enmity, without knowledge."* (Qur'an 6:108)

**A Note on Living Alongside Difference**

The historical record of Muslim societies applying this principle is mixed, as it is for every civilisation. But the principle itself is clear, and it was given institutional form early: the recognition of religious communities with their own law, the explicit prohibition of compulsion in religion (*"There is no compulsion in religion"* — 2:256), and the Prophet's ﷺ constitution of Madinah, which bound Muslims and Jews into a single polity with each community retaining its own religion.

The verse before us is the personal-level form of that arrangement. Its formula — *my deeds are mine, yours are yours* — is a workable basis for living beside people whose deepest convictions differ from one's own, and it is a great deal more durable than either forced agreement or pretended indifference.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:42

> **And among them are those who listen to thee. But dost thou make the deaf to hear, though they understand not?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Listening Without Hearing**

*Wa-minhum man yastamiʿūna ilayk.*

The verb is *yastamiʿūn* — form VIII of *s-m-ʿ*, which carries the sense of deliberately giving ear, listening attentively. These are not people who happened to overhear. They came, sat, and listened.

The sīrah records exactly this behaviour. Several of the Makkan leaders — Abū Sufyān, Abū Jahl, and al-Akhnas ibn Sharīq among them — would slip out at night, individually and without one another's knowledge, to listen to the Prophet ﷺ reciting the Qur'an in his house before dawn. They discovered each other on the road home, rebuked one another, swore not to return, and did the same thing the following night. On the third occasion they bound themselves by a solemn oath, and al-Akhnas went to ask each of the others what he thought of what he had heard. Abū Jahl's reply, recorded in Ibn Isḥāq, is one of the most revealing statements in the whole sīrah:

> *"We and the Banū ʿAbd Manāf have competed in honour: they fed and we fed; they carried burdens and we carried burdens; they gave and we gave, until we were neck and neck like two racehorses. Then they said, 'A prophet from among us, to whom revelation comes from heaven!' When shall we ever attain that? By God, we will never believe in him and never accept him as true."*

That is not a man who found the message unconvincing. It is a man who found it convincing and could not bear the implication for his own standing.

**"Dost Thou Make the Deaf to Hear?"**

*A-fa-anta tusmiʿu al-ṣumma wa-law kānū lā yaʿqilūn.*

The rhetorical question addresses the Prophet ﷺ, and its tone is consoling as much as it is condemning. The commentators direct the reader to 6:25 for the parallel:

> *"And among them are those who listen to thee, but We have placed coverings over their hearts lest they understand it, and in their ears a deafness. And though they see every sign, they will not believe in it."* (Qur'an 6:25)

The deafness described is obviously not physical. Their ears worked; that is the whole point of the verse — they *listened*. What was missing was the connection between the ear and the understanding.

**"Though They Understand Not" — The Location of the Failure**

The clause *wa-law kānū lā yaʿqilūn* identifies where the breakdown occurs. The verb *ʿaqala* means to comprehend, to reason, to tie things together — its root sense is to bind, from the *ʿiqāl*, the cord used to tether a camel's leg. To *ʿaqala* something is to bind the meaning to the mind so it does not wander off.

So the failure is not at the level of reception but of *processing*. The words arrive; they are not tied to anything. They enter as sound and exit as sound.

The Qur'an makes this explicit elsewhere with an image of extraordinary vividness:

> *"Their hearts are therein preoccupied. And they conceal private conversation, those who have wronged, saying: Is this but a man like yourselves? Then will you succumb to magic while you see it?"* (Qur'an 21:3)

And, most directly:

> *"Truly it is not the eyes that go blind, but it is the hearts within the breasts that go blind."* (Qur'an 22:46)

That verse — which the source cites in connection with the next verse of our sūrah — locates the organ of religious perception in the *qalb*, the heart, understood in Qur'anic usage as the seat of both feeling and understanding.

**Why Attentive Listening Can Produce Nothing**

The phenomenon this verse describes is entirely familiar, and it is worth analysing because it happens to believers as much as to deniers.

*Listening in order to reply.* A person can attend closely to every word while his mind is engaged solely in formulating objections. He hears with precision and understands nothing, because comprehension requires temporarily entertaining the possibility that the speaker is right.

*Listening with the conclusion pre-set.* Where the verdict has been reached in advance — as in Abū Jahl's case — listening becomes an exercise in locating grounds for a decision already made.

*Listening as consumption.* Perhaps the most common form today. A person listens to sermons, lectures, and recitations continuously, experiences them as moving, and changes nothing. The material is processed as content rather than as instruction. The Qur'an's word for this failure is precise:

> *"And be not like those who say, 'We hear,' while they hear not."* (Qur'an 8:21)

> *"O you who believe, obey God and His Messenger, and do not turn away from him while you hear."* (Qur'an 8:20)

**The Contrast: What Real Listening Looks Like**

The Qur'an describes the alternative in terms that make the difference clear:

> *"So give glad tidings to My servants — those who listen to the word and follow the best of it. It is they whom God has guided, and it is they who are the people of understanding."* (Qur'an 39:17–18)

Two elements: *yastamiʿūna al-qawl* (they listen to the word) and *yattabiʿūna aḥsanah* (they follow the best of it). The second is what distinguishes them, and the Qur'an names the result — *ulū al-albāb*, people of understanding.

And the Qur'an describes the physical effect of genuine reception:

> *"God has sent down the most beautiful discourse: a Book consistent in its oft-repeated parts, whereat shudder the skins of those who fear their Lord; then their skins and their hearts soften unto the remembrance of God."* (Qur'an 39:23)

The sequence there is striking: first a shudder (*taqshaʿirru*), a physical recoil, and then a softening. Something happens in the body.

**A Consolation for the Speaker**

The verse's primary addressee is the Prophet ﷺ, and its function in context is to relieve him of an impossible burden.

He is being told: this is not your failure. You cannot make the deaf hear. The equipment required for reception is not something a speaker can install in a listener.

This connects directly to v. 41's separation of accounts and to v. 35's distinction between showing the way and causing acceptance. Together these verses build a consistent picture of the responsibilities of anyone who teaches or invites: convey clearly, conduct yourself well, and do not measure your worth by outcomes you do not control.

The verse also contains, implicitly, a warning about method. If the failure is one of the heart rather than the ear, then louder argument will not help. Volume does not cure deafness. What sometimes does reach a closed heart is the very thing this sūrah has already emphasised — the observed character of the messenger (v. 16) — and the passage of time and circumstance, which God controls and the speaker does not.

**The Question to Turn Inward**

The most useful application of this verse is not to identify the deaf among one's opponents. It is to ask whether one's own listening is producing anything.

A practical test: take the last religious talk one attended, or the last passage of the Qur'an one read attentively, and ask what changed as a result. If nothing did, the listening was of the type this verse describes — technically successful, functionally deaf.

The tradition's remedy is small and concrete. Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī is reported to have said that the earlier generations, when they learned ten verses of the Qur'an, would not proceed to the next ten until they had acted upon them. That approach produces very slow progress through the text and very fast progress through the self, which is the correct ratio.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:43

> **And among them are those who look at thee. But couldst thou guide the blind, though they see not?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Companion Verse**

This verse pairs with the one before it, and the pairing is deliberate. There, hearing; here, sight. There, *"among them are those who listen to thee"*; here, *"among them are those who look at thee."*

*Wa-minhum man yanẓuru ilayk. A-fa-anta tahdī al-ʿumya wa-law kānū lā yubṣirūn.*

The Qur'an frequently pairs these two faculties (see 2:7; 6:46; 16:78; 32:9; 41:20–22), because together they constitute the whole apparatus of receiving evidence: hearing takes in testimony, sight takes in demonstration.

**What There Was to See**

The verse says they *looked at him*. It is worth asking what was available to be seen.

They saw a man who had lived among them for forty years without a recorded lie. They saw him praying through the night until his feet swelled — ʿĀʾishah reported that when she asked why, given that his sins were forgiven, he answered: *"Should I not be a grateful servant?"* (al-Bukhārī 4837; Muslim 2820). They saw him refuse wealth and kingship when Quraysh offered both for a compromise. They saw him mending his own sandals and patching his own clothes (al-Tirmidhī, *Shamāʾil*). They saw a household in which, as ʿĀʾishah reported, *"the crescent would appear, then the crescent, then the crescent — three crescents in two months — and no fire would be lit in the houses of the Messenger of God"* (al-Bukhārī 6459; Muslim 2972). They saw a man in a position to take anything who took nothing.

That is a considerable amount of visible evidence, and it was in front of them daily for years.

**"Couldst Thou Guide the Blind?"**

The source's comment on this verse is exact: although deniers of the Prophet look at him, they are spiritually blind to his message, *"for it is not the eyes that go blind, but it is hearts within breasts that go blind"* (22:46).

That verse from Sūrat al-Ḥajj is the definitive statement of the Qur'anic doctrine of blindness, and its wording deserves attention:

> *"Have they not travelled in the land, so that they might have hearts by which to understand, or ears by which to hear? For truly it is not the eyes that go blind, but it is the hearts within the breasts that go blind."* (Qur'an 22:46)

The verse anticipates the objection that blindness is a physical condition and relocates it. And it identifies the organ precisely: *al-qulūb allatī fī al-ṣudūr*, "the hearts which are in the breasts" — a curious specification, since where else would hearts be. The classical commentators read the apparent redundancy as emphasis: the failure is in the actual, located, physical seat of a person's inner life, not in some abstract faculty.

**The Difference Between Looking and Seeing**

There is a distinction here that is worth making precise, because it applies well beyond religion.

*Naẓar* — the verb used in this verse — means to direct the gaze. *Baṣar*, the verb in the second clause (*lā yubṣirūn*), means to perceive, to apprehend visually with understanding. One can *naẓar* without *baṣar*: direct the eyes at something and take in nothing.

This is a commonplace of ordinary experience. A person can read a page and reach the bottom having absorbed nothing. A person can walk past a building daily for a decade and be unable to describe it. Perception requires more than the operation of the eye; it requires attention directed by interest, and interest is a function of what one cares about.

Which means that what a person sees is determined, to a significant degree, by what he *wants* to see. This is not a modern insight; it is the Qur'an's claim throughout this sūrah. The deniers' blindness is not a defect in their optical equipment but in their disposition, and the disposition was formed by interest — as Abū Jahl's confession, quoted in the previous verse's commentary, makes explicit.

**The Sunnah as a Visible Argument**

There is a positive implication in this verse that should not be missed. The Prophet ﷺ was *worth looking at*. His life was itself part of the evidence, which is why the Qur'an describes him as a model:

> *"Truly in the Messenger of God you have a beautiful example, for whoever hopes in God and the Last Day and remembers God much."* (Qur'an 33:21)

And ʿĀʾishah, asked to describe his character, gave the famous answer:

> *"His character was the Qur'an."* (Muslim 746, and Aḥmad 24601)

The statement means that the text and the life were the same content in two media. What the Qur'an said in words, he displayed in conduct. This is why the science of hadith developed as it did — not as a collection of anecdotes but as the preservation of a demonstration.

And it establishes the standard by which believers themselves become either evidence or counter-evidence. A community that claims to follow a Book of this quality is, whether it wishes to be or not, a visible argument about the Book. People look at Muslims the way the Makkans looked at the Prophet ﷺ, and reach conclusions accordingly.

**Two Kinds of Blindness in the Hereafter**

The Qur'an extends the metaphor into the next life, and the extension is severe:

> *"And whoever turns away from My remembrance, truly his shall be a miserable life, and We shall raise him blind on the Day of Resurrection. He will say: My Lord, why hast Thou raised me blind, when I used to see? He will say: Thus it is. Our signs came unto thee, and thou didst forget them; and thus this Day thou art forgotten."* (Qur'an 20:124–126)

The exchange is precise in its justice. The man protests that he could see; he is told that he was blind to what mattered, and that his condition now simply corresponds to what it was then. The Hereafter, once again, discloses rather than imposes.

**The Practical Response**

If blindness of the heart is the condition, what is the treatment? The Qur'an and Sunnah offer a consistent answer, and it is not primarily intellectual.

The heart is described as subject to rust (83:14 and the hadith cited at v. 33), and its polishing is prescribed as *dhikr*, remembrance:

> *"Truly in the remembrance of God do hearts find rest."* (Qur'an 13:28)

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"For everything there is a polish, and the polish of hearts is the remembrance of God."* (al-Bayhaqī, *Shuʿab al-Īmān*, from Ibn ʿUmar)

And he identified the mechanism by which the capacity is lost and regained, in the hadith of the black spot quoted at v. 33: sin darkens, repentance polishes.

The order of operations implied is worth noting, because it inverts the assumption most modern people make. We tend to assume that one first becomes convinced and then acts accordingly. The Qur'anic model runs the other way at least as often: one acts, and the acting clears the sight. This is why the tradition prescribes practice — prayer, charity, fasting, the avoidance of what is forbidden — to those whose faith is weak, rather than prescribing further argument. Argument addresses the ear. This verse is about the eye, and the eye is cleaned differently.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:44

> **Truly God does not wrong human beings in the least, but rather human beings wrong themselves.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Central Ethical Statement**

Everything the sūrah has said about the deniers — their deafness, their blindness, the sealing of hearts, the word that came due against them — raises an obvious objection. If they cannot hear, cannot see, and are subject to a divine determination, in what sense is their condition their fault?

This verse is the answer, and it is stated as an absolute.

*Inna Allāha lā yaẓlimu al-nāsa shayʾan wa-lākinna al-nāsa anfusahum yaẓlimūn.*

**"Not in the Least" — The Force of *Shayʾan***

The negation is total. *Lā yaẓlimu ... shayʾan* — He does not wrong them *by anything at all*, not by the smallest amount. The Qur'an elsewhere quantifies the same claim:

> *"Truly God does not wrong so much as an atom's weight."* (Qur'an 4:40)

> *"And they shall not be wronged by so much as the thread of a date-stone."* (Qur'an 4:49; cf. 4:77)

That last image is worth noticing: *fatīl*, the thin filament running along the groove of a date pit. The Qur'an chooses the most negligible quantity available to a desert audience and denies even that.

The reason this matters is that divine justice, in Islamic theology, is not merely a policy God has adopted; it is a consequence of what He is. *Al-ʿAdl* — the Just — is among His names, and injustice is not something He refrains from but something incompatible with His nature. The hadith qudsī puts it in the first person:

> *"O My servants, I have forbidden injustice upon Myself and have made it forbidden among you, so do not wrong one another."* (Muslim 2577, from Abū Dharr)

**"But Rather Human Beings Wrong Themselves"**

*Wa-lākinna al-nāsa anfusahum yaẓlimūn.*

The Arabic word order places *anfusahum* — "themselves" — before the verb, which in Arabic grammar produces emphasis. The sense is: it is *themselves* that they wrong.

This is the verse's real content, and it recurs across the Qur'an as a formula:

> *"And they wronged Us not, but they used to wrong themselves."* (Qur'an 2:57; 7:160)

> *"God wronged them not, but they used to wrong themselves."* (Qur'an 9:70; 16:33; 29:40; 30:9)

Note the first of these especially: *they wronged Us not*. God is not injured by human sin. He gains nothing from obedience and loses nothing by disobedience. The hadith qudsī states this with startling directness:

> *"O My servants, if the first of you and the last of you, the human of you and the jinn of you, were as pious as the most pious heart of any one man among you, that would not increase My kingdom in anything. O My servants, if the first of you and the last of you, the human of you and the jinn of you, were as wicked as the most wicked heart of any one man among you, that would not decrease My kingdom in anything."* (Muslim 2577)

Which means the entire moral structure of religion exists for the benefit of the creature, not the Creator. Commands are not tribute. They are instructions issued to beings who will suffer from ignoring them.

**How Free Will Is Preserved**

Al-Rāzī draws the theological consequence explicitly, and the source preserves it: since God is not unjust to human beings, He does not coerce them into wrong actions; when they wrong themselves, they do so out of their free choice.

This is the classical resolution of the tension that runs through the sūrah. The verses on sealing (v. 74), on the word coming due (v. 33), and on belief occurring only by God's leave (v. 100) describe God's response to prior human orientation, not the origination of it. The order is always: human movement first, divine confirmation second — as at 61:5, *"So when they deviated, God caused their hearts to deviate."*

Al-Rāzī adds the Qur'anic characterisation that explains the tendency: *"the human being is an ignorant wrongdoer"* (33:72) — the closing words of the verse of the Trust, in which the heavens, the earth, and the mountains declined to bear the *amānah* and man accepted it.

**Why Sin Is Self-Harm: The Mechanism**

The verse states that wrongdoing injures the wrongdoer. It is worth setting out how, because the claim is often taken as pious consolation rather than as description.

*It damages the faculty of perception.* This is the mechanism of *rān*, the rust on the heart, examined at v. 33. Each wrong act reduces the capacity to recognise the next one. The person is not merely doing damage; he is disabling the instrument that would detect it.

*It creates internal division.* A person who acts against what he knows must maintain two accounts of himself — the one he acts on and the one he believes. Psychology calls this dissonance; the tradition calls it the loss of *sakīnah*, tranquillity. The Prophet's ﷺ definition of sin, quoted at v. 23, describes the symptom exactly: *"sin is what wavers in your breast and which you would hate for people to find out about."*

*It builds a self.* Actions repeated become dispositions, and dispositions become character. Nobody chooses to become a bitter person; one arrives there by a long sequence of individually small choices, none of which felt decisive.

*It removes protections.* The Qur'an describes obedience as a form of shelter — *taqwā* is literally a shield, as noted at v. 6. To abandon it is not to gain freedom but to remove cover.

**The Objection From Suffering**

The hardest objection to this verse is not philosophical but experiential: what about the innocent? The child born into famine, the victim of a crime, the person crushed by circumstances he did not create — surely these are wronged, and not by themselves.

The Qur'an's answer is layered, and honesty requires acknowledging that it is not a complete explanation.

First, much human suffering is caused by other humans, and the Qur'an says so plainly:

> *"Corruption has appeared on land and sea because of what the hands of men have earned."* (Qur'an 30:41)

Famines are overwhelmingly distributional; wars are chosen; most poverty is structural. The Qur'an does not attribute these to God's arrangement but to human *ẓulm* — the wronging of others, which is also, per this verse, self-wronging.

Second, suffering that is not caused by wrongdoing is placed in a different category altogether. It is *balāʾ*, trial, and the Qur'an treats it not as punishment but as an occasion:

> *"And We shall surely test you with something of fear and hunger, and loss of wealth and lives and fruits; but give glad tidings to the patient — those who, when affliction befalls them, say, 'Truly we are God's, and unto Him we return.'"* (Qur'an 2:155–156)

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"No fatigue, illness, anxiety, sorrow, harm, or distress befalls a Muslim — even the prick of a thorn — but that God expiates by it some of his sins."* (al-Bukhārī 5641; Muslim 2573, from Abū Saʿīd and Abū Hurayrah)

Third — and this is where the verse belongs in the sūrah's argument — the final accounting has not yet occurred. This is the point of vv. 4, 23, 30 and 45–46. A ledger examined mid-year does not balance. The Qur'an's claim of perfect justice is a claim about the completed accounts, and it explicitly locates the settlement in the Hereafter.

**The Empowering Reading**

There is a way of hearing this verse that is not accusatory but liberating, and it is probably the intended emphasis.

If God does not wrong anyone, then the universe is not rigged. Nothing is happening to you that is unjust at the level of ultimate accounting. There is no malice in the system, no arbitrary victimisation, no divine indifference.

And if the harm a person suffers from his own actions is self-inflicted, then it is also, in principle, self-correctable. A victim of an external force is helpless. A person who has been damaging himself has, by that very fact, been in a position to stop.

This is the note the Qur'an strikes immediately after its harshest descriptions of self-wronging:

> *"Say: O My servants who have wronged themselves, despair not of God's Mercy. Truly God forgives all sins. Truly He is the Forgiving, the Merciful."* (Qur'an 39:53)

Note the address: *"O My servants who have wronged themselves"* — the very phrase from our verse, used as a term of endearment rather than accusation. The people described by this verse are the ones addressed by that one.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:45

> **On the Day when He shall gather them, it will be as if they tarried but an hour of the day, acquainting themselves with one another. Lost indeed are those who denied the meeting with God, and they were not rightly guided.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Compression of a Lifetime**

*Wa-yawma yaḥshuruhum ka-an lam yalbathū illā sāʿatan mina al-nahār.*

The scene returns to the gathering of v. 28, but the focus now is on a peculiar feature of that moment: the experience of time.

They will feel that they *tarried but an hour of the day*. The source directs the reader to 79:46 for the parallel, and the Qur'an makes the same observation repeatedly:

> *"On the Day they see it, it will be as though they had tarried but an evening or its morning."* (Qur'an 79:46)

> *"He will say: How long did you tarry on the earth, in number of years? They will say: We tarried a day or part of a day; ask those who keep count. He will say: You tarried but a little, if only you had known."* (Qur'an 23:112–114)

> *"And on the Day the Hour comes, the guilty will swear that they tarried not save an hour."* (Qur'an 30:55)

**Why the Estimate Will Be So Short**

Two readings are available, and the source notes both: the shortening may apply to the life lived in this world, or to the interval between death and resurrection (the *barzakh*).

Both are psychologically intelligible.

The interval of the *barzakh* passes without conscious duration — the Qur'an's story of the man who was made to die for a hundred years and asked, on waking, *"How long have you tarried?"* answers *"A day, or part of a day"* (2:259).

But the shortening of the *life* is the more instructive reading, and it corresponds to something everyone has already begun to notice.

Time in memory is not measured in hours; it is measured in *distinct events*. A month of novelty leaves a large deposit; a decade of routine leaves almost nothing. This is why childhood summers feel enormous in retrospect and adult decades feel brief: the child was encountering everything for the first time, and the adult was repeating.

A life spent in acquisition and routine therefore compresses. Fifty years of the same commute, the same anxieties, the same acquisitions, produce very little in memory that can be laid end to end. When such a life is reviewed in its totality, the honest estimate really is *"an hour of the day."*

**"Acquainting Themselves With One Another"**

*Yataʿārafūna baynahum.*

The phrase is from *ʿ-r-f*, to know or recognise — the same root as *taʿāruf*, mutual acquaintance, in the famous verse:

> *"O mankind, We created you from a male and a female, and made you peoples and tribes, that you may know one another (li-taʿārafū). Truly the noblest of you before God is the most reverent among you."* (Qur'an 49:13)

The commentators read the phrase here in two ways.

On one reading, it describes the brief moment of mutual recognition at the resurrection: people rise and recognise each other, as one does on waking among familiar faces after a short sleep — which reinforces the impression of a brief interval.

On another, more sobering reading, the recognition is momentary and immediately dissolves. They know each other for an instant, and then the terror of the Day separates them, as the Qur'an describes:

> *"And no friend will ask about a friend, though they are made to see one another. The guilty will wish that he could ransom himself from the punishment of that Day by his children, and his wife and his brother, and his kin who sheltered him, and all who are on the earth — that it might save him."* (Qur'an 70:10–14)

> *"Then when the Deafening Blast comes — the Day when a man will flee from his brother, and his mother and his father, and his wife and his children."* (Qur'an 80:33–36)

The pairing of recognition with flight is deliberate. The relationships are real; they are simply no longer able to help.

**"Lost Indeed Are Those Who Denied the Meeting With God"**

*Qad khasira alladhīna kadhdhabū bi-liqāʾi Allāhi wa-mā kānū muhtadīn.*

*Khasira* is a commercial term: to suffer loss in a transaction, to have one's capital diminished. *Khusrān* is the state of having traded badly.

The Qur'an uses this vocabulary consistently for the ultimate accounting, and it is worth appreciating how apt it is for the Makkan audience — merchants who understood profit and loss with professional precision.

The whole of Sūrat al-ʿAṣr is built on this word:

> *"By time! Truly mankind is in loss (khusr) — save those who believe, and perform righteous deeds, and counsel one another to truth, and counsel one another to patience."* (Qur'an 103:1–3)

Al-Shāfiʿī's celebrated comment on that sūrah was that if people reflected on it alone, it would suffice them.

**What Exactly Was Lost**

The nature of the loss deserves specification, because it is not merely a failure to gain.

A person who denies the meeting with God does not merely miss a reward. He *spends* something: a lifetime, a finite and unrepeatable allocation of days, hours, faculties, and opportunities. That capital was issued to him, and it was expended. The question at the accounting is what he acquired with it.

The Prophet ﷺ framed the final examination in exactly these terms:

> *"The two feet of a servant will not move on the Day of Resurrection until he is asked about four things: about his life, how he spent it; about his knowledge, what he did with it; about his wealth, where he earned it and where he spent it; and about his body, how he used it."* (al-Tirmidhī 2417, from Ibn Masʿūd)

Four questions, all about expenditure. None about what he intended, believed abstractly, or felt.

**The Practical Use of the Compression**

The most useful application of this verse is to run its calculation now.

The estimate the deniers give on that Day — *"an hour of the day"* — is not a distortion produced by shock. It is an accurate assessment made without the illusions that ordinarily inflate our sense of time. What the verse offers is the chance to make the same assessment early, while there is still capital left to deploy.

Two practices in the tradition are designed for exactly this. The first is the frequent remembrance of death:

> *"Remember often the destroyer of pleasures — death."* (al-Tirmidhī 2307; Ibn Mājah 4258, from Abū Hurayrah)

This is not morbidity. It is calibration: the deliberate importing of the perspective of the end into the middle, so that current decisions are made with accurate information about their weight.

The second is the nightly accounting (*muḥāsabah*), which ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb is reported to have summarised:

> *"Take account of yourselves before you are taken to account, and weigh your deeds before they are weighed for you."*

Both practices do the same thing this verse does: they shorten the perceived distance to the reckoning, on the grounds that the distance is in fact short.

**"And They Were Not Rightly Guided"**

The verse's final clause, *wa-mā kānū muhtadīn*, closes the case. The loss was not accidental; it followed from a failure to be guided — which, as v. 35 established, is available to those who seek it and withheld from those who turn away.

The clause also links this verse to the sūrah's controlling contrast. Verse 9 said of the believers: *"their Lord guides them by their faith."* Here: *"and they were not rightly guided."* One group is conducted; the other wanders — *yaʿmahūn*, as v. 11 put it, staggering without bearings.

The difference between them, on this sūrah's account, is not intelligence, or opportunity, or the quality of the evidence they received. It is whether they *anticipated the meeting* — the very quality named in v. 7 as the first symptom of the disease and named here again, at the moment the meeting arrives.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:46

> **Whether We show thee a part of that which We promise them, or We take thee, their return shall be unto Us. Then God is Witness over that which they do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse Addressed to the Prophet's Own Impatience**

This verse speaks directly to a pressure that anyone in the Prophet's ﷺ position would feel. He had been warning for years. The warnings concerned consequences that had not arrived. His followers were suffering, his opponents were prospering, and there was no visible vindication.

*Wa-immā nuriyannaka baʿḍa alladhī naʿiduhum aw natawaffayannaka fa-ilaynā marjiʿuhum.*

The two possibilities are named without preference: either you will see some of it, or you will be taken first. Either way, *their return is unto Us*.

**"A Part of That Which We Promise Them"**

Ājībah's comment, preserved in the source, is that God might show the Prophet the Makkan idolaters' punishment in his lifetime, as happened at the Battle of Badr.

Badr is the obvious reference. Two years after the emigration, the leadership of Quraysh marched out with roughly a thousand men against three hundred and thirteen Muslims. By the end of the day, Abū Jahl was dead, ʿUtbah ibn Rabīʿah was dead, Umayyah ibn Khalaf — who had tortured Bilāl in the sun — was dead, and some seventy of the Makkan nobility lay in a pit.

The detail preserved in the collections is striking. The Prophet ﷺ stood at the edge of the well into which the bodies had been thrown and addressed them by name:

> *"O ʿUtbah ibn Rabīʿah! O Shaybah ibn Rabīʿah! O Umayyah ibn Khalaf! O Abū Jahl ibn Hishām! Have you found what your Lord promised you to be true? For I have found what my Lord promised me to be true."* (al-Bukhārī 3976; Muslim 2874)

Note the word *baʿḍ* in the verse: *a part* of what is promised. Badr was not the promised punishment; it was an instalment, a preview of an accounting whose main body lies elsewhere.

**"Or We Take Thee"**

*Aw natawaffayannaka.* The verb *tawaffā* means to take in full, to receive something completely — from *w-f-y*, to fulfil or complete. It is the Qur'an's characteristic word for death:

> *"God takes the souls at the time of their death."* (Qur'an 39:42)

The choice of verb is significant. Death is not described as an ending or a subtraction but as a *taking in full* — the collection of something that was on loan, complete and entire. Nothing is lost in the transaction; the soul is received.

The verse thus contemplates, calmly, the possibility that the Prophet ﷺ will die before seeing vindication. And this possibility is treated as making no difference whatsoever to the outcome — because the outcome does not depend on his witnessing it.

**Why This Is a Consolation Rather Than a Dismissal**

At first hearing, "you may not live to see it" sounds like cold comfort. It is not, and the reason is worth drawing out.

The anxiety of the reformer, the teacher, the parent, and the activist is always the same: *will I see this work?* And the fear underneath it is that if one does not see it, it did not happen — that the effort was wasted.

The verse severs that link. The result is not contingent on your presence at it. *Ilaynā marjiʿuhum* — their return is to Us — states that the process is running on a timescale and under an authority that your lifespan does not affect.

The Qur'an says the same to the Prophet ﷺ at 40:77 and elsewhere, and it is one of the recurring consolations offered to messengers. Most prophets did not see the fruit of their work. Noah preached for nine hundred and fifty years, as the Qur'an records (29:14), and the sūrah will note at v. 73 that only those with him in the ark were saved. Moses died before entering the land. The Prophet ﷺ was unusual in seeing his mission succeed within his lifetime, and even he died with the Byzantine and Persian frontiers untouched.

**The Ethic of Working Without Seeing**

This produces a distinctive and, on reflection, healthier relationship to one's work.

The Prophet's ﷺ instruction about the palm-shoot, quoted at v. 14, is the paradigm:

> *"If the Hour comes upon one of you while he has a palm-shoot in his hand, let him plant it if he is able to do so before he rises."* (al-Bukhārī, *al-Adab al-Mufrad* 479)

A tree planted at the end of the world will never fruit. The instruction is therefore about the value of the *action* independent of its result — which is precisely this verse's teaching, transposed from prophecy to gardening.

The practical consequence is a release from a specific kind of despair. A person who measures his worth by outcomes he does not control will eventually be crushed, because most outcomes are not controlled by anyone. A person who understands his responsibility as the *doing* rather than the *achieving* can sustain effort indefinitely, including in circumstances that show no sign of improving.

**"Then God Is Witness Over That Which They Do"**

*Thumma Allāhu shahīdun ʿalā mā yafʿalūn.*

The verse closes with the divine witness — the same theme as v. 29's *"God suffices as a Witness"* and v. 61's fuller statement of comprehensive divine observation.

Its function here is to explain why the delay does not endanger the justice. In human courts, delay is dangerous: witnesses die, memories fade, evidence is lost, and the passage of time is the friend of the guilty. The verse states that the divine witness is not subject to any of these degradations. Nothing expires. The record does not decay.

This is why postponement is compatible with perfect justice in the Qur'anic frame, and why the sūrah could say at v. 11 that God *leaves* the wrongdoers in their rebellion without that being an abdication. Delay is only injustice where delay causes loss of evidence. Here it causes none.

**The Verse Applied**

The most immediate application is to anyone waiting for a wrong to be addressed.

Everyone has at least one: an injustice suffered that was never acknowledged, a person who caused serious harm and moved on comfortably, a situation in which the truth was known and no one acted on it. The natural human response is either corrosive resentment or the abandonment of the belief that justice exists.

This verse offers a third option, and it is the one the Prophet ﷺ was given: *the matter is filed, the witness is competent, and the hearing is scheduled.* You may or may not be present at it. That is not the relevant question.

What makes this bearable rather than merely deferred is the clause *ilaynā marjiʿuhum* — the return is *to Us*. Not to a process, not to history's verdict, not to eventual public opinion. To the One who saw it happen.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:47

> **For every community there is a messenger, and when their messenger comes, judgment shall be rendered between them with justice, and they will not be wronged.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**One of the Qur'an's Most Consequential Verses**

*Wa-li-kulli ummatin rasūl.*

The source describes this as one of the clearest Qur'anic verses affirming the universality of revelation as a source of guidance for humanity — a truth it identifies as one of the Qur'an's major themes.

The claim is simple and its implications are enormous: no community has been left without a messenger.

**The Universality of Prophecy in the Qur'an**

The Qur'an states this doctrine repeatedly and in various forms:

> *"And there is no community but that a warner has passed among it."* (Qur'an 35:24)

> *"And We have certainly sent into every community a messenger, [saying]: Worship God and avoid false gods."* (Qur'an 16:36)

> *"And We never sent a messenger save with the tongue of his people, that he might make clear to them."* (Qur'an 14:4)

> *"And messengers We have already told thee about, and messengers We have not told thee about."* (Qur'an 4:164)

That last verse is critically important. The Qur'an names roughly twenty-five prophets, and it explicitly states that this is not the complete list. The tradition transmits reports of very large numbers — one hadith mentions one hundred and twenty-four thousand prophets (Aḥmad 22288, from Abū Dharr), though scholars discussed its chain — and the Qur'an's own statement makes any fixed enumeration impossible.

**What Follows From This Doctrine**

Several conclusions follow, and they shape the Islamic view of religious history profoundly.

*No people has been abandoned.* Every human community, in every region and era, received guidance in its own language, adapted to its own circumstances. Whatever traces of truth exist in the world's religious traditions may reflect that original guidance, altered over time.

*Accountability requires prior notification.* The verse's structure makes judgment consequent upon the messenger's arrival: *"when their messenger comes, judgment shall be rendered."* Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabāṭabāʾī draw the conclusion the source records: people are not taken to task until a messenger from God has offered them the option of following divine guidance. The Qur'an states the principle as an absolute:

> *"And never do We punish until We have sent a messenger."* (Qur'an 17:15)

*Difference in law is expected.* Since each messenger came to a specific community in specific circumstances, the Qur'an anticipates that the detailed laws of different revealed religions will differ, even where the core is identical:

> *"For each of you We have appointed a law and a way. And had God willed, He would have made you one community, but that He may try you in what He has given you. So vie with one another in good deeds."* (Qur'an 5:48)

**The Question of Those Who Never Heard**

This verse bears directly on a question that troubles many people: what of those who lived and died without any exposure to the message?

The Qur'anic framework, built on this verse and 17:15, holds that no one is condemned for failing to accept a message that never reached them. The classical scholars developed the category of *ahl al-fatrah* — people of an interval, those who lived between prophetic missions or beyond their reach — and the majority position, held by al-Ghazzālī among others, is that such people are not held accountable for what they had no access to.

Al-Ghazzālī wrote in *Fayṣal al-Tafriqah* that he hoped God's mercy would encompass the majority of the Christians of Byzantium and the Turks of his day who had never received the message in an accurate form — distinguishing those who heard the truth and rejected it from those who never heard it, or heard only a distortion.

A hadith transmitted in several collections describes four categories being tested on the Day of Judgment — the deaf man who could hear nothing, the senile, the imbecile, and the man who died during an interval between prophets — each saying that no message reached him, and each being given a direct test at that point (Aḥmad 16301, from al-Aswad ibn Sarīʿ; graded sound by scholars of hadith).

The underlying principle in all of this is the one this verse states: judgment follows the messenger, and no one is wronged.

**"With Justice, and They Will Not Be Wronged"**

*Quḍiya baynahum bi-l-qisṭi wa-hum lā yuẓlamūn.*

The phrase *bi-l-qisṭ* recurs from v. 4 — equity, the full allotted portion, from the same root as the balance-scale. And the verse ends with the same assurance as v. 44: *lā yuẓlamūn*, they are not wronged.

The repetition of this assurance throughout the sūrah (vv. 44, 47, 54) is not accidental. The sūrah has been describing severe outcomes — the Fire, the veiled faces, the abasement — and it repeatedly interrupts those descriptions to insist that no one receives more than his due.

**Why the Standard Differs by Community**

There is an important implication in this verse for how one thinks about the moral standing of others, and it is a check on a common form of religious arrogance.

If judgment is rendered *when their messenger comes*, then the standard applied to a person is calibrated to what he received. A person who was given clear guidance and rejected it is not in the same position as one who received a distorted version, or none at all.

And this cuts in an uncomfortable direction for anyone who considers himself well-guided. Greater access means greater accountability. The Muslim who has the Qur'an in his own language, access to scholarship, and a community that supports his practice is being examined by a stricter standard than the person who has none of these.

The Qur'an applies this principle explicitly, and it applies it to the Prophet's own household:

> *"O wives of the Prophet, whoever among you commits a clear indecency, the punishment for her will be doubled twice over. And that is easy for God."* (Qur'an 33:30)

Proximity to guidance raises the standard rather than lowering it. This is the opposite of the way religious communities usually reason about themselves.

**The Verse as a Basis for Humility Toward Others**

The practical ethic that follows is one of the more attractive features of the Islamic framework, and it is worth stating.

A Muslim is required to hold that his religion is true — the sūrah has been unambiguous about that (v. 32). He is *not* required, and not permitted, to hold that everyone outside it is damned, because he does not know what messenger reached them, in what form, or what they were in a position to understand.

The two positions are compatible because they answer different questions. *What is true?* is a question about reality, and it has one answer. *Who will be saved?* is a question about individual accountability before God, and it depends on facts about each person that no observer possesses.

The sūrah has already reserved the second question to God at v. 40 (*"thy Lord knows best the workers of corruption"*), at v. 19 (*"judgment would have been made between them"* — deferred), and at v. 93 (*"Thy Lord will surely judge between them on the Day of Resurrection concerning that wherein they differed"*).

This verse supplies the reason: because the standard is individual, and calibrated to what each community received. It is the most just possible arrangement, and it is also the one that makes it impossible for any human being to run the calculation on anyone else.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:48

> **And they say, "When will this promise come to pass, if you are truthful?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question Asked in Bad Faith**

*Wa-yaqūlūna matā hādhā al-waʿdu in kuntum ṣādiqīn.*

The source is clear about the tone: this verse cites what the disbelievers mockingly asked the Prophet ﷺ concerning the coming of the Hour and the Day of Resurrection, about which he had warned them. They asked mockingly because they expected a prophet to be able to foretell the future, and the Prophet did not have knowledge of when these events would take place.

The same demand recurs across the Qur'an (21:38; 27:71; 34:29; 36:48; 67:25), always in the mouths of deniers, and always with the same rhetorical structure: a demand for a date, framed as a test of sincerity.

**Why the Demand Is Not a Genuine Inquiry**

It is worth being precise about what makes this question dishonest, because on its surface it is reasonable. If someone predicts an event, asking when is a fair question.

But the deniers were not asking in order to evaluate an answer. Three features expose this.

First, they had already announced their conclusion. The same people who said *"When will this promise come?"* also said, of resurrection, *"Shall we, when we have become bones and fragments, really be raised up as a new creation?"* (17:49). The question is not asked by someone weighing evidence.

Second, the demand for a date is calculated to produce an unanswerable position. If a date is given and passes, the claim is refuted. If no date is given, the claim is called vague. There is no answer that could satisfy the questioner, which is the hallmark of a rhetorical rather than an epistemic question.

Third — and most tellingly — knowing the date would change nothing about the substance. The claim being denied is that there *is* a reckoning. Whether it occurs in five years or five thousand is irrelevant to whether it occurs, and even more irrelevant to how one should live in the meantime, since every individual's personal deadline is his own death, which arrives long before the Hour for almost everyone.

**Why the Hour Is Deliberately Concealed**

The Qur'an is emphatic that the timing is withheld from everyone, including the Prophet ﷺ:

> *"They ask thee about the Hour: when will it come to port? Say: Knowledge of it lies only with my Lord. None shall reveal it at its proper time but He."* (Qur'an 7:187)

> *"Truly God — with Him is knowledge of the Hour."* (Qur'an 31:34)

And in the hadith of Gabriel, when the questioner asked about the Hour, the Prophet ﷺ replied:

> *"The one asked about it knows no more than the questioner."* (al-Bukhārī 50; Muslim 8)

The concealment is purposeful, and its purpose is straightforward. A known date would produce exactly the wrong behaviour. People would live as they pleased and reform at the last moment — the pattern this sūrah has already described in the passengers of v. 22 and will describe in Pharaoh at v. 90. Deathbed repentance would become a universal strategy rather than an occasional tragedy.

The Qur'an says as much of the individual death, which is concealed for the same reason:

> *"No soul knows what it will earn tomorrow, and no soul knows in what land it will die."* (Qur'an 31:34)

An unknown deadline is the only kind that produces continuous readiness. Any student who has had an unannounced examination understands the mechanism.

**"If You Are Truthful" — The Phrase Turned Around**

*In kuntum ṣādiqīn.* This is the same phrase used against them ten verses earlier: *"Then bring a sūrah like it ... if you are truthful"* (v. 38).

The symmetry is unlikely to be accidental. They had been issued a test of their sincerity and failed to attempt it. They now issue one of their own, which — as the following verses will show — is not a test at all but an evasion.

**The Modern Form of the Same Question**

The demand for a date has largely disappeared; the underlying manoeuvre has not.

Its contemporary version is the demand that a religious claim be verified by a mechanism appropriate to a different kind of claim: *show me the evidence, in the laboratory, now*. Where the claim concerns an event outside the observable order and located in the future, that demand cannot be met by construction — which is precisely what makes it attractive as a debating position.

This is not to say that religious claims are exempt from evidence. This sūrah has spent forty verses offering evidence of several kinds: the ordered cosmos (vv. 5–6, 31), the character of the messenger (v. 16), the inimitability of the text (v. 38), the record of history (v. 39), and the moral structure of the conscience. What it declines to offer is a demonstration on demand of an event that has not yet happened — and it declines because that is not something any truthful person could offer.

The honest form of the question would be: *what reason is there to think there will be a reckoning at all?* That question the Qur'an answers at length, most directly at v. 4: creation implies the capacity to recreate, and justice requires a venue.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:49

> **Say, "I have no power over what harm or benefit may come to me, save as God wills. For every community there is a term. When their term comes, they shall not delay it by a single hour, nor shall they advance it."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer: A Disclaimer, Then a Principle**

The reply to the mocking demand has two parts. The first is a disclaimer of personal power; the second is a statement about how divine timing works.

*Qul lā amliku li-nafsī ḍarran wa-lā nafʿan illā mā shāʾa Allāh.*

Al-Qurṭubī, al-Thaʿlabī and al-Zamakhsharī explain the point: neither the Prophet nor anyone else possesses the power to bring about God's punishment, or for that matter any benefit from Him. The same disclaimer appears at 7:188.

**"I Have No Power Even Over Myself"**

The construction is worth noticing: *lā amliku li-nafsī* — "I have no power *for myself*."

The Prophet ﷺ does not say "I cannot bring punishment upon you." He says something far more radical: *I have no power over my own harm or benefit.* If he cannot control what happens to himself, the question of his controlling the fate of a nation does not arise.

This is a stunning statement for a religious leader to make publicly, and it is worth pausing on how unusual it is. Founders of movements accumulate claims of power; the claims are the currency of authority. The Qur'an has its Prophet ﷺ systematically divest himself of every such claim:

> *"Say: I have no power to bring benefit or harm to myself, save as God wills. Had I knowledge of the Unseen, I would have amassed much good, and no evil would have touched me. I am only a warner and a bearer of glad tidings to a people who believe."* (Qur'an 7:188)

That verse contains an argument as well as a disclaimer: if I had access to hidden knowledge, my own life would show it. It does not. Look at my circumstances.

**"Save as God Wills" — The Necessary Qualification**

*Illā mā shāʾa Allāh.* The exception preserves the truth of the matter: the Prophet ﷺ did possess remarkable capacities, and miracles occurred through him. But every one of them was granted, not owned.

The distinction is between an *instrument* and a *source*. A lamp gives light; it does not generate it. Remove it from the current and it is a shaped piece of metal and glass. The Qur'an is consistently careful to keep the Prophet ﷺ on the correct side of this distinction, which is why the same sūrah that describes the parting of the sea for Moses (v. 90) attributes it entirely to God's command.

**"For Every Community There Is a Term"**

*Li-kulli ummatin ajal.*

The second half of the verse moves from the personal to the historical, and it introduces one of the Qur'an's most important concepts: *ajal*, the appointed term.

Al-Qurṭubī explains that every community has a specific lifespan known only to God, at the end of which it perishes; alternatively, it may be punished before its earthly life comes to an end. The Qur'an states the principle repeatedly:

> *"And for every community there is a term. When their term comes, they shall not delay it by a single hour, nor shall they advance it."* (Qur'an 7:34 — nearly identical wording)

> *"And no community can advance its term, nor can it delay."* (Qur'an 15:5; 23:43)

**The Precision of the Timing**

*Lā yastaʾkhirūna sāʿatan wa-lā yastaqdimūn.* Not by a single hour, in either direction.

The specification of both directions is significant. It says two things at once.

*The punishment cannot be hastened* — which answers the mockers directly. They wanted it now; it does not come on demand, and their impatience has no effect on the schedule. This picks up v. 11: *"Were God to hasten evil for mankind, even as they would hasten the good, their appointed term would have been concluded."*

*The punishment cannot be delayed either* — which is the more sobering half. The respite they are currently enjoying, and which they take as evidence that nothing is coming, has an expiry that is equally fixed. No amount of power, wealth, or preparation will extend it.

**What Determines a Community's Term?**

The Qur'an does not present the *ajal* as arbitrary. As discussed at v. 13, the mechanism it describes is largely internal:

> *"And when We desire to destroy a town, We command its affluent ones, and they commit iniquity therein; thus the Word comes due against it, and We destroy it utterly."* (Qur'an 17:16)

And the general law:

> *"Truly God does not change the condition of a people until they change what is in themselves."* (Qur'an 13:11)

This last verse is the counterweight to any fatalistic reading of *ajal*. The term is fixed in God's knowledge, but what is fixed includes the choices the community will make. Determinism about the outcome does not eliminate the causal role of the conduct that produces it — as al-Kalbī's gloss on v. 14 explained, God's knowledge does not remove the reality of what occurs.

**The Individual Application**

Everything said about communities applies with equal force to persons. The Qur'an uses the same term:

> *"And it is He Who created you from clay, then decreed a term — and a term is specified with Him."* (Qur'an 6:2)

> *"And when their term comes, they shall not delay it by an hour, nor shall they advance it."* (Qur'an 16:61)

There is a peculiar comfort in this that believers have drawn on for fourteen centuries, and it is worth naming, because it is the psychological benefit of the doctrine.

If the term is fixed, then no amount of caution will extend it and no amount of danger will shorten it. This does not license recklessness — the Prophet ﷺ tied the camel and took precautions, and the tradition treats the taking of means as obligatory. But it removes the *anxiety* that ordinarily accompanies risk.

Khālid ibn al-Walīd, who fought in a hundred battles and died in his bed, is reported to have said as he lay dying:

> *"There is not a hand's breadth on my body but that it bears a wound from a sword or an arrow or a spear — and here I am, dying in my bed as a camel dies. May the eyes of the cowards never sleep."*

The man who exposed himself most had the longest life. That is the practical demonstration of *ajal*.

**Why This Answer Silences the Mockers**

The Makkans' question assumed a model in which the Prophet ﷺ was a magician with a schedule. His answer dismantles the model entirely.

He does not know the time. He could not bring it forward if he did. He has no power over his own harm or benefit, let alone theirs. And the timing is not a function of anyone's request but of a term already fixed — which means their taunt has precisely the same effect on the outcome as their silence would.

This is a complete answer, and it is also a lesson in how to respond to a question designed to trap. The Prophet ﷺ does not accept the framing, does not attempt to bluff, and does not become defensive. He states what he does not know, states what he does, and lets the matter rest with the One who governs it.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:50

> **Say, "Have you considered: if His Punishment should come upon you by night or by day, what part thereof would the guilty seek to hasten?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Turning the Question Back**

Having declined to provide a date, the Prophet ﷺ is now instructed to ask a question of his own — and it is designed to make the mockers hear their own demand.

*Qul a-raʾaytum in atākum ʿadhābuhu bayātan aw nahāran, mādhā yastaʿjilu minhu al-mujrimūn.*

The construction *a-raʾaytum* — literally "have you seen?" — is an Arabic idiom for "consider this," "tell me," "what is your view of this scenario?" It invites the listener to run a thought experiment.

**"By Night or by Day"**

*Bayātan aw nahāran.* The phrase echoes the parable of v. 24, where God's command came upon the flourishing garden *"by night or by day."*

*Bayāt* specifically denotes a night attack — the raid that comes upon a sleeping camp. It is one of the oldest terrors of Arabian life: the tribe that goes to sleep secure and wakes to find the enemy inside the tents.

The pairing of night and day, as at v. 24, forecloses preparation. There is no window in which one is safe.

**"What Part Thereof Would the Guilty Seek to Hasten?"**

The question is the verse's whole force. Once the punishment is imagined as actually present — the raiders in the camp, the disaster arriving — *which portion of it, exactly, are you eager to receive sooner?*

The answer is obviously none. And the question exposes the demand of v. 48 for what it was: not a request but a rhetorical posture that depended entirely on the speaker's confidence that nothing would happen.

This is a technique of argument worth noticing. The mockers had made a demand that was safe only because it was abstract. The reply makes it concrete, and the concreteness destroys it. Anyone who has heard a person casually invite a catastrophe he does not expect ("let it come, then!") knows that the invitation evaporates the moment the catastrophe becomes plausible.

**The Psychology of Distant Consequences**

The verse illuminates something general about how human beings handle future harms, and it is a well-documented feature of our reasoning.

Consequences that are distant, uncertain, or abstract have very little motivational force. Consequences that are near, vivid, and concrete have a great deal. This is why people who fully accept that smoking causes cancer continue to smoke, why saving for retirement is difficult, and why climate projections for 2080 produce less action than a leaking roof.

The Qur'an understands this and works with it rather than against it. Its descriptions of the Hereafter are relentlessly *concrete* — boiling water, shade, fruit, garments, faces, conversations — precisely because abstraction does not move anyone. And here it uses the same technique in reverse: taking an abstract demand and making the demander imagine the concrete reality he has requested.

**A Related Instruction**

The Prophet ﷺ warned against exactly the kind of speech the Makkans were engaging in — inviting harm rhetorically. When he visited a sick man who had become emaciated and asked whether he had been supplicating for something, the man said he had prayed:

> *"O God, whatever You would punish me with in the Hereafter, hasten it for me in this world."*

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Glory be to God! You cannot bear it, or you would not be able to bear it. Why did you not say: 'Our Lord, give us good in this world and good in the Hereafter, and protect us from the punishment of the Fire'?"* (Muslim 2688, from Anas)
