The story is instructive because the man was sincere — he was not mocking. And the Prophet's ﷺ correction was not about his theology but about his capacity: *you cannot bear it*. Human beings systematically overestimate what they can endure when the endurance is hypothetical.

**Why Delay Should Be Received as Mercy**

The verse's implicit argument is that the mockers have misread their own situation entirely. They treat the delay as evidence of the warning's falsity. The verse invites them to consider that it is instead the most valuable thing they possess.

The Qur'an makes this explicit elsewhere:

> *"And if God were to take mankind to task for what they have earned, He would not leave upon its surface a single creature; but He grants them respite until a term appointed."* (Qur'an 35:45)

Every day of delay is a day in which repentance remains available. This sūrah's own climax — the people of Jonah at v. 98 — is the story of a community that used the delay correctly. They had received the warning; the punishment was visibly approaching; and in the interval they turned, and it was lifted.

The interval is the mercy. Asking for it to be shortened is asking for the mercy to be withdrawn — which is precisely what this verse invites the mockers to notice.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:51

> **"Is it then, when it has come to pass, that you will believe in it? Now? And you were seeking to hasten it!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Moment of Too Late**

*A-thumma idhā mā waqaʿa āmantum bih. Āl-āna wa-qad kuntum bihi tastaʿjilūn.*

The verse is constructed as a piece of direct address delivered at the moment of the punishment's arrival, and its tone is one of terrible irony. The single word *āl-āna* — "*Now?*" — carries the whole weight.

**Why Belief at That Point Is Worthless**

The source states the principle plainly: since their challenge in hastening God's punishment is itself evidence of their rejection of the truth, if they "believe" when it comes to pass, it will be of no benefit to them — this would be like a deathbed repentance, much like that of Pharaoh, who, when death was imminent, said *"I am among those who submit"* (v. 90).

The Qur'an establishes this principle in Sūrat al-Nisāʾ with legal precision:

> *"But repentance is not for those who go on doing evil deeds until, when death comes to one of them, he says, 'Truly I repent now,' nor for those who die as disbelievers. For them We have prepared a painful punishment."* (Qur'an 4:18)

And it describes the identical scene in Sūrah Ghāfir:

> *"And when they saw Our punishment, they said, 'We believe in God alone, and we reject that which we used to associate with Him.' But their belief was of no benefit to them once they saw Our punishment — the way of God which has passed away among His servants; and the disbelievers were lost."* (Qur'an 40:84–85)

Note the phrase *sunnat Allāh allatī qad khalat fī ʿibādih* — "the way of God which has passed away among His servants." This is not an ad hoc ruling. It is a standing law.

**Why the Law Is Just Rather Than Harsh**

At first hearing, this seems severe. Why should a genuine change of heart be rejected merely because of its timing?

The answer lies in what faith is *for*, and it is worth working out carefully, because it resolves what would otherwise be a real difficulty.

Faith, in the Qur'anic sense, is *īmān bi-l-ghayb* — belief in the unseen (2:3). Its entire moral significance consists in being a free response to sufficient-but-not-coercive evidence. As discussed at vv. 19 and 20, the deferral of judgment and the withholding of overwhelming signs exist precisely to preserve that freedom.

Once the punishment is visibly present, the situation has changed categorically. The unseen has become seen. There is no longer any room for trust, because there is no longer anything to trust *about*. What remains is not faith but recognition — and recognition of the inescapable is not a moral achievement. Everyone in the burning building believes in fire.

An analogy makes the point. A student who does not study, and who is offered repeated warnings and opportunities, and who then, upon receiving the failing grade, sincerely resolves to have studied, has not thereby passed. His resolve is real. It is simply not the thing that was being assessed. What was being assessed was what he would do during the period when the outcome was still open.

**The Testing Period Has a Boundary**

The Qur'an is explicit that the window closes at two moments, both of them absolute.

The first is the individual's death:

> *"Truly God accepts the repentance of His servant so long as the death-rattle has not reached his throat."* (al-Tirmidhī 3537; Ibn Mājah 4253, from Ibn ʿUmar)

The wording is precise. The window extends remarkably far — a person may repent at the last conscious moment and be accepted — but it does not extend past the point at which death is upon him and unmistakable. The Prophet ﷺ himself accepted the conversion of men in their final illness, but not of the man for whom the reality had already become visible.

The second is the arrival of the universal sign:

> *"Whoever repents before the sun rises from its place of setting, God turns to him in forgiveness."* (Muslim 2703, from Abū Hurayrah)

> *"The Hour will not come until the sun rises from its place of setting. When it rises and the people see it, they will all believe — and that is when no soul's faith will benefit it that had not believed before."* (al-Bukhārī 4636; Muslim 157, citing 6:158)

**The Rebuke Inside the Question**

*Wa-qad kuntum bihi tastaʿjilūn* — "and you were seeking to hasten it!"

The final clause is a reminder delivered at the worst possible moment. It says: *this is what you asked for*.

There is a hard justice in this. They had not merely disbelieved; they had actively demanded the arrival of the thing they disbelieved in, treating the demand as a demonstration of their confidence. The clause simply holds up their own words at the moment of fulfilment.

The Qur'an frequently uses this technique — confronting people at judgment with their own recorded speech:

> *"Read thy book! Sufficient is thy soul this Day as a reckoner against thee."* (Qur'an 17:14)

**The Only Rational Response**

The practical conclusion of vv. 48–51 is simple and it is urgent.

If the window is open now and will close at an unknown moment, and if what is done inside the window is the only thing that counts, then the single most irrational thing a person can do is postpone. And postponement is precisely what almost everyone does, because the window's closing is not visible and the present moment always contains something more pressing.

The Prophet ﷺ gave the practical instruction in the form of a list:

> *"Take advantage of five before five: your youth before your old age, your health before your illness, your wealth before your poverty, your free time before your preoccupation, and your life before your death."* (al-Ḥākim, *al-Mustadrak*, from Ibn ʿAbbās; graded sound)

Every item on that list is a resource that is currently available and that will not be. That is the entire content of this verse, stated positively.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:52

> **Then it shall be said unto the wrongdoers, "Taste the punishment everlasting. Are you recompensed for aught save that which you used to earn?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sentence Pronounced**

*Thumma qīla li-lladhīna ẓalamū dhūqū ʿadhāba al-khuld.*

Ibn Kathīr notes that these words will be spoken on the Day of Judgment, and the commentators are divided as to the speaker: God Himself, or the angels who guard Hell (see 32:14). The wrongdoers addressed are those typified in vv. 49–51 by the Makkan idolaters.

**"Taste" — The Verb Returns**

The imperative *dhūqū* is the same verb encountered at v. 21, where God caused people to *taste* mercy after hardship.

The symmetry is deliberate and it is bitter. They tasted mercy and responded with scheming. Now they taste the other thing.

The verb's precision, noted earlier, applies here too: tasting is immediate, personal, and sensory. It cannot be experienced at a distance or in theory. The punishment described is not a legal status but a felt condition.

**"That Which You Used to Earn"**

*Hal tujzawna illā bi-mā kuntum taksibūn.*

The rhetorical question presents the punishment as a wage. Al-Bayḍāwī glosses *that which you used to earn* as their disobedience of God and disbelief in Him. The same construction appears at 27:90 and, in inverted form, at 83:36.

The vocabulary of earning (*kasaba*) has appeared throughout this sūrah — at vv. 8, 27, and now here — and its cumulative effect is to present the entire moral economy in commercial terms that the audience understood professionally. Every act is a transaction. Every transaction is recorded. The final statement of account is delivered on a fixed date.

And the question form — *"Are you recompensed for aught save...?"* — invites the condemned to identify any discrepancy. There is none. This is the sūrah's repeated insistence, stated at vv. 44, 47, and 54: nothing is added; nothing is unjust.

**The Word *Khuld* and a Significant Scholarly Discussion**

*ʿAdhāb al-khuld* — "the punishment everlasting," or more literally, the punishment of *khuld*.

The source raises an important point of classical scholarship here, and it deserves careful treatment because it concerns one of the most difficult questions in Islamic theology.

Ibn Qayyim al-Jawziyyah (d. 751/1350), the jurist and theologian, argued — among others — that the root *kh-l-d*, from which *khuld* derives, can refer to a long but ultimately finite measure of time. He set out the argument at length in *Shifāʾ al-ʿalīl*. On this reading, the punishment mentioned here and at 32:14 would denote a chastisement of very long but not absolutely infinite duration.

The linguistic basis for the argument is real. In classical Arabic usage, *khallada* is used of things that endure for a very long time without implying literal infinity — the Arabs spoke of a long-lived prisoner as *mukhallad* in his imprisonment. And the Qur'an itself, in describing the Fire, uses a striking qualifier:

> *"Abiding therein so long as the heavens and the earth endure, save as thy Lord wills. Truly thy Lord does whatsoever He wills."* (Qur'an 11:107)

The exception clause — *illā mā shāʾa rabbuk* — has occupied commentators for centuries.

**The Range of Positions**

It is important to represent the tradition accurately here, because this is a contested question.

The majority position among Sunni scholars is that the punishment of those who die in disbelief is unending, on the strength of verses that appear to state it explicitly:

> *"They will wish to come out of the Fire, but they will not come out of it; and theirs is a lasting punishment."* (Qur'an 5:37)

> *"Abiding therein forever (khālidīna fīhā abadan)."* (Qur'an 4:169; 33:65; 72:23)

The addition of *abadan* to *khālidīn* in these verses is generally taken to close off the finite reading.

The minority position, associated with Ibn Taymiyyah and Ibn al-Qayyim and traced by them to reports from ʿUmar, Ibn Masʿūd, Abū Hurayrah and others, holds that the Fire will eventually come to an end, while Paradise endures — since Paradise is explicitly described as *"a gift uninterrupted"* (11:108). Their argument rests partly on the exception clause in 11:107, partly on the linguistic point about *khuld*, and partly on a theological principle: that God's mercy precedes and outweighs His wrath, as the hadith qudsī states:

> *"When God decreed the creation, He wrote in His Book, which is with Him above the Throne: 'Truly My mercy prevails over My wrath.'"* (al-Bukhārī 3194; Muslim 2751, from Abū Hurayrah)

The source also notes that such a reading would accord with the position that punishment in the Hereafter is not eternal in an absolute sense, and directs the reader to 78:23.

**What Is Not in Dispute**

Several things are agreed by all parties, and they are the practically relevant points.

The punishment is real, severe, and to be feared. No scholar in any camp used the finite reading to diminish the warning; Ibn al-Qayyim's own writings on the Fire are among the most vivid in the literature.

Believers who enter the Fire for their sins will certainly be removed from it. This is established beyond dispute by the hadith of the intercession:

> *"Then God will say: The angels have interceded, the prophets have interceded, and the believers have interceded, and none remains but the Most Merciful of the merciful. He will take a handful from the Fire and bring out of it a people who never did any good."* (Muslim 183, from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī)

And no human being knows his own outcome with certainty, which is why the tradition insists on holding fear and hope together.

**Why This Discussion Matters for the Reader**

Some might ask why such a question should be raised at all in a commentary aimed at spiritual benefit.

The answer is that the alternative — pretending the tradition speaks with one voice where it does not — serves no one. A reader who later discovers that serious scholars held a range of positions on this question, and who was told there was only one, has been given reason to doubt everything else he was told.

More importantly, the discussion illustrates something valuable about the Islamic scholarly tradition itself. Ibn al-Qayyim was not a marginal figure; he was among the most rigorous minds the tradition produced, and he advanced a minority reading with full argumentation, and was neither excommunicated nor suppressed. The tradition contained the disagreement.

And the discussion has a spiritual point. What drove Ibn al-Qayyim's inquiry was not laxity but the attempt to think consistently about the God described by the hadith just quoted — the One whose mercy prevails over His wrath. Whichever position one holds, the question is a serious one asked by serious people, and the correct posture toward it is the one the Qur'an itself models at 3:7: to hold what is clear, to acknowledge what is not, and to leave the resolution with God.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:53

> **And they inquire of thee, "Is it true?" Say, "Yea, by my Lord it is true, and you cannot thwart [it]."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Short Verse Containing an Oath**

*Wa-yastanbiʾūnaka a-ḥaqqun huwa. Qul ī wa-rabbī innahu la-ḥaqqun wa-mā antum bi-muʿjizīn.*

Al-Ṭabarī explains the context: the Makkan idolaters asked the Prophet whether the promise of God's chastisement in the Hereafter is true. He is told to reply in the affirmative and to inform them that they cannot thwart it — they will not be able to flee from or prevent God's promised punishment.

**The Verb *Yastanbiʾūnaka***

The verb is form X of *n-b-ʾ*, the root of *nabaʾ* (momentous news) and *nabī* (prophet — one who conveys momentous news). *Yastanbiʾūnaka* means "they seek news from you," "they ask you for information."

The choice of verb has a subtle irony. They are asking the *nabī* for a *nabaʾ*. Whether they intended the question mockingly — as they had at v. 48 — or with a flicker of genuine unease, they were addressing the right office.

**The Oath: One of Three in the Qur'an**

The answer is remarkable for its form. The Prophet ﷺ is instructed to swear.

*Ī wa-rabbī* — "Yes, by my Lord." (*Ī* is an emphatic affirmative particle, roughly "yea," used only before an oath.)

The Qur'an instructs the Prophet ﷺ to swear by his Lord in exactly three places, and each concerns the resurrection:

> *"Those who disbelieve claim that they will never be raised. Say: Yea, by my Lord, you will surely be raised; then you will surely be informed of what you did. And that is easy for God."* (Qur'an 64:7)

> *"And those who disbelieve say, 'The Hour will not come upon us.' Say: Yea, by my Lord, it will surely come upon you — the Knower of the Unseen."* (Qur'an 34:3)

> And our verse.

The consistency is striking. On the specific question of the reality of the Hereafter, the Prophet ﷺ is told not merely to affirm but to *swear*.

**Why an Oath Is Used Here**

Oaths are used in human discourse where evidence has been exhausted and the speaker's own integrity becomes the final warrant. One swears when the matter cannot be demonstrated and one is prepared to stake one's honour on it.

That is exactly the situation. The Hereafter cannot be demonstrated in advance — as established at vv. 20 and 48, its demonstration is precisely what is withheld. What remains is the testimony of the one who received the report, and the credibility of that testimony is what vv. 15–16 established at length: forty years of *al-Amīn*.

There is also a specific weight to swearing by *rabbī*, "my Lord." The Prophet ﷺ is invoking the very authority whose message is in question. To swear falsely by God, in the Qur'anic framework, is among the gravest sins — the *yamīn al-ghamūs*, the "immersing oath," so called because it immerses its swearer in sin and then in the Fire. The Prophet ﷺ listed it among the major sins (al-Bukhārī 6675, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr).

**The Sparing Use of Oaths in Islam**

It is worth noting how carefully the tradition restricts swearing, because that restriction is what gives the oath in this verse its force.

Swearing by anything other than God is forbidden:

> *"Whoever swears by other than God has committed disbelief or ascribed a partner."* (al-Tirmidhī 1535; Abū Dāwūd 3251, from Ibn ʿUmar)

And frequent swearing, even truthfully, is discouraged in commerce:

> *"The oath sells the merchandise but wipes out the blessing."* (al-Bukhārī 2087; Muslim 1606, from Abū Hurayrah)

The Qur'an also warns against making God's name a shield for one's convenience: *"And make not God, by your oaths, a hindrance to your being righteous"* (2:224).

A tradition this careful about oaths does not deploy one lightly. Its use here marks the resurrection as the point on which everything else turns.

**"You Cannot Thwart It"**

*Wa-mā antum bi-muʿjizīn.*

The word *muʿjiz* is from *ʿ-j-z*, to be incapable or weak. To *iʿjāz* someone is to render him incapable — to defeat him, to escape his grasp, to frustrate his purpose. The same root gives *muʿjizah*, the term for a prophetic miracle: something that renders all rivals incapable of matching it (see v. 38).

So the clause means: you cannot render God incapable of carrying this out; you cannot escape.

Al-Ṭabarī's gloss is that they will not be able to flee from or prevent God's promised punishment. The Qur'an makes the point graphically elsewhere:

> *"O company of jinn and men! If you are able to pass beyond the confines of the heavens and the earth, then pass. You will not pass save with an authority."* (Qur'an 55:33)

**The Question Beneath the Question**

There is a psychological reading of this exchange worth considering.

Why did they ask? They had already declared the resurrection false, mocked the delay, and demanded the punishment as a taunt. Asking whether it is true is, strictly, redundant for someone who has settled the matter.

People ask questions they have already answered when the answer has not fully settled. A man does not repeatedly check a lock he is confident about. The persistence of the question — *is it true?* — suggests something the mockery was covering.

This connects to the sūrah's whole psychological argument, developed in vv. 12 and 22: the denial is a surface phenomenon, and beneath it lies a recognition that never entirely goes quiet. The Qur'an says of an earlier people: *"And they rejected them, out of wrongdoing and arrogance, though their souls were convinced of them"* (27:14).

**The Verse's Directness as a Model**

There is something instructive in the *form* of the answer, quite apart from its content.

The Prophet ﷺ is not instructed to hedge, contextualise, or soften. He is instructed to say *yes* — and to swear to it.

There is a temptation, in every age where religion is embattled, to reduce its claims to something less objectionable: to reinterpret the Hereafter as a symbol, the resurrection as a metaphor for moral renewal, judgment as a psychological process. These reinterpretations have the advantage of being unfalsifiable and inoffensive.

This verse forecloses that route. Asked directly whether it is true, the answer is an oath. Whatever else Islam is, it is a set of claims about what is actually the case — and this verse is the point at which the Qur'an refuses to be anything less.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:54

> **Were it so that each soul that had done wrong possessed all that is on the earth, he would surely seek to ransom himself thereby, and they will hide their remorse when they see the punishment. And judgment shall be rendered between them with justice, and they will not be wronged.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Ransom That Cannot Be Paid**

*Wa-law anna li-kulli nafsin ẓalamat mā fī al-arḍi la-ftadat bih.*

Al-Qurṭubī notes that *had done wrong* here refers to disbelief in God and the ascription of partners to Him. The theme of attempted ransom recurs across the Qur'an (3:91; 5:36; 13:18; 39:47; 70:11).

The image is a thought experiment, and it works by placing an infinite-seeming quantity against a demand it cannot meet. Suppose one person owned *everything on earth* — every mine, every harvest, every city, the accumulated wealth of the entire species. Suppose he offered all of it.

It would not be accepted.

**Why the Offer Fails**

Three reasons, and each is worth stating.

*The currency is worthless in that jurisdiction.* Wealth is a claim on the goods and labour of other people, enforced by social convention and law. On a Day when the social order has dissolved and everyone is preoccupied with himself (80:34–37), a claim on other people's labour purchases nothing.

*The offering party owns nothing.* The premise "if he possessed all that is on the earth" is counterfactual in a deeper sense than the verse states. Nothing on earth was ever his; it was held in trust, as v. 14 established. One cannot ransom oneself with the property of the creditor.

*The debt is not of a kind that money settles.* This is the deepest reason. What is owed is not a quantity but a quality — a life lived rightly. There is no exchange rate between assets and conduct.

The Qur'an states the failure of every substitute:

> *"So today no ransom shall be taken from you, nor from those who disbelieved. Your refuge is the Fire; it is your protector, and an evil destination."* (Qur'an 57:15)

> *"And fear a Day when no soul shall avail another in any way, nor shall intercession be accepted from it, nor shall compensation be taken from it, nor shall they be helped."* (Qur'an 2:48)

**How This World Trains Us to Expect Otherwise**

The instinct to ransom is not irrational; it is trained by lifelong experience.

In this world, almost everything can be settled financially. Fines replace imprisonment. Settlements replace trials. Reputations are repaired by public relations. Access is purchased. A sufficiently wealthy person can insulate himself from a large fraction of the consequences that others face — better lawyers, better medicine, better schools for the children, better outcomes at almost every juncture.

Given a lifetime of this training, the reflex described in the verse is entirely predictable. Faced with a catastrophic consequence, the wealthy person's first thought is: *what will this cost?*

The verse describes the moment that reflex meets a system it cannot operate in. And that is the point of raising it now, while the reflex is still forming.

**"They Will Hide Their Remorse"**

*Wa-asarrū al-nadāmata lammā raʾawu al-ʿadhāb.*

This is one of the most psychologically acute details in the Qur'an, and the source notes the parallel at 34:33.

*Nadāmah* is remorse, regret. *Asarrū* means they concealed it, kept it private.

Why conceal it? The commentators offered several explanations. Some read *asarrū* in its secondary sense of "revealed" — an Arabic word that can bear opposite meanings (*min al-aḍdād*). But the primary reading is more interesting: they hid their regret out of shame before their followers, or out of the residual pride that had brought them there in the first place.

Consider what that means. Standing before the punishment, with everything lost, the mechanism that produced the disaster is *still running*. They are still managing appearances. They are still concerned with how they look to the people around them.

This is a devastating observation about the persistence of pride. It suggests that ego does not dissolve on contact with reality; it adapts. And it explains something about why change is so difficult in this life: the faculty that would need to initiate the change is the same one that is invested in not changing.

The concealment does not last. Al-Qurṭubī notes that when they are actually punished in the Fire, they will exclaim:

> *"Our Lord! Our wretchedness overwhelmed us, and we were a people astray."* (Qur'an 23:106)

**Regret That Comes Too Late**

The Day of Judgment is called, in the Qur'an, *yawm al-ḥasrah* — the Day of Regret:

> *"And warn them of the Day of Regret, when the matter will be decided; and they are heedless, and they do not believe."* (Qur'an 19:39)

*Ḥasrah* is a stronger word than *nadāmah*. Its root sense is the stripping away of a covering, and it denotes grief so intense that it exhausts the griever — the regret that consumes.

The Prophet ﷺ described a specific form of it:

> *"No one enters Paradise but that he is shown the place he would have occupied in the Fire had he done evil, so that he may be more grateful; and no one enters the Fire but that he is shown the place he would have occupied in Paradise had he done good, so that it may be a source of regret for him."* (al-Bukhārī 6569, from Abū Hurayrah)

The punishment described there is not fire. It is *being shown the alternative*.

**"Judgment Shall Be Rendered With Justice"**

*Wa-quḍiya baynahum bi-l-qisṭi wa-hum lā yuẓlamūn.*

The verse ends with the identical formula used at v. 47, and the repetition is the sūrah's method of insisting on the point.

Al-Qurṭubī grounds it in God's own declaration: they will be judged with justice because *"God wrongs not His servants"* (3:182; 8:51; 22:10).

The placement of this assurance is significant. It comes immediately after the most emotionally distressing image in the passage — people concealing their remorse before an approaching punishment. The Qur'an interrupts the horror to state, once again, that nothing unjust is occurring.

This is the sūrah's consistent method: severity and justice held together, never one without the other. The Fire is real; nobody is in it who does not belong there; and the standard by which that was determined was, as v. 47 established, calibrated to what each person received.

**The Practical Question**

The verse invites an inventory, and it can be taken now.

What am I relying on to get me out of trouble? For most people the honest answer is a combination of money, connections, competence, and the general expectation that things tend to work out. Each of these is a form of the ransom described here.

None of them is forbidden, and none is useless in this world. The verse's point is narrower and sharper: they have a jurisdiction, and the jurisdiction ends. The only currency accepted beyond it is the one named in v. 26 — *iḥsān*, work done well — and unlike money, it cannot be acquired in a hurry at the end.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:55

> **Truly unto God belongs whatsoever is in the heavens and on the earth. Verily God's Promise is true, but most of them know not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Foundation Beneath the Previous Verse**

*A-lā inna lillāhi mā fī al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍ.*

The verse opens with *a-lā* — an attention-particle, "Behold!", "Take note!" — which the Qur'an uses to introduce statements of particular weight. It will use it again at v. 62 (*"Behold! Truly the friends of God..."*) and v. 66.

The statement it introduces explains why the ransom of v. 54 was impossible: *everything already belongs to God.* There was never anything to offer. The man who proposed to buy his freedom with all that is on the earth was proposing to pay a creditor with the creditor's own property.

**The Doctrine of Divine Ownership**

*Lillāhi mā fī al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍ* is one of the Qur'an's standing formulas (2:284; 3:109, 129; 4:126, 131–132; 10:66; 14:2; 16:52; 20:6; 22:64; 24:64; 42:4, 53; 53:31).

The *lām* of possession in *lillāh* is absolute. It is not the ownership of one party among others; it is the relation of the Creator to everything that exists by His act.

And this is not a doctrine about the next world only. It has immediate consequences for how a person holds what he has, and the Islamic legal tradition built on it directly:

*Zakāt* is calculated as a proportion of accumulated wealth, and the Qur'an describes it not as generosity but as a *right*: *"And in their wealth is a recognised right for the beggar and the deprived"* (70:24–25). One does not "give" zakāt in the sense of transferring one's own property; one delivers what was already assigned.

*Waqf*, the charitable endowment, removes property from private ownership permanently and dedicates its yield — a legal form that only makes sense if ownership was always provisional.

The prohibition of *isrāf* (waste) and the rules governing water, pasture and fire as common goods all follow from the same premise: the holder is a trustee.

**"God's Promise Is True"**

*A-lā inna waʿda Allāhi ḥaqqun.*

The second *a-lā* introduces the second statement, and it is the one the whole passage has been building toward.

The source notes that vv. 53–54 suggest the promise here refers to God's punishment in the Hereafter (see also 46:17), and the connection is clear: the sūrah has been establishing the reality of the reckoning against those who mocked it.

But the word *waʿd* is broader. In the Qur'an it covers both the promise of reward and the promise of consequence, and the sūrah has issued both.

**Why Ownership Guarantees the Promise**

There is a logical link between the verse's two halves that is easy to miss, and it is the reason they appear together.

A promise is only as reliable as the promiser's capacity to deliver. Human promises fail routinely, and they fail for a specific reason: the promiser does not control the conditions. He promises payment and the business fails; he promises protection and is himself overwhelmed; he promises to be there and dies.

The first clause removes that failure mode entirely. If everything in the heavens and the earth belongs to God, then nothing exists that could obstruct the fulfilment of what He has undertaken. There is no external factor, no rival authority, no unforeseen circumstance.

The Qur'an makes this connection explicit:

> *"God does not fail in His promise, but most people do not know."* (Qur'an 30:6)

**"But Most of Them Know Not"**

*Wa-lākinna aktharahum lā yaʿlamūn.*

The source explains: because of their ignorance and their heedlessness of God, most of them know not.

This closing clause appears throughout the Qur'an in slight variations — *lā yaʿlamūn* (know not), *lā yashkurūn* (are not grateful), *lā yaʿqilūn* (do not reason), *lā yuʾminūn* (do not believe) — and it functions as a diagnosis appended to a statement of fact.

Note the structure of the sentence: the truth is stated, and then the fact of its general non-recognition is stated as a separate matter. The Qur'an does not treat majority opinion as evidence about truth. On the contrary, it repeatedly warns against exactly that inference:

> *"And if thou obeyest most of those on earth, they will lead thee astray from God's way. They follow naught but conjecture, and they do but surmise."* (Qur'an 6:116)

That verse's second half is the same phrase this sūrah uses at v. 66. The link between majority opinion and *ẓann*, conjecture, is one the Qur'an makes deliberately: most people hold most of their views by inheritance and social absorption, not by examination, which means that counting heads measures the prevalence of a transmission, not the truth of a claim.

**The Word *Yaʿlamūn* and What Kind of Knowing Is Meant**

The verse says most people do not *know* — and the choice of *ʿilm* rather than *īmān* (belief) is worth noting.

Many of the people addressed would have assented, if asked, to the proposition that God owns everything. What they lacked was not the proposition but the *knowledge* in the operative sense: the recognition that actually governs conduct.

This distinction runs through the whole sūrah. Its complaint is almost never that people lack information. It is that the information is inert — held without being used, professed without being applied. Verse 31 established that they would answer "God" to every question about ultimate causation, and v. 32 asked how, having answered so, they could be turned away.

Real *ʿilm*, in the Qur'anic sense, changes behaviour. The Qur'an's own definition of who possesses it is instructive:

> *"Only those of His servants who have knowledge fear God."* (Qur'an 35:28)

Knowledge is identified by its effect, not by its content. A person who holds accurate propositions about God and lives as though they were false does not, on this definition, know them.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:56

> **He gives life and causes death, and unto Him shall you be returned.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Eight Words Containing the Whole Argument**

*Huwa yuḥyī wa-yumītu wa-ilayhi turjaʿūn.*

The Arabic is even shorter than the English: three clauses, seven words. And it compresses the entire framework the sūrah has been constructing — origin, term, and return.

The source notes the plain sense: life and death are entirely in God's hands (see 2:258; 3:156; 7:158; 9:116), and we will all meet God when we die.

**"He Gives Life" — The Unexplained Event**

*Yuḥyī.* The verb is in the imperfect, denoting continuous action: He *is giving* life, now, continually.

Life remains the one phenomenon that has resisted every attempt at reduction. We can describe its chemistry exhaustively — the replication of nucleic acids, the folding of proteins, the maintenance of gradients across membranes — and we can manipulate it with increasing precision. What has never been done is to make it. Every living cell that has ever been observed descended from a prior living cell. The gap between organic chemistry and a living organism has not been crossed in a laboratory, and the more that is known about the complexity involved, the wider the gap appears.

The Qur'an's argument here does not depend on that gap remaining permanent; it is not a "god of the gaps" argument. Its point is more basic: whatever the mechanism, the mechanism itself requires explanation. A process that reliably produces life from non-life would itself be a designed process, and the question of who arranged the arrangement would remain.

**"And Causes Death" — The One Certainty**

*Wa-yumīt.* The Qur'an consistently attributes death to God as an act, not as a failure or an absence:

> *"Blessed is He in Whose Hand is sovereignty, and He has power over all things — Who created death and life to test you as to which of you is best in deed."* (Qur'an 67:1–2)

Note the order in that verse: death is mentioned *before* life, and both are described as *created*. Death is not the breaking of the machine; it is a designed transition, and the Qur'an treats it as a positive act with a purpose.

The Qur'an's word for the act, as noted at v. 46, is *tawaffā* — to take in full. The soul is collected, complete, by an appointed agent:

> *"Say: The angel of death, who has been given charge of you, will take you; then unto your Lord you will be returned."* (Qur'an 32:11)

That verse's ending is identical to ours.

**Why Life and Death Are Named Together**

The pairing is not decorative. It states a symmetry that human beings systematically refuse to hold.

We treat life as normal and death as an interruption — as though existence were the default condition and its ending an anomaly requiring explanation. The verse reverses the framing. Both are acts. Neither is a default. The same authority that issues the one issues the other, and the interval between them is a loan with a term (*ajal*, v. 49).

This reframing has an immediate practical effect. A person who regards his continued existence as a given experiences each day as unremarkable and each threat to it as an outrage. A person who regards each day as issued experiences the same days as gifts and their eventual cessation as the fulfilment of the terms. The facts are identical; the experience is entirely different.

**"Unto Him Shall You Be Returned"**

*Wa-ilayhi turjaʿūn.* The passive voice — *you shall be returned* — is worth noticing. Not "you will return," as though it were a journey one undertakes, but "you will be returned," as something done to you.

The formula recurs throughout the sūrah (vv. 4, 23, 30, 45, 46) and throughout the Qur'an. It is the counterpart of the great verse of consolation that Muslims recite at every death:

> *"Truly we belong to God, and truly unto Him we return."* (Qur'an 2:156)

The first half of that verse is the ground of the second. Return presupposes belonging. One is returned to where one is from.

**Rūzbihān al-Baqlī's Spiritual Reading**

The source preserves a striking interpretation from the Sufi commentator Rūzbihān al-Baqlī, who reads the verse as describing how God deals with two spiritual types — the gnostics (those given to knowing Him directly and experientially) and the ascetics (those who approach Him by detachment from the world):

> *"He enlivens the hearts of the gnostics through their knowing and witnessing Him, and slays the egos of the ascetics through the lights of their awe at and vigilance toward Him. The Return of the gnostics is to the witnessing of His Beauty and Majesty, whereas the Return of the ascetics is to His Bounty and Blessing."*

This reading takes *yuḥyī wa-yumīt* as describing an inner process: a giving of life to the heart and a putting to death of the ego. The two are complementary rather than opposed — the same operation viewed from two sides, since what kills the ego is precisely what enlivens the heart.

Such readings do not displace the literal meaning; the classical tradition was clear that spiritual interpretation (*ishārah*) is legitimate only when it does not deny the plain sense. But they draw out an implication the plain sense contains. The Qur'an itself uses life and death as metaphors for spiritual states:

> *"Is one who was dead, and We gave him life and made for him a light by which to walk among the people, like one whose likeness is in darkness from which he cannot emerge?"* (Qur'an 6:122)

**The Verse as a Response to the Preceding Passage**

Placed where it is — after the failed ransom of v. 54 and the affirmation of divine ownership at v. 55 — this verse functions as the closing argument of that whole movement.

The man who would have offered all the earth to escape could not, because it was not his. And behind that, more fundamentally: *he* was not his. His life was given, his death is administered, and his return is not optional. The entity attempting to negotiate was itself the property under discussion.

**The Practical Weight of Remembering Death**

The Prophet ﷺ made the remembrance of death a recommended practice, and it is worth understanding why a religion that celebrates life would do so.

> *"Remember often the destroyer of pleasures — death."* (al-Tirmidhī 2307; Ibn Mājah 4258, from Abū Hurayrah)

He also permitted, and eventually encouraged, the visiting of graves for exactly this purpose:

> *"I used to forbid you from visiting graves; now visit them, for they remind you of the Hereafter."* (Muslim 976, from Buraydah)

The function is calibration. Almost every distortion in human judgment comes from a failure to price time correctly — the postponement of what matters, the pursuit of what will not last, the accumulation of what cannot be taken. Remembering death is the corrective, and it works not by producing gloom but by restoring proportion.

The evidence that it produces something other than gloom is in the character of those who practised it most. The generation that remembered death most vividly was also the one that crossed continents, built institutions, and produced a literature of extraordinary vitality. Fear of death paralyses; *remembrance* of death, in the Islamic sense, clarifies.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:57

> **O mankind! There has come unto you an exhortation from your Lord, and a cure for that which lies within breasts, and a guidance and a mercy for the believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Tone Changes**

After a long stretch of argument, warning, and courtroom scenes, the sūrah turns. The address is *yā ayyuhā al-nās* — O mankind — and what follows is not a threat but an offer.

Four names are given to the revelation in a single verse: *mawʿiẓah* (exhortation), *shifāʾ* (cure), *hudā* (guidance), and *raḥmah* (mercy). Each names a different function, and together they describe a complete therapeutic sequence.

**"O Mankind" — The Widest Address**

The vocative *yā ayyuhā al-nās* appears four times in this sūrah (vv. 23, 57, 104, 108) and is the Qur'an's most inclusive form of address. It is contrasted with *yā ayyuhā alladhīna āmanū* ("O you who believe"), which addresses the community, and with the addresses to specific peoples.

Its use here means the offer is universal. The cure is not being made available to a group; it is being announced to the species.

**First: *Mawʿiẓah*, Exhortation**

Ājībah, al-Kalbī and al-Ṭabāṭabāʾī note that *an exhortation* refers to the Qur'an.

*Waʿẓ* is from *w-ʿ-ẓ*, and it denotes admonition that softens the heart — counsel delivered in a way that moves rather than merely informs. The Arabs distinguished it from *taʿlīm* (instruction): a teacher informs the mind, a *wāʿiẓ* stirs the will.

The Qur'an's own method illustrates the difference. It rarely presents doctrine as a list. It narrates, it questions, it draws pictures, it swears oaths, it shifts abruptly between registers. This is not disorganisation; it is the method appropriate to *waʿẓ*. A person is not usually changed by an argument he cannot refute; he is changed by something that reaches him.

**Second: *Shifāʾ*, a Cure for What Is in the Breasts**

*Wa-shifāʾun li-mā fī al-ṣudūr.*

This is the verse's central image, and the commentators expand it precisely. Ājībah, al-Kāshānī and al-Kalbī explain that the Qur'an is a cure for every kind of spiritual sickness that plagues the heart — such as ignorance, doubt, hypocrisy, and hatred.

The same claim is made elsewhere:

> *"And We send down of the Qur'an that which is a healing and a mercy for the believers; but it increases the wrongdoers in naught but loss."* (Qur'an 17:82)

> *"Say: For those who believe, it is a guidance and a healing."* (Qur'an 41:44)

**The Diagnostic Frame: Sin as Illness**

The medical metaphor is worth taking seriously, because it shapes the whole Islamic approach to moral failure.

To call wrongdoing a *disease* rather than merely a crime changes several things.

It implies that the sufferer is, in an important sense, harmed rather than merely guilty — which is exactly what v. 44 asserted: *"human beings wrong themselves."*

It implies that treatment is possible and that the appropriate response to a sick person is therapy rather than contempt. This is why the Prophet ﷺ, when a man was brought to him repeatedly for drinking and someone cursed him, said:

> *"Do not curse him. By God, I know nothing of him but that he loves God and His Messenger."* (al-Bukhārī 6780, from ʿUmar)

And it implies that the patient's own cooperation is required. No medicine works on a patient who will not take it, which is why the same Qur'an that heals believers *"increases the wrongdoers in naught but loss"* (17:82). The identical text produces opposite effects depending on the receiver — as v. 5 already established with its varying qualifiers.

**"That Which Lies Within Breasts" — The Interior as the Site of Illness**

*Mā fī al-ṣudūr.* The Qur'an locates the problem inside.

This is a significant claim. Most systems of reform address behaviour: they legislate, incentivise, punish, and monitor. Islam does all of these too — it has a detailed legal system. But this verse identifies the primary site of disease as the interior, and the Prophet ﷺ made the priority explicit:

> *"Truly in the body there is a piece of flesh: if it is sound, the whole body is sound; and if it is corrupt, the whole body is corrupt. Truly it is the heart."* (al-Bukhārī 52; Muslim 1599, from al-Nuʿmān ibn Bashīr)

The logic is straightforward. External regulation can only reach observable conduct, and most human conduct is unobserved. A system that relies on enforcement therefore fails precisely where it matters most — in private, where character is actually located. The only mechanism that operates in private is an internalised one, which is why the Qur'an works on the *ṣadr*.

**Third and Fourth: Guidance and Mercy "For the Believers"**

*Wa-hudan wa-raḥmatun li-l-muʾminīn.*

Note the shift in the addressee. The first two descriptions — exhortation and cure — are offered to *mankind*. The last two are specified *for the believers*.

The distinction is not arbitrary; it reflects the sequence of a treatment. Exhortation is addressed to anyone who will listen. The cure is available to anyone who will take it. But *guidance* — being actively conducted along a path — and *mercy* as an experienced condition require that the patient have accepted the treatment. One cannot be led by a map one has not opened.

This is the same structure as v. 25: God *calls* everyone to the Abode of Peace, and *guides* whom He wills. The invitation is universal; the conducting begins when the invitation is accepted.

**How the Cure Actually Operates**

It is worth being concrete about the mechanism, because "the Qur'an heals hearts" can be heard as a pious generality.

*It supplies an accurate diagnosis.* Much psychological suffering is worsened by misidentification. The Qur'an names conditions precisely: heedlessness (v. 7), false security, the beautification of one's own deeds (v. 12), conjecture mistaken for knowledge (v. 36), pride that persists even in disaster (v. 54). To have one's condition correctly named is itself relieving.

*It reframes suffering as meaningful.* The single most damaging feature of pain is its apparent pointlessness. The Qur'an's framework — trial, expiation, elevation of rank — does not remove pain but removes its meaninglessness, which is what makes it unbearable.

*It relocates the source of worth.* Most chronic anxiety in modern life derives from a self-worth staked on things that can be lost: status, achievement, appearance, the opinions of others. The Qur'an relocates worth to *taqwā* (49:13), which no one can take from a person.

*It prescribes practices, not only beliefs.* Prayer imposes structure on the day; fasting breaks the tyranny of appetite; charity loosens attachment; remembrance interrupts rumination. The Qur'an is explicit that the practice is the mechanism:

> *"Those who believe and whose hearts find rest in the remembrance of God — truly in the remembrance of God do hearts find rest."* (Qur'an 13:28)

**The Cure Must Be Taken**

A final observation, and it is the most practical one.

A medicine kept in the cabinet does nothing. The Qur'an is the most widely printed, most frequently recited, and most beautifully produced book in the world, and much of that engagement does not involve consulting it as a physician's prescription.

The Prophet ﷺ complained, in the words the Qur'an gives him for the Day of Judgment:

> *"And the Messenger will say: O my Lord, truly my people have taken this Qur'an as something abandoned (mahjūran)."* (Qur'an 25:30)

The commentators noted that *hajr*, abandonment, has degrees: not reciting it, reciting without understanding, understanding without acting, and acting without seeking judgment from it in one's disputes. A community can be extremely active around a book and still, in the sense that matters, have abandoned it.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:58

> **Say, "In the Bounty of God and His Mercy—in that let them rejoice! It is better than that which they amass."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Command to Rejoice**

*Qul bi-faḍli Allāhi wa-bi-raḥmatihi fa-bi-dhālika fa-l-yafraḥū.*

This is one of the few places in the Qur'an where joy is commanded in the imperative. The verb *fariḥa* appears with a *lām* of command: *fa-l-yafraḥū*, "let them rejoice."

The choice of verb is notable, because *faraḥ* has generally been a negative word in this sūrah and in the Qur'an at large. At v. 22, the passengers *rejoiced* in the favourable wind just before the storm. Elsewhere, Qārūn's people are told: *"Do not exult (lā tafraḥ); truly God loves not the exultant"* (28:76).

The word is therefore being deliberately reclaimed. Exultation is not wrong in itself; it is wrong when its object is wrong. Redirect the object and the same emotion becomes a duty.

**What Is the Bounty and What Is the Mercy?**

The commentators identify them. Mujāhid, al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī hold that *God's Bounty* is the Qur'an and *His Mercy* is Islam. Sahl al-Tustarī takes the Mercy to be Muhammad ﷺ, who was sent as *"a mercy unto the worlds"* (21:107).

Each identification is well grounded. The Qur'an calls itself a mercy (v. 57 of this sūrah), and it calls the Prophet ﷺ a mercy (21:107). The religion as a whole is described as God's completed favour:

> *"Today I have perfected for you your religion, and completed My favour upon you, and approved for you Islam as religion."* (Qur'an 5:3)

**"Better Than That Which They Amass"**

*Huwa khayrun mimmā yajmaʿūn.*

The verb *jamaʿa* means to gather, collect, accumulate. It is the word for hoarding, and the Qur'an uses it pointedly of those whose lives are organised around accumulation:

> *"Woe unto every backbiting slanderer, who amasses wealth and counts it over, supposing that his wealth will make him immortal."* (Qur'an 104:1–3)

That final clause is a precise piece of psychology: *yaḥsabu anna mālahu akhladah* — he supposes his wealth has made him eternal. The accumulator is not merely greedy; he is engaged in an unconscious project of self-preservation, using assets as a hedge against mortality.

**A Comparison Worth Making Carefully**

The verse invites a comparison between two categories of good, and it is worth conducting the comparison rather than merely asserting the result.

*What can be amassed:* wealth, property, status, credentials, followers, security. These are real goods; the Qur'an does not deny it. But they share a set of properties. They are finite, so their pursuit is competitive: what I gain, someone else does not. They are external, so they can be taken by force, accident, or policy. They are subject to habituation — the pleasure of any acquisition decays and requires escalation. And they are non-transferable across death, as v. 54 established at length.

*What is given as bounty and mercy:* guidance, forgiveness, the relationship with God, the peace described at v. 25. These have the opposite properties. They are not finite, so their pursuit is not competitive — one person's guidance costs no one else anything. They are internal, so they cannot be confiscated. They do not habituate in the same way; the tradition's testimony is that the sweetness of faith deepens rather than dulls. And they are precisely what survives the transition.

The verse's claim, then, is not sentimental. It is a comparison on the merits, and it can be checked.

**Why the Command Is Needed**

If the comparison is that clear, why does joy have to be *commanded*?

Because emotion does not automatically follow assessment. People routinely feel more delight at a financial windfall than at a spiritual one, even when they sincerely hold that the second is more valuable. The gap between what we believe and what we *feel* is the space in which this command operates.

The Qur'an is instructing a discipline: direct the emotion deliberately toward what actually merits it. This is not the manufacture of false feeling; it is the training of attention. Joy follows attention, and attention can be directed.

**The Practical Discipline of Gratitude**

The tradition supplies concrete methods for this training, and they are worth naming because the command is otherwise abstract.

The Prophet ﷺ taught a technique of comparison that inverts the usual direction:

> *"Look at those below you and do not look at those above you, for that is more likely to keep you from belittling God's favour upon you."* (al-Bukhārī 6490; Muslim 2963, from Abū Hurayrah)

The instruction is precisely calibrated to the psychology of comparison. Human beings assess their condition relatively, not absolutely, which is why increases in absolute wealth produce almost no lasting increase in satisfaction. The Prophet's ﷺ instruction does not deny the mechanism; it redirects it.

He also identified the specific blessings that go unregistered:

> *"Whoever among you wakes up secure in his home, healthy in his body, with his food for the day — it is as though the whole world has been gathered for him."* (al-Tirmidhī 2346, from ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Miḥṣan)

And he named the two most commonly wasted:

> *"Two blessings in which many people are deceived: health and free time."* (al-Bukhārī 6412, from Ibn ʿAbbās)

**Joy as a Feature of the Religion**

There is a corrective in this verse worth stating plainly, because religious communities frequently need it.

Islam is not a religion of gloom. Its command here is to *rejoice*, and the Prophet ﷺ smiled frequently — Jarīr ibn ʿAbd Allāh reported: *"The Messenger of God did not screen himself from me since I accepted Islam, nor did he see me but that he smiled"* (al-Bukhārī 3035). ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Ḥārith said: *"I have not seen anyone who smiled more than the Messenger of God"* (al-Tirmidhī 3641).

A person who has been given guidance, forgiveness, and the promise of the Abode of Peace, and who walks around resembling someone who has suffered a bereavement, has misunderstood something. The appropriate response to the offer described in v. 57 is the one commanded in v. 58.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:59

> **Say, "Have you seen that which God sent down unto you for provision? Then you made some of it forbidden and some lawful." Say, "Has God granted you leave, or do you fabricate against God?"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Specific Practice Being Challenged**

The source directs the reader to the commentary on 6:136–139 for the context: this verse concerns the Makkan idolaters' arbitrary ritualistic practices.

The Qur'an describes those practices at length in Sūrat al-Anʿām. The Arabs had developed an elaborate system of dietary and sacrificial prohibitions with no revealed basis: certain animals were dedicated to deities and forbidden to women but permitted to men; certain camels — the *baḥīrah*, *sāʾibah*, *waṣīlah*, and *ḥāmī* mentioned at 5:103 — were set loose and declared untouchable after producing a certain number of offspring; portions of crops were assigned to the idols and portions to God, with the accounting arranged so the idols never lost.

> *"And they say: These cattle and crops are forbidden; none may eat them save whom we will — so they claim. And there are cattle whose backs are forbidden, and cattle over which they do not pronounce the name of God — a fabrication against Him. He will recompense them for what they used to fabricate."* (Qur'an 6:138)

**"Sent Down Unto You for Provision"**

*Mā anzala Allāhu lakum min rizq.*

Al-Qurṭubī notes that *sent down* here conveys the idea of being created, as at 39:6, which says that God *"sent down for you of cattle eight pairs."* Cattle are not literally lowered from the sky; the expression indicates provision issuing from God's decree.

The phrase establishes the premise on which the whole argument rests. The provision is *His*, and He *gave it to you*. Two facts, and the second follows from the first: only the owner can set the terms of use.

**The Argument: Who Has Legislative Authority?**

*Qul ā-Allāhu adhina lakum am ʿalā Allāhi taftarūn.*

The question is a clean dilemma, and it admits only two answers. Either God authorised these rules, or you invented them and attributed them to Him. There is no third possibility, because a religious prohibition just *is* a claim about what God requires.

And the first horn is unavailable: no one claimed a revelation establishing these customs. They were inherited practice. Which leaves *iftirāʾ* — fabrication against God, the offence identified at v. 17 as the greatest of wrongs.

**The Principle of Original Permissibility**

This verse is one of the foundations of a major principle in Islamic jurisprudence: *al-aṣl fī al-ashyāʾ al-ibāḥah* — the default status of things is permissibility.

The reasoning is exactly the reasoning of this verse. God created things for human use; therefore they are available unless He has said otherwise. Prohibition requires evidence; permission does not.

The Qur'an makes the point repeatedly:

> *"He it is Who created for you all that is on the earth."* (Qur'an 2:29)

> *"Say: Who has forbidden the adornment of God, which He has brought forth for His servants, and the good things of provision?"* (Qur'an 7:32)

> *"O you who believe, do not forbid the good things which God has made lawful for you, and do not transgress. Truly God loves not transgressors."* (Qur'an 5:87)

That last verse was revealed, according to the reports, when some Companions resolved to renounce marriage, sleep, and meat in pursuit of asceticism. The Prophet ﷺ responded:

> *"I fast and I break my fast; I pray and I sleep; and I marry women. Whoever turns away from my way is not of me."* (al-Bukhārī 5063; Muslim 1401, from Anas)

**Adding to the Religion as a Form of Fabrication**

The offence this verse names is usually understood as prohibition, but the principle cuts both ways: fabrication includes anything attributed to God that He did not say — additional prohibitions, additional obligations, additional rituals.

The Qur'an issues a general warning that is among its most severe:

> *"And do not say, concerning what your tongues falsely describe, 'This is lawful and this is forbidden,' so as to fabricate a lie against God. Truly those who fabricate lies against God will not prosper."* (Qur'an 16:116)

Notice that the verse condemns *both* directions — declaring lawful and declaring forbidden — as equally fabrication.

**Why Excessive Prohibition Is Spiritually Dangerous**

There is a psychology behind this that deserves attention, because inventing prohibitions feels like piety and is therefore hard to resist.

Adding restrictions produces a sense of seriousness. It creates visible markers of commitment. It generates authority for whoever defines the restrictions. And it substitutes something measurable and controllable — dietary rules, dress codes, ritual detail — for the far harder work the Qur'an actually demands: the purification of the heart described at v. 57, honesty in dealings, patience under trial, justice toward those one has power over.

This is precisely the substitution the Qur'an condemns in earlier communities:

> *"And monasticism they invented — We did not prescribe it for them — only seeking God's pleasure; yet they did not observe it with due observance."* (Qur'an 57:27)

The parenthesis is doubly devastating: they invented it, *and* they did not even keep it.

The Prophet ﷺ warned repeatedly against the tendency:

> *"Beware of extremism in religion, for those before you were destroyed only by extremism in religion."* (al-Nasāʾī 3057; Ibn Mājah 3029, from Ibn ʿAbbās)

> *"Religion is ease, and no one makes the religion difficult except that it overwhelms him."* (al-Bukhārī 39, from Abū Hurayrah)

And he expressed relief at what was left unspecified:

> *"God has laid down obligations, so do not neglect them; He has set limits, so do not transgress them; He has forbidden things, so do not violate them; and He has remained silent about things, as a mercy to you and not out of forgetfulness — so do not inquire into them."* (al-Dāraquṭnī, from Abū Thaʿlabah al-Khushanī)

The silence is described as *mercy*. The space the law leaves open is not an oversight to be filled by the zealous.

**The Balance the Verse Requires**

It would be an error to read this verse as licence. Islam has extensive prohibitions, and they are binding. The verse does not attack prohibition; it attacks *unauthorised* prohibition.

The distinction is between the two questions: *what has God forbidden?* — a question of evidence, answered by scholarship — and *what do I feel ought to be forbidden?* — a question of temperament, which has no authority whatsoever.

The tradition's discipline on this point was strict. A jurist issuing a ruling was expected to trace it to a source, and the absence of a source was itself decisive: *lā dalīl*, no evidence, meant no prohibition. This is why the reluctance of the early scholars to give rulings, discussed at v. 17, was not diffidence but precision. They knew what this verse says about the alternative.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:60

> **What will those who fabricate lies against God conjecture on the Day of Resurrection? Truly God is Possessed of Bounty for mankind, but most of them do not give thanks.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question With No Answer Prepared**

*Wa-mā ẓannu alladhīna yaftarūna ʿalā Allāhi al-kadhiba yawma al-qiyāmah.*

The verse asks the fabricators of v. 59 a question about the future: *what do you suppose will happen?*

The verb is *ẓann* — the sūrah's word for conjecture, examined at v. 36. Its use here is pointed. These are people who have built a religion on supposition; the verse asks them to apply the same faculty to the one event on which supposition will be tested.

The question is unanswerable, and that is its function. Nobody who invents religious rules has a worked-out account of how the invention will be defended when its Author is present. The invention proceeded on the assumption that the question would never be put.

**Two Ways to Read the Question**

Commentators read the question in two complementary ways.

*What do they suppose will be done to them?* — a warning: have they considered the consequence?

*What do they suppose the Day will be like?* — an exposure: they have not thought about it at all, which is precisely how they were able to act as they did.

The second reading is the more penetrating. Fabrication of the kind described in v. 59 is only psychologically possible for someone who has stopped taking the Day of Judgment seriously as an actual event. The behaviour is itself evidence of the mental state.

**"God Is Possessed of Bounty for Mankind"**

*Inna Allāha la-dhū faḍlin ʿalā al-nāsi wa-lākinna aktharahum lā yashkurūn.*

The second half of the verse turns from warning to a statement of divine generosity, and the juxtaposition is deliberate. The people just described had *restricted* what God had given — declaring forbidden what He had made lawful. The verse answers by describing His actual disposition toward them: *faḍl*, bounty, overflow, giving beyond what is due.

The commentators expand what the bounty consists of. Ājībah, al-Ālūsī, Ibn Ḥayyān and al-Rāzī explain that God gives people numerous blessings such as intelligence and sends them messengers who come with divinely revealed books; but most do not give thanks — that is, most do not employ their intellects to ponder over God's signs, follow the call of His messengers, or listen to and benefit from His revealed books.

**The Meaning of *Faḍl***

*Faḍl* is from *f-ḍ-l*, to exceed, to be in surplus. *Faḍlah* is what remains over after a need is met. So *faḍl* is not the meeting of an obligation but the giving of a surplus — generosity that exceeds any claim.

This is theologically important. God owes His creatures nothing; He is not under obligation, since obligation implies a prior claim, and nothing has a claim on the One who gives it existence. Everything received is therefore *faḍl*.

And yet the Qur'an describes God as having *imposed* mercy upon Himself:

> *"Your Lord has prescribed mercy upon Himself."* (Qur'an 6:54)

The verb is *kataba ʿalā nafsihi* — He wrote it upon Himself. An obligation freely assumed, binding no one but by His own decree.

**Ingratitude as the Default Condition**

*Wa-lākinna aktharahum lā yashkurūn* — "but most of them do not give thanks."

The Qur'an says this repeatedly (2:243; 12:38; 27:73; 40:61), and the consistency of the observation is itself worth noting. Ingratitude is presented not as an occasional failing of bad people but as the *ordinary human condition*.

Iblīs is even given a prediction to this effect:

> *"Then I shall come upon them from before them and from behind them, and from their right and from their left; and Thou wilt not find most of them thankful."* (Qur'an 7:17)

And the Qur'an, describing those who escape it, uses a striking phrase:

> *"And few of My servants are thankful."* (Qur'an 34:13)

**Why Gratitude Is So Difficult**

It is worth understanding the mechanism, because the difficulty is structural rather than moral.

*Habituation.* Human perception is built to register *change*, not steady states. A blessing that is continuous becomes invisible — this is not laziness but neurology. One notices the heating when it fails, never while it works.

*Comparison upward.* As noted at v. 58, satisfaction is assessed relatively, and the reference group is almost always those who have more.

*Attribution to self.* Success is readily explained by one's own effort and judgment; only failure requires external explanation. This is the mechanism of v. 23's transgression after rescue.

*The invisibility of the counterfactual.* One cannot perceive the harms that did not occur. The illness not contracted, the accident avoided, the disaster that passed elsewhere — these generate no gratitude because they generate no experience.

**What *Shukr* Actually Requires**

The Qur'anic concept of thankfulness is more demanding than the English word suggests, and the tradition specified it precisely.

The classical formulation is that *shukr* has three components: acknowledgement in the heart that the blessing is from God; expression of it on the tongue; and *use of the blessing in obedience to the Giver*.

The third is the substance. To be grateful for wealth is to spend it lawfully and give from it. To be grateful for health is to employ the body in what pleases God. To be grateful for knowledge is to teach it and act on it. To be grateful for eyesight is to guard what it looks at.

On this understanding, the ingratitude the verse describes is not a failure of feeling but a failure of *use*. A person who has received an intellect capable of examining the evidence and does not use it is ungrateful in exactly the sense the commentators specify — and this is why al-Rāzī's expansion, quoted above, defines ingratitude as failing to employ the intellect to ponder God's signs.

**The Promise Attached to Gratitude**

The Qur'an attaches an explicit promise to *shukr*, and it is one of the few places where a return is guaranteed in the strongest terms:

> *"And when your Lord proclaimed: If you are thankful, I shall surely increase you; but if you are ungrateful, truly My punishment is severe."* (Qur'an 14:7)

Note the asymmetry in the sentence. The consequence of gratitude is stated definitely: *la-azīdannakum*, "I shall surely increase you." The consequence of ingratitude is stated as a general fact about God's punishment rather than as a threat of immediate application — a small mercy embedded in the grammar.

The mechanism of the increase is not mysterious. A grateful person perceives more of what he has, which functionally *is* an increase; he uses what he has well, which tends to produce more; and he is the sort of person to whom others and God give further.

**Practical Steps Toward the Discipline**

The tradition treated gratitude as a trainable habit rather than a spontaneous feeling, and prescribed accordingly.

The Prophet ﷺ taught a supplication to be said each morning and evening:

> *"O God, whatever blessing has come to me or to any of Your creation this morning is from You alone, without partner. To You belongs praise and to You belongs thanks."* (Abū Dāwūd 5073, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ghannām)

He instructed ordinary attention to what is ordinarily overlooked, in the hadith of the man secure, healthy, and fed, quoted at v. 58.

And he took his own advice to an extreme that ʿĀʾishah found puzzling — standing in prayer until his feet swelled, and answering her question with: *"Should I not be a grateful servant?"* (al-Bukhārī 4837; Muslim 2820).

That answer contains the whole doctrine. Gratitude is not an emotion he was reporting. It is a thing he was *doing*, at cost, deliberately, in the middle of the night.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:61

> **Thou art not upon any task, nor dost thou recite any part of the Quran, nor do you perform any deed, save that We are a Witness over you when you engage therein. Not a mote's weight evades thy Lord on earth or in Heaven, nor smaller than that nor larger, but that it is in a clear Book.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Most Comprehensive Statement of Divine Awareness in the Sūrah**

This verse expands the theme of divine witness (vv. 29, 46) into its fullest form, and it does so by an accumulation of clauses that leaves no category unaccounted for.

*Wa-mā takūnu fī shaʾnin wa-mā tatlū minhu min qurʾānin wa-lā taʿmalūna min ʿamalin illā kunnā ʿalaykum shuhūdan idh tufīḍūna fīh.*

**The Three Categories Named**

*Shaʾn* — a matter, affair, concern, state. This is the broadest possible term: any situation one is *in*. Not an action performed but a condition occupied.

*Tilāwat al-Qurʾān* — recitation. The source notes al-Kalbī's gloss that *any part of the Quran* refers to reading any portion of it, and records a fine grammatical observation: the phrase might more literally be rendered "Nor dost thou recite therefrom (*minhu*) any of the Qur'an," which al-Rāzī notes could also be read as indicating that recitation of the Qur'an is one of the many tasks of the Prophet.

*ʿAmal* — deed, work, action. Any performance.

The three together cover states, sacred acts, and ordinary acts. Nothing falls outside.

**The Shift in Address**

The verse begins in the second-person singular — *"Thou art not upon any task, nor dost thou recite"* — addressing the Prophet ﷺ. Then it shifts to the plural: *"nor do you (plural) perform any deed ... We are a Witness over you (plural)."*

The shift widens the address from the Prophet to the whole community, and then to any reader. The verse begins with the most scrutinised man in history and ends with everyone.

**"We Are a Witness Over You When You Engage Therein"**

*Illā kunnā ʿalaykum shuhūdan idh tufīḍūna fīh.*

The verb *tufīḍūna* is worth noticing. From *f-y-ḍ*, to overflow, to pour forth, to plunge into. *Ifāḍah* is the word used for the pilgrims' surging departure from ʿArafāt (2:198). To be *mufīḍ* in a matter is to be immersed in it, engaged, absorbed.

The choice is precise. The verse describes God's witnessing at exactly the moment when human self-awareness is lowest — when one is *absorbed*, caught up, not observing oneself. That is when most of what is worth recording occurs, and it is when the recording is happening.

**"Not a Mote's Weight"**

*Wa-mā yaʿzubu ʿan rabbika min mithqāli dharratin fī al-arḍi wa-lā fī al-samāʾ.*

*Yaʿzubu* means to be absent, hidden, remote from — the word is used of something that has wandered out of range.

*Mithqāl dharrah* — the weight of a *dharrah*. The classical lexicographers explained *dharrah* variously as the smallest ant, or a speck of dust visible in a shaft of sunlight. In modern Arabic it renders "atom." Whatever the precise referent, the intent is clear: the smallest quantity the language could name.

The Qur'an uses this measure elsewhere in one of its most quoted passages:

> *"So whoever does an atom's weight of good shall see it, and whoever does an atom's weight of evil shall see it."* (Qur'an 99:7–8)

**"Nor Smaller Than That Nor Larger"**

*Wa-lā aṣgharu min dhālika wa-lā akbaru illā fī kitābin mubīn.*

The verse anticipates and forecloses an objection. Having named the smallest unit available, it adds: *nor smaller than that*. The language is being pushed to the point where it acknowledges its own limits and covers what lies beyond them.

This clause has a striking resonance for a modern reader. The seventh-century audience had no conception of anything smaller than a dust mote. The verse's insistence on *"smaller than that"* was, for them, an assertion about a category they could not populate. For a reader who knows that the *dharrah* is itself composed of components several orders of magnitude smaller, the clause reads differently. This should not be pressed into a claim of scientific prediction — the verse is making a theological point about the exhaustiveness of divine knowledge, not a physical one — but the precision of the phrasing is worth noticing.

**"A Clear Book"**

*Kitāb mubīn.* Al-Rāzī's comment is preserved in the source:

> *"A book that God 'has written' and upon which He has established the forms of knowable things."*

The Qur'an refers to this record in several places:

> *"With Him are the keys of the Unseen; none knows them but He. He knows what is on land and in the sea. Not a leaf falls but that He knows it, nor a grain in the darkness of the earth, nor anything moist or dry, but that it is in a clear Book."* (Qur'an 6:59)

> *"Dost thou not know that God knows what is in the heaven and the earth? Truly that is in a Book; truly that is easy for God."* (Qur'an 22:70)

**Why Continuous Observation Is Not Oppressive**

The natural modern response to this verse is discomfort. We live in an age of surveillance and have learned to experience being watched as a violation. It is worth setting out why the Qur'anic doctrine is different in kind.

*The Observer is not a rival.* Human surveillance is threatening because the observer has interests that may conflict with yours: an employer looking for grounds to dismiss, a state looking for dissent, a rival looking for leverage. God has no such interest; He gains nothing from your failure, as the hadith qudsī at v. 44 makes explicit.

*The record is complete rather than selective.* The damage done by human observation comes largely from decontextualisation — a fragment extracted and presented without what surrounds it. The *kitāb mubīn* contains everything, including the circumstances, the intention, the pressure, and the struggle.

*The Observer is also the Forgiver.* Human records are permanent; the divine record is subject to erasure by repentance:

> *"God will replace their evil deeds with good deeds."* (Qur'an 25:70)

*And the doctrine is stated as consolation as often as warning.* The person whose good is unseen is not unseen. This was noted at v. 29, and the Qur'an states it directly:

> *"And whatever good you do, God knows it."* (Qur'an 2:197)

**The Effect of Actually Believing This**

The verse's practical implication is the station of *iḥsān*, defined in the hadith of Gabriel and quoted at v. 21: *"That you worship God as though you see Him; for if you do not see Him, truly He sees you."*

The whole of Islamic ethics rests on the internalisation of this single fact, and the reason is straightforward. Every external system of accountability has a boundary. Laws operate where enforcement reaches; reputation operates where people can see; conscience alone operates everywhere — and conscience, unsupported, is easily talked down.

What this verse supplies is a fact rather than a feeling: *you are seen*. A person who genuinely holds it does not need to be supervised, and a society composed of such people can afford institutions that a society of the unsupervised cannot.

There is a well-known account of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, walking the streets of Madinah at night, overhearing a woman instruct her daughter to dilute the milk for sale. The daughter replied: *"And where is ʿUmar? ʿUmar does not see us."* Her mother said: *"If ʿUmar does not see us, the Lord of ʿUmar sees us."* The report says the caliph married the girl to his son, and from that line came ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz.

Whatever the details, the story's popularity across the centuries reflects an accurate instinct about what this verse produces when it is believed.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:62

> **Behold! Truly the friends of God, no fear shall come upon them, nor shall they grieve—**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Most Beloved Verse**

*A-lā inna awliyāʾa Allāhi lā khawfun ʿalayhim wa-lā hum yaḥzanūn.*

The verse opens with *a-lā*, the attention-particle — "Behold!" — used at v. 55 and again here for a statement of exceptional weight.

This verse and the two that follow form a unit, and they are among the most quoted passages in Islamic spiritual literature.

**The Meaning of *Walī***

*Awliyāʾ* is the plural of *walī*, from *w-l-y* — the root already encountered at v. 30 in *mawlā*, and whose basic sense is *nearness*. A *walī* is one who is close: hence friend, ally, protector, patron, guardian.

Crucially, the relationship is reciprocal in this root. God is the *walī* of the believers (2:257: *"God is the Protector of those who believe"*), and they are His *awliyāʾ*. The word describes a bond of mutual nearness and allegiance.

English "friend" is a reasonable rendering but loses the dimension of protection and allegiance. "Saint" — the term used in the Christian context, and the source notes the correspondence — captures the sense of a recognised spiritual station but imports a formal canonisation process that Islam does not have.

**Two Readings: General and Special**

The source presents both readings and does not force a choice.

On the *general* reading, followed by Ājībah and Ibn Kathīr, God's friends are simply those among the believers who are reverent. This is supported directly by the next verse, which defines them: *"those who believe and are reverent"* (v. 63). On this reading, *wilāyah* is not an elite category but the ordinary condition of a sincere believer.

On the *special* reading, also noted by Ājībah, the term refers to a particular group of individuals corresponding to what would be called saints in a Christian context — people who, having conquered their egos, are in perpetual proximity to God and totally consumed by Him.

The two readings need not conflict. *Wilāyah* admits of degrees: every believer with *taqwā* has a share of it, and some have a great deal more. The Qur'an itself grades the believers into categories (56:7–11; 35:32).

**The Sign of the Friends of God**

The source preserves a report attributed to the Prophet ﷺ concerning the *awliyāʾ*, transmitted by Ibn Kathīr and Sahl al-Tustarī:

> *"When they are seen, God is remembered."*

This is the most useful practical description of the station available, precisely because it is not about the person's own experiences, visions, or claims. It describes an *effect on others*. A friend of God is someone whose presence makes God present to the people around him — not by preaching, necessarily, but by something in the bearing.
