Everyone has encountered this at some scale: a person in whose company one becomes less inclined to say something petty, more inclined toward what is better. That effect, magnified, is the sign.

**"No Fear Shall Come Upon Them, Nor Shall They Grieve"**

*Lā khawfun ʿalayhim wa-lā hum yaḥzanūn.*

This formula appears repeatedly in the Qur'an (2:38, 62, 112, 262, 274, 277; 3:170; 5:69; 6:48; 7:35; 43:68; 46:13), and its precision is worth examining.

*Khawf* is fear, which is always directed at the *future*: something that might happen, a harm not yet arrived.

*Ḥuzn* is grief, which is always directed at the *past*: something lost, a harm already sustained.

Between them, these two account for nearly the whole of human psychological suffering. Almost everything that torments a person is either anxiety about what may come or sorrow about what has gone. The present moment, considered by itself, is rarely unbearable; it is the anticipation and the memory that make it so.

**How the Two Are Removed**

The verse does not say the friends of God are exempt from difficulty. They are not. The Prophet ﷺ was asked who is most severely tested and replied:

> *"The prophets, then those next in rank, then those next. A man is tested according to his religion: if he is firm in his religion, his trial is increased in severity."* (al-Tirmidhī 2398; Ibn Mājah 4023, from Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ)

So what is removed is not hardship but the two *responses* to hardship named in the verse. And the mechanism is intelligible.

*Fear of the future dissolves where the future is entrusted.* A person who genuinely holds that what reaches him could not have missed him, and what misses him could not have reached him — the Prophet's ﷺ formulation to Ibn ʿAbbās (al-Tirmidhī 2516) — has removed the object of anxiety. He may still take precautions; he simply is not tormented by outcomes.

*Grief over the past dissolves where the past is understood as decreed.* The corrosive element in grief is usually the counterfactual: *if only I had*. The Prophet ﷺ addressed exactly this:

> *"If anything befalls you, do not say, 'If only I had done such-and-such, it would have been so-and-so.' Rather say: 'God decreed, and what He willed He did.' For 'if only' opens the door to Satan's work."* (Muslim 2664, from Abū Hurayrah)

What remains after both are removed is not numbness. Grief as an emotion is permitted — the Prophet ﷺ wept at the death of his son Ibrāhīm and said: *"The eye sheds tears and the heart grieves, but we do not say anything except what pleases our Lord"* (al-Bukhārī 1303). What is removed is the *torment* — the grief that corrodes rather than passes.

**Rūmī's Summary and the Cost of the Station**

The source cites the poet Rūmī (d. 672/1273), who summarised the spiritual trajectory of his life:

> *"The sum of my life is three sentences, no more: / I was raw, I was cooked, I was burned."*

The line is worth dwelling on because it corrects a sentimental reading of this verse. The station of *wilāyah* is not a comfortable one attained by pleasant means. Rūmī's three verbs describe a process of transformation under heat.

The source adds Rūzbihān al-Baqlī's explanation of why the friends of God are free of fear and grief: they shall have no fear because they constantly behold God's Beauty, and they do not grieve because they are continually bathed in the splendour of His Majesty.

Whether or not one adopts the language of the mystical tradition, the structural point stands: the removal of fear and grief follows from a preoccupation so complete that there is no attention left over for either.

**The Threat to Those Who Attack Them**

There is a hadith qudsī of exceptional severity concerning the *awliyāʾ*, and it is the standard reference for this verse:

> *"God says: Whoever shows enmity to a friend of Mine, I declare war upon him. My servant does not draw near to Me with anything more beloved to Me than what I have made obligatory upon him. And My servant continues to draw near to Me with voluntary works until I love him; and when I love him, I become his hearing with which he hears, his sight with which he sees, his hand with which he strikes, and his foot with which he walks. Were he to ask Me, I would surely give him; and were he to seek refuge in Me, I would surely grant him refuge."* (al-Bukhārī 6502, from Abū Hurayrah)

Two points in this hadith are essential for reading our verse correctly.

First, the route to *wilāyah* begins with *the obligatory*. Nothing is more beloved to God than what He has made compulsory. The voluntary comes *after*, as an addition to a foundation, never as a substitute for it. This rules out any conception of sainthood that bypasses ordinary religious practice.

Second, the transformation described — God becoming the servant's hearing and sight — is a transformation of *how the person perceives and acts*, not a claim about ontological union. The classical commentators, including Ibn Ḥajar in his commentary on al-Bukhārī, were careful about this: the meaning is that the servant's faculties come to operate only in what God loves.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:63

> **those who believe and are reverent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Definition**

*Alladhīna āmanū wa-kānū yattaqūn.*

Four words. This is the Qur'an's own definition of the *awliyāʾ*, and its brevity is the point.

Anyone reading v. 62 might reasonably wonder what qualifications the "friends of God" possess. The answer given is deliberately modest: *belief and reverence*. There is no mention of miracles, visions, lineage, ordination, unusual ascetic practices, or a formal recognition process.

**Why This Definition Matters**

The definition is a safeguard, and it operates in two directions.

*Against exclusion.* It places the station within reach of the ordinary believer. There is no priesthood in Islam and no canonisation. The verse forecloses any claim that closeness to God requires access to an institution or membership in a class. A person praying alone in an unremarkable life, with belief and *taqwā*, is described by this verse.

*Against imposture.* It supplies a test. Anyone claiming special standing with God can be measured against two criteria that are, in their outward effects, observable. Does this person believe what God revealed? Does he live with reverence — that is, does he keep the boundaries?

The tradition applied this test rigorously. The formulation attributed to several early scholars, including al-Junayd, became a maxim:

> *"If you see a man flying in the air and walking on water, do not be deceived by him until you see how he stands with regard to the commands and prohibitions."*

Ibn Taymiyyah pressed the same criterion in his treatise *al-Furqān bayna Awliyāʾ al-Raḥmān wa-Awliyāʾ al-Shayṭān*, distinguishing the friends of the Merciful from the friends of Satan by exactly this measure. Extraordinary phenomena, he argued, are not a criterion at all, since they occur among the misguided as well; the criterion is adherence to the revealed law.

**The Two Components in Order**

*Āmanū* — belief. The foundation, without which nothing else stands. Belief in Islam has content: God's oneness, His angels, His books, His messengers, the Last Day, and the decree. It is not a general spiritual sentiment.

*Kānū yattaqūn* — and they *were* (habitually) reverent. Note the grammatical form: *kānū* plus the imperfect indicates continuous, habitual action over time. Not "they had *taqwā*" as a state achieved but "they used to be reverent" as a settled practice.

*Taqwā*, as examined at v. 6, is protective alertness — the state of the person walking carefully on a thorny path, or driving attentively on a dangerous road. It is not fear that paralyses but awareness that guides.

**The Order Is Not Reversible**

Belief precedes *taqwā*, and this order is structural rather than incidental.

*Taqwā* is the careful navigation of a path whose hazards one has been told about. Without belief, there is no map, no account of what the hazards are, and no reason to think the road leads anywhere. Careful conduct in the absence of any conviction about what matters is not *taqwā*; it is merely caution, and it will be calibrated to whatever the person happens to fear.

Conversely, belief without *taqwā* is inert. The Makkans of v. 31 believed a great deal — that God created, provided, and governed — and it changed nothing about how they lived. This sūrah's entire complaint concerns exactly that gap.

**What Reverence Looks Like Day to Day**

Since the definition is so brief, it is worth filling in what the tradition understood *taqwā* to require in practice.

*Fulfilling obligations.* The hadith qudsī quoted at v. 62 is explicit: nothing is more beloved to God than what He has made obligatory. Prayer at its times, zakāt on its due, fasting in its month, honesty in dealings, kindness to parents.

*Avoiding the forbidden.* And, beyond it, avoiding what is doubtful. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The lawful is clear and the unlawful is clear, and between them are ambiguous matters about which many people do not know. So whoever guards against the doubtful has protected his religion and his honour, and whoever falls into the doubtful falls into the unlawful — like a shepherd grazing around a sanctuary, about to graze within it."* (al-Bukhārī 52; Muslim 1599, from al-Nuʿmān ibn Bashīr)

The image is exact. *Taqwā* is not standing at the boundary and testing it; it is maintaining a margin.

*Awareness in private.* ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz is reported to have written: *"The one who has taqwā is not the one who fasts by day and prays by night and mixes the two, but the one who leaves what God has forbidden and performs what God has made obligatory."*

*Consistency.* The Prophet ﷺ was asked which deed God loves most and answered:

> *"The most consistent, even if it be small."* (al-Bukhārī 6464; Muslim 783, from ʿĀʾishah)

**The Democratisation of Sainthood**

There is a final observation worth making, and it distinguishes Islam sharply from several other religious systems.

By defining the friends of God as *those who believe and are reverent*, this verse makes the highest spiritual station available to anyone, in any circumstance, without institutional mediation.

The consequence, in the history of Muslim societies, is a striking pattern in the biographical literature. The people revered as *awliyāʾ* have very often been of no social consequence: cobblers, water-carriers, women in unremarkable households, an obscure man in a corner of a mosque. Uways al-Qaranī, of whom the Prophet ﷺ spoke and whom ʿUmar and ʿAlī sought out, was a camel-herd in Yemen whom nobody in his own village regarded as anything in particular.

The Qur'an had already stated the principle in the verse that abolishes all other hierarchies:

> *"Truly the noblest of you before God is the most reverent (atqākum) among you."* (Qur'an 49:13)

Our verse is that principle applied. The criterion of rank is *taqwā*, the criterion of *wilāyah* is *taqwā*, and *taqwā* is available to everyone.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:64

> **For them are glad tidings in the life of this world and in the Hereafter. There is no altering the Words of God. That is the great triumph.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Glad Tidings in Both Abodes**

*Lahumu al-bushrā fī al-ḥayāti al-dunyā wa-fī al-ākhirah.*

The completion of the passage on the friends of God. Verse 62 removed fear and grief; this verse adds a positive: *bushrā*, glad tidings.

The word is from *b-sh-r*, examined at v. 2 — news so good that it visibly changes the complexion of the face receiving it. And the striking element is the location: *fī al-ḥayāti al-dunyā* — in *this* life, not only the next.

**What the Glad Tidings of This World Are**

The commentators offer several answers, and they are complementary rather than competing.

*The experiential.* Ājībah explains that the glad tidings the friends of God receive in this life come by way of the sweet taste and ecstasy they experience as they contemplate their Lord. This is the reality the Prophet ﷺ named:

> *"Three things: whoever has them has found the sweetness of faith — that God and His Messenger be more beloved to him than anything else; that he love a person only for God's sake; and that he hate to return to disbelief after God has rescued him from it, as he would hate to be thrown into the Fire."* (al-Bukhārī 16; Muslim 43, from Anas)

The phrase *ḥalāwat al-īmān*, the sweetness of faith, describes something experienced rather than inferred — an actual pleasure in the religious life that the tradition treats as evidence of its authenticity.

*The true dream.* There is a well-known hadith interpreting this exact verse. Abū Dharr reported asking the Prophet ﷺ about *"For them are glad tidings in the life of this world,"* and he replied:

> *"It is the good vision that a Muslim sees, or that is seen about him."* (al-Tirmidhī 2273 and others, from Abū al-Dardāʾ; the interpretation is transmitted from several Companions)

And he described such visions as *"one of forty-six parts of prophecy"* (al-Bukhārī 6987).

*The reputation among the righteous.* When the Prophet ﷺ was asked about the man who does good and is praised for it by people, he said:

> *"That is the immediate glad tiding of the believer."* (Muslim 2642, from Abū Dharr)

*The angels at death.* The Qur'an describes the final glad tidings received before departure:

> *"Truly those who say, 'Our Lord is God,' and then remain steadfast — the angels descend upon them, saying: Fear not, nor grieve, and receive glad tidings of the Garden which you were promised."* (Qur'an 41:30)

Notice that the angels' words in that verse are precisely the words of v. 62 of our sūrah: *lā takhāfū wa-lā taḥzanū*. The promise made in Yūnus is delivered verbatim at the moment of death.

**"There Is No Altering the Words of God"**

*Lā tabdīla li-kalimāti Allāh.*

The source explains: this means that none can change or act contrary to this promise of God — that is, His promise in vv. 62 and 64 concerning His friends. Ibn Kathīr reads it the same way.

The phrase recurs in the Qur'an (6:34, 115; 18:27) and it functions here as a guarantee clause. It anticipates a specific anxiety: *can this be relied upon?*

The anxiety is reasonable. Human promises are subject to alteration for a range of reasons — the promiser changes his mind, circumstances shift, a superior authority intervenes, the promise turns out to have been beyond the promiser's power. The verse addresses all of these at once by stating that God's word is not subject to revision.

And this connects directly back to v. 15, where the Prophet ﷺ was told to say: *"It is not for me to alter it of my own accord."* The same root, *b-d-l*. What could not be altered under Makkan pressure cannot be altered at all — and that unalterability, which the deniers experienced as an obstacle, is here revealed as the believers' security.

**"That Is the Great Triumph"**

*Dhālika huwa al-fawzu al-ʿaẓīm.*

*Fawz* means success, attainment, deliverance — the winning of what was sought and the escape from what was feared. It is a stronger and more decisive word than *falāḥ* (v. 17), which carries the agricultural sense of a harvest. *Fawz* is victory.

The Qur'an uses the phrase *al-fawz al-ʿaẓīm* about a dozen times, always for the final outcome, and it defines it once with complete precision:

> *"Every soul shall taste death, and you will be paid your wages in full only on the Day of Resurrection. Whoever is drawn away from the Fire and admitted to the Garden has triumphed (fāza). And the life of this world is naught but the enjoyment of delusion."* (Qur'an 3:185)

That verse contains the definition: to be *removed from the Fire* and *admitted to the Garden*. Two components, one negative and one positive. Everything else that human beings call success is, by the same verse's judgment, *matāʿ al-ghurūr* — the enjoyment of delusion.

**Reframing What Counts as Winning**

This is worth sitting with, because it inverts the scoring system by which almost everyone actually operates.

Consider how success is normally assessed: income, position, recognition, the achievements of one's children, health maintained, a comfortable retirement. Every one of these is measured against other people and every one is terminated by death.

The Qur'an does not say these things are worthless — the sūrah has already acknowledged that the world's provision is real and given by God (v. 24, v. 31). It says they are not *fawz*. They are the enjoyment along the way, not the arrival.

The practical consequence is that a life can look, by every visible measure, like a failure, and be a triumph; and it can look like a triumph and be, in the terms that this verse establishes, nothing at all. The Prophet ﷺ described both cases:

> *"Many a person with dishevelled hair and covered in dust, turned away from doors, would, if he swore an oath by God, have God fulfil it."* (Muslim 2622, from Abū Hurayrah)

> *"On the Day of Resurrection a huge, corpulent man will be brought who will not weigh, with God, the wing of a gnat."* (al-Bukhārī 4729; Muslim 2785, from Abū Hurayrah)

**A Word on the Structure of Verses 62–64**

The three verses form a complete unit, and it is worth reviewing them together.

Verse 62 announced the station and named its two negative benefits: no fear, no grief. Verse 63 defined the qualification in four words: belief and reverence. Verse 64 adds the positive benefits — glad tidings in both worlds — and then seals the whole with a guarantee of unalterability and a name: *the great triumph*.

The structure is that of a formal declaration: the parties, the qualifying conditions, the benefits conferred, and the warranty. Its placement in the sūrah is equally deliberate. It comes immediately after the description of the Qur'an as cure and mercy (v. 57), and immediately before the sūrah returns to confront the deniers again (vv. 65 onwards).

The believers, in other words, are given their security before the argument resumes. That is a pattern in the Qur'an's arrangement worth noticing: the difficult passages are almost always adjacent to consolation.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:65

> **And let not their speech grieve thee. Truly might belongs altogether to God; He is the Hearing, the Knowing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse Addressed to the Prophet's Pain**

*Wa-lā yaḥzunka qawluhum.*

Having promised the friends of God that they will not grieve (v. 62), the sūrah turns to the Prophet ﷺ, who was in fact grieving, and addresses him directly.

This is one of several places where the Qur'an acknowledges his distress at the treatment he received:

> *"Perhaps thou wouldst destroy thyself with grief that they are not believers."* (Qur'an 26:3)

> *"So let not thy soul be expended in regrets over them."* (Qur'an 35:8)

> *"We know that what they say grieves thee. Truly it is not thee that they deny; rather it is the signs of God that the wrongdoers reject."* (Qur'an 6:33)

That last verse is the closest parallel, and it contains the same consolation in an expanded form: the attack is not personal, whatever it feels like.

**What Was Being Said**

The source directs the reader to 36:76 for the type of speech in question. That verse — *"So let not their speech grieve thee; truly We know what they conceal and what they proclaim"* — appears in the same context of denial and mockery.

The specific accusations are recorded throughout the Qur'an and the sīrah: sorcerer (v. 2 of this sūrah), poet, soothsayer, madman, fabricator (v. 38), a man taught by others (16:103). His followers were called weak-minded, and his lineage was insulted — one of his standing epithets among the Makkans, recorded in the sīrah, was *mudhammam* ("the reviled"), a deliberate inversion of *muḥammad* ("the praised"). The Prophet ﷺ remarked on this with equanimity:

> *"Do you not wonder at how God turns away from me the abuse of Quraysh? They revile Mudhammam and curse Mudhammam, while I am Muhammad."* (al-Bukhārī 3533, from Abū Hurayrah)

**Why the Consolation Takes This Particular Form**

*Inna al-ʿizzata lillāhi jamīʿan.* "Truly might belongs altogether to God."

At first this seems an odd response. The Prophet ﷺ is grieved by insults; he is answered with a statement about where power resides. Why is that the relevant consolation?

Because insults are attempts to *diminish*, and diminishment presupposes that the insulter has the standing to assign worth. The verse denies the premise. If *ʿizzah* — might, honour, unassailability — belongs entirely to God, then no one else is in a position to confer or withdraw it.

The source draws out the same point: *might belongs altogether to God* means that all victory is with God; and since *"all things perish, save His Face"* (28:88; cf. 55:26–27), nothing other than God can lay any claim to true might or greatness.

**The Root *ʿ-z-z***

*ʿIzzah* comes from a root meaning to be strong, rare, precious, unassailable. Land that is *ʿazīz* is hard ground that does not yield. A thing that is *ʿazīz* is scarce and therefore valuable; and *al-ʿAzīz*, as a divine name, combines all these senses: the Mighty, the Unassailable, the One whose standing cannot be diminished.

The Qur'an is explicit about the futility of seeking *ʿizzah* elsewhere:

> *"Whoever desires might — then to God belongs all might."* (Qur'an 35:10)

> *"Those who take disbelievers as allies rather than believers — do they seek might with them? Then truly all might belongs to God."* (Qur'an 4:139)

That last verse identifies a specific and very common error: attaching oneself to the powerful in order to acquire standing. The Qur'an's answer is that the transaction is impossible, because the commodity is not theirs to transfer.

**Ibn ʿAṭāʾ Allāh's Aphorism**

The source cites the Egyptian Sufi Ibn ʿAṭāʾ Allāh al-Iskandarī (d. 709/1309), from his *Ḥikam*, no. 86:

> *"If you seek greatness that does not perish, then seek not greatness that does perish."*

The aphorism is a piece of practical logic. The two are not merely different; they are alternatives. Energy spent acquiring perishable standing is energy not spent acquiring the other kind, and the perishable kind carries an additional cost: it must be continuously defended, since it can be lost.

Anyone who has watched a person organise his life around reputation understands the cost. Every conversation becomes a performance; every criticism is a threat; every rival's success is a subtraction. The person is not merely spending effort; he is living in a permanent defensive posture.

**"He Is the Hearing, the Knowing"**

*Huwa al-Samīʿu al-ʿAlīm.*

The two names close the verse, and their selection is exact.

*Al-Samīʿ*, the Hearing — because the injury was *speech*. Every word said against the Prophet ﷺ was heard by God at the moment it was uttered. Nothing was missed, nothing needs to be reported, and no record needs to be reconstructed.

*Al-ʿAlīm*, the Knowing — because speech is only the surface. God knows what was *behind* the words: the envy that Abū Jahl admitted privately (quoted at v. 42), the fear of losing position, the calculation about which accusation would be most damaging.

The pairing supplies a specific comfort. The most painful feature of slander is not usually the words themselves but the impossibility of correcting the record — the knowledge that people now hold a false picture and that no amount of protest will fully dislodge it. What this verse offers is not vindication in the eyes of the slanderers but the existence of a hearer who requires no correction because He never received the false version.

**How to Receive Criticism**

There is a practical teaching in this verse for anyone in a position to be criticised — which, in an age of public commentary, is nearly everyone.

*Distinguish the accurate from the malicious.* Not all criticism is slander. Some of it is true, and true criticism is a gift. ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb is reported to have said: *"May God have mercy on the man who shows me my faults."*

*Do not stake your worth on the assessment.* This is the verse's core teaching. If *ʿizzah* belongs to God, then the estimation of others is information about them, not a measurement of you.

*Do not respond in kind.* The Qur'an is specific:

> *"And the servants of the Merciful are those who walk upon the earth humbly, and when the ignorant address them, they say: Peace."* (Qur'an 25:63)

*Leave the matter with the Hearing, the Knowing.* This is not passivity; it is the recognition that the case is already filed with a competent authority, as established at v. 46.

**The Historical Vindication**

There is a final note worth making, because the verse's claim about *ʿizzah* was tested publicly.

The men who insulted the Prophet ﷺ in the streets of Makkah were, at the time, the most powerful people in Arabia. Their names survive today almost exclusively in the record of what they said about him. The name they mocked is pronounced, with the invocation of blessings, hundreds of millions of times daily, in every time zone, without interruption, fourteen centuries later.

The Qur'an had promised him exactly this in the shortest and most extraordinary terms:

> *"And We raised high for thee thy renown."* (Qur'an 94:4)

That is what it means for might to belong to God: He assigns it, and the assignment does not require anyone's consent.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:66

> **Behold! Truly unto God belongs whosoever is in the heavens and whosoever is on the earth. And what is it that they follow, those who call upon partners apart from God? They follow naught but conjecture, and they do but surmise.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Shift in the Formula**

*A-lā inna lillāhi man fī al-samāwāti wa-man fī al-arḍ.*

The third *a-lā* of the sūrah (after vv. 55 and 62), introducing another statement of weight.

But notice the change from v. 55. There the formula was *mā fī al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍ* — "whatever is in the heavens and the earth," using *mā*, the relative pronoun for non-rational things. Here it is *man* — "whoever," the pronoun for rational beings.

The shift is deliberate and it sharpens the argument. Verse 55 established that God owns all *property*. This verse establishes that He owns all *persons* — every rational, willing, conscious being in existence, in the heavens and on the earth: angels, jinn, humans, and whatever else possesses will.

**Why the Shift Matters for the Argument**

The relevance becomes clear in the second half of the verse, which concerns those who call upon *partners*.

Whatever the idolaters were worshipping — whether stones, spirits, angels, jinn, or deified human beings — every candidate falls under *man fī al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍ*. The proposed partners are *themselves owned*. They are on the inventory, not on the ownership side of it.

This is the same logical structure as v. 54's failed ransom. One cannot pay the owner with his own property; and one cannot appoint a partner to the owner from among the owner's possessions.

The Qur'an makes the argument explicit in a memorable parable:

> *"He sets forth for you a parable from yourselves: Do you have, among those whom your right hands possess, any partners in what We have provided for you, so that you are equal in respect of it, fearing them as you fear one another?"* (Qur'an 30:28)

The parable puts the question in terms the audience could feel. Would a Makkan slave-owner regard his slave as an equal shareholder in his estate? The idea was unthinkable to them — which is precisely the point being made about the far greater gap between the Creator and the created.

**"What Is It That They Follow?"**

*Wa-mā yattabiʿu alladhīna yadʿūna min dūni Allāhi shurakāʾ.*

The question is rhetorical, and it is answered in the same breath.

The verb *yattabiʿu* — "follow" — is significant. It reframes idolatry as an act of *following* rather than of knowing. They are not tracking evidence; they are tracking something else. And the verse names what.

**"They Follow Naught but Conjecture"**

*In yattabiʿūna illā al-ẓanna wa-in hum illā yakhruṣūn.*

The source notes that this clause is also found at 6:116, and directs the reader to 6:116–117 for the fuller discussion of how false beliefs are naught but conjecture. The sūrah has already made the point at v. 36.

The repetition matters. This is now the third time the sūrah has diagnosed the deniers' epistemology as *ẓann*, and the diagnosis has become the sūrah's settled verdict on the whole enterprise of idolatry.

**The Second Verb: *Yakhruṣūn***

The verse adds a word not used at v. 36: *yakhruṣūn*.

*Kh-r-ṣ* has a fascinating semantic range. Its original agricultural sense is *to estimate the yield of a date-palm by eye* — the practice by which a landowner or tax-assessor would look at a tree in fruit and guess how many measures of dates it would produce.

From this comes the general sense of guessing, estimating without measuring — and then, by extension, of speaking falsely, since a guess presented as a fact is a lie. The Qur'an uses the root for both:

> *"Slain be the conjecturers (al-kharrāṣūn)."* (Qur'an 51:10)

The agricultural image is illuminating for the verse's argument. Estimating a date harvest by eye is a *legitimate* practice for its purpose — the Islamic law of *kharṣ* permits it for assessing zakāt on standing crops, precisely because exact measurement before harvest is impossible and an experienced estimator is reliable enough.

The point is that *kharṣ* is a technique appropriate to a domain where precision is unavailable and the stakes are low. Applying it to the question of who created the universe and to whom one is answerable is a catastrophic mismatch of method to subject.

**The Cumulative Argument of the Sūrah on Knowledge**

It is worth stepping back to see how consistently this sūrah has pressed the epistemological point, because it constitutes one of the Qur'an's most sustained treatments of the theme.

Verse 24: signs are expounded for those who *reflect*. Verse 32: what is beyond truth but error? Verse 35: is one who guides worthier to be followed than one who cannot guide himself? Verse 36: conjecture does not avail against truth. Verse 39: they deny what they have not encompassed with knowledge. Verse 42: they listen but do not reason. Verse 55: most of them do not *know*. Verse 60: what do they *conjecture* about the Day of Resurrection? And now v. 66: they follow nothing but conjecture and guesswork.

Nine separate points, all making the same charge: the opposition's position is not the product of inquiry.

**Applying the Charge Fairly**

Honesty requires that the charge be applied to believers as well, because it is a charge about *method*, not about conclusions.

A person who inherited Islam and never examined it holds his position by exactly the mechanism the verse condemns. That his conclusion happens to be correct does not convert his *ẓann* into *ʿilm*. The Qur'an's demand throughout this sūrah is that belief be grounded, and it addresses that demand to whoever is listening.

The Qur'an criticises inherited religion in general terms, and the criticism does not exempt anyone:

> *"And when it is said to them, 'Follow what God has sent down,' they say, 'Nay, we follow that which we found our fathers upon.' What! Even though their fathers understood nothing and were not guided?"* (Qur'an 2:170)

This sūrah quotes Pharaoh's people making the same appeal at v. 78.

The remedy is not doubt but examination. The tradition has always distinguished *taqlīd* (following an authority) from *taḥqīq* (verification), and while *taqlīd* is unavoidable and legitimate in matters of legal detail — nobody can derive every ruling personally — the classical theologians were largely agreed that the foundations of belief require something more: at minimum, an understanding of why one holds what one holds.

The sūrah has supplied the materials for exactly that examination. Verses 5–6 on the cosmos, v. 16 on the messenger's character, v. 31 on providence, v. 38 on the text, v. 39 on the historical record. It has not asked to be believed on the basis of *ẓann*, and it does not permit its adherents to hold it that way.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:67

> **He it is Who made the night for you, that you might rest therein, and the day by which to see. Truly in that are signs for a people who hear.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Back to the Sky**

After a long stretch of argument, the sūrah returns to the method it used at vv. 5–6: pointing at something everyone can see.

*Huwa alladhī jaʿala lakumu al-layla li-taskunū fīhi wa-l-nahāra mubṣiran.*

The source notes that the first half of this verse is identical to part of 40:61, and the second half to part of 30:23.

**"That You Might Rest Therein"**

*Li-taskunū fīhi.* The verb *sakana* means to be still, to settle, to dwell — from it come *sakīnah* (tranquillity, the divine peace that descends on hearts), *maskan* (a dwelling), and *sākin* (a resident, and also, in grammar, a consonant without a vowel: at rest).

Ibn Ḥayyān explains the phrase as the respite from weariness after the work of the day, and the day by which to see as relating to the pursuit of one's livelihood.

The Qur'an elsewhere adds a further dimension:

> *"And We made your sleep a rest, and We made the night a covering, and We made the day for livelihood."* (Qur'an 78:9–11)

*Libās* — a covering, a garment. Night is described as clothing: something that wraps, conceals, and protects.

**The Design Argument From Sleep**

There is a serious point available here that is easy to pass over because sleep is so familiar.

Consider what would be required if darkness did not fall. A species capable of continuous activity, in an environment permitting it, would face relentless competitive pressure to *use* that capacity. Rest would become a luxury, then a disadvantage, then an impossibility. The natural cycle imposes a limit that no individual has to negotiate.

And consider the physiology. Sleep is not merely the absence of activity; it is an active state during which memory is consolidated, tissue repaired, and metabolic waste cleared from the brain. It occupies roughly a third of a human life. An organism that spends a third of its existence unconscious and defenceless has not arrived at that arrangement by any obvious competitive logic; it is a design feature with costs that must be outweighed by functions.

The Qur'an treats sleep as a sign in its own right, and connects it to something larger:

> *"God takes the souls at the time of their death, and those that have not died, during their sleep. He retains those for whom He has decreed death and releases the others until an appointed term."* (Qur'an 39:42)

Sleep is presented here as a nightly rehearsal for death — a surrender of consciousness and control that is returned each morning as a gift. This is why the Prophet ﷺ taught a supplication on waking:

> *"Praise be to God, Who has given us life after having caused us to die, and unto Him is the resurrection."* (al-Bukhārī 6312, from Ḥudhayfah)

**"And the Day by Which to See"**

*Wa-l-nahāra mubṣiran.*

The Arabic is a striking construction. *Mubṣir* is an active participle: "seeing," "sight-giving." Literally the day is described as *seeing* — the attribute of the perceiver applied to the medium of perception.

The rhetoricians call this *majāz ʿaqlī*, a transferred attribution, and it is the same figure by which we say "a sleepless night" when it is the person who did not sleep. Its effect here is to present the day as an active agent that makes seeing possible rather than a passive interval during which one happens to see.

**The Sufi Reading**

The source preserves a spiritual interpretation from al-Sulamī: on a Sufi understanding, *the night that you might rest therein* refers to the mystics' engaging in spiritual retreat (*khalwah*) and intimate discourse with God (*munājāt*), whereas *the day by which to see* refers to their seeing the wonders of God's power and wisdom in the created order that surrounds them.

This reading tracks a genuine pattern in Islamic practice. The night is the time of *qiyām al-layl*, the standing prayer, which the Qur'an singles out:

> *"Truly the vigil of the night is stronger in tread and more upright in speech."* (Qur'an 73:6)

The reason given in the following verse is practical: *"Truly during the day thou hast prolonged occupation"* (73:7). The night is available because nothing else claims it.

And the last third of the night has a specific status:

> *"Our Lord descends each night to the lowest heaven when the last third of the night remains, and says: Who is calling upon Me, that I may answer him? Who is asking of Me, that I may give him? Who is seeking My forgiveness, that I may forgive him?"* (al-Bukhārī 1145; Muslim 758, from Abū Hurayrah)

**"For a People Who Hear"**

*Inna fī dhālika la-āyātin li-qawmin yasmaʿūn.*

The sūrah's varying qualifier appears again. At v. 5 the signs were for those who *know*; at v. 6, those who are *reverent*; at v. 24, those who *reflect*; here, those who *hear*.

Ājībah, al-Baghawī and al-Ṭūsī explain that *a people who hear* refers to those who "listen" by reflecting upon God's signs — not physical audition but responsive attention, the opposite of the deafness described at v. 42.

The choice of *hearing* for this particular verse is worth a moment's thought. Why hearing, when the subject is night and day — visual phenomena?

Perhaps because the signs in question are, precisely, *not* dramatic to look at. Sunset happens daily and looks like nothing in particular by the ten-thousandth viewing. What is required to extract a sign from it is not sharper eyes but the willingness to *receive a message* from something ordinary — and reception is what hearing models. The eye takes; the ear receives.

**A Reflection on the Modern Loss of Night**

There is a contemporary application that the verse invites, and it is not a small one.

Artificial light has effectively abolished night as a distinct condition for most of the world's population. The consequences are documented: chronic sleep deprivation, disrupted circadian rhythm, and the associated metabolic and psychological costs. What was described here as a divine provision — an enforced boundary on activity — has been engineered away, and the result has not been an increase in human flourishing.

There is a spiritual dimension too. The night was, for most of human history, the time when a person was alone with his thoughts, because there was nothing else available. That condition has been almost entirely eliminated. The hours the verse describes as *sakan*, stillness, are now the hours of maximum stimulation for a large fraction of the population.

The Islamic practice of night prayer is, among other things, a deliberate reclamation of this. It requires the believer to place himself, at least sometimes, in exactly the condition the verse describes: darkness, silence, no audience, and nothing to do but address God. Whatever else it accomplishes, it restores access to a category of experience that the modern arrangement has made scarce.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:68

> **They say, "God has taken a child." Glory be to Him! He is the Self-Sufficient. Unto Him belongs whatsoever is in the heavens and whatsoever is on the earth. You have no authority for this. Do you say about God that which you know not?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Claim Being Refuted**

*Qālū ittakhadha Allāhu waladan.*

The source directs the reader to 2:116, 4:171, and 5:117–118 for the extended discussions of the Islamic theological rejection of God's having a child and the manner in which this constitutes disbelief.

The claim had several forms in the Prophet's ﷺ environment. The Makkan Arabs said the angels were God's daughters (16:57; 37:149–153; 43:19) — a claim the Qur'an notes is doubly insulting given their own contempt for daughters. The Jews of Madinah were reported to have said ʿUzayr was the son of God (9:30). And the Christians affirmed the divine sonship of Jesus (9:30; 19:88–92).

The Qur'an's response to all three is identical in structure, and this verse contains it in compressed form: a glorification, an attribute, a statement of ownership, a demand for evidence, and a rebuke.

**"Glory Be to Him!" — The Instinctive Response**

*Subḥānah.* The verse's first response is not an argument but an exclamation.

*Tasbīḥ*, as examined at v. 10, is the declaration that God is free of every deficiency and unworthy attribution. Placing it first indicates that the claim is not merely mistaken but *unbecoming* — the appropriate first reaction is recoil, not refutation.

The Qur'an's strongest expression of this recoil is in Sūrah Maryam:

> *"And they say, 'The Merciful has taken a son.' You have certainly put forth a monstrous thing. The heavens are almost torn apart thereby, and the earth split asunder, and the mountains collapse in devastation — that they should ascribe a son to the Merciful, when it is not fitting for the Merciful to take a son. There is none in the heavens and the earth but that he comes to the Merciful as a servant."* (Qur'an 19:88–93)

**"He Is the Self-Sufficient" — The Central Argument**

*Huwa al-Ghanī.*

This single word carries the whole refutation, and it deserves careful unpacking because it is the most economical argument in the passage.

*Ghanī* is from *gh-n-y*, to be free of need, to be self-sufficient, to be rich in the sense of requiring nothing. *Al-Ghanī* is one of God's names, and its opposite in the Qur'an is *faqīr*, the needy:

> *"O mankind! You are the ones in need of God, and God is the Self-Sufficient, the Praiseworthy."* (Qur'an 35:15)

Now consider why offspring exist. Reproduction, in every case known to us, answers a *need*. Biologically, it is the mechanism by which a mortal organism perpetuates its genetic line — necessary precisely because the individual dies. Socially, children provide continuity, labour, support in age, and the survival of a name. Psychologically, they answer loneliness and the desire to be succeeded.

Every one of these is a deficiency being remedied. A being who has no deficiency has no use for offspring. The claim of divine sonship therefore does not merely mis-describe God; it *presupposes* that He is lacking something — which contradicts the concept of God.

Sūrat al-Ikhlāṣ makes the argument in four lines, and it is the Qur'an's densest theological statement:

> *"Say: He is God, One. God, the Eternally Self-Sufficient (al-Ṣamad). He begets not, nor was He begotten. And there is none comparable unto Him."* (Qur'an 112:1–4)

Note the order. *Al-Ṣamad* comes first; *lam yalid wa-lam yūlad* follows as its consequence. The denial of offspring is derived from the affirmation of self-sufficiency.

**"Unto Him Belongs Whatsoever Is in the Heavens and the Earth"**

*Lahu mā fī al-samāwāti wa-mā fī al-arḍ.*

The second argument, and it approaches from a different angle.

A child is, in every meaningful sense, an *other* — a separate being with its own existence. But everything that exists already belongs to God as His creation and property. There is no ontological space for a being who stands in a relation to God other than that of created servant.

This is the point of the verse from Sūrah Maryam quoted above: *"There is none in the heavens and the earth but that he comes to the Merciful as a servant"* (19:93). The category "child of God," understood literally, would require a being who is neither Creator nor creature — and there is no such category.

The Qur'an presses the same argument specifically regarding Jesus:

> *"The Messiah, son of Mary, was no more than a messenger; messengers had passed away before him. And his mother was a truthful woman. Both of them used to eat food. See how We make clear to them the signs, and see how they are deluded."* (Qur'an 5:75)

The argument in that verse is elegantly indirect. *Both of them used to eat food* — that is, both were dependent beings, requiring input from outside themselves to continue existing. Dependency is the definitive mark of the creature.

**"You Have No Authority for This"**

*In ʿindakum min sulṭānin bi-hādhā.*

*Sulṭān* means authority, warrant, evidence — a proof that establishes a claim. The source notes that this notion is invoked elsewhere in the Qur'an when discussing the ascription of partners to God (3:151; 6:81; 7:33, 71; 12:40; 30:35; 53:23).

The demand is for evidence, and it is the same demand made at v. 59 concerning invented prohibitions: *"Has God granted you leave, or do you fabricate against God?"*

The Qur'an consistently treats claims about God as requiring warrant of a specific kind: revelation. One cannot arrive at conclusions about the divine nature by speculation, inference from myth, or theological development over centuries. The doctrine of divine names being *tawqīfiyyah* — established only by revelation, not by human designation — follows from this principle.

**"Do You Say About God That Which You Know Not?"**

*A-taqūlūna ʿalā Allāhi mā lā taʿlamūn.*

The closing rebuke returns to the sūrah's dominant theme. This is the same charge made at v. 36 (*conjecture*), v. 39 (*denying what they have not encompassed*), v. 59 (*fabrication*), and v. 66 (*guesswork*).

The Qur'an elsewhere places this among the gravest of prohibitions, in a list that is worth quoting because of the company it keeps:

> *"Say: My Lord has forbidden only indecencies, whether open or hidden, and sin, and wrongful oppression, and that you associate with God that for which He has sent down no authority, and that you say about God that which you do not know."* (Qur'an 7:33)

Note the final item on the list. Speaking about God without knowledge is placed alongside indecency, oppression, and idolatry — and is mentioned *last*, which in Arabic rhetorical convention often marks the climax.

**Why Religious Innovation Is Treated So Severely**

The source makes an observation worth developing: often in the Qur'an, the religious innovations of previous communities are connected with the enormity of fabricating lies against God (see 3:24; 5:104; 6:21, 93, 137–140, 144; 7:37, 152; 16:56, 116).

The severity is not arbitrary. Consider what a false claim about God actually does.

It misdirects worship, which is the purpose for which human beings exist. It corrupts the standard by which all other claims are judged, since a religion's conception of God determines everything downstream. It is uncorrectable by ordinary means, because there is no empirical test available. And it propagates: a false doctrine adopted by a community is transmitted to children who receive it as inherited certainty — the mechanism the Qur'an condemns at 2:170 and at v. 78 of this sūrah.

A lie about a person harms that person's reputation among those who hear it. A lie about God harms the relationship between the entire species and its Creator, potentially for centuries.

**The Positive Doctrine**

It is worth stating what the Qur'an affirms as well as what it denies, because the denial can be heard as merely negative.

The Qur'an denies sonship while affirming, in the most intimate terms, the relationship between God and human beings. He is nearer than the jugular vein (50:16). He answers the caller (2:186). He loves — the Qur'an uses the verb repeatedly: He loves the doers of good, the just, the repentant, the pure, those who rely on Him. And the hadith qudsī of drawing near (al-Bukhārī 6502) describes a closeness that no doctrine of sonship could exceed.

What Islam refuses is not intimacy but a *category error*: the description of that relationship in terms drawn from biology and lineage. God is not a father in the sense of having generated; He is *rabb*, the One who creates, sustains, nurtures, and brings to completion. And the Qur'an's judgment is that this description is not a lesser one but a truer and, properly understood, a greater one.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:69

> **Say, "Surely those who fabricate a lie against God will not prosper."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verdict Repeated**

*Qul inna alladhīna yaftarūna ʿalā Allāhi al-kadhiba lā yufliḥūn.*

This verse restates almost exactly the closing clause of v. 17: *"Surely the guilty will not prosper."* The repetition is the sūrah's method of establishing a principle by return rather than by argument.

The same statement appears at 16:116, immediately after the prohibition of arbitrarily declaring things lawful and forbidden — the offence described at v. 59 of this sūrah.

**The Verb *Yufliḥūn* Revisited**

As examined at v. 17, *aflaḥa* carries an agricultural image: *falāḥ* is originally the ploughman's success — the splitting of the earth, the sowing, the waiting, and the harvest.

Applied here, the statement is: whatever the fabricators appear to accumulate, there will be no harvest. They may gather, but they have planted nothing that can yield.

**Why Fabrication Cannot Yield**

The claim is worth examining rather than merely asserting, because on its face it seems contradicted by history. Religious falsehood has founded empires, enriched priesthoods, and organised societies for millennia.

Several things can be said.

*The yield is measured at the end.* This is the meaning of *falāḥ* as harvest and of the sūrah's repeated insistence on the *ʿāqibah*, the outcome (v. 39). A crop is not assessed in the growing season.

*The fabricator's own position is uniquely hopeless.* Consider what he has done: he has constructed a claim about God, in God's name, and staked his life on people believing it. Unlike an ordinary sinner, he cannot even hope to be judged leniently on grounds of ignorance, since he knows precisely what he invented.

*And the structure is self-undermining even within history.* Religious systems built on fabrication require continuous maintenance: the suppression of inquiry, the control of texts, the management of embarrassing evidence. This is expensive, and the expense compounds. The moments at which such systems collapse are almost always moments at which the maintenance failed.

**Why This Verse Follows the Previous One**

The placement is logical. Verse 68 identified a specific fabrication — the ascription of a child to God — and demanded evidence for it. This verse announces the outcome for those who engage in such fabrication.

But there is also a rhetorical function. Verse 68 ended with a question: *"Do you say about God that which you know not?"* A question invites a response, and the responses available to the accused are denial, justification, or silence. Verse 69 forecloses the discussion by stating the consequence, and it does so in the imperative: *Qul* — say it.

**Who Falls Under This Verse**

It is worth being careful about the scope, because the verse could be misapplied.

The Arabic describes those who *yaftarūna* — who *fabricate*. The verb implies manufacture: the deliberate construction of a claim and its attribution to God. It does not describe someone who inherited a false doctrine in good faith, nor someone who examined the evidence with sincerity and reached a conclusion the Qur'an regards as mistaken.

This distinction is essential, and the sūrah itself has already drawn it. Verse 47 established that judgment follows the arrival of a messenger, and that people are not held accountable for what did not reach them. Verse 40 established that God alone knows who is who. And v. 44 established that no one is wronged.

So the verse condemns an *act* — the knowing invention of religious claims — and leaves the assessment of individuals where the sūrah has consistently placed it: with God.

**The Warning to the Religious Class**

The people most exposed to this verse are not sceptics but the religiously active. This deserves stating plainly.

An atheist does not fabricate against God; he denies. The people who make claims in God's name are, definitionally, religious people — preachers, scholars, teachers, and anyone with a platform who speaks about what God wants.

The tradition's awareness of this danger was acute. The reluctance of the early scholars to issue rulings, discussed at v. 17, was fear of exactly this verse. Sufyān al-Thawrī said: *"We used to see the most knowledgeable among us as the one who most often said, 'I do not know.'"* And al-Shāfiʿī's rule for himself was that he would not answer until he knew whether silence or speech was closer to God.

The contrast with the current situation, in which anyone can speak to a large audience about religion without qualification, review, or accountability, is not flattering. This verse is addressed to that situation as directly as to any in Makkah.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:70

> **An enjoyment in this world, then unto Us shall be their return, and We shall make them taste severe punishment for their having disbelieved.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Full Accounting of the Fabricators**

*Matāʿun fī al-dunyā thumma ilaynā marjiʿuhum.*

Verse 69 said the fabricators will not prosper. This verse anticipates the obvious objection — *but they appear to be doing very well* — and answers it by supplying the complete timeline.

Yes, there is enjoyment. It is real, and the Qur'an does not deny it. But it is *matāʿ*, and it is *fī al-dunyā*, and it is followed by a return.

**The Word *Matāʿ* Again**

*Matāʿ*, encountered at v. 23, denotes goods or provisions used temporarily — from *m-t-ʿ*, to derive benefit for a period. It is the word for supplies taken on a journey: real, useful, and finite.

The Qur'an uses it consistently for the world's offerings, and always with the same evaluative force:

> *"And the life of this world is naught but the enjoyment of delusion (matāʿ al-ghurūr)."* (Qur'an 3:185; 57:20)

> *"Say: The enjoyment of this world is little, and the Hereafter is better for whoever is reverent."* (Qur'an 4:77)

The single word *matāʿun*, standing alone at the head of this verse without any verb, functions almost as a dismissal. What is all of it? *An enjoyment.* Then the sentence moves on.

**The Structure of the Verse as an Argument**

The verse is constructed as a compressed timeline, and its persuasive force lies in the compression:

*matāʿun fī al-dunyā* — a brief enjoyment here;
*thumma ilaynā marjiʿuhum* — then the return;
*thumma nudhīquhumu al-ʿadhāba al-shadīd* — then the tasting of severe punishment.

Three stages, of which the first occupies a lifetime and the last does not end. Placed side by side in a single sentence, the disproportion becomes visible in a way it never is when one is living inside the first stage.

This is precisely what the sūrah has been doing throughout: supplying perspective that is unavailable from inside the situation. It did the same at v. 24 with the garden that was flourishing *yesterday*, and at v. 45 with the lifetime that felt like *an hour of the day*.

**Why Worldly Success Is Not Evidence of Divine Approval**

This verse addresses a confusion that is ancient and remains widespread: the assumption that prosperity indicates favour.

The Qur'an rejects the inference explicitly and repeatedly:

> *"Do they suppose that what We extend to them of wealth and children means that We hasten good things to them? Nay, but they do not perceive."* (Qur'an 23:55–56)

> *"And let not those who disbelieve suppose that the respite We grant them is good for their souls. We only grant them respite that they may increase in sin."* (Qur'an 3:178)

> *"Let it not deceive thee, the going to and fro of those who disbelieve in the land. A brief enjoyment; then their refuge is Hell."* (Qur'an 3:196–197)

That last passage uses *matāʿ qalīl*, "a brief enjoyment," in the same construction as our verse.

And the Prophet ﷺ named the mechanism, as quoted at v. 11:

> *"When you see God giving a servant of this world what he loves while he persists in disobedience, that is only a gradual enticement (istidrāj)."* (Aḥmad 17311, from ʿUqbah ibn ʿĀmir)

*Istidrāj* is a precise term. Its root *d-r-j* means to proceed by degrees, step by step — the same root as *darajah*, a rank or stair. To be subject to *istidrāj* is to be led downward one comfortable step at a time, each step feeling like an improvement.

**The Counterpart: Hardship Is Not Evidence of Disfavour**

The inverse inference is equally common and equally wrong, and this is the more urgent correction for most believers.

The Prophet ﷺ stated the principle directly:

> *"When God intends good for His servant, He hastens the punishment for him in this world; and when He intends ill for His servant, He withholds the punishment for his sin until He gives it to him in full on the Day of Resurrection."* (al-Tirmidhī 2396, from Anas)

And, as quoted at v. 62, the severity of a person's trials is proportionate to the strength of his religion (al-Tirmidhī 2398).

Taken together, these teachings dismantle the entire framework by which people ordinarily read their circumstances. Neither prosperity nor difficulty carries the meaning we instinctively assign to it. The Qur'an's alternative reading is that both are forms of *test*:

> *"And We test you with evil and with good, as a trial; and unto Us you will be returned."* (Qur'an 21:35)

Note the ending of that verse: identical to ours.

**"We Shall Make Them Taste"**

*Nudhīquhum.* The verb *adhāqa* returns for the third time in the sūrah — mercy tasted at v. 21, punishment at v. 52, and now here.

Its use maintains the sūrah's insistence that the Hereafter is *experienced* rather than merely undergone as a status. And the pairing across the sūrah is deliberate: those who tasted mercy and responded with scheming (v. 21) will taste something else.

**"For Their Having Disbelieved"**

*Bi-mā kānū yakfurūn.* The verse closes, as v. 4 did, by naming the cause. And, as at v. 4, the word is *kufr* — which, as examined there, carries the root sense of *covering over*: both the concealment of a recognised truth and the covering over of a benefit received, which is why the same root supplies the Arabic for ingratitude.

The connection to this verse's opening word is therefore exact, and probably deliberate. They received *matāʿ* — enjoyment, provision, benefit — and their response was *kufr*, the covering over of the giver. The verse begins with the gift and ends with the failure to acknowledge it.

**What This Passage Asks of the Reader**

Verses 68–70 form a unit on fabrication and its outcome, and the practical question they raise for an ordinary believer is not whether he has invented a religion.

It is narrower and more searching: *what do I say about God that I do not actually know?*

The candidates are common. Confident assertions about why a particular calamity befell someone. Claims about who God will forgive and who He will not. Statements about what God "wants" that are really statements about what the speaker prefers. Assurances offered to the grieving that go well beyond anything revelation supports. The declaration that a text means something because one wishes it did.

None of these is the fabrication of a false religion. Each of them is, in a small way, speaking about God without knowledge — the offence this passage names. The safeguard is the formula the tradition attached to every scholarly opinion, and which any believer can adopt: *wa-Allāhu aʿlam*. And God knows best.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:71

> **And recite unto them the story of Noah, when he said to his people, "O my people! If my presence is grievous to you, and [so too] my reminding of the signs of God, then in God do I trust. So decide on your plan, you and your partners; then let there be no doubt concerning your plan, but carry it out against me, and grant no respite!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah Turns to Narrative**

At this point the sūrah changes mode. Having argued for seventy verses, it begins to tell stories — first Noah (vv. 71–73), then the messengers after him (v. 74), then Moses and Pharaoh at length (vv. 75–93).

The transition is signalled by a command: *wa-tlu ʿalayhim nabaʾa Nūḥ* — "recite unto them the account of Noah." *Nabaʾ* is momentous news, the same root as *nabī*, prophet.

The purpose of the shift is clear from the sūrah's context. The Prophet ﷺ was in a specific situation: a messenger among a hostile people who had rejected him, mocked him, and were plotting against him. The narrative that follows is chosen because it is *the same situation*, several thousand years earlier, with a known outcome.

**Noah in the Qur'an**

The source notes the other places where Noah's story is told: 7:59–64; 11:25–48; 23:23–30; 26:105–121; 37:75–82; 54:9–15; and the whole of Sūrah 71, which bears his name.

Each telling emphasises a different dimension. Sūrah Hūd gives the fullest narrative, including the construction of the ark and the drowning of Noah's son. Sūrah Nūḥ preserves his prayer and his description of his people's obstinacy — including the detail that he called them *"by night and by day"*, *"publicly and in secret"*, and that they *"thrust their fingers in their ears and covered themselves with their garments"* (71:5–7).

The version here is the briefest, and it concentrates on a single speech.

**The Length of the Mission**

The Qur'an gives a figure that frames everything else in Noah's story:

> *"And We certainly sent Noah to his people, and he remained among them a thousand years less fifty."* (Qur'an 29:14)

Nine hundred and fifty years of calling, and the number who believed with him is described elsewhere as small: *"And none believed with him save a few"* (11:40).

Whatever one makes of the figure, its function in the narrative is unmistakable. This is the Qur'an's extreme case of persistence without result, and it is being recited to a man who had been calling for roughly a decade.

**"If My Presence Is Grievous to You"**

*In kāna kabura ʿalaykum maqāmī wa-tadhkīrī bi-āyāti Allāh.*

The verb *kabura* means to be great, heavy, burdensome. *Jalālayn* and al-Ṭabarī explain *if my presence is grievous to you* as "if my being among you causes you distress," and *my reminding of the signs of God* as "my warning and admonishment to you."

The speech begins by *acknowledging the other side's grievance*. Noah does not dismiss their discomfort or pretend it is unreasonable. He names it accurately: my presence is heavy on you, and so is my constant reminding.

There is something instructive in this. A person delivering an unwelcome truth is, in fact, a burden on those who do not want to hear it, and pretending otherwise is a form of dishonesty. Noah's opening concedes the point without conceding the message.

**"Then in God Do I Trust"**

*Fa-ʿalā Allāhi tawakkaltu.*

The pivot of the speech. Ibn Jarīr and al-Ṭūsī gloss it: "I care not what you will try to do to me, because I have consigned my affair to God on account of my firm reliance upon Him."

*Tawakkul* is one of the central concepts of Islamic spirituality. From *w-k-l*, to entrust or appoint as an agent — a *wakīl* is a legal representative, someone authorised to act on another's behalf. To make *tawakkul* upon God is to appoint Him as one's agent in a matter.

The classical scholars were careful to distinguish *tawakkul* from passivity. The Prophet ﷺ was asked by a man whether he should tie his camel or trust in God, and replied:

> *"Tie it and trust in God."* (al-Tirmidhī 2517, from Anas)

And he described the birds as the model:

> *"If you were to rely upon God with the reliance He is due, He would provide for you as He provides for the birds: they go out hungry in the morning and return full in the evening."* (al-Tirmidhī 2344; Ibn Mājah 4164, from ʿUmar)

The birds are not idle. They *go out*. The reliance concerns the outcome, not the effort.

**The Challenge: "So Decide on Your Plan"**

*Fa-ajmiʿū amrakum wa-shurakāʾakum, thumma lā yakun amrukum ʿalaykum ghummatan, thumma qḍū ilayya wa-lā tunẓirūn.*

The speech now becomes extraordinary. Noah invites his enemies to do their worst.

*Ajmiʿū amrakum* — "resolve upon your affair," gather your decision. *Jalālayn* explains: resolve what you will try to do to me.

*Wa-shurakāʾakum* — "and your partners." Bring your gods too; include them in the deliberation. There is a controlled contempt in this: he is inviting the deities to participate in a council of war against him.

*Thumma lā yakun amrukum ʿalaykum ghummatan* — "then let there be no doubt concerning your plan." The commentators — al-Bayḍāwī, al-Baghawī, Ibn Jarīr, *Jalālayn*, al-Kāshānī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Zamakhsharī — explain *ghummah* as obscurity or concealment: do not conceal what you intend, or act in a manner that is dubious, unclear, or equivocal.

*Ghummah* is from *gh-m-m*, to cover — the same root as *ghamām*, cloud, and *ghamm*, the anxiety that clouds the heart. So the demand is: no fog. Let it be open.

*Thumma qḍū ilayya wa-lā tunẓirūn* — "then carry it out against me, and grant no respite."

**The Psychology of the Challenge**

This is one of the most striking speeches in the Qur'an, and its confidence deserves examination.

Noah is an isolated man, in his ninth century of failure, addressing a hostile population, and he tells them to organise their combined resources, including their gods, and to attack him without delay.

What makes such a speech possible is stated in the middle of it: *fa-ʿalā Allāhi tawakkaltu*. Everything else follows from that clause.

And there is a strategic dimension as well as a spiritual one. A person who visibly cannot be intimidated removes the primary weapon of his opponents. Threats work by producing fear; where no fear is produced, the threat has no purchase. By inviting the attack, Noah has taken from them the ability to hold it over him.

The same posture is recorded of Hūd, addressing his people in almost identical terms:

> *"I call God to witness, and bear you witness, that I am quit of what you associate besides Him. So scheme against me, all of you together, and grant me no respite. Truly I rely upon God, my Lord and your Lord. There is no creature but that He holds it by its forelock. Truly my Lord is on a straight path."* (Qur'an 11:54–56)

The phrase *"there is no creature but that He holds it by its forelock"* explains the confidence exactly. The *nāṣiyah* is the forelock, the hair at the front of the head by which an animal is led. Every creature that might attack is itself held by the One being relied upon.

**The Model for the Prophet and His Community**

The reason this passage is recited to the Prophet ﷺ becomes clear when one considers what he faced in the years surrounding this sūrah's revelation: a boycott, an assassination plot, the deaths of Khadījah and Abū Ṭālib, the stoning at al-Ṭāʾif.

The Qur'an records the same *tawakkul* being adopted by the Muslims in a later crisis, after the losses at Uḥud, when they were told that the enemy had regrouped:

> *"Those to whom people said: The people have gathered against you, so fear them — but it increased them in faith, and they said: God is sufficient for us, and an excellent Guardian is He."* (Qur'an 3:173)

*Ḥasbunā Allāhu wa-niʿma al-wakīl.* The word *wakīl* is from the same root as *tawakkul*. It has been the standing formula of Muslims under pressure ever since, and its ancestor is the speech in this verse.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:72

> **And if you turn your backs, I have not asked of you any reward. My reward lies only with God, and I am commanded to be among those who submit."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Conclusion of Noah's Speech**

*Fa-in tawallaytum fa-mā saʾaltukum min ajr. In ajriya illā ʿalā Allāh.*

Having invited his enemies to do their worst, Noah closes with a different kind of argument — one addressed not to their hostility but to their suspicion.

**"One of the Marks of Prophecy"**

The source states the principle directly: one of the marks of prophecy is that prophets do not seek recompense from the people to whom they have been sent. It directs the reader to 36:21:

> *"Follow those who ask of you no reward and who are guided."* (Qur'an 36:21)

That verse is spoken by the believing man who came running from the far end of the city in Sūrah Yā Sīn, and it offers a *criterion* by which ordinary people can evaluate a religious claim: check whether the messenger is charging.

The Qur'an has each of its prophets make the same declaration, in almost identical words, in a remarkable sequence in Sūrat al-Shuʿarāʾ. Noah says it (26:109), then Hūd (26:127), then Ṣāliḥ (26:145), then Lot (26:164), then Shuʿayb (26:180) — five times, verbatim:

> *"And I ask of you no reward for it. My reward lies only with the Lord of the worlds."*

The repetition is itself a teaching. It establishes disinterest as a defining feature of authentic prophecy rather than an incidental virtue of particular prophets.

**Why This Criterion Works**

The test is simple and it is remarkably effective, which is why the Qur'an keeps returning to it.

Almost every false religious claim in history has had a discernible payoff: money, power, sexual access, immunity, or status. The claim was the mechanism; the payoff was the point. And this is detectable — not always immediately, but over time, because the payoff has to be collected.

A messenger who asks for nothing removes the most obvious explanation of his behaviour. He does not remove all possible explanations — he might be deluded, or seeking a subtler reward — but he removes the one that accounts for the overwhelming majority of cases.

Applied to the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, the criterion is unusually easy to check, because his circumstances at the end of his life are well documented. He died in a room of mud brick, on a mat that left marks on his side. ʿĀʾishah reported that his family went for months without cooking, living on dates and water (al-Bukhārī 6459). He had pawned his armour to a Jewish merchant in Madinah for barley, and it was still pledged when he died (al-Bukhārī 2916). And he legislated that his own family could not receive zakāt, cutting them off from a revenue stream that other Muslims could access.

He had, by then, been for years the effective ruler of Arabia. Whatever he was seeking, it was not the ordinary payoff.

**"My Reward Lies Only With God"**

*In ajriya illā ʿalā Allāh.*

The construction is exclusive: my wage is upon none but God.

The word *ajr* is the ordinary Arabic for wages — what a hired worker receives. Noah is not saying he works without pay; he is saying his employer is someone else. The relationship with his audience is therefore not commercial at all: they are not his clients, and he is not selling them anything.

This reframing has practical consequences for anyone who teaches or serves.

It removes the pressure to please. A person whose income depends on his audience will, inevitably and often unconsciously, adjust his message toward what the audience wants to hear. The dependency does the work; no corruption of intent is required. A person whose *ajr* is with God has no such incentive gradient.

And it removes the resentment that comes from unreciprocated effort. Anyone who has served others at cost knows the specific bitterness of ingratitude. That bitterness is a symptom of having expected payment from the wrong party.

**"And I Am Commanded to Be Among Those Who Submit"**

*Wa-umirtu an akūna mina al-muslimīn.*

The final clause is theologically significant, and it is easy to pass over.

Noah — a prophet many millennia before the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ — describes himself as commanded to be among *al-muslimīn*.

The word here is not a denominational label. *Muslim* is an active participle from *aslama*, to surrender, to hand over, to submit — from the root *s-l-m*, which also gives *salām*, peace, and *salāmah*, safety (see v. 25).

The Qur'an uses the term for every prophet and every believing community before Muhammad ﷺ. Abraham prays: *"Our Lord, make us both submitters (muslimayni) unto Thee"* (2:128). Joseph prays: *"Take me as a submitter (musliman)"* (12:101). The disciples of Jesus say: *"Bear witness that we are submitters (muslimūn)"* (3:52). Pharaoh, too late, claims: *"I am among those who submit"* (v. 90 of this sūrah).

The Qur'an's claim is that *islām* in this sense is the single religion delivered by every prophet, and that the historical religion brought by Muhammad ﷺ is its final and completed form.

**"Commanded" — Even the Prophet Is Under Orders**

*Umirtu* — "I have been commanded."

Note the passive. Noah does not say "I have chosen to submit" but "I have been ordered to." His own submission is itself an act of obedience to a command, not a spiritual preference he adopted.

The Prophet Muhammad ﷺ is given the same formula repeatedly:

> *"Say: I have been commanded to worship God, devoting religion sincerely to Him."* (Qur'an 39:11)

> *"Say: I have been commanded to be the first of those who submit."* (Qur'an 39:12)

And this sūrah will give him the same at v. 104: *"And I am commanded to be among the believers."*

The consistency establishes something about the structure of authority in Islam. The messenger is not above the message. He is its first recipient and its most bound subject — a point already made at v. 15, where he declared that he feared punishment if he disobeyed.

**The Two Halves of the Verse Together**

Read as a unit, the verse gives the complete posture of the messenger: *I want nothing from you, and I am not acting on my own initiative.*

Both halves remove a motive. The first removes material interest; the second removes personal ambition. What remains, if the claim is false, is only delusion — and delusion is not consistent with the sustained, coherent, and practically successful work that prophets performed.

This is the structure of the Qur'an's argument about its own messenger, and it is why the sūrah spent its early verses on his biography (v. 16) and his inability to alter the text (v. 15). Noah's speech, recited to the Makkans, is the same argument transposed into a story they could not dispute.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:73

> **Yet, they denied him. So We saved him and those with him in the Ark. And We made them vicegerents, and We drowned those who denied Our signs. So observe how those who were warned fared in the end.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Outcome, Compressed**

*Fa-kadhdhabūhu fa-najjaynāhu wa-man maʿahu fī al-fulki wa-jaʿalnāhum khalāʾifa wa-aghraqnā alladhīna kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā.*

Nine hundred and fifty years of preaching, the building of the ark, the flood, the destruction of a civilisation, and the repopulation of the earth — all in a single verse.

The compression is a deliberate feature of Qur'anic narrative. The Qur'an is not a history book; it recounts events for the sake of the lesson they carry, and it gives detail only where detail serves that purpose. Here the point is the *pattern*, and the pattern is stated in four clauses: they denied, We saved him, We established them, We drowned the deniers.

**The Parallel Verses**

The source notes the close correspondence with two verses in Sūrat al-Aʿrāf:

> *"Yet they denied him. So We saved him and those who were with him in the Ark, and We drowned those who denied Our signs."* (Qur'an 7:64)

> *"Remember when He made you vicegerents after the people of Noah, and increased you amply in stature."* (Qur'an 7:69)

The near-identity of the wording is characteristic. The Qur'an frequently repeats narrative formulas across sūrahs with slight variation, and the variations reward attention.

**"So We Saved Him and Those With Him"**

*Najjaynāhu wa-man maʿahu.* The verb *najjā* means to deliver, to rescue from danger — from *n-j-w*, whose sense is escape and safety.

The phrasing *man maʿahu* — "those with him" — is worth noting for what it does not say. It does not say "his family" or "his people." It says: those who were *with* him.

The Qur'an is explicit that biological relation did not qualify anyone. Noah's own son drowned:

> *"And Noah called out to his Lord and said: My Lord, truly my son is of my family, and truly Thy promise is true, and Thou art the most just of judges. He said: O Noah, truly he is not of thy family; truly it is a deed not righteous. So ask Me not concerning that of which thou hast no knowledge. I admonish thee, lest thou be among the ignorant."* (Qur'an 11:45–46)

*Laysa min ahlik* — "he is not of thy family." The redefinition is total. Kinship in the sense that mattered was constituted by faith, not by blood.

This is one of the Qur'an's hardest teachings and one of its most consistent. Abraham's father was an idolater. Lot's wife perished with her town. Pharaoh's wife was among the believers. The dividing line runs through households.

**"And We Made Them Vicegerents"**

*Wa-jaʿalnāhum khalāʾif.*

The word returns from v. 14 — *khalāʾif*, successors, those who come after and take the place of others.

The theological point established there applies here with added force. The survivors of the flood were made successors *to a civilisation that had been destroyed for cause*. They inherited the earth on precisely the terms v. 14 described: an appointment, held in trust, with an explicit purpose clause — *"that We might observe how you behave."*

And there is a sobering implication in the sequence. The generations after Noah did not maintain the lesson. The very next verse of this sūrah records that messengers had to be sent to them, and that they too denied. Inheriting the earth from the destroyed did not prevent the inheritors from repeating the pattern.

**"And We Drowned Those Who Denied Our Signs"**

*Wa-aghraqnā alladhīna kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā.*

The identification of the drowned is by their *action*, not by their tribe, their status, or their relation to Noah. They are *"those who denied Our signs."*

This is characteristic of Qur'anic narrative. The condemned are almost always identified by a description of what they did rather than by a name or a category. It keeps the lesson transferable: the reader is invited to check himself against the description rather than to reassure himself that he belongs to a different group.

**"So Observe How Those Who Were Warned Fared in the End"**

*Fa-nẓur kayfa kāna ʿāqibatu al-mundharīn.*

The imperative *unẓur* — "look!" — is the same as at v. 39, and it is directed at the present audience.

But note the precise wording of the category: *al-mundharīn*, "those who were warned." Not "the deniers," not "the wrongdoers," but *the warned*.

This choice is pointed. It identifies the destroyed by the one feature they share with the audience being addressed. The Makkans listening to this recitation were, at that moment, *mundharīn* — people who had received a warning. The verse invites them to look at the outcome for the last group who occupied that position.

The Qur'an makes the same move at v. 13 (*"generations before you"*) and at v. 102 (*"Do they wait for aught save the like of the days of those who passed away before them?"*).

**The Flood as a Recurring Structure**

It is worth noting how the flood functions in the Qur'an's larger argument, because it is not merely a story about ancient punishment.

The pattern it establishes — warning, refusal, separation, deliverance of the few, destruction of the many — is presented as a template rather than an isolated event. The sūrah applies it to the messengers after Noah (v. 74), to Pharaoh (vv. 75–92), and, by clear implication, to the Makkans.

And the Qur'an states the template's continuing operation as a general promise, which the sūrah will make explicit at v. 103:
