> *"Then We save Our messengers and those who believe. Thus is it incumbent upon Us to save the believers."* (Qur'an 10:103)

**A Note on the Ark**

The ark itself deserves a moment, because it is one of the Qur'an's most resonant images.

Sūrah Hūd describes its construction as an act of sustained public humiliation:

> *"And he was making the ark, and whenever the notables of his people passed by him, they mocked him. He said: If you mock us, we shall mock you as you mock. And you will surely know upon whom will come a punishment that disgraces him."* (Qur'an 11:38–39)

An old man building a large ship on dry land, over an extended period, in full public view, on the strength of a warning about water that had not appeared. It is difficult to construct a more complete image of faith exercised against every available appearance.

The Qur'an also records the ark's function as a lasting sign:

> *"And We carried him upon a thing of planks and nails, sailing under Our eyes — a recompense for him who had been rejected. And We left it as a sign; so is there any who will remember?"* (Qur'an 54:13–15)

The phrase *tajrī bi-aʿyuninā* — "sailing under Our eyes" — is one of the most tender in the Qur'an. The tiny vessel on a drowned world, watched.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:74

> **Then, after him, We sent messengers unto their people, and they brought them clear proofs. But it was not for them to believe in that which they had denied beforehand. Thus do We set a seal upon the hearts of the transgressors.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Pattern Repeats**

*Thumma baʿathnā min baʿdihi rusulan ilā qawmihim fa-jāʾūhum bi-l-bayyināt.*

The narrative does not proceed to a single next story. It summarises an entire era: after Noah, messengers were sent, repeatedly, to their own peoples.

Al-Kalbī and al-Qurṭubī identify these messengers as the pre-Islamic Arabian prophets Hūd, Ṣāliḥ and Shuʿayb, together with Abraham and Lot, among others.

The compression makes a point that individual stories cannot. What is being described is not a series of exceptional events but a *standing arrangement*: guidance sent, repeatedly, to every people — the doctrine stated at v. 47, *"For every community there is a messenger."*

**"They Brought Them Clear Proofs"**

*Bi-l-bayyināt.* The same term as at v. 13: evidence that makes a matter unmistakable, from *b-y-n*, to be distinct.

The clause is essential to the justice of what follows. No community described here was condemned for failing to work out the truth unaided. Each was given *bayyināt*.

**"It Was Not for Them to Believe in That Which They Had Denied Beforehand"**

*Fa-mā kānū li-yuʾminū bi-mā kadhdhabū bihi min qabl.*

This is the verse's central and most difficult clause, and al-Bayḍāwī's gloss is precise: those people who rejected their respective messengers were already habituated to denying the truth, thereby closing themselves off to the possibility of accepting divine guidance when it was brought to them.

The construction *mā kānū li-yuʾminū* — encountered at v. 13 — expresses settled disposition: *it was not in them to believe.*

**The Mechanism of Habituated Denial**

The claim being made is psychological before it is theological, and it can be examined on its own terms.

Denial is not a single act; it is a practice. And practices form dispositions.

Consider what happens when a person publicly rejects a claim. He has now made a commitment. Others associate the position with him. His social relations are organised around it. To reverse would require admitting error before people who watched him assert it. And each repetition of the rejection deepens the commitment, until reversal would require the dismantling of an identity.

At that point the person is not weighing evidence at all. He is defending a self. And any new evidence is processed not as information but as a threat.

The Qur'an describes this state as a *closure* — and this is the meaning of the sealing that follows.

**"Thus Do We Set a Seal Upon the Hearts of the Transgressors"**

*Kadhālika naṭbaʿu ʿalā qulūbi al-muʿtadīn.*

The source directs the reader to 2:7 for the doctrine of the sealing of hearts:

> *"God has set a seal upon their hearts and upon their hearing, and over their eyes is a covering, and theirs is a tremendous punishment."* (Qur'an 2:7)

*Ṭabaʿa* means to stamp, to impress a seal — the word used for sealing a letter with wax so that nothing can enter or leave. The related word *khatama* (2:7) has the same sense.

**How the Sealing Is Consistent With Justice**

This doctrine has troubled readers in every generation, and it must be addressed squarely, because on a careless reading it makes God the author of the disbelief He then punishes.

Three considerations resolve it, and all three are supplied by the Qur'an itself.

*The sealing follows the transgression; it does not precede it.* The verse's own grammar establishes this. It is the hearts of *al-muʿtadīn* that are sealed — the transgressors, those who have already exceeded the bounds (*iʿtadā*, from *ʿ-d-w*, to overstep). The sealing is a response to a prior condition. Compare 61:5: *"So when they deviated, God caused their hearts to deviate."*

*The sealing describes a natural law, and God is the author of natural laws.* When we say that a muscle atrophies from disuse, we describe a process; attributing it to God is a statement about who established the process, not a claim that God individually weakened each fibre. The Qur'an attributes the moral analogue to God for the same reason it attributes rainfall to Him: because He is the author of the order within which such processes operate.

*And this sūrah has already ruled out injustice in the plainest terms.* Verse 44: *"Truly God does not wrong human beings in the least, but rather human beings wrong themselves."* Any reading of the sealing that contradicts that verse is a misreading, since the Qur'an does not contradict itself.

**The Warning to the Reader**

The practical implication is not speculation about who is sealed. It is urgency about the *first* refusals, because the process the verse describes is gradual and begins small.

Every hardening starts as a single act of turning away, and at that stage the reversal is easy and costs almost nothing. What makes it expensive later is accumulation — the commitment publicly made, the identity constructed, the habit repeated. The Prophet's ﷺ hadith of the black spot, quoted at v. 33, describes exactly this incremental mechanism and names the point at which it becomes the *rān* of 83:14.

The remedy is correspondingly simple and correspondingly urgent: respond to the first recognition rather than the tenth. The Qur'an praises exactly this promptness:

> *"And those who, when they commit an indecency or wrong themselves, remember God and seek forgiveness for their sins — and who forgives sins but God? — and do not knowingly persist in what they did."* (Qur'an 3:135)

The operative phrase is *wa-lam yuṣirrū*: they did not *persist*. Persistence is what converts an act into a disposition, and a disposition into a seal.

**A Note on Reading This Verse About Others**

Nothing in this verse licenses the identification of any living person as sealed. The point established at v. 40 stands: God alone knows which of those currently rejecting will come to believe, and the history of the first Muslim generation is a record of the most confident such predictions being overturned.

What the verse describes is a mechanism, disclosed so that one may avoid it — not a roster, disclosed so that one may consult it.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:75

> **Then, after them, We sent Moses and Aaron with Our signs to Pharaoh and his notables. But they waxed arrogant, and they were a guilty people.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Longest Narrative in the Sūrah Begins**

*Thumma baʿathnā min baʿdihim Mūsā wa-Hārūna bi-āyātinā ilā Firʿawna wa-malaʾih.*

From here to v. 93 — nineteen verses — the sūrah recounts the confrontation between Moses and Pharaoh. It is the longest sustained narrative in the chapter, and its length signals its importance to the argument.

The source lists the other principal accounts: 2:49–61; 7:103–155; 20:9–97; 26:10–66; 28:3–46; 79:15–25. Moses is the most frequently mentioned prophet in the Qur'an, named some 136 times.

**Why This Story, and Why at This Length**

The choice is not arbitrary. The Moses narrative offers the Makkan audience — and every subsequent reader — a case with specific features that the Noah story lacks.

Noah's opponents were a people. Pharaoh's opposition is a *state*: an organised political order with a ruler, an elite (*malaʾ*), a professional class of religious functionaries (the sorcerers), and the coercive apparatus to enforce its will.

This makes the story directly applicable to the sūrah's situation. Quraysh was not a mob; it was a governing structure with a *malaʾ* of its own — the assembly of clan chiefs who met at the *dār al-nadwah*. The Qur'an uses the same word for both.

And the story has an outcome that was known to everyone: the empire fell, and the man it had enslaved and hunted survived it.

**"Moses and Aaron" — A Mission Given to Two**

The pairing is significant, and its origin is recorded in the Qur'an. When commanded to go to Pharaoh, Moses made a specific request:

> *"He said: My Lord, expand for me my breast, and ease for me my task, and untie the knot from my tongue, that they may understand my speech. And appoint for me a helper from my family — Aaron, my brother. Strengthen my back through him, and let him share my task, that we may glorify Thee much and remember Thee much."* (Qur'an 20:25–34)

And the request was granted: *"You have been given your request, O Moses"* (20:36).

Two observations follow.

*A prophet asked for help and was not rebuked for it.* The request acknowledged a limitation — a difficulty in speech, and the weight of the task — and it was answered. There is a lesson here about the legitimacy of recognising one's own insufficiency and asking for support.

*And the mission was given jointly.* God addresses them together: *"Go, both of you, to Pharaoh; truly he has transgressed. And speak to him gently; perhaps he may remember or fear"* (20:43–44).

That instruction — *qawlan layyinan*, gentle speech, addressed to the most tyrannical figure in the Qur'an — is one of the most remarkable in scripture, and it is worth remembering whenever the manner of religious speech is under discussion.

**"With Our Signs"**

*Bi-āyātinā.* The commentator Muqātil notes that the signs include Moses' hand and staff. The Qur'an refers to them collectively as *"nine signs"* (17:101; 27:12), and the fuller list — the flood, the locusts, the lice, the frogs, the blood, and others — appears at 7:133.

The story of Moses is the Qur'an's principal case of a prophet given overwhelming, repeated, publicly verifiable miracles. And its outcome is the sūrah's answer to the demand for signs made at v. 20. Pharaoh saw every one of them and believed none.

**"Pharaoh and His Notables"**

*Firʿawn wa-malaʾuh.*

*Firʿawn* is a title rather than a personal name — the Qur'an, unlike the biblical account, never assigns him a name, which has the effect of making him a type rather than an individual.

*Malaʾ* is from *m-l-ʾ*, to fill. The *malaʾ* are those who *fill* an assembly — the notables whose presence constitutes the council, and whose bearing fills the eye. Ibn ʿAṭiyyah, al-Qummī and al-Thaʿlabī describe them as a group of dignitaries who had the highest social standing in Egyptian society.

The Qur'an consistently identifies the *malaʾ* as the primary source of opposition to prophets. It is the *malaʾ* of Noah's people who accuse him of error (7:60), the *malaʾ* of ʿĀd who call Hūd a fool (7:66), the *malaʾ* of Ṣāliḥ's people who oppress the weak believers (7:75), and the *malaʾ* of Pharaoh here.

The pattern is not accidental, and the Qur'an explains it at 34:34–35:

> *"And We sent not into any town a warner but that its affluent ones said: Truly we disbelieve in that with which you have been sent. And they said: We have more wealth and children, and we shall not be punished."*

Those with the most invested in the existing arrangement have the most to lose from a message that unsettles it. This is not a claim that wealth is evil; it is an observation about incentive.

**"They Waxed Arrogant"**

*Fa-stakbarū.*

The verb is form X of *k-b-r*, to be great: *istakbara* means to make oneself great, to consider oneself great. Muqātil and al-Maybudī gloss it: they were too proud to believe.

Arrogance is, in the Qur'an's account, the original sin — the sin of Iblīs, who refused to prostrate to Adam:

> *"He refused and was arrogant (istakbara), and was among the disbelievers."* (Qur'an 2:34)

And the Qur'an states its consequence directly, in a verse the source cites:

> *"Truly those who are too arrogant to worship Me shall enter Hell debased."* (Qur'an 40:60)

**Why Arrogance Specifically Blocks Faith**

The connection between pride and unbelief is not incidental, and it is worth setting out because it explains the whole Pharaoh narrative.

Faith requires two admissions that pride cannot make: *I did not know*, and *I am not in charge*.

The first is required because accepting a message means conceding that someone else had access to a truth one lacked. The second is required because *islām* means surrender — the handing over of one's will to another's.

For an ordinary person these admissions are difficult. For a man who has been treated as divine, they are impossible without the destruction of everything he is. Pharaoh's claim, recorded in the Qur'an, was total:

> *"I am your lord, most high."* (Qur'an 79:24)

> *"O notables, I know of no god for you other than myself."* (Qur'an 28:38)

A man who has said this in public cannot accept a message about God's oneness without ceasing to exist as the person he has been.

The Prophet ﷺ gave the definition that ties arrogance to this sūrah's whole argument:

> *"No one shall enter Paradise who has an atom's weight of arrogance in his heart."* A man said: *"But a man likes his clothes to be good and his sandals to be good."* He said: *"God is beautiful and loves beauty. Arrogance is rejecting the truth and looking down on people."* (Muslim 91, from Ibn Masʿūd)

*Baṭar al-ḥaqq* — rejecting the truth. That is the definition, and it is exactly what this verse describes.

**"And They Were a Guilty People"**

*Wa-kānū qawman mujrimīn.*

*Mujrim*, as examined at v. 13, is from *j-r-m*, to cut or sever. The *mujrim* has severed himself from truth and obligation.

The clause functions as a verdict appended to the description, and the tense — *kānū*, they *were* — treats it as a settled characterisation rather than an incidental fact. The narrative that follows will demonstrate it in detail: the accusation of sorcery (v. 76), the appeal to ancestral custom (v. 78), the mobilisation of state resources against a truth (v. 79), the persecution of those who believed (v. 83), and finally the deathbed conversion that came too late (v. 90).

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:76

> **So when the truth came to them from Us, they said, "Truly this is manifest sorcery."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Standard Accusation**

*Fa-lammā jāʾahumu al-ḥaqqu min ʿindinā qālū inna hādhā la-siḥrun mubīn.*

Confronted with the signs of Moses, Pharaoh's court did not deny that something extraordinary had occurred. They could not; the staff had become a serpent in front of them. What they denied was its *source*.

They said: this is *siḥr*.

**What the Accusation Accomplishes**

This is the intelligent form of denial, and it deserves to be understood rather than dismissed.

A crude denier says *nothing happened*. That position is untenable when the audience has seen the event. The sophisticated denier concedes the phenomenon and reassigns its cause to a known, non-divine category.

*Siḥr* — sorcery — was such a category in the ancient Near East. It was a recognised craft with practitioners, techniques, and a professional class. Egypt in particular was famous for it, which is precisely why Pharaoh's counter-move was to summon his own sorcerers (v. 79).

So the accusation was not obviously absurd. It was the naming of a real and familiar phenomenon as the explanation for an unfamiliar one. And it had the enormous advantage of requiring no change in anyone's beliefs or behaviour.

**"Manifest" Sorcery**

*Siḥrun mubīn* — the same word *mubīn* (from *b-y-n*, clear, evident) that describes the *bayyināt* of the previous verse.

There is a bitter irony in the choice. What was *manifestly* a sign, they declared *manifestly* sorcery. The adverb of certainty is deployed on the side of the falsehood.

This is worth noticing as a general feature of confident error. The vehemence of an assertion is not evidence of its truth, and it is often inversely related: a person who is uncertain reaches for intensifiers. Pharaoh's court did not say "this may be sorcery"; they said *inna hādhā la-siḥrun mubīn* — with the emphatic particle *inna*, the emphatic *lām*, and the adjective *mubīn*, three separate intensifiers in five words.

**The Charge Made Against Every Prophet**

The source draws the connection to the same accusation levelled at the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ, and the Qur'an records it repeatedly:

> *"And they marvel that a warner has come to them from among themselves, and the disbelievers say: This is a lying sorcerer."* (Qur'an 38:4)

> *"Likewise, no messenger came to those before them but that they said: A sorcerer or a madman!"* (Qur'an 51:52)

That last verse is the general statement. The accusation is not a considered response to particular evidence; it is the standard-issue reply, produced across cultures and centuries by people who had no contact with each other.

The Qur'an's inference from this is at 51:53:

> *"Have they enjoined this upon one another? Nay, but they are a rebellious people."*

The uniformity of the response has a cause, and the cause is not in the evidence. It is in the disposition of those responding.

**The Specific Case of the Qur'an**

The accusation against the Prophet ﷺ is instructive because in his case there were no serpents and no parted seas. The sign was a text.

And yet the charge was the same, which tells us something about what the Makkans experienced when they heard the Qur'an recited. The most celebrated instance is that of al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah, whose deliberation is recorded in Sūrat al-Muddaththir (74:18–25) and preserved in the biographical literature.

Al-Walīd, asked to produce a verdict on Muhammad ﷺ that Quraysh could take to the pilgrims, went through the options aloud. A soothsayer? He has none of their mutterings. A madman? We have seen no madness in him. A poet? We know poetry in all its forms, and this is not it. A liar? He has never been known to lie. And then he settled on: *"This is naught but sorcery handed down"* (74:24).

The Qur'an's comment on this process is severe:

> *"Truly he reflected and deliberated. So may he be killed! How he deliberated! Then may he be killed! How he deliberated! Then he looked. Then he frowned and scowled. Then he turned away and waxed arrogant. Then he said: This is naught but sorcery handed down; this is naught but the speech of a mortal."* (Qur'an 74:18–25)

Note *thumma istakbara* — "then he waxed arrogant" — the same verb as v. 75. The sequence is: reflection, then arrogance, then verdict. The verdict came after the arrogance, not after the reflection.

**Why "Sorcery" Was the Chosen Word**

There is a linguistic point worth making, because it illuminates what the accusers were actually conceding.

*Siḥr* in Arabic usage covers more than the occult. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Truly some eloquence is sorcery."* (al-Bukhārī 5767)

The sense is that certain speech affects its hearers in a way that resembles enchantment — it moves them beyond their capacity to resist.

When the Makkans called the Qur'an *siḥr*, they were therefore admitting something remarkable: that the text had a power over them they could not account for and could not withstand. They needed a word for an effect they were experiencing. The word they chose acknowledged the effect while denying its source.

This is why the Qur'an's response to them was never to deny the effect but to challenge its explanation — the *taḥaddī* examined at v. 38. If it is a human craft, produce it.

**The Modern Form of the Same Move**

The specific vocabulary changes; the structure does not.

The characteristic contemporary version of "this is manifest sorcery" is the explanation that reassigns a phenomenon to a category that dissolves its claim on us. Religious experience becomes a neurological event; moral conviction becomes an evolved disposition; the coherence of a text becomes a psychological projection of the reader.

Some of these explanations may be partly correct as descriptions of mechanism. The error lies in treating the description of a mechanism as a refutation of a claim — what philosophers call the genetic fallacy. That an experience has a neural correlate says nothing about whether its object exists; every true perception also has a neural correlate.

Pharaoh's court made exactly this error. They named a mechanism — *siḥr* — and treated the naming as an answer. It was not an answer, and the events that followed demonstrated it.

**The Practical Lesson**

For the believer, the verse counsels a specific patience.

When one presents evidence and it is reassigned rather than examined, argument has reached its limit. The obstacle is not informational, and supplying more information will not remove it. The sūrah has said this repeatedly: v. 42 (they hear but do not listen), v. 43 (they look but do not see), v. 74 (the sealed heart).

What remains is what Noah did at v. 71 and what Moses will do at v. 84: state the position clearly, place one's trust in God, and continue. The outcome is not the messenger's responsibility — a principle the sūrah will state to the Prophet ﷺ directly at v. 99.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:77

> **Moses said, "Do you say this about the truth when it comes to you? Is this sorcery? Sorcerers will not prosper."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reply**

*Qāla Mūsā a-taqūlūna li-l-ḥaqqi lammā jāʾakum. A-siḥrun hādhā wa-lā yufliḥu al-sāḥirūn.*

Moses answers with two questions and a statement. The structure is worth tracing, because it is a compressed piece of reasoning rather than an exclamation.

**The First Question: The Charge of Reflexive Rejection**

*A-taqūlūna li-l-ḥaqqi lammā jāʾakum?* — "Do you say this about the truth when it comes to you?"

The interrogative here is *inkārī* — rhetorical, expressing rebuke rather than requesting information.

But note the precise object of the rebuke. Moses does not say "do you say this about *my signs*." He says "do you say this about *al-ḥaqq* — the truth." He is naming the thing correctly and forcing them to face what their verdict applies to.

And the temporal clause is pointed: *lammā jāʾakum* — "when it came to you." The accusation was produced immediately, at the moment of arrival, before examination. Moses is identifying the speed of the response as evidence of its character. A verdict delivered instantly is not a verdict; it is a reflex.

**The Second Question: The Category Error**

*A-siḥrun hādhā?* — "Is this sorcery?"

The Qur'an's word order places *siḥrun* first, which in Arabic throws the emphasis onto it: *Sorcery — is this?*

Moses is asking them to actually apply their own category and see whether it fits. And it does not, for reasons his audience was equipped to assess better than most, since Egypt housed the world's most developed school of the craft.

Sorcery, as they knew it, operated within recognisable limits. It was performed by trained practitioners; it dealt in illusion and manipulation of perception; it served the interests of those who commissioned it; and it was, above all, a *trade* — something learned, priced, and sold.

Moses' signs matched none of this. He had no training in the craft; he had been raised in Pharaoh's own house and then spent ten years as a shepherd in Midian. He asked for no payment (a point the sūrah has just established as a mark of prophecy at v. 72). And his signs served no interest of his own — they demanded the release of slaves, which brought him nothing.

**The Statement: "Sorcerers Will Not Prosper"**

*Wa-lā yufliḥu al-sāḥirūn.*

The verb is *yufliḥu* — from *f-l-ḥ*, the root examined at v. 17: to split the earth, to plough, and hence to reap. *Falāḥ* is the harvest that follows honest labour.

The statement is a general law, not a prediction about a particular contest. And it can be read at several levels.

*Sorcery has no truth in it, so it cannot bear fruit.* Illusion produces nothing; it only rearranges appearances. A craft that works by deception has nothing to harvest at the end.

*Sorcery is forbidden, so its practitioners are ruined in the Hereafter.* The Qur'an states this of Hārūt and Mārūt's students:

> *"And they knew well that whoever purchased it would have no share in the Hereafter. Evil indeed is that for which they sold their souls, if they but knew."* (Qur'an 2:102)

*And a claim founded on deception cannot survive contact with reality.* This is the sense that connects to the sūrah's argument. Verse 81 will state the outcome: *"God will bring it to naught. Truly God makes not right the work of those who work corruption."*

**The Argument Turned Back**

There is a further logical force to Moses' statement that is easy to miss.

If sorcerers do not prosper, and if Moses were a sorcerer, then his enterprise would fail. He is, in effect, offering them a test and inviting them to wait for the result.

And the result came. The most famous sorcerers in Egypt were assembled against him (v. 79), and they lost — not merely in the contest, but in their allegiance. The Qur'an records their immediate prostration and their reply to Pharaoh's threats:

> *"They said: We shall never prefer thee over the clear proofs that have come to us, nor over Him who originated us. So decree whatever thou wilt decree; thou canst only decree for this life of the world."* (Qur'an 20:72)

The professionals recognised what the amateurs could not. They knew what their own craft could do, and they knew that what they had witnessed was not it. The Qur'an presents their testimony as expert evidence: those best placed to identify sorcery declared this was not sorcery, and paid for the declaration with their lives.

**A Note on Method in Argument**

Moses' reply models something worth learning.

He does not become indignant, and he does not counter-accuse. He asks them to *apply their own claim*. The whole force of his response is: you have named a category; check whether the thing belongs to it.

This is a more effective procedure than assertion, because it makes the interlocutor do the work. And it respects him — it treats him as capable of reasoning, which is itself a form of courtesy that the sūrah's earlier verses associated with *daʿwah*.

The Qur'an uses the same method constantly. *"Say: Bring your proof, if you are truthful"* (2:111). *"Have they been created from nothing, or are they the creators?"* (52:35). *"Say: Have you considered what you invoke apart from God? Show me what they have created of the earth"* (46:4).

The consistency of the method is itself instructive for anyone who takes up religious argument.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:78

> **They said, "Have you come to us to turn us away from that which we found our fathers practicing, that the two of you might have greatness in the land? We are not believers in the two of you."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Objection: Ancestral Custom**

*Qālū a-jiʾtanā li-talfitanā ʿammā wajadnā ʿalayhi ābāʾanā?*

Having called it sorcery, they now shift ground. This is the second of three arguments Pharaoh's court will deploy, and it is entirely different in kind from the first.

The first was an epistemological claim: *this is sorcery, not revelation.* The second is not a claim about truth at all. It is an appeal to inheritance.

**"To Turn Us Away"**

*Li-talfitanā.* The verb is from *l-f-t*, to twist, to turn something from its direction — the root of *iltifāt*, the turning of the head or gaze.

The choice of verb is revealing. It presents change as *distortion*. Their position is not described as a claim that might be false; it is described as a direction, and Moses is accused of *bending* them out of it.

**"That Which We Found Our Fathers Practicing"**

*ʿAmmā wajadnā ʿalayhi ābāʾanā.*

The phrasing is exact and it recurs throughout the Qur'an with almost no variation. *Wajadnā* — "we found." Not "we investigated," not "we concluded," not "we were taught and verified." We *found*. It was there when we arrived.

The Qur'an records this as the standard reply of every people to every prophet:

> *"And when it is said to them: Follow what God has sent down, they say: Nay, we follow that which we found our fathers doing. What! Even if their fathers understood nothing and were not rightly guided?"* (Qur'an 2:170)

> *"And likewise, We sent no warner unto a town before thee but that those living in luxury therein said: Truly we found our fathers upon a creed, and we are following in their footsteps."* (Qur'an 43:23)

That second verse repays attention. It identifies the speakers as *mutrafūhā* — those living in luxury — the same class as the *malaʾ* of v. 75. The connection between comfort and traditionalism is not coincidental: those whom the existing order serves have the strongest reason to defend it, and "our fathers did this" is a more respectable defence than "this arrangement suits me."

**The Qur'an's Response to Traditionalism**

The reply given at 2:170 is a single question: *a-wa-law kāna ābāʾuhum lā yaʿqilūna shayʾan wa-lā yahtadūn?* — "Even if their fathers understood nothing and were not guided?"

This is the whole refutation, and it is decisive.

The appeal to ancestry is not an argument, because it does not address the question at issue. If the fathers were right, then what they practised is true — but then its truth, not its ancestry, is the reason to hold it. If the fathers were wrong, then its ancestry is no reason at all. Either way the appeal does no work. The question of whether they were right must be settled independently, and once it is settled, the appeal is redundant.

**What Tradition Legitimately Is**

It would be a serious misreading to take this as a blanket condemnation of inherited practice, and Islam is emphatically not a tradition-hostile religion. It transmits its own knowledge through chains of teachers (*isnād*); it venerates the first three generations (*al-salaf al-ṣāliḥ*); it maintains schools of law defined by continuity; and the Prophet ﷺ warned against innovation in worship.

The distinction is between *taqlīd* as a substitute for truth and transmission as a *conveyor* of truth.

The Qur'an's objection is not that the Makkans inherited their religion. It is that they treated inheritance as a *reason* — as something that settled the question of truth without reference to evidence. Islamic tradition, by contrast, always grounds its authority in a claim about origin: this practice is valid because it goes back, through a verifiable chain, to revelation. The chain is a mechanism of verification, not a substitute for it.

The test is simple: ask a traditionalist *why*. If the answer is "because it is true, and here is how we know," the tradition is functioning as transmission. If the answer is "because that is what we do," it is functioning as *taqlīd*.

**"That the Two of You Might Have Greatness in the Land"**

*Wa-takūna lakumā al-kibriyāʾu fī al-arḍ.*

The third element of their reply is an accusation of motive, and it is the most psychologically revealing thing in the verse.

*Kibriyāʾ* is from *k-b-r* — the same root as *istakbarū* in v. 75. It means greatness, supremacy, dominion.

The accusation is that Moses and Aaron want power.

And here the irony is complete, and the Qur'an does not need to point it out. It is *Pharaoh's court* making this charge — a court whose entire existence was organised around one man's claim to be lord most high, a court that had enslaved a nation and was killing its infant sons. They accuse two men, one of them a fugitive shepherd, of wanting *kibriyāʾ*.

**Projection as a Mode of Accusation**

The mechanism at work here is universal and worth naming.

People tend to explain others' behaviour by reference to the motives they themselves would have. A person for whom every action is a bid for advantage will read every action as a bid for advantage; the possibility of disinterested conviction is not available to him, because he has no experience of it from the inside.

This is why the *malaʾ* could not process what Moses was doing. Within their model of human behaviour, a man who challenges a ruler in public is making a play for the throne. There is no other slot to put him in. Their accusation is not a lie in the ordinary sense; it is a sincere misreading produced by a limited moral imagination.

Note also that the verse pairs the accusation with the appeal to tradition. This is characteristic. The defender of an inherited order rarely stops at defending it — he also impugns the challenger's motives, because the two moves together relieve him of the obligation to examine the challenge. *It is what we have always done, and anyway he only wants power.*

**The Answer Already Given**

The sūrah has pre-empted this accusation. Verse 72, in Noah's mouth: *"I have not asked of you any reward."* And it will be demonstrated in the narrative that follows: Moses' demand was never for a throne. It was, in the words the Qur'an gives him, *"Send forth with us the Children of Israel"* (26:17). He asked for the release of slaves and then led them into the desert.

**"We Are Not Believers in the Two of You"**

*Wa-mā naḥnu lakumā bi-muʾminīn.*

The conclusion is a flat declaration, and its grammar is emphatic: the negative *mā* with the pronoun, the fronted *lakumā*, the redundant *bi-* prefixed to the predicate — every device Arabic has for underlining a refusal.

And note that they say *lakumā bi-muʾminīn* — "believers *in you two*," addressing the messengers rather than the message. The whole verse has been about persons: Moses and Aaron, their supposed ambitions, the fathers. The content of what they brought is never discussed.

This is the shape of the reply throughout: an argument about the truth met with an argument about people.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:79

> **And Pharaoh said, "Bring me every knowledgeable sorcerer."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The State Responds**

*Wa-qāla Firʿawnu-ʾtūnī bi-kulli sāḥirin ʿalīm.*

Having declared the matter sorcery, Pharaoh acts on the declaration. He summons the professionals.

The verse is short, but the move it records is significant, and there are two ways to read it.

**Reading One: Confidence**

On the surface this is a confident act. If the phenomenon is sorcery, then better sorcerers will surpass it. Pharaoh has an entire national institution at his disposal, and he deploys it.

The word he uses is *ʿalīm* — knowledgeable, expert, from *ʿ-l-m*, knowledge. He does not want ordinary practitioners; he wants the best. And the Qur'an elsewhere records the scale of the mobilisation: the sorcerers were gathered *"for the appointed time of a well-known day"* (26:38), and the people were summoned to assemble in the forenoon (20:59) — a national spectacle, publicly announced.

**Reading Two: Anxiety**

But the mobilisation itself is a confession.

If the matter were as obvious as the court declared at v. 76 — *"manifest sorcery"* — no response would be required. One does not summon the nation's entire guild of experts to deal with something manifest.

The scale of the counter-measure reveals the size of the threat as Pharaoh privately assessed it. And the Qur'an gives us his private assessment elsewhere, in the words of his own advisers:

> *"They said: Put him and his brother off a while, and send heralds into the cities to bring thee every knowledgeable sorcerer."* (Qur'an 7:111–112)

*Arjih* — "put him off," delay him. The court needed time. And in Sūrat Ṭā Hā the fear is stated openly:

> *"He said: Hast thou come to us to drive us from our land with thy sorcery, O Moses?"* (Qur'an 20:57)

The ruler who claimed to be lord most high was afraid of being driven from his land by a man with a stick.

**The Instrumentalisation of Expertise**

There is a broader theme here that the narrative will develop and that deserves naming at its point of origin.

Pharaoh's response to a truth claim is not to investigate it but to *hire against it*. He does not ask whether Moses might be right. He asks who can be brought in to produce a competing display.

This is the characteristic move of power confronted by a claim it cannot afford to be true: it does not engage the claim; it commissions a counter-claim. The scholars, the experts, the credentialed authorities are summoned — not to determine what is the case, but to produce the conclusion the patron requires.

The Qur'an will record what the sorcerers themselves expected from the arrangement. When they arrive, their first question is about the fee:

> *"And the sorcerers came to Pharaoh. They said: Truly there will be a reward for us if we are the victors. He said: Yes, and you shall then be among those brought nigh."* (Qur'an 7:113–114)

Payment, and proximity to power. This is exactly the arrangement that v. 72 identified as the mark of the *false* claim — and here it is, in the open, on the other side.

The contrast is the whole point of the sequence. Moses: *"I ask of you no reward."* The sorcerers: *"Truly there will be a reward for us."* The sūrah has given the criterion at v. 72 and now stages a demonstration of it.

**What the Sorcerers Discovered**

The outcome is one of the great reversals in the Qur'an, and the verses that follow (80–82) will treat it. But it is worth anticipating here, because it completes the meaning of Pharaoh's summons.

The experts he hired became the first believers.

They came for a fee and proximity to the throne. They saw what happened to their ropes and staves, and they knew — as no one else in that assembly could know — that they had just witnessed something outside their craft. And they prostrated.

Pharaoh's threat was immediate and specific:

> *"He said: You have believed in him before I gave you permission. Truly he is your chief who taught you sorcery. I shall surely cut off your hands and your feet on opposite sides, and I shall surely crucify you on the trunks of palm trees, and you shall surely know which of us is more severe in punishment and more lasting."* (Qur'an 20:71)

And their answer, quoted above at v. 77, contains the sentence that measures the whole distance between them and the court that had employed them: *"Thou canst only decree for this life of the world"* (20:72).

Men who had arrived that morning negotiating a fee were, by the afternoon, indifferent to torture. Nothing in the sūrah illustrates more vividly the transformation that recognition of truth can produce — or how quickly it can occur when the obstacle removed is ignorance rather than arrogance.

**"Bring Me" — The Grammar of Command**

A final small observation. *Iʾtūnī* — "bring *me*."

Pharaoh's speech throughout the Qur'anic narrative is saturated with the first person. *"I am your lord, most high"* (79:24). *"I know of no god for you other than myself"* (28:38). *"Is not the kingdom of Egypt mine?"* (43:51). *"I shall surely cut off your hands"* (20:71).

Compare the speech of Moses in the same narrative, which is saturated with the third person and the divine name: *"the truth… from Us"* (v. 76), *"my Lord"*, *"upon God we rely"* (v. 84).

The two grammars are the two theologies. One organises reality around a self; the other around a Lord. The sūrah has been making this contrast since its opening verses, and here it is embedded in the pronouns.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:80

> **So when the sorcerers came, Moses said unto them, "Cast down what you would cast."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Contest Begins**

*Fa-lammā jāʾa al-saḥaratu qāla lahum Mūsā alqū mā antum mulqūn.*

The sorcerers arrive, and Moses speaks first — but what he says is an invitation to *them* to go first.

**Why Moses Let Them Begin**

The concession is not incidental, and the Qur'an records it in more than one account. In Sūrat al-Aʿrāf the sorcerers themselves raise the question:

> *"They said: O Moses, either thou cast, or we shall be the ones who cast. He said: Cast! And when they cast, they bewitched the eyes of the people and struck them with awe, and produced a mighty sorcery."* (Qur'an 7:115–116)

And in Sūrah Ṭā Hā:

> *"They said: O Moses, either thou cast, or we shall be the first to cast. He said: Nay, you cast."* (Qur'an 20:65–66)

*Bal alqū* — "Nay, you cast." Moses actively declines the first position.

Several reasons can be discerned, and they are complementary rather than competing.

*It is the confidence of one who knows the outcome.* A man uncertain of his case wants to speak first, to frame the terms. A man certain of it can afford to let the other side exhaust its resources.

*It allows the sorcerers to deploy everything they have.* Whatever they produce will be their maximum. If Moses' sign then surpasses it, the demonstration is complete and unanswerable — no one can say the sorcerers were unprepared or that they had more in reserve.

*And it removes any suggestion of interference.* Had Moses gone first, it might have been claimed that his display disrupted theirs. By going second he ensures their performance is entirely their own.

The Qur'an notes that their performance was formidable. *"They bewitched the eyes of the people and struck them with awe, and produced a mighty sorcery"* (7:116) — *siḥrin ʿaẓīm*. And Moses himself was affected: *"And Moses conceived a fear within himself"* (20:67), for which he received the reassurance *"Fear not; truly thou art the higher"* (20:68).

That detail is precious. The prophet felt fear at the sight and was reassured — not rebuked. Fear is not the opposite of faith; acting against the truth out of fear is.

**"Cast Down What You Would Cast"**

*Alqū mā antum mulqūn.*

The construction is an imperative followed by a participial phrase: *cast whatever you are casters of.* Arabic uses this pattern to indicate totality — everything you are prepared to cast, hold nothing back.

Al-Qurṭubī and others note that this is not a command in the legal sense — Moses is not *ordering* an act of sorcery, which would be forbidden. It is what the grammarians call *amr al-taʿjīz* or *amr al-taḥaddī*: a command issued to demonstrate the incapacity of the one commanded.

The Qur'an uses the same device against the Makkans in the challenge examined at v. 38: *"Then bring a sūrah like it."* The imperative there is likewise not an instruction but a demonstration.

**The Courtesy of the Prophet**

There is something in Moses' manner here that repays attention, given what these men had come to do.

They had come as hired opponents, for a fee, to discredit him publicly and thereby to keep a nation in slavery. And he addresses them without insult, gives them the advantage of the first move, and invites them to do their utmost.

The Qur'an in Sūrah Ṭā Hā records that before the contest he offered them a warning that was also a counsel:

> *"Moses said unto them: Woe unto you! Do not fabricate a lie against God, lest He destroy you with a punishment. Whosoever fabricates has failed."* (Qur'an 20:61)

*Waylakum* — "woe to you" — is a warning, not an insult; it is followed immediately by the reason. He is telling them what they stand to lose.

And the Qur'an records the effect: *"Then they disputed their affair among themselves and kept secret their private counsel"* (20:62). His words caused a disagreement in their ranks before the contest even began. Some of the commentators say this warning was the seed of their later belief.

This is worth holding onto. The most consequential thing Moses said to the sorcerers may not have been what he did with his staff, but the sentence of warning he gave them beforehand — the recognition that even hired opponents are human beings with souls at stake.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:81

> **And when they cast, Moses said, "What you have brought is sorcery. God will bring it to naught. Truly God makes not right the work of those who work corruption.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verdict**

*Fa-lammā alqaw qāla Mūsā mā jiʾtum bihi al-siḥr. Inna Allāha sa-yubṭiluh.*

They cast. And before anything has happened, Moses names it.

**"What You Have Brought Is Sorcery"**

The identification is flat and immediate. Whatever the crowd saw — and the Qur'an says the ropes and staves *appeared* to move (20:66) — Moses saw a craft.

Note the precision of the diagnosis. He does not say "what you have brought is nothing" or "you have done nothing." He concedes that something has been produced, and he classifies it correctly.

There is an important discipline here. Truth does not require the denial of an opponent's real capacities. The sorcerers were genuinely skilled; their display was genuinely impressive; the crowd was genuinely affected. Moses grants all of it and simply names what it is.

**The Nature of What They Produced**

The Qur'an is careful about the mechanism, and the care is theologically significant:

> *"So behold, their ropes and their staffs appeared to him, from their sorcery, to be moving swiftly."* (Qur'an 20:66)

*Yukhayyalu ilayhi* — "it was made to *appear* to him." The verb is from *kh-y-l*, the root of *khayāl*, imagination or phantom.

And at 7:116: *"they bewitched the eyes of the people" — saḥarū aʿyuna al-nās.* The effect was in the *eyes* of the observers.

The Qur'an therefore locates sorcery in the domain of perception rather than creation. Nothing was actually transformed; perception was manipulated. This matters doctrinally, because it preserves the distinction between the sorcerer's illusion and the prophet's sign. Moses' staff *became* a serpent — the Qur'an uses ordinary verbs of transformation for it (*fa-idhā hiya thuʿbānun mubīn*, 7:107). The sorcerers' staves *seemed* to move.

The distinction between changing what is and changing what is seen is the distinction between a miracle and a trick, and it holds however sophisticated the trick becomes.

**"God Will Bring It to Naught"**

*Inna Allāha sa-yubṭiluh.*

*Baṭala* means to be void, false, without effect — the root of *bāṭil*, falsehood, the standing opposite of *ḥaqq* in the Qur'an's vocabulary. Form IV *abṭala* is to render void, to nullify.

Note the future particle *sa-*: *sa-yubṭiluh*, "He *will* nullify it." Moses is issuing a prediction, publicly, before the outcome, with the entire nation watching. This is the posture of a man who is not managing an impression.

And note who is named as the agent. Not "I will defeat it" but "*God* will bring it to naught." Moses removes himself from the sentence. The contest is not between his skill and theirs; it is between an illusion and reality, and he is merely present at the point where they meet.

**"God Makes Not Right the Work of Those Who Work Corruption"**

*Inna Allāha lā yuṣliḥu ʿamala al-mufsidīn.*

The verse closes with a general law, which is the pattern of Moses' speech throughout this passage (compare v. 77: *"sorcerers will not prosper"*).

The vocabulary is built on the Qur'an's central opposition: *ṣ-l-ḥ* against *f-s-d*.

*Ṣalaḥa* means to be sound, whole, functioning as it should — hence *ṣāliḥ*, righteous, and *iṣlāḥ*, repair or reform, and *ṣulḥ*, reconciliation. Form IV *aṣlaḥa* is to make sound, to set right.

*Fasada* is its exact opposite: to be spoiled, rotten, corrupted — used of food that has gone bad, of a machine that no longer works. *Fasād* is the breakdown of an order that was meant to function.

So the sentence reads: *God does not make sound the work of those who spoil.*

**Why Corruption Cannot Be Made to Work**

This is not merely a promise of punishment. It is a statement about the structure of reality, and it is worth unpacking.

A thing functions when it is arranged according to its nature. A body is healthy when its organs do what they are for; a society is sound when its parts serve their purposes; a life goes well when it is lived according to what a human being actually is.

Corruption is, by definition, the disruption of that arrangement. And a disrupted arrangement cannot be made to yield the fruits of an intact one, any more than a shattered instrument can be made to play in tune by wishing.

This is why the Qur'an treats *fasād* as self-defeating rather than merely wicked. The corrupt project may run for a time on borrowed momentum — this is the *matāʿ* of v. 70 — but it has no principle of endurance in it. It is consuming a capital it did not create and cannot replenish.

The sūrah has stated this several times in different registers: the mown field of v. 24, the *baghy* recoiling on its author at v. 23, the *makr* that God out-plans at v. 21. Here it receives its most general formulation.

**The Verse as Consolation**

For the believer in a hostile setting — the Makkans hearing this, and every reader since — this verse is one of the sūrah's most sustaining.

It says that the apparatus arrayed against truth has a defect built into it. The resources may be vast; the display may be overwhelming; the crowd may be swayed. But the thing is *bāṭil*, and *bāṭil* does not hold.

The Qur'an gives the image directly:

> *"Rather, We hurl the truth against falsehood, and it crushes its skull, and behold, it vanishes. And woe unto you for that which you ascribe."* (Qur'an 21:18)

And:

> *"Say: The truth has come and falsehood has vanished. Truly falsehood is ever bound to vanish."* (Qur'an 17:81)

The Prophet ﷺ recited this second verse while circling the Kaʿbah at the conquest of Makkah, striking the idols one by one as he passed (al-Bukhārī 4287). The three hundred and sixty gods of Quraysh fell in an afternoon, and the men who had commissioned them stood watching.

**A Caution on Applying the Verse**

One qualification must be entered, because this verse is easily misused.

It does not promise that every corrupt enterprise will visibly collapse within an observer's lifetime, and the sūrah has been explicit about this. Verse 11 explains that God does not hasten the reckoning; v. 70 gives the corrupt their *matāʿ* in this world before the return; v. 46 tells the Prophet ﷺ that he may or may not live to see the outcome.

The law is certain; its timetable is not disclosed. Reading the verse as a schedule rather than a principle produces exactly the impatience the sūrah keeps warning against — and the demand *"when will this promise be fulfilled?"* which it answers at vv. 48–52.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:82

> **And God verifies the truth through His Words, though the guilty be averse."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Positive Counterpart**

*Wa-yuḥiqqu Allāhu al-ḥaqqa bi-kalimātihi wa-law kariha al-mujrimūn.*

The previous verse stated the negative law: God does not make the corrupt's work succeed. This verse states its positive counterpart: God establishes the truth.

The two together form a complete account. Falsehood is not merely left to fail; truth is actively vindicated.

**"Verifies the Truth"**

*Yuḥiqqu al-ḥaqq.* The verb and the object share the root *ḥ-q-q*, producing a construction of great density that English cannot reproduce.

*Ḥaqqa* means to be established, to be real, to be due. The noun *ḥaqq* accordingly carries all of: truth, reality, right, entitlement, and obligation. It is one of the divine names — *al-Ḥaqq*, the Real (used at v. 32 of this sūrah: *"That is God, your true Lord"*).

Form IV *aḥaqqa* is causative: to make real, to establish as true, to cause to be realised.

So *yuḥiqqu al-ḥaqqa* means something like: *He makes the real, real* — He brings into actuality what is genuinely the case, He causes truth to occupy the position that is its due.

There is an important implication in this. Truth does not become true by being vindicated; it was already true. What the vindication does is make it *manifest* and *effective*. The victory of truth is not the creation of a fact but the removal of what was obscuring it.

**"Through His Words"**

*Bi-kalimātihi.*

The commentators read *kalimāt* here in two complementary ways.

*The words of command* — the creative *kun*, "Be," by which God brings about what He wills. On this reading, God establishes truth by decree: what He says, is. The Qur'an: *"His command, when He desires a thing, is only to say to it Be, and it is"* (36:82).

*The words of revelation* — the scripture itself, whose arguments, promises, and warnings are the instrument by which truth is made evident. On this reading the verse is self-referential: the Qur'an being recited is itself the *kalimāt* by which God verifies the truth.

Both readings are sound and neither excludes the other. In the Moses narrative both are operating: the revealed message is the content of the truth, and the divine command is what causes the staff to swallow the illusion.

**"Though the Guilty Be Averse"**

*Wa-law kariha al-mujrimūn.*

The concessive clause is one of the Qur'an's recurring formulas, and its force is worth stating plainly: *the aversion of the opposition is irrelevant to the outcome.*

The same formula appears with variations:

> *"They desire to extinguish the Light of God with their mouths, but God refuses to do aught but complete His Light, though the disbelievers be averse."* (Qur'an 9:32)

> *"He it is who sent His Messenger with guidance and the religion of truth, that He may make it prevail over all religion, though the idolaters be averse."* (Qur'an 9:33; also 61:9)

The image at 9:32 is worth pausing on: men attempting to blow out the sun. It is not that their effort is wicked and will be punished — though it is and will be. It is that the effort is *absurd*, disproportionate to its object in a way that makes it almost comic.

**The Psychology of the Formula**

There is a specific consolation in *wa-law kariha* that is different from the consolation of v. 81.

Verse 81 says the opposition's work will fail. This verse says something further: the opposition's *feelings* have no bearing on the matter at all.

This is aimed at a particular kind of discouragement — the discouragement of being disliked. Anyone who holds an unpopular position knows the weight of ambient disapproval, which operates independently of arguments and is often more effective than arguments at producing conformity. The mechanism is social, not rational: human beings are built to feel the displeasure of the group.

The verse addresses this directly. Their aversion is noted, and dismissed as causally irrelevant. It does not need to be answered, refuted, or accommodated. It simply does not enter into the determination of what will be.

**The Immediate Fulfilment**

The verse's claim was demonstrated within minutes of its utterance. The Qur'an records the outcome:

> *"And We revealed unto Moses: Cast thy staff. And behold, it swallowed up what they had falsified. So the truth came to pass, and whatsoever they did was made vain. Thus were they vanquished there and turned about, humbled. And the sorcerers were cast down prostrate."* (Qur'an 7:117–120)

*Fa-waqaʿa al-ḥaqqu wa-baṭala mā kānū yaʿmalūn* — the two verbs of the previous two verses, *ḥaqqa* and *baṭala*, appearing in narrative sequence exactly as Moses had predicted them.

And the last clause is the astonishing one: *ulqiya al-saḥaratu sājidīn* — "the sorcerers were *cast down* prostrate." The passive verb *ulqiya* is from the same root as *alqū*, "cast," used three times of what they did with their ropes. They had been casting; now they were cast. The reversal is written into the verb.

Their confession follows:

> *"They said: We believe in the Lord of the worlds, the Lord of Moses and Aaron."* (Qur'an 7:121–122)

**The Deeper Point About the Sorcerers**

It bears repeating, because it is the moral centre of this passage: the only people in Egypt who believed that day were the ones who had come to oppose Moses.

Not the crowd, who saw the same events. Not the *malaʾ*, who had the best education. Not Pharaoh, who had known Moses since infancy. The hired opponents.

The explanation is expertise combined with the absence of a stake. The sorcerers alone knew precisely where the boundary of their craft lay, so they alone could recognise that this had crossed it. And they had no throne to lose — only a fee, which they abandoned in an instant.

Everyone else had something invested. That, and not the evidence, determined the outcome — which is the thesis this sūrah has argued since v. 7.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:83

> **But none believed in Moses, save some progeny among his people, for fear that Pharaoh and their notables would persecute them. And truly Pharaoh was high-handed in the land, and truly he was among the prodigal.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Meagre Result**

*Fa-mā āmana li-Mūsā illā dhurriyyatun min qawmihi ʿalā khawfin min Firʿawna wa-malaʾihim an yaftinahum.*

After the signs, the contest, and the public defeat of the sorcerers — this is the tally. *Illā dhurriyyah*: only an offspring, only a remnant.

**"Some Progeny Among His People"**

*Dhurriyyah* is from *dh-r-r*, and means offspring, descendants, progeny — usually the young.

Al-Ṭabarī and others take it in this literal sense: it was the *young* who believed. The older generation of the Children of Israel, long habituated to servitude and to accommodation with the regime, did not.

Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have said that these were a small number of the descendants of those who had come to Egypt with Joseph, and that the fear referred to was theirs.

Whether taken as "the young" or simply as "a small remnant," the point stands: the response was minimal, and it came from the least powerful.

**Why the Young**

The observation, if al-Ṭabarī's reading is taken, is psychologically acute and it recurs throughout the history of religions.

The young have less invested in the existing arrangement. They have not yet built careers within it, made the compromises it requires, or constructed identities that depend on it. They have not spent decades explaining to themselves why things are as they are.

The first Muslims in Makkah illustrate the same pattern. ʿAlī was a boy; Zayd was a youth; Muṣʿab ibn ʿUmayr, Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ, Ibn Masʿūd, Zubayr, and many others were in their teens or early twenties. The opposition — Abū Jahl, Abū Lahab, al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah, Umayyah ibn Khalaf — was the generation of the established.

This is not a rule without exceptions; Abū Bakr was of an age with the leadership, and Khadījah was older than the Prophet ﷺ. But the tendency is real, and it has an explanation that does not flatter either party. The young are more open because they have less to lose, not necessarily because they are wiser.

**"For Fear That Pharaoh and Their Notables Would Persecute Them"**

*ʿAlā khawfin min Firʿawna wa-malaʾihim an yaftinahum.*

The believers believed *in a state of fear*. The preposition *ʿalā* here indicates the condition they were in while believing — faith held under duress, not faith held in safety.

*Yaftinahum* is from *f-t-n*, the root examined at v. 85. Its original sense is the assaying of gold by fire — heating metal to separate the pure from the alloy. From this it comes to mean trial, temptation, persecution, and the civil strife that tests a community.

Here the sense is persecution: the deliberate application of pressure to break faith.

And the Qur'an records what that pressure consisted of in Egypt:

> *"And when We delivered you from the House of Pharaoh, who inflicted upon you a terrible punishment, slaying your sons and sparing your women. And in that was a great trial from your Lord."* (Qur'an 2:49)

**A Note on Fearful Faith**

The verse is remarkable for recording the fear without condemning it.

These believers are described as having believed *ʿalā khawf* — afraid. The Qur'an does not present them as deficient. It presents them as the only ones who believed at all.

This matters, because it corrects a common misunderstanding of courage. Courage is not the absence of fear; it is action taken despite fear. The Qur'an has already shown Moses himself afraid at the sorcerers' display (20:67), and the Prophet ﷺ in the cave saying to Abū Bakr *"Grieve not; truly God is with us"* (9:40) — words spoken to a frightened man.

Faith under fear is faith. It is, if anything, the more valuable kind, because it has cost something.

**"Truly Pharaoh Was High-Handed in the Land"**

*Wa-inna Firʿawna la-ʿālin fī al-arḍ.*

*ʿĀlin* is the active participle of *ʿalā*, to be high — the same root as *al-ʿAlī*, the Most High, one of God's names.

The word choice is deliberate and damning. Pharaoh had claimed *"I am your lord, most high"* (79:24), using the elative *al-aʿlā*. Here the Qur'an grants him the participle and no more: he was *high* — that is, he had exalted himself above others, in the land.

The Qur'an elsewhere uses the verbal noun of the same root for his conduct:

> *"Truly Pharaoh exalted himself in the land and divided its people into factions, oppressing a group among them, slaughtering their sons and sparing their women. Truly he was among the workers of corruption."* (Qur'an 28:4)

*Innahu kāna mina al-mufsidīn* — "among the workers of corruption," the same term Moses used at v. 81.

That verse at 28:4 gives the mechanism of his rule in a single phrase: *jaʿala ahlahā shiyaʿan*, "he divided its people into factions." Division is the technique of tyranny. A population that is fragmented cannot combine against the power that fragmented it, and a group singled out for persecution serves the additional function of giving the others someone to be grateful they are not.

**"And Truly He Was Among the Prodigal"**

*Wa-innahu la-mina al-musrifīn.*

*Musrif* is from *s-r-f*, to exceed proper bounds, to squander. *Isrāf* in ordinary usage means wastefulness — spending beyond need. The Qur'an uses it of food and drink (7:31) and of wealth (17:26–27, 25:67).

Applied to Pharaoh, the sense is transgression beyond all measure: a man who had exceeded not merely the bounds of law but the bounds of the possible for a creature, claiming for himself what belongs to God alone.

There is a further nuance worth drawing out. *Isrāf* is fundamentally *waste* — the expenditure of a resource on something that does not warrant it.

Pharaoh possessed extraordinary resources: a throne, a treasury, an army, the labour of a nation, and a mind capable of governing an empire. Every one of these was spent on the maintenance of a lie about himself. That is *isrāf* in its most exact sense: the squandering of enormous means on a worthless object.

And the Qur'an's summary of what he received for it is the verse a few pages later in this sūrah — the body preserved as a sign (v. 92), and *"an evil watering-place, that to which they are led"* (11:98).

**The Verse's Function in the Sūrah's Argument**

This verse is, in a sense, the Moses narrative's answer to the question the Makkan situation posed.

The Prophet ﷺ in Makkah had few followers, and they were mostly the young, the poor, and the enslaved, and they were being persecuted. The obvious inference — the one Quraysh drew loudly — was that a true message would attract better people in greater numbers.

The sūrah's reply is Moses. He had nine incontestable signs and a public victory over the assembled experts of an empire, and *illā dhurriyyah* — only a remnant of the young believed, and they in fear.

If that was the yield of Moses' mission, the size of a following says nothing whatever about the truth of a message.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:84

> **And Moses said, "O my people! If you believe in God, then trust in Him, if you are submitters."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Counsel to the Frightened**

*Wa-qāla Mūsā yā qawmi in kuntum āmantum bi-Allāhi fa-ʿalayhi tawakkalū in kuntum muslimīn.*

The previous verse described believers living in fear of persecution. This verse is Moses' address to exactly those people.

He does not tell them their fear is unwarranted; it was entirely warranted, and the regime made good on its threats. He tells them where to place their reliance.

**The Conditional Structure**

The verse is built on two conditionals bracketing one imperative:

*If you have believed in God* → **then rely upon Him** ← *if you are submitters.*

The rhetorical effect of the double conditional is to present *tawakkul* not as an additional virtue but as an *entailment* of what they have already professed. It is presented as a matter of consistency: you have said something; here is what it commits you to.

**"Then Trust in Him"**

*Fa-ʿalayhi tawakkalū.*

The word order is significant. Arabic would ordinarily place the verb before the prepositional phrase; here *ʿalayhi* ("upon Him") is fronted, which in Arabic rhetoric indicates *ḥaṣr* — restriction, exclusivity.

So the sense is: *upon Him — and upon none other — rely.*

This is the same fronting used in al-Fātiḥah: *iyyāka naʿbudu wa-iyyāka nastaʿīn*, "Thee alone we worship, and from Thee alone we seek help." The grammar carries the theology.

**The Meaning of Tawakkul**

*Tawakkul* is form V of *w-k-l*, to entrust, to appoint an agent. A *wakīl* is a proxy, a representative, one authorised to act on another's behalf. *Al-Wakīl* is a divine name.

To perform *tawakkul* is therefore to make God one's *wakīl* — to hand over the management of an outcome to Him.

The doctrine was treated at v. 71 in connection with Noah's declaration, and the crucial clarification bears repeating here, because this verse is often misread.

*Tawakkul is not the abandonment of means.* The Prophet ﷺ was explicit:

> A man said: *"O Messenger of God, shall I tie my camel and rely on God, or leave it loose and rely on God?"* He said: *"Tie it and rely on God."* (al-Tirmidhī 2517)

And:

> *"Were you to rely upon God as He should be relied upon, He would provide for you as He provides for the birds: they go out hungry in the morning and return full in the evening."* (al-Tirmidhī 2344, Ibn Mājah 4164)

The birds do not sit in the nest. They go out. The hadith is a description of effort undertaken without anxiety, not of effort forgone.

The scholars formulate the balance as: *al-iʿtimādu ʿalā Allāh maʿa al-akhdhi bi-l-asbāb* — reliance upon God together with the taking of means. To neglect the means while claiming reliance is not *tawakkul* but *tawākul*, a term the scholars coined precisely to mark the counterfeit.

**Why Tawakkul Is the Answer to Fear**

The connection between this verse and the fear described in v. 83 is exact, and it repays examination.

Fear is, in its structure, a projection about a future outcome combined with a sense of one's own insufficiency to control it. The frightened person is running a simulation of a bad future and finding no adequate response in his own resources.

*Tawakkul* does not work by denying the danger or by inflating one's own capacities. It works by changing the location of control. If the outcome is not, in the end, in the hands of Pharaoh, and if the one in whose hands it is has promised justice — then the calculation changes, without any of the facts on the ground changing.

The Qur'an gives the classic instance at 3:173, quoted at v. 71:

> *"Those to whom the people said: Truly the people have gathered against you, so fear them. But it increased them in faith, and they said: God suffices us; an excellent Guardian is He."*

*Ḥasbunā Allāhu wa-niʿma al-wakīl.* The word *wakīl* is from the same root as *tawakkul*. The formula is the verbal expression of the state.

And note that this did not make the danger less real. The believers at Ḥamrāʾ al-Asad had just come from Uḥud with their wounds. It made the danger *bearable*, and it made action possible where paralysis would otherwise have set in.

**The Practical Physiology of the Matter**

There is an observation worth making about how this operates in a human being.

Chronic fear is disabling. It narrows attention, degrades judgement, and consumes energy that the situation requires. The frightened person often makes worse decisions than the danger itself warrants — and in a situation of persecution, bad decisions are expensive.

*Tawakkul*, by relocating responsibility for the outcome, frees the person's actual capacities for the part of the situation he can affect. He does what is his to do, and he does it more competently, because he is not simultaneously carrying a weight that was never his.

This is not a psychological trick. It rests on a claim about how the world is: that outcomes are decreed, that effort is commanded, and that these are two different departments. If the claim is true, the resulting calm is not self-deception but accuracy.

**"If You Are Submitters"**

*In kuntum muslimīn.*

The closing conditional uses the same word that Noah used of himself at v. 72 — *muslim*, one who has surrendered.

And its placement here is exact, because *islām* and *tawakkul* are two faces of the same act. To surrender is to hand over one's will; to rely is to hand over the outcome. A person who has genuinely done the first cannot coherently withhold the second.

Moses is therefore doing something gentle but firm. He is not commanding these frightened people to feel differently — feelings do not answer to commands. He is reminding them of what they have already said they are, and letting the implication do the work.

**Note on the Address: "O My People"**

*Yā qawmi.* Moses addresses the Children of Israel as *my people* — with the possessive, and with the vocative of intimacy.

The same address is used by Noah at v. 71 (*yā qawmi*), and by Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Shuʿayb, and Lot in their respective narratives.

It is a small thing, but it is consistent, and it establishes the relation from which prophetic speech is delivered. Even in rebuke — and Noah's *yā qawmi* preceded a challenge to his enemies — the prophets speak as members of the community they address, not as outsiders standing over it.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:85

> **They said, "In God do we trust. Our Lord! Make us not a trial for the wrongdoing people,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Response and the Prayer**

*Fa-qālū ʿalā Allāhi tawakkalnā. Rabbanā lā tajʿalnā fitnatan li-l-qawmi al-ẓālimīn.*

They answer Moses with the very words of his command — *ʿalā Allāhi tawakkalnā*, using the same fronted preposition, the same exclusivity. And then they pray.

The prayer they make is one of the most theologically interesting in the Qur'an, because at first reading it is not obvious what they are asking for.

**"Make Us Not a Trial for the Wrongdoing People"**

*Lā tajʿalnā fitnatan li-l-qawmi al-ẓālimīn.*

*Fitnah*, as noted at v. 83, comes from the assaying of gold: heating ore to separate metal from dross. From this it spreads to mean test, trial, temptation, seduction, persecution, and civil discord.

The ambiguity of the word generates several readings of the prayer, and the classical commentators offer at least three. They are not in conflict; the density of the word allows all of them.

**Reading One: Do Not Let Them Persecute Us**

The most immediate reading, given the context of v. 83, takes *fitnah* in the sense the previous verse used: *an yaftinahum*, that they should persecute them.

On this reading the prayer is: *do not deliver us into their hands as objects of their torture.*

This is the plainest sense and connects the verse directly to the situation. It is a prayer for protection.

**Reading Two: Do Not Let Us Become the Instrument of Their Deception**

The second reading — favoured by al-Ṭabarī, Mujāhid, and Abū Mijlaz — takes *fitnah* as something that *misleads others*.

If the believers are persecuted, humiliated, and destroyed while the wrongdoers flourish, this becomes evidence, in the eyes of onlookers and of the wrongdoers themselves, that the believers' religion is false. Their suffering is read as a verdict on their faith.

On this reading the prayer is: *do not let our defeat become a proof for their unbelief.* Do not let us be the thing that confirms them in error.

This is a strikingly selfless prayer. The petitioners are not primarily asking to be spared pain; they are asking that their pain not be *useful* to falsehood.

Mujāhid's gloss, recorded by al-Ṭabarī, puts it directly: do not give them power over us, lest they be tempted to think they are upon truth and we upon falsehood.

**Reading Three: Do Not Punish Us Through Them**

A third reading takes the prayer as asking that God not use the wrongdoers as the instrument of chastisement for the believers' own shortcomings.

The Qur'an establishes elsewhere that a community's subjugation can be a consequence of its own failings:

> *"Whatever misfortune befalls you is because of what your own hands have earned."* (Qur'an 42:30)

On this reading: *do not make us the ones through whom Your wrongdoers are given a mandate; correct us some other way.*

**The Prayer as a Model**

Whichever reading is primary, the structure of the prayer is instructive, and it differs sharply from what a frightened person would naturally produce.

The natural prayer of the persecuted is *destroy them* or *rescue me*. This prayer is neither. It is concerned with the *meaning* of the outcome — with what the situation will demonstrate, and to whom.

That is a remarkably mature orientation, and it reflects an understanding that the believers' role is testimonial. Their function in Egypt was to be a sign; their prayer is that they not become a counter-sign.

The Qur'an describes this function directly for the Muslim community:

> *"Thus have We made you a middle community, that you may be witnesses unto mankind, and that the Messenger may be a witness unto you."* (Qur'an 2:143)

*Shuhadāʾ ʿalā al-nās* — witnesses over humanity. A community that exists to bear witness has reason to care about what its condition testifies.

**The Weight of Being Watched**

There is a practical implication here that extends far beyond the Egyptian case, and it is one of the heavier responsibilities of religious identity.

A person known to be religious is read. His conduct is taken, fairly or not, as evidence about what his religion produces. His failures are attributed not to him but to it.

The Prophet ﷺ was acutely aware of this. When a bedouin urinated in the mosque and the companions rushed at him, he stopped them, let the man finish, and then had water poured over the spot, saying:

> *"You have been sent to make things easy, not to make things difficult."* (al-Bukhārī 220)

The concern was not merely for the man's comfort. It was for what he would carry away about the religion.

And ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb is reported to have said: *"Do not let a man's prayer and fasting impress you; look at his truthfulness when he speaks, his trustworthiness when entrusted, and his restraint when the world is available to him."*

**Note the Sequence: Trust, Then Prayer**

One structural observation. They say *ʿalā Allāhi tawakkalnā* — we rely on God — and then immediately ask Him for something.

For some this seems inconsistent: if you have handed the outcome over, why petition?

The answer is that supplication is itself one of the means, and the most important of them. *Tawakkul* is not the cessation of asking; it is asking without anxiety, and accepting whatever comes.

The Prophet ﷺ, who possessed *tawakkul* in its perfection, prayed constantly and intensely — at Badr so persistently that his cloak fell from his shoulders and Abū Bakr said, *"Enough, O Messenger of God; your Lord will fulfil what He promised you"* (Muslim 1763).

Reliance and petition are not alternatives. Petition is what reliance *does*.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:86

> **and deliver us, through Thy Mercy, from the disbelieving people."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Petition**

*Wa-najjinā bi-raḥmatika mina al-qawmi al-kāfirīn.*

The prayer of the believers concludes with a request for deliverance.

**"Deliver Us"**

*Najjinā*, from *n-j-w* — the same verb used of Noah's rescue at v. 73 (*najjaynāhu*). Its root sense is escape, extrication, being brought out of a danger.

The Qur'an uses this verb consistently for prophetic deliverances: Noah from the flood, Lot from Sodom, Abraham from the fire, Moses from Pharaoh, Jonah from the fish (as this sūrah will note at v. 98), and the Prophet ﷺ from the plots of Quraysh.

The word carries the sense of being *lifted out* rather than of the danger being removed. The flood still came; Noah was taken out of it. This is an important nuance for what deliverance means in practice — it does not usually mean that the trial evaporates.

**"Through Thy Mercy"**

*Bi-raḥmatika.*

The believers do not ask on the basis of their merit. They do not say "deliver us because we believed while others did not," though it would have been true — v. 83 establishes that they were the only ones.

They ask *by Your mercy*.

This is the standard posture of prophetic supplication in the Qur'an, and it reflects a settled doctrine. Nothing a creature does establishes a claim on the Creator, because the capacity to do it was itself a gift. The Prophet ﷺ stated it in the plainest terms:

> *"No one's deeds will admit him to Paradise."* They said: *"Not even you, O Messenger of God?"* He said: *"Not even I, unless God covers me with mercy from Himself."* (al-Bukhārī 5673, Muslim 2816)

And his practice reflected it. Among the supplications he taught was one for the prayer itself:

> *"O God, I have wronged myself greatly, and none forgives sins but Thou. So forgive me with a forgiveness from Thyself, and have mercy upon me. Truly Thou art the Forgiving, the Merciful."* (al-Bukhārī 834, taught to Abū Bakr)

The invocation of mercy rather than desert is not false modesty. It is an accurate statement of the relation.

**The Full Prayer as a Unit**

Read together, vv. 85–86 form a complete supplication with a discernible architecture:

*A declaration of reliance* — "In God do we trust."
*A negative petition* — "Make us not a trial for the wrongdoing people."
*A positive petition* — "And deliver us, through Thy mercy, from the disbelieving people."

The order is instructive. Reliance first, then the request. This matches the etiquette of supplication established in the Sunnah: the Prophet ﷺ taught that one should begin by praising God and sending blessings upon him before asking (al-Tirmidhī 3477).

And the negative petition precedes the positive one — they ask first that harm to the cause be averted, and only then for their own rescue. The ordering reflects the priority.

**The Answer**

The prayer was answered, and the sūrah records it in the verses immediately following. Verse 87 gives the instruction to establish houses of worship; vv. 88–89 record Moses' further prayer and its acceptance; v. 90 records the crossing of the sea and the drowning of Pharaoh.

But note the interval. The deliverance came, but not immediately. The Children of Israel remained in Egypt through the sequence of signs recorded at 7:130–135 — years of famine, floods, locusts, lice, frogs, and blood — before the exodus.

The Qur'an records their impatience during that interval, and Moses' answer:
