> *"They said: We were hurt before thou camest to us and after thou camest to us. He said: It may be that your Lord will destroy your enemy and make you vicegerents upon the earth, that He may observe how you behave."* (Qur'an 7:129)

*Fa-yanẓura kayfa taʿmalūn* — "that He may observe how you behave." The formula of v. 14 of this sūrah, applied to them.

There is a hard truth in that verse. Deliverance is not the end of the test; it is a change of test. The people delivered from Egypt asked for an idol within days of crossing the sea (7:138), and refused to enter the land they had been promised (5:22–24).

**On the Timing of Answers**

This bears on a difficulty every believer meets: prayers that are not answered when made.

The Prophet ﷺ addressed it directly:

> *"No Muslim makes a supplication in which there is no sin and no severing of ties of kinship but that God gives him one of three things: either He hastens for him what he asked, or He stores it up for him in the Hereafter, or He turns away from him an evil like it."* They said: *"Then we shall ask abundantly."* He said: *"God is more abundant."* (Aḥmad 11133, al-Tirmidhī with similar wording)

Three outcomes, all of them answers. And the second and third are invisible at the time of asking.

He also warned against the specific impatience that ends supplication:

> *"The supplication of any of you is answered so long as he is not hasty, saying: I supplicated and I was not answered."* (al-Bukhārī 6340, Muslim 2735)

*Mā lam yaʿjal* — "so long as he is not hasty." The word is from the root *ʿ-j-l*, the same root as *istiʿjāl* at v. 11 of this sūrah, where the theme of divine unhurriedness was established. The sūrah's argument against hastening the punishment applies equally to hastening the answer.

**A Closing Observation on the Believers of Egypt**

These anonymous people — *dhurriyyah min qawmih*, a remnant of the young — receive two verses of direct quotation in the Qur'an, and what is preserved of them is not a deed but a prayer.

They did nothing recorded. They led no revolt, performed no miracle, and are given no names. What survives is that under a regime that killed their infant sons, they said: *upon God we rely; do not let us become a proof for the wrongdoers; deliver us by Your mercy.*

The Qur'an judged that worth keeping for all time.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:87

> **And We revealed unto Moses and his brother, "Settle your people in houses in Egypt, and make your houses places of worship, and perform the prayer and give glad tidings to the believers."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Instruction**

*Wa-awḥaynā ilā Mūsā wa-akhīhi an tabawwaʾā li-qawmikumā bi-Miṣra buyūtan wa-jʿalū buyūtakum qiblatan wa-aqīmū al-ṣalāta wa-bashshiri al-muʾminīn.*

The prayer of the persecuted is answered — but not, at first, with rescue. It is answered with a programme.

This is one of the most practically instructive verses in the sūrah, and its logic deserves careful attention.

**Four Commands**

The verse contains four imperatives in sequence:

*Settle your people in houses.*
*Make your houses places of worship.*
*Establish the prayer.*
*Give glad tidings to the believers.*

Together they constitute a survival strategy for a persecuted minority — and, read more broadly, a template for religious life under conditions where public practice is impossible.

**"Settle Your People in Houses in Egypt"**

*Tabawwaʾā li-qawmikumā bi-Miṣra buyūtan.*

The verb *tabawwaʾa* is from *b-w-ʾ*, to take up residence, to occupy a place, to make a dwelling. The form here suggests the deliberate assignment of dwellings.

Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr report the sense: the Children of Israel were instructed to take houses in a particular quarter, in proximity to one another. The community was to be *gathered* rather than dispersed.

The wisdom of this is evident to anyone who has considered how minorities survive. A scattered minority assimilates and disappears; a concentrated one retains its practices, transmits them to its children, and provides mutual support. Physical proximity is not a spiritual matter, but it has enormous spiritual consequences.

Contemporary Muslim communities have discovered the same principle by experience. The families who live near a masjid raise children who pray; the families who live an hour away, in most cases, do not — not through any difference in conviction, but because habit follows geography.

**"And Make Your Houses Places of Worship"**

*Wa-jʿalū buyūtakum qiblatan.*

*Qiblah* is from *q-b-l*, to face, to be opposite — the direction one turns in prayer. The commentators offer two main readings.

*Places of prayer.* Since public worship in the temples was impossible under persecution, the homes themselves were to serve as the sites of worship. Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have said: they were commanded to pray in their houses because they were prevented from praying openly.

*Facing one another.* Some read *qiblah* as meaning that the houses should face each other — arranged so that the community was oriented inward, aware of itself.

*Facing the direction of prayer.* A third reading takes it as an instruction that the houses be built oriented toward the sacred direction.

The first reading is the most widely held and the most consequential. Under persecution, the home becomes the mosque.

**The Home as the Last Redoubt**

This is a principle of the deepest importance, and it applies far beyond conditions of persecution.

A religion that lives only in its public institutions is fragile. Close the institutions and it stops. A religion that lives in homes cannot be shut down, because it has no address to raid.

The Prophet ﷺ made the home a site of worship in his own teaching:

> *"Do not make your houses graves. Truly Satan flees from a house in which Sūrat al-Baqarah is recited."* (Muslim 780)

A house without remembrance is a *grave* — a place where nothing living happens. The image is severe and deliberate.

And:

> *"The best prayer of a man is in his house, except the obligatory prayer."* (al-Bukhārī 731, Muslim 781)

The obligatory prayers belong in congregation; the voluntary ones belong at home — precisely so that the household witnesses worship, and so that the children grow up in a place where prayer is an ordinary event.

The historical record of Muslim communities under pressure bears this out. Communities that survived centuries of prohibition — in Spain after the Reconquista, in Central Asia under Soviet rule, in China during the Cultural Revolution — survived through households, not institutions. Where the transmission was institutional only, it ended when the institutions did.

**"And Perform the Prayer"**

*Wa-aqīmū al-ṣalāh.*

Note that the command to pray comes *during* the persecution, not after the deliverance.

This is the pattern throughout revelation. The five prayers were made obligatory in Makkah, during the hardest period of the Prophet's ﷺ mission, before there was a state, an army, or safety. Worship is not the reward for deliverance; it is the practice that sustains people through the interval before it.

The verb *aqāma* — to establish, to make stand upright — is used of prayer rather than a simple verb of performance. *Iqāmat al-ṣalāh* means to set it up as a standing structure in one's life: at its times, with its conditions, with attention. The Qur'an never says merely *ṣallū*.

**"And Give Glad Tidings to the Believers"**

*Wa-bashshiri al-muʾminīn.*

The final imperative shifts from plural to singular — *bashshir*, addressed to Moses alone. The three preceding commands were for the community; this one is for the leader.

*Bashshara* is from *b-sh-r*, the root of *bashar*, human skin, and *bushrā*, good news — so called because good news changes the complexion. The relation to v. 64 (*lahumu al-bushrā*) is direct.

The content of the glad tidings is not specified here, which allows it to encompass everything: deliverance from Egypt, victory over Pharaoh, inheritance of the land, and the Hereafter.

**Why Glad Tidings Are a Command**

The placement of this instruction at the end of the programme is not decorative. It is structurally necessary.

A persecuted community's greatest danger is not physical destruction; it is despair. Despair ends the transmission. People who believe there is no future stop investing in one — they stop teaching their children, stop maintaining their practices, and eventually stop believing that any of it mattered.

So the leader's task, alongside the organisation of dwellings and the establishment of prayer, is the maintenance of hope. And notice that it is framed as an *obligation* upon him, not as a matter of temperament.

The Prophet ﷺ practised this consistently. In the worst period at Makkah, Khabbāb ibn al-Aratt came to him complaining — the Prophet was resting in the shade of the Kaʿbah, and Khabbāb, who had been tortured, asked him to pray for help. The Prophet ﷺ sat up, his face reddened, and he described the sufferings of earlier believers who were sawn in half and combed with iron combs, and then said:

> *"By God, this matter will be completed, until a rider travels from Ṣanʿāʾ to Ḥaḍramawt fearing none but God and the wolf for his sheep. But you are a people who are hasty."* (al-Bukhārī 3612)

A promise of ordinary safety on an ordinary road — given to a tortured man by a persecuted preacher with no army. Within a century it was a mundane fact.

And his general instruction on the matter:

> *"Make things easy and do not make them difficult; give glad tidings and do not repel people."* (al-Bukhārī 69, Muslim 1734)

**The Whole Programme in Summary**

Community, worship, discipline, hope. Four instructions, given to people who had no power, no protection, and no visible prospect of relief.

They are not instructions for changing the political situation. They are instructions for *remaining intact* until the political situation changes — which, in the event, it did, but not for years.

That distinction is the practical heart of this verse, and it is applicable wherever believers find themselves without leverage. The task in such conditions is not to force an outcome. It is to hold together, keep praying, stay hopeful, and let God manage the timetable — which is precisely the *tawakkul* of v. 84 translated into a set of concrete actions.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:88

> **And Moses said, "Our Lord! Truly Thou hast given Pharaoh and his notables adornment and wealth in the life of this world, our Lord, that they may lead astray from Thy way. Our Lord! Obliterate their wealth and harden their hearts, such that they believe not till they see the painful punishment."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prayer Against Pharaoh**

*Wa-qāla Mūsā rabbanā innaka ātayta Firʿawna wa-malaʾahu zīnatan wa-amwālan fī al-ḥayāti al-dunyā.*

This is one of the most difficult prayers in the Qur'an, and it must be read carefully rather than quickly, because a careless reading produces a Moses who prays for people to be damned.

**"Adornment and Wealth in the Life of This World"**

*Zīnatan wa-amwālan.*

*Zīnah* is from *z-y-n*, to adorn — the same root used at v. 24 of this sūrah for the earth putting on its ornament before the harvest is destroyed. The connection is deliberate: what Pharaoh possessed is described with the vocabulary the sūrah has already established for what is beautiful and about to end.

*Amwāl* is the plural of *māl*, property, wealth — from a root meaning to incline, since wealth is what hearts incline toward.

Note the qualifier: *fī al-ḥayāti al-dunyā* — in the life of *this* world. Moses locates their possessions in the register the sūrah defined at v. 24: *al-dunyā*, the nearest and lowest, the field before the mowing.

**"Truly Thou Hast Given"**

*Innaka ātayta.*

The prayer opens with an acknowledgement that is theologically vital: Pharaoh's wealth came *from God*.

This is not a small concession. It would be easier to say that the tyrant's wealth was stolen, or that it came from some other source. Moses says it was given.

The doctrine here is the same as v. 70: the wrongdoers receive *matāʿ fī al-dunyā*, enjoyment in this world, from the same Lord who will call them to account. God's provision is not a certificate of approval; the sun rises on the tyrant's palace as on the believer's hut.

This resolves a confusion that troubles many. Prosperity is not evidence of divine favour, and hardship is not evidence of divine displeasure. The sūrah has said this repeatedly — at v. 12, where relief produces forgetfulness; at v. 70, where enjoyment precedes reckoning; and in the doctrine of *istidrāj* set out at v. 21.

**"That They May Lead Astray from Thy Way"**

*Li-yuḍillū ʿan sabīlik.*

The lām here is grammatically ambiguous, and the two readings are both defensible.

*Lām al-taʿlīl* — the lām of purpose: You gave them this *in order that* they might lead astray. On this reading the misguidance is presented as the divine purpose, which requires the same careful handling as the sealing of hearts at v. 74: God's decree encompasses all outcomes, and He permits what He does not approve.

*Lām al-ʿāqibah* — the lām of consequence, sometimes called *lām al-ṣayrūrah*. On this reading it means: You gave them this, and the *result* was that they led astray. The lām indicates outcome, not intention.

The second is the reading most commentators prefer, and Arabic supplies a clear precedent. The Qur'an says of Pharaoh's household finding the infant Moses:

> *"So the family of Pharaoh picked him up, that he might become for them an enemy and a sorrow."* (Qur'an 28:8)

*Li-yakūna lahum ʿaduwwan.* Obviously they did not pick him up *in order to* acquire an enemy. The lām states the outcome.

**How Wealth Leads Astray**

Moses' observation is worth taking seriously as a sociological claim, because it is accurate.

Wealth in the hands of the powerful is not merely consumed; it is *displayed*, and display is persuasive. The Qur'an records the mechanism in the story of Qārūn:

> *"So he went forth before his people in his adornment. Those who desired the life of this world said: Would that we had the like of what Qārūn has been given! Truly he possesses tremendous fortune."* (Qur'an 28:79)

*Fī zīnatih* — in his adornment, the same word as here. And the effect on the onlookers was immediate: envy, and the conclusion that his way must be the successful one.

Then the ground swallowed him, and the same people said: *"Ah, God expands and straitens provision for whomsoever He will among His servants. Had God not been gracious to us, He would have caused it to swallow us too"* (28:82).

The lesson of Qārūn is the lesson of this verse. Visible prosperity attached to a false way is itself a form of argument — the most effective form, because it bypasses reasoning entirely.

**"Obliterate Their Wealth"**

*Rabbanā-ṭmis ʿalā amwālihim.*

*Ṭamasa* means to efface, to blot out, to erase the features of a thing until it is unrecognisable. The Qur'an uses it of the stars being effaced (77:8) and of faces being obliterated (4:47).

Al-Ṭabarī and others report that this prayer was answered literally: their wealth — grain, gold, gardens — was rendered useless, some reports saying it was turned to stone. Ibn ʿAbbās is cited to this effect.

The request is intelligible as a response to the problem Moses has just identified. If the wealth is what makes their way persuasive, then removing it removes the argument.

**"And Harden Their Hearts"**

*Wa-shdud ʿalā qulūbihim.*

This is the difficult half, and it requires precision.

*Ushdud ʿalā qulūbihim* literally means: *tighten upon their hearts*, bind them, constrict them. The verb *shadda* is to bind fast.

Read superficially, Moses appears to be praying for their damnation — which sits badly with the Qur'an's insistence that prophets desire guidance for their people (compare 9:128, of the Prophet ﷺ: *"grievous to him is your suffering"*).

Three considerations resolve it.

*First: this came after long refusal, not before it.* The Qur'anic sequence is clear. Moses had presented sign after sign over years — the flood, the locusts, the lice, the frogs, the blood (7:133) — and after each one Pharaoh promised belief and then reneged:

> *"And when the plague fell upon them, they said: O Moses, call upon thy Lord for us by the covenant He has made with thee. If thou removest the plague from us, we shall surely believe thee and send forth with thee the Children of Israel. But when We removed the plague from them, for a term they were to reach, behold, they broke their word."* (Qur'an 7:134–135)

This is the pattern of v. 12 of this sūrah, repeated at national scale over years. Moses' prayer comes at the end of that process, when the case was closed.

*Second: the prayer describes rather than requests a change.* On the reading of many commentators, Moses is asking that the state they have already entered be *confirmed* — that the divine judgment already implicit in their conduct be finalised. The hardening is the sealing of v. 74, and v. 74 established that the sealing follows the transgression.

*Third: it is a prayer for justice, and prophets are permitted such prayers.* Noah prayed at 71:26: *"My Lord, leave not upon the earth any inhabitant from among the disbelievers."* And the Qur'an records that his prayer followed 950 years of refusal and a divine notification that no more would believe (11:36).

**"Such That They Believe Not Till They See the Painful Punishment"**

*Fa-lā yuʾminū ḥattā yarawu al-ʿadhāba al-alīm.*

The clause states the outcome: they will not believe until they see the punishment — at which point, as the sūrah established at v. 51 and will demonstrate at v. 90, belief is no longer accepted.

And it is exactly this that happened. Pharaoh believed — in the water, going down. Verse 90 records the words, and v. 91 records the answer: *āl-āna*, "Now?"

**God's Response**

The next verse records that the prayer was answered. But it also records something else, which is essential to understanding what has just been read: God tells Moses and Aaron to *be steadfast* and not to follow the way of those who know not.

Even a prayer that is granted is followed by an instruction in patience. The granting was not the end of the matter; the exodus, the sea, and forty years in the wilderness still lay ahead.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:89

> **He said, "Your supplication has been answered; so go straight, and follow not the way of those who know not."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer**

*Qāla qad ujībat daʿwatukumā fa-staqīmā wa-lā tattabiʿānni sabīla alladhīna lā yaʿlamūn.*

The prayer of v. 88 is answered — but the answer arrives with a condition attached, and the condition is more instructive than the granting.

**"Your Supplication Has Been Answered"**

*Qad ujībat daʿwatukumā.*

Note the dual: *daʿwatukumā*, "the supplication of you *two*."

But it was Moses alone who spoke in v. 88. The dual therefore includes Aaron, and the classical commentators explain: Moses supplicated and Aaron said *āmīn*.

Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr both record this from the early authorities, and it establishes a point of some importance in the law of supplication: the one who says *āmīn* is, in God's reckoning, a supplicant.

The Prophet ﷺ confirmed this in the matter of the *āmīn* after al-Fātiḥah:

> *"When the imam says 'Nor of those who go astray,' say Āmīn, for whoever's saying of Āmīn coincides with the saying of the angels, his past sins are forgiven."* (al-Bukhārī 782, Muslim 410)

The word *āmīn* means roughly *O God, respond* — it is itself a petition. This is why congregational supplication is efficacious, and why raising one's hands with a supplicating brother is not a passive act.

**"So Go Straight"**

*Fa-staqīmā.*

The verb is form X of *q-w-m*, to stand: *istaqāma* is to be upright, straight, on a direct course. It gives *istiqāmah*, steadfastness or uprightness, and *mustaqīm*, the adjective in *al-ṣirāṭ al-mustaqīm*, the straight path of al-Fātiḥah.

The connective *fa-* is consequential: *the prayer is answered, so be steadfast.*

**The Logic of the Condition**

Why should the granting of a prayer be followed by a command to persevere?

Because the granting is the beginning of a process, not its conclusion. The wealth of Pharaoh was effaced and his heart hardened — but Pharaoh was still on the throne, the Children of Israel were still enslaved, and years of the plagues, the exodus, the pursuit, and the sea still lay between the answer and its visible fruit.

There is a general principle here that is easily missed. Divine answers rarely arrive as finished results. They arrive as *conditions* within which further work must be done, over time, under strain. The person who prayed and received an answer is not thereby released from effort; he is usually placed in a situation requiring more of it.

**The Weight of Istiqāmah**

The command *istaqim* is one of the heaviest in the Qur'an.

It is given to the Prophet ﷺ directly:

> *"So go straight as thou hast been commanded, thou and whosoever has repented with thee, and be not rebellious. Truly He sees whatsoever you do."* (Qur'an 11:112)

And Ibn ʿAbbās reported that the Prophet ﷺ said of this verse:

> *"No sūrah was revealed to me harder or more severe than Hūd."* (al-Tirmidhī 3297, and in other narrations he attributed the greying of his hair to it: *"Hūd and its sisters have turned my hair grey."*)

The severity is in that word. Not *do great things*; not *achieve*. Just: keep straight. Because straightness is unrelenting — there is no point at which one has finished being upright.

The Prophet ﷺ also gave *istiqāmah* as the whole of the religion in a single exchange:

> Sufyān ibn ʿAbd Allāh said: *"O Messenger of God, tell me something about Islam about which I need ask no one after you."* He said: *"Say: I believe in God — and then go straight."* (Muslim 38)

*Qul āmantu bi-Llāhi thumma-staqim.* Declaration, then straightness. Everything else is commentary.

**"And Follow Not the Way of Those Who Know Not"**

*Wa-lā tattabiʿānni sabīla alladhīna lā yaʿlamūn.*

The prohibition is emphatic — the doubled *nūn* of emphasis (*nūn al-tawkīd al-thaqīlah*) is attached to the verb, giving it the force of a solemn warning.

*Alladhīna lā yaʿlamūn* — "those who do not know." This is the sūrah's characteristic designation for its opponents, and it connects directly to the epistemological argument running from v. 36: they follow *ẓann*, conjecture; they *kharaṣ*, guess (v. 66); they have no *sulṭān*, no authority (v. 68).

**Why This Warning to Moses?**

A prophet is not in danger of adopting Pharaoh's religion. So what is the warning about?

Two answers are given by the commentators, and both are worth holding.

*It is a warning against impatience.* "Those who know not" are characterised throughout this sūrah by haste — the demand for immediate signs (v. 20), the demand for immediate punishment (v. 48). The temptation for Moses, having received the answer, would be to expect immediate results and to be shaken when they did not come. The prohibition guards the interval.

*And it is the standard form of prophetic address.* The Qur'an frequently issues warnings to the Prophet ﷺ that are, in substance, instruction for the community. *"And be not among the idolaters"* (10:105); *"So be not among the doubters"* (10:94); *"Had you associated, your work would have come to naught"* (39:65).

These are not accusations. They are the pedagogical device of addressing the highest example so that the lesson reaches everyone below him. If the warning is given to Moses, no one else can consider himself exempt.

**The Verse's Place in the Sūrah**

There is a further connection worth drawing.

The two commands given here — *be steadfast*, and *do not follow the ignorant* — are precisely the two commands the sūrah will give to the Prophet ﷺ in its closing section:

> *"And follow that which is revealed unto thee, and be patient till God renders judgement. And He is the best of judges."* (Qur'an 10:109)

Follow the revelation, and be patient. The same pair, at the sūrah's final verse.

This is the sūrah's method throughout: the story of an earlier prophet is told, and its instruction is then handed to the present one. The Makkans hearing this would have understood exactly whose situation was under discussion.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:90

> **And We brought the Children of Israel across the sea, and Pharaoh and his hosts pursued them out of envy and enmity, till, when drowning overtook him, he said, "I believe that there is no god but the One in whom the Children of Israel believe, and I am among those who submit."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Crossing and the Pursuit**

*Wa-jāwaznā bi-banī Isrāʾīla al-baḥra fa-atbaʿahum Firʿawnu wa-junūduhu baghyan wa-ʿadwan.*

The exodus is compressed into a clause, and the emphasis falls not on the miracle of the parting but on the motive of the pursuit and the words spoken in the water.

**"Out of Envy and Enmity"**

*Baghyan wa-ʿadwan.*

Two verbal nouns in the accusative of purpose — *he pursued them out of* these two things.

*Baghy* is the word examined at v. 23: from *b-gh-y*, to seek, and by extension to seek beyond one's right — insolence, oppression, aggression. The sūrah has already stated its law: *"O mankind! Your rebellion (baghyukum) is only against yourselves"* (v. 23).

That law is about to be demonstrated with unusual literalness. Pharaoh's *baghy* drives him into the seabed, and the water closes.

*ʿAdw* is from *ʿ-d-w*, to run, to transgress, to overstep — the root of *ʿaduww*, enemy, and *iʿtidāʾ*, aggression. It is the same root as *al-muʿtadīn*, the transgressors whose hearts are sealed at v. 74.

The pairing gives the two dimensions of the pursuit: an internal disposition (rancour at losing his slaves) and an external act (unlawful aggression).

**The Irrationality of the Pursuit**

Consider what Pharaoh actually did.

The sea had opened. He watched a nation walk across the floor of it between walls of water. And his response was to ride in after them.

There is no rational calculation that produces this act. A man who has just seen the sea divide has been given the clearest possible demonstration that he is not the power in the situation. To enter the corridor is to place oneself inside another's miracle.

But rage does not calculate. Pharaoh had spent his entire life on a claim about himself, and the claim had just been publicly falsified in the most spectacular manner available. He was not pursuing slaves; he was pursuing the restoration of a self-image that had already been destroyed.

The Qur'an notes elsewhere that this is how such men end: *"So We seized him and his hosts and cast them into the sea. So behold how the wrongdoers fared in the end!"* (28:40).

**"Till, When Drowning Overtook Him"**

*Ḥattā idhā adrakahu al-gharaq.*

*Adraka* means to catch up with, to overtake — the same verb used of one thing reaching another. The drowning is the subject: it *overtook him*. He had been the pursuer; now something pursues him and catches him.

The reversal is written into the grammar, exactly as it was with the sorcerers at v. 82 (*alqū* → *ulqiya*).

**The Deathbed Declaration**

*Qāla āmantu annahu lā ilāha illā alladhī āmanat bihi banū Isrāʾīla wa-anā mina al-muslimīn.*

Pharaoh's confession is, in its content, perfectly correct. He affirms the *shahādah* in substance: there is no god but the One. And he adds *anā mina al-muslimīn* — the exact phrase Noah used of himself at v. 72.

So why is it rejected?

**The Doctrine of Īmān al-Yaʾs**

The scholars call this *īmān al-baʾs* or *īmān al-yaʾs* — the faith of desperation, faith at the point where the outcome has become visible.

The sūrah has already laid the groundwork at v. 51: *"Is it, then, when it befalls that you will believe in it? What! Now? And you had been seeking to hasten it!"* And at v. 98 it will state the general rule: no town believed and profited from its belief except the people of Jonah.

The Qur'an gives the principle explicitly at 40:84–85:

> *"Then, when they saw Our Might, they said: We believe in God alone, and we disbelieve in that which we used to associate with Him. But their believing profited them not when they saw Our Might — the way of God which has passed away among His servants. And there did the disbelievers lose."*

*Sunnat Allāh allatī qad khalat fī ʿibādih* — the established way of God, unchanged.

**Why the Deadline Exists**

The rule can seem harsh. Why should a true declaration be refused simply because it comes late?

The answer lies in what faith *is*. *Īmān* is not the recognition of a fact; it is the free submission of a will. And free submission requires the genuine availability of the alternative.

Once the punishment is visible, the alternative is gone. Nobody, at that point, is choosing anything. Pharaoh in the water is not a man who has come to believe; he is a man who has run out of options and is attempting a final manoeuvre.

The Prophet ﷺ set the boundary precisely:

> *"God accepts the repentance of a servant so long as the death rattle has not reached his throat."* (al-Tirmidhī 3537, Ibn Mājah 4253)

*Mā lam yugharghir* — as long as he has not begun the death gurgle. Before that, the door is open however late; after it, closed.

The mercy in this rule is easily overlooked. The deadline is set as late as it can possibly be set while still requiring a genuine choice. A man may live eighty years in rejection and be forgiven in his final conscious hour. The rule excludes only the moment when choice has ceased to exist.

**The Formula He Used**

One detail of Pharaoh's declaration deserves notice, because the commentators have long remarked on it.

He does not say *āmantu bi-Llāh* — "I believe in God." He says: *"I believe that there is no god but the One in whom the Children of Israel believe."*

He identifies the deity by reference to the people who worship Him — the slaves, whom he had been pursuing moments before. Even at the end, he cannot pronounce the name directly; he approaches it through a description.

Some commentators read this as evidence that the declaration was not truly submission at all but a formula produced under compulsion — the shape of faith without its substance.

**The Contrast the Sūrah Has Built**

Note what the verse establishes by juxtaposition.

Pharaoh's *anā mina al-muslimīn* is verbally identical to Noah's at v. 72. But Noah said it after 950 years of preaching to hostile people, while free to say anything else. Pharaoh said it with water in his lungs.

The words were the same. The acts were opposites.

This is one of the sūrah's most important teachings, and it applies well beyond the case of tyrants. Religious language is easy to produce; it costs nothing in itself. What gives a declaration its weight is the situation in which it is made — whether it was chosen when refusal was available, and whether it was followed by anything.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:91

> **"Now? While earlier thou didst disobey and wast among those who work corruption?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refusal**

*Āl-āna wa-qad ʿaṣayta qablu wa-kunta mina al-mufsidīn.*

The reply comes in two words and a charge. *Āl-āna* — "Now?"

**The Force of "Āl-āna"**

The single word carries an enormous weight, and it is the same word used at v. 51: *"What! Now? And you had been seeking to hasten it!"*

It is an interrogative of rejection — *istifhām inkārī* — expressing not a question but incredulity and refusal. English "Now?" or "At this moment?" catches something of it, but the Arabic is more compressed and more final.

The commentators note the implication: *now, when the door has closed; now, when nothing remains to be chosen; now, after the entire duration in which the choice was available.*

**Who Says It?**

The Qur'an does not identify the speaker. The verse simply begins with the reply.

Most commentators take it as God's own address to Pharaoh, delivered directly. Others, on the authority of certain reports, take it as spoken by Gabriel.

There is a well-known narration, related from Ibn ʿAbbās and recorded by al-Tirmidhī (3108), in which the Prophet ﷺ said that Gabriel began stuffing mud from the sea into Pharaoh's mouth, fearing that mercy might reach him. Some scholars have questioned the chain and others have accepted it; al-Tirmidhī himself graded it *ḥasan*.

Whatever the mechanism, the substance is agreed: the declaration was refused.

**"While Earlier Thou Didst Disobey"**

*Wa-qad ʿaṣayta qablu.*

*ʿAṣā* is to disobey, to rebel — the root of *maʿṣiyah*, sin as insubordination. The particle *qad* with the perfect tense emphasises the completedness of the act: *you had already, definitively, disobeyed.*

And *qablu* — "before." The whole force of the rebuke is temporal. There was a *before*, and in it a course was taken.

**The Extent of the "Before"**

It is worth recalling how long that *before* was, because the verse's severity depends on it.

Moses was raised in Pharaoh's own household (28:8–9). Pharaoh knew him personally from infancy and reminded him of it: *"Did we not raise thee among us as a child, and thou didst tarry among us for years of thy life?"* (26:18).

Then came the mission, and the signs: the staff, the hand, and the nine signs of 17:101. Then the public defeat of the sorcerers and their mass conversion. Then the sequence of plagues — years, according to the commentators — during each of which Pharaoh promised belief and then reneged (7:133–135).

Then the sea itself opened in front of him.

The verse's *qablu* covers all of that. This was not a man who lacked information.

**"And Wast Among Those Who Work Corruption"**

*Wa-kunta mina al-mufsidīn.*

The final charge uses the term Moses used at v. 81 — *al-mufsidīn*, the workers of corruption — and which the Qur'an applies to Pharaoh at 28:4.

The verb *kunta*, "you *were*," with the partitive *min*: you belonged to that category. It was not an act he committed but a class he inhabited.

And the specification matters. He is not condemned merely for unbelief but for *fasād* — for what he did to other people. The enslavement, the killing of infants, the division of the population into factions (28:4). The Qur'an consistently pairs the theological charge with the social one, and refuses to let the first stand alone.

**Why the Deadline Is Just: A Fuller Treatment**

The rejection of Pharaoh's faith is among the passages most often raised as an objection to divine mercy, and it deserves a full answer rather than a formula.

*Consider what acceptance would mean.* If deathbed faith were accepted, then the rational strategy for any human being would be to live entirely as one pleased and submit at the last moment. Faith would become a wager placed at the closing of the book, and everything the Qur'an says about *ʿamal ṣāliḥ* — righteous action, mentioned alongside faith in more than fifty verses — would be emptied of meaning.

*Consider also what such faith is.* Belief compelled by the visible arrival of consequence is not belief in the relevant sense. Every person will hold it, universally, at the moment of death; the Qur'an says the disbelievers in Hell will say *"Our Lord, we have seen and we have heard, so return us; we shall work righteousness; truly now we are certain"* (32:12). Certainty at that point is not a virtue; it is an unavoidable perception.

*And consider the alternative.* If faith at the sight of punishment were sufficient, the entire structure of the test would collapse, because the test consists precisely in believing what is not yet visible. The Qur'an describes the righteous as *"those who believe in the Unseen"* (2:3). Remove the unseen and there is nothing left to believe.

**The Contrast: Jonah's People**

The sūrah will supply, seven verses later, the case that proves the rule is not arbitrary:

> *"If only there had been a town that believed and profited from its belief — save the people of Jonah. When they believed, We removed from them the punishment of disgrace in the life of this world, and We granted them enjoyment for a while."* (Qur'an 10:98)

Jonah's people believed *when the punishment was visible on the horizon* — the Qur'an says the sign of it had appeared. And their faith was accepted.

The difference is instructive. They turned *before* it arrived, while the outcome was still genuinely open and refusal still genuinely possible. Pharaoh turned while going under.

The window is generous. It is not infinite.

**The Verse as a Warning to the Living**

The practical import is not a judgement about Pharaoh, whose case is closed. It is about the reader, whose case is not.

Every person reading this verse is, at this moment, in Pharaoh's *qablu* — the "before" during which the choice is real. That is the entire content of the warning.

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Take advantage of five before five: your youth before your old age, your health before your illness, your wealth before your poverty, your free time before your preoccupation, and your life before your death."* (al-Ḥākim, *al-Mustadrak*; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

And the Qur'an, on the moment when the *before* ends:

> *"Until, when death comes unto one of them, he says: My Lord, send me back, that I may work righteousness in that which I have left behind. Nay! It is but a word he speaks. And behind them is a barrier until the Day they are resurrected."* (Qur'an 23:99–100)

*Kallā, innahā kalimatun huwa qāʾiluhā* — "Nay, it is but a word he speaks." The same verdict as *āl-āna*, in a different register.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:92

> **So this day We shall save thee in thy body, that thou mayest be a sign for those after thee. And truly many among mankind are heedless of Our signs."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Preservation of the Body**

*Fa-l-yawma nunajjīka bi-badanika li-takūna li-man khalfaka āyah.*

The reply to Pharaoh's declaration concludes with something no one in the water could have anticipated: a promise of salvation, immediately qualified in a way that turns it into a sentence.

**The Bitter Word: "We Shall Save Thee"**

*Nunajjīka.* The verb is *najjā* — the same verb used of Noah at v. 73 (*najjaynāhu*) and of the believers' prayer at v. 86 (*najjinā*).

The word is one of deliverance. It is the vocabulary of prophetic rescue throughout the Qur'an. And it is applied here to Pharaoh.

The irony is exact and merciless. He is *delivered* — but only *bi-badanik*, in his body. The corpse is saved; the man is not.

Al-Zamakhsharī and others note the sharpness of this usage: the word that has meant salvation for every believer in scripture is turned, in this one instance, into the description of a carcass washed ashore.

**"In Thy Body"**

*Bi-badanik.*

*Badan* in Arabic means specifically the body, and more particularly the torso — the physical trunk, as distinct from the *nafs* or *rūḥ*, the soul or spirit.

The precision of the word is doctrinally significant. What is preserved is expressly the *badan* and nothing else. The Qur'an is careful to specify that this preservation has no bearing whatever on the man's standing; his destination is given elsewhere:

> *"The Fire, to which they are exposed morning and evening; and on the Day when the Hour comes to pass: Cause the family of Pharaoh to enter the most severe punishment!"* (Qur'an 40:46)

Some commentators note a further nuance in *badan*: that it can indicate a body stripped bare, without armour or ornament. Pharaoh, who had ridden in with his hosts in the panoply of empire, is returned naked.

**"That Thou Mayest Be a Sign for Those After Thee"**

*Li-takūna li-man khalfaka āyah.*

The purpose is stated: he is to become an *āyah* — the sūrah's governing word, examined at v. 1. A sign, a token, a piece of evidence.

The reversal here is complete, and it is the most severe judgement in the passage.

Pharaoh had spent his life demanding to be recognised as a god. He built monuments; he had his image carved into stone; he organised an entire civilisation around the proposition of his own divinity. He wanted, above all, to be *seen*.

He got exactly that. He is seen — as a specimen. His body is preserved and displayed, and what it demonstrates is the opposite of what he claimed. The monument to his divinity is his corpse.

**"For Those After Thee"**

*Li-man khalfak.* Those who come behind.

The phrase is open-ended. It does not specify a generation. And the verse has been read across the centuries as encompassing everyone from the Israelites who saw the body on the shore to any subsequent people who encounter it.

The Qur'an notes at 7:136–137 that the immediate function was for the Children of Israel, who had lived under Pharaoh's power and needed to see, physically, that it had ended. The commentators mention that some among them could not believe he was truly dead — a natural response after generations of treating him as more than human.

**A Note on the Modern Reading**

Many contemporary readers connect this verse to the discovery and display of ancient Egyptian royal mummies, and the connection has been widely discussed since the nineteenth century.

Some restraint is appropriate here. The Qur'an does not identify Pharaoh by name, does not specify a dynasty, and does not state that the body will be preserved *permanently* or *recovered* at any particular time. Scholars differ on which pharaoh is meant, and the historical and archaeological questions remain genuinely open.

What the verse states is that the body was preserved and became a sign. That was fulfilled at the shore, immediately, for the people who most needed to see it. Whether it has been fulfilled again in a museum case is a question the text itself does not settle, and building doctrine on such identifications is unwise — the Qur'an's own argument does not depend on them.

What can be said without overreach is this: the man who wanted to be remembered as a god is remembered, and not as a god. That much is beyond dispute, and it is the verse's actual claim.

**"And Truly Many Among Mankind Are Heedless of Our Signs"**

*Wa-inna kathīran mina al-nāsi ʿan āyātinā la-ghāfilūn.*

The closing clause is one of the most sobering in the sūrah, because it undercuts the very device just described.

A sign has been provided. And the verse immediately observes that most people will not attend to it.

*Ghāfil* is from *gh-f-l*, heedlessness — the state examined at v. 7, where those who are *"heedless of Our signs"* were identified as destined for the Fire. The word denotes not ignorance but *inattention*: the information is available and is not being processed.

**Why Signs Do Not Compel**

This is the sūrah's most consistent thesis, and here it receives its final statement in the Moses narrative.

Consider what has just been narrated. Nine signs. A staff that became a serpent. A hand that shone. A sea that opened. And the man who saw all of it, from the closest possible vantage, was the last to believe and believed too late.

The sūrah asked at v. 20 what the demand for a sign is worth, and here is the answer, demonstrated over eighteen verses. Signs do not produce faith. They provide *evidence*, and evidence must be *received*, and reception depends on a disposition that evidence cannot supply.

The Qur'an states the principle at 6:111:

> *"And even if We sent down the angels unto them, and the dead spoke to them, and We gathered all things before them, they would still not believe unless God willed."*

**The Sadness in "Many Among Mankind"**

There is a tone in this closing clause that is worth registering.

It is not triumphant. Pharaoh has just been destroyed; the believers have been delivered; the promise has been kept. And the verse ends not with celebration but with an observation about human inattention.

The same tone appears throughout the sūrah — at v. 60 (*"most of them do not give thanks"*), at v. 55 (*"most of them do not know"*), at v. 36 (*"most of them follow naught but conjecture"*).

The Qur'an is not describing a species that is malicious. It is describing one that is *distracted*. The signs are there; people are looking at something else.

**The Application**

The final clause is addressed to the reader more than to any historical audience, and its function is diagnostic.

Every reader is inclined to place himself among the observers of the sign rather than among the heedless. But *ghaflah* is by its nature invisible from the inside; the heedless man does not experience himself as heedless, only as busy.

The sūrah has offered a test, at v. 61: *"You are not engaged in any matter, nor recite any part of the Qur'an, nor do you perform any deed, but that We are witness over you when you are engrossed in it."* The word there was *tufīḍūna* — absorbed, poured into. Absorption is the ordinary human state, and the question is only what one is absorbed in.

The remedy the sūrah prescribes is not the pursuit of more signs. It is *dhikr* — the deliberate, repeated, disciplined recalling of what one already knows. The Qur'an's word for the opposite of *ghaflah* is not *ʿilm*, knowledge, but *dhikr*, remembrance. The problem was never that we did not know.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:93

> **And indeed We settled the Children of Israel in a sure settlement, and We provided them with good things. And they differed not till knowledge came to them. Truly thy Lord will judge between them on the Day of Resurrection concerning that wherein they used to differ.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Aftermath**

*Wa-la-qad bawwaʾnā banī Isrāʾīla mubawwaʾa ṣidqin wa-razaqnāhum mina al-ṭayyibāt.*

The Moses narrative closes not with the sea but with what happened afterwards — and what happened afterwards is a warning of a different kind from the one Pharaoh provided.

**"A Sure Settlement"**

*Mubawwaʾa ṣidq.*

The verb *bawwaʾa* is from the same root as *tabawwaʾā* at v. 87 — to settle, to establish in a dwelling. Here it is a cognate accusative construction: *bawwaʾnā… mubawwaʾa*, "We settled them with a settling."

The genitive *ṣidq* — truth, veracity, integrity — functions adjectivally in a construction Arabic uses to indicate excellence and genuineness. Compare *maqʿad ṣidq* (54:55), *mudkhal ṣidq* and *mukhraj ṣidq* (17:80), *qadam ṣidq* (10:2, in this very sūrah).

The sense is: a true settlement, a genuine and honourable one — not a precarious refuge but a real homeland.

The commentators identify it variously as Egypt itself after Pharaoh's destruction, or as Syria-Palestine, or as both across the generations.

**"And We Provided Them With Good Things"**

*Wa-razaqnāhum mina al-ṭayyibāt.*

*Ṭayyibāt* is from *ṭ-y-b*, the root of *ṭayyib*: good, wholesome, pleasant, pure. It covers both what is materially good and what is lawfully good.

The Qur'an details the provision elsewhere: the manna and the quails (2:57; 7:160), the twelve springs struck from the rock (2:60; 7:160), the cloud that shaded them (2:57).

So the picture is complete. Rescue from slavery, destruction of the oppressor, a homeland, and abundant lawful provision. Every prayer of vv. 85–86 answered, and more.

**"And They Differed Not Till Knowledge Came to Them"**

*Fa-mā-khtalafū ḥattā jāʾahumu al-ʿilm.*

This is the verse's turn, and it is unexpected.

The natural expectation, having received all of the above, would be gratitude and unity. What the verse records is *ikhtilāf* — disagreement, division, schism.

And the timing is the shocking part: the division began *when knowledge came*.

**The Paradox of Knowledge as a Cause of Division**

Why should knowledge divide rather than unite?

The sūrah's answer, given at v. 19, is that the cause is not the knowledge but what people do with it: *"And mankind was but one community; then they differed."*

Several mechanisms are at work, and they are all observable.

*Knowledge creates positions, and positions create parties.* Where there is nothing to know, there is nothing to disagree about. The arrival of a body of teaching produces interpretations, and interpretations produce factions.

*Knowledge confers status, and status is contested.* Once a scripture exists, those who master it acquire authority. Authority attracts competition, and competition finds doctrinal grounds for what are in fact contests over position.

*And knowledge removes the excuse of ignorance, which makes division culpable.* This is the point the verse is actually making. Before the *ʿilm* came, disagreement would have been innocent. After it came, disagreement was a choice made against clarity.

The Qur'an is explicit that the motive was not intellectual:

> *"And those who were given the Book differed not until after knowledge had come to them, out of envy among themselves."* (Qur'an 3:19)

*Baghyan baynahum* — out of mutual *baghy*, the same word as at v. 90 of this sūrah, describing Pharaoh's pursuit. The sūrah's law at v. 23 applies here too: *"your rebellion is only against yourselves."*

**The Warning to the Muslim Community**

The placement of this verse is deliberate, and its target is not primarily the Children of Israel.

The Qur'an recounts their history to a community that was about to acquire the same things: a scripture, a homeland, victory over its enemies, and abundance. The question posed to the listeners is whether they will do differently.

The Prophet ﷺ answered that question, and his answer was not reassuring:

> *"You will surely follow the ways of those before you, span by span and cubit by cubit, so much so that if they entered the hole of a lizard, you would follow them in."* They said: *"O Messenger of God, the Jews and the Christians?"* He said: *"Who else?"* (al-Bukhārī 3456, Muslim 2669)

And:

> *"The Children of Israel split into seventy-two sects, and my community will split into seventy-three."* (Abū Dāwūd 4596, al-Tirmidhī 2640)

The prediction was made in a community of a few thousand people who prayed behind one man. It has been amply fulfilled.

**What This Verse Does Not License**

A caution is required, because this verse and its parallels are frequently deployed as weapons within the community they were meant to warn.

The verse condemns division motivated by *baghy* — by envy, by the pursuit of position, by the will to dominate. It does not condemn the existence of scholarly disagreement as such.

Islamic law has always distinguished *ikhtilāf al-tanawwuʿ*, disagreement of variety, from *ikhtilāf al-taḍādd*, disagreement of contradiction. The four Sunni schools differ on thousands of rulings; this is not the *ikhtilāf* the Qur'an condemns, and the imams themselves modelled the distinction. Al-Shāfiʿī prayed behind the people of Baghdad without the *qunūt* out of respect for Abū Ḥanīfah's view. Aḥmad ibn Ḥanbal, asked whether he would pray behind an imam who did not consider bleeding to break ablution, said he would pray behind Mālik and Saʿīd ibn al-Musayyab.

The test is not whether people hold different views. It is whether the difference is pursued for the sake of truth or for the sake of a self.

**"Truly Thy Lord Will Judge Between Them on the Day of Resurrection"**

*Inna rabbaka yaqḍī baynahum yawma al-qiyāmati fīmā kānū fīhi yakhtalifūn.*

The verse closes with the formula the sūrah has used repeatedly — at v. 19, and again at v. 19's parallel elsewhere.

*Yaqḍī* is from *q-ḍ-y*, to decide, to conclude, to settle definitively. The *qāḍī* is the judge; *qaḍāʾ* is the verdict.

The clause performs two functions.

*It defers the settlement.* Not every dispute will be resolved in this world, and the attempt to force resolution — by coercion, by excommunication, by violence — usurps a judgement that has been reserved.

*And it guarantees the settlement.* Nothing is left unadjudicated. Every disagreement, including those in which both parties were sincere and both partly wrong, will be resolved by the One who knows what each person actually possessed by way of knowledge, capacity, and intention.

This is, on reflection, the only tolerable arrangement. No human tribunal can weigh sincerity, and no human being knows enough about another's circumstances to assign final blame. Deferring the verdict to God is not evasion; it is the recognition that the necessary evidence is not available to us.

**A Closing Note on the Narrative**

The Moses story in this sūrah runs from v. 75 to v. 93, and its architecture is worth observing as a whole.

It begins with arrogance (v. 75) and ends with division (v. 93). Between them: false accusation (v. 76), appeal to ancestry (v. 78), mobilised expertise (v. 79), the collapse of falsehood (vv. 81–82), a fearful remnant (v. 83), reliance and prayer (vv. 84–86), a programme for survival (v. 87), the answer with its condition (v. 89), deliverance and the drowning (v. 90), the refused confession (v. 91), and the preserved body (v. 92).

The Makkan audience was invited to locate itself in this. And the final verse ensures that the invitation extends to those who win, not only to those who lose — because the story does not end at the shore. It ends with a delivered, prosperous, well-provided people falling into schism the moment they had a scripture to argue about.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:94

> **So if thou art in doubt concerning that which We have sent down unto thee, ask those who have recited the Book before thee. The truth has certainly come unto thee from thy Lord; so be not among the doubters.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse That Has Puzzled Readers**

*Fa-in kunta fī shakkin mimmā anzalnā ilayka fa-sʾali alladhīna yaqraʾūna al-kitāba min qablik.*

The narrative section closes, and the sūrah turns back to the Prophet ﷺ with a verse that has generated extensive discussion since the earliest period.

Is the Prophet ﷺ being told that he might be in doubt?

**The Conditional Is Not an Attribution**

The first and decisive point is grammatical. The verse begins with *in kunta* — "if you are." Arabic conditionals of this type do not assert that the condition obtains; they specify what would follow *if* it did.

The Qur'an uses this construction elsewhere with conditions that are known to be impossible:

> *"Say: If the Compassionate had a son, I would be the first of worshippers."* (Qur'an 43:81)

Nobody reads that as conceding that God has a son. The conditional form is being used to make a rhetorical point.

Similarly:

> *"Had We desired to take a diversion, We would have taken it from Ourselves — if We were to do so."* (Qur'an 21:17)

**The Reported Response of the Prophet ﷺ**

The classical commentators record the Prophet's ﷺ own reaction to this verse. Qatādah and others report that he said:

> *"I do not doubt, and I do not ask."*

Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have said: *"He neither doubted nor asked."*

Whether or not the specific wording is established, the position it expresses is the consensus of the community: the Prophet ﷺ had no doubt concerning the revelation.

**Three Readings of the Address**

The commentators offer several ways of understanding whom the verse actually addresses.

*It addresses the Prophet ﷺ but intends the community.* This is the most widely held view, and it corresponds to a recognised Arabic idiom: *iyyāki aʿnī wa-smaʿī yā jārah* — "I mean you, but listen, O neighbour." The address is directed at the highest figure so that everyone below is included. The doubter in the audience hears the verse addressed to the Prophet ﷺ and understands that his own doubt is being met.

*It is a rhetorical device of emphasis.* By raising a hypothetical doubt and immediately dismissing it, the verse strengthens the assertion that follows. The structure is: *even if there were doubt, here is where it would be resolved — and there is no doubt.*

*And it addresses any hearer.* Some read the second person as generic, as English sometimes uses "you" impersonally: if one is in doubt, here is the remedy.

All three converge on the same practical meaning, and none of them requires attributing doubt to the Prophet ﷺ.

**"Ask Those Who Have Recited the Book Before Thee"**

*Fa-sʾali alladhīna yaqraʾūna al-kitāba min qablik.*

The remedy offered is remarkable and deserves attention in its own right.

The Qur'an directs its own audience to consult the *previous scriptural communities* — the Jews and the Christians — as a source of confirmation.

This is a striking move, and it rests on a specific claim: that the substance of what the Qur'an teaches was already known to those who possessed earlier revelation, and that its truth could therefore be corroborated by them.

The Qur'an makes this claim repeatedly:

> *"Truly it is in the scriptures of those of old. Is it not a sign for them that the learned of the Children of Israel know it?"* (Qur'an 26:196–197)

> *"Those unto whom We have given the Book recognise him as they recognise their own children."* (Qur'an 2:146; 6:20)

And the historical record supplies instances. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Salām, a learned Jew of Madinah, accepted Islam and is reported to have said that he recognised the Prophet ﷺ more surely than he recognised his own son (al-Bukhārī 3329, in substance). The Christian monk Baḥīrā, the Abyssinian Negus, and Waraqah ibn Nawfal — Khadījah's cousin, who told the Prophet ﷺ after the first revelation, *"This is the Nāmūs that God sent down upon Moses"* (al-Bukhārī 3) — all belong to the same pattern.

**The Confidence Behind the Instruction**

There is an implicit argument in the instruction that is easy to miss.

A fabricator does not tell his audience to go and check with the rival religious authorities. That is the last thing he would do, because those authorities have every incentive to expose him and considerable expertise with which to do it.

The Qur'an's willingness to invite that scrutiny is itself a form of evidence. It is the posture of a text that expects the comparison to go in its favour.

**A Necessary Qualification**

The instruction does not establish the earlier scriptures as an independent authority over the Qur'an, and the second half of the verse forecloses that reading.

The Qur'an's own position on the previous books is that they contain truth that has been subject to alteration (*taḥrīf*) — a charge it makes explicitly (2:75; 4:46; 5:13). The consultation recommended here is therefore corroborative, not adjudicative. The Prophet ﷺ instructed:

> *"Do not believe the People of the Book, and do not disbelieve them. Say: We believe in God and what has been sent down to us."* (al-Bukhārī 4485, from Abū Hurayrah)

A position of neither acceptance nor rejection, pending verification against what is established.

**"The Truth Has Certainly Come Unto Thee From Thy Lord"**

*La-qad jāʾaka al-ḥaqqu min rabbik.*

The conditional is closed off decisively. The particle *la-qad* combines the emphatic *lām* with *qad* before a perfect verb — the strongest form of affirmation Arabic possesses.

And the source is named: *min rabbik*, from *your Lord* — with the possessive pronoun that recurs throughout the sūrah's addresses to the Prophet ﷺ, carrying the sense of particular nurturing care.

**"So Be Not Among the Doubters"**

*Fa-lā takūnanna mina al-mumtarīn.*

The prohibition carries the doubled *nūn* of emphasis, as at v. 89.

*Mumtarīn* is from *m-r-y*, whose original sense is to stroke a camel's udder to draw milk — and hence, in form VIII (*imtarā*), to go back and forth over a matter, to be in wavering doubt. The word suggests a doubt that is worried at repeatedly rather than a single moment of uncertainty.

**Doubt: A Note on Its Status**

Since the verse raises the subject, a word is warranted on how Islam treats doubt, because the tradition's position is more careful than is often supposed.

*The involuntary arrival of a doubting thought is not sin.* The companions came to the Prophet ﷺ saying that they found in themselves thoughts too terrible to speak aloud. He said: *"Have you really experienced that?"* They said yes. He said: *"That is the clear proof of faith."* (Muslim 132). The very distress at the thought was evidence that faith was present.

*What is blameworthy is the cultivation of doubt* — turning it over, feeding it, making it a residence rather than a passage. This is what *imtirāʾ* denotes.

*And the remedy prescribed is action, not rumination.* When the companions asked about intrusive thoughts on the origin of creation, the Prophet ﷺ said: *"Let him say: I believe in God — and let him desist."* (Muslim 134). In another narration: *"Let him seek refuge with God and let him stop."* (al-Bukhārī 3276).

The instruction is practical. Some doubts are not resolved by further thinking; they are resolved by turning to what one knows and continuing.

And this verse offers the same structure. It does not say *reason your way out*. It says: consult those with knowledge, and remember that the truth has come. Enquiry, then affirmation.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:95

> **And be not among those who deny God's signs, lest thou be among the losers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Prohibition**

*Wa-lā takūnanna mina alladhīna kadhdhabū bi-āyāti Allāhi fa-takūna mina al-khāsirīn.*

The pair of prohibitions is completed. Verse 94 forbade doubt; this verse forbids denial. Together they cover the two ways of failing to affirm: wavering, and rejecting.

**The Escalation**

Note the progression between the two verses.

*Mumtarīn* — doubters. An internal state of unresolved wavering.

*Alladhīna kadhdhabū bi-āyāti Allāh* — those who denied God's signs. An external act of rejection.

The order is not accidental. Doubt entertained becomes denial expressed. The sūrah has described this trajectory before, at v. 74, where habituated denial hardens into a sealed heart.

The rhetorical structure of the two verses therefore constitutes a warning about a *process*: stop it at the first stage, because the second stage is where it goes.

**"Those Who Deny God's Signs"**

*Kadhdhabū bi-āyāti Allāh.*

The verb is *kadhdhaba*, form II of *k-dh-b*, to lie — so, to declare something a lie, to give it the lie. This is the sūrah's characteristic verb for its opponents: v. 17, v. 39, v. 41, v. 73, v. 74.

And the object is *āyāt* — the sūrah's governing term. Signs: the verses of revelation, and the phenomena of the natural world, and the events of history, all of which the sūrah has presented as evidence.

**"Lest Thou Be Among the Losers"**

*Fa-takūna mina al-khāsirīn.*

*Khasira* means to lose, to suffer loss in a transaction — the vocabulary of commerce. *Khusrān* is a deficit, the state of having ended up with less than one started with.

The Qur'an's use of commercial imagery for the moral life is extensive and deliberate: *tijārah* (trade, 35:29; 61:10), *ribḥ* (profit, 2:16), *bayʿ* (sale, 9:111), *qarḍ ḥasan* (a goodly loan, 2:245), *rahīnah* (a pledge, 74:38).

The metaphor works because it captures something true about existence: a life is a capital that is being spent, and the question is what is being acquired with it.

The Qur'an's shortest statement of the matter is Sūrat al-ʿAṣr:

> *"By time! Truly mankind is in loss — save those who believe, and perform righteous deeds, and exhort one another to truth, and exhort one another to patience."* (Qur'an 103:1–3)

*Inna al-insāna la-fī khusr.* The default is deficit. Time is being spent whether or not anything is purchased with it.

Al-Shāfiʿī said of this sūrah: *"If people reflected on this sūrah alone, it would suffice them."*

**Why the Prophet ﷺ Receives This Warning**

The question raised at v. 94 arises again with more force here. Denial of God's signs is not a live possibility for a messenger of God.

The answer is the same, and it is worth stating fully because the principle governs a whole class of Qur'anic verses.

*The address to the highest example establishes that no one is exempt.* If the Messenger ﷺ is told that denial would make him a loser, then no rank, no service, and no proximity constitutes an exemption from the law. The point is precisely that the warning is universal, and the way to demonstrate universality is to apply it to the one who least needs it.

The Qur'an uses this device repeatedly with the Prophet ﷺ:

> *"And it has indeed been revealed unto thee and unto those before thee: If thou shouldst ascribe partners, thy work will surely come to naught, and thou wilt surely be among the losers."* (Qur'an 39:65)

> *"Had We not made thee firm, thou wouldst have inclined toward them a little."* (Qur'an 17:74)

> *"Say: Truly I fear, should I disobey my Lord, the punishment of a tremendous Day."* (Qur'an 10:15, in this sūrah)

The last is from this very chapter, and it establishes the pattern: the messenger is under the law, and the law does not bend for him.

**A Second Function: Protecting the Community's Theology**

There is a further purpose in these addresses that becomes visible when one looks at what happened to earlier communities.

The reverence owed to a prophet has a tendency to inflate into something else. The Qur'an is emphatic that this happened to Jesus (5:116–117; 4:171) and warns against it.

Verses that place the Prophet ﷺ visibly under command — that show him being instructed, warned, and corrected — are a permanent structural safeguard against his deification. A figure who is shown receiving warnings cannot easily be turned into an object of worship.

The Prophet ﷺ stated the boundary himself:

> *"Do not extol me as the Christians extolled the son of Mary. I am only a servant, so say: the servant of God and His Messenger."* (al-Bukhārī 3445)

*ʿAbd Allāh wa-rasūluh* — the formula recited in every *tashahhud* of every prayer, five times a day, forever. Servant first, then messenger.

**The Pair of Verses as a Unit**

Verses 94 and 95, read together, form the sūrah's transition out of narrative and into its concluding section.

The narrative demonstrated the consequences of denial — Noah's people drowned, Pharaoh's body on the shore. These two verses convert the demonstration into instruction: *do not doubt; do not deny.*

And the sequence that follows (vv. 96–103) will elaborate on why some people cannot be reached, why Jonah's people were the exception, and why faith cannot be compelled — before the sūrah's closing declarations at vv. 104–109.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:96

> **Truly those who deserve thy Lord's Word will not believe,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Decree Concerning the Obdurate**

*Inna alladhīna ḥaqqat ʿalayhim kalimatu rabbika lā yuʾminūn.*

After the twin prohibitions of vv. 94–95, the sūrah turns to a hard doctrine: there exists a category of people concerning whom the divine word has already been established, and they will not believe.

**"Those Who Deserve Thy Lord's Word"**

*Alladhīna ḥaqqat ʿalayhim kalimatu rabbik.*

The verb *ḥaqqat* is from *ḥ-q-q* — the root examined at v. 82. Its sense here is: to become established as due, to be confirmed as rightfully applicable. The construction *ḥaqqa ʿalā* means that something has become binding upon a person, has fallen due against him.

*Kalimatu rabbik* — "the word of your Lord." The commentators identify this as the divine decree regarding the destiny of such people.

The Qur'an uses the phrase in several places with the same sense:

> *"But the word of punishment has come due against the disbelievers."* (Qur'an 39:71)

> *"Thus has the word of thy Lord come due against those who transgressed: that they shall not believe."* (Qur'an 10:33, in this very sūrah)

That last reference is important. Verse 33 of this sūrah made exactly the same statement, and the present verse resumes it. The doctrine is not new here; it is being applied.

**The Order of Cause and Effect**

The theological difficulty is identical to that of the sealed hearts at v. 74, and the resolution is identical.

Verse 33 supplies the decisive detail that this verse omits: the word came due *ʿalā alladhīna fasaqū* — against those who *transgressed*. The transgression is named as the ground of the decree.

So the sequence is not: the decree was issued, therefore they transgressed. It is: they transgressed, therefore the decree became due against them.

This is why the sūrah insisted at v. 44: *"Truly God does not wrong human beings in the least, but rather human beings wrong themselves."* That verse is the interpretive key to every passage of this kind, and it was placed early in the sūrah for exactly that purpose.

**Divine Knowledge and Human Freedom**

The underlying question — how a decreed outcome is compatible with a free choice — has occupied Muslim theologians since the earliest period, and the mainstream position deserves a clear statement.

The Ashʿarī and Māturīdī schools, and Ibn Taymiyyah in his own formulation, converge on the following: God's knowledge is *inclusive* rather than *causal*. His knowing that a person will refuse does not compel the refusal; it is knowledge *of* the refusal the person will freely make.

The standard illustration: a teacher who has taught a student for years may know with complete confidence how that student will answer a particular question. The teacher's knowledge does not write the answer. It tracks the student's own disposition.

The analogy is imperfect — God's knowledge is not inferential, and He is also the creator of the one who chooses — but it isolates the relevant point. Foreknowledge and compulsion are different relations.

Ibn Taymiyyah's formulation adds a further distinction that is helpful: God's *will* (*irādah kawniyyah*, His creative will, which encompasses everything that happens) must be distinguished from His *command* (*irādah sharʿiyyah*, what He approves and enjoins). Disbelief occurs within the first and against the second. God permits what He does not approve — otherwise the world would contain no evil at all, and no test.

**"Will Not Believe"**

*Lā yuʾminūn.*

The plain present tense. And the following verse will intensify it: *"even if every sign should come to them."*

The category the sūrah is describing is not people who have not yet been persuaded. It is people whose refusal has passed the point at which evidence is operative — the state described at v. 74 as a sealed heart, and diagnosed at v. 42 as hearing without listening and at v. 43 as looking without seeing.

**Why This Doctrine Is Disclosed**

The obvious question is what a believer is supposed to do with this information.

Three uses, and one prohibition.

*It consoles the caller.* The person who invites others to faith and meets sustained refusal is prone to concluding that he has failed. This verse tells him that the outcome was never within his power. The sūrah will make this explicit at v. 99 (*"wouldst thou compel people to become believers?"*) and at v. 108 (*"whosoever is guided is guided only for the good of his own soul"*).

*It sets a limit on effort.* Resources spent on the unreachable are resources withheld from the reachable. The Prophet ﷺ was told plainly: *"So remind, if the reminder benefits"* (87:9) and *"Turn away from them, for thou art not to blame"* (51:54). There is a point at which one moves on.

*It warns the reader about himself.* The mechanism described is a real one, and the reader is not exempt from it. The response the verse calls for is not curiosity about who is in the category but urgency about not entering it.

*And the prohibition:* nothing here licenses the identification of any living person as belonging to this group. Verse 40 established the point — some of them will believe, and God knows which. The Makkans of this sūrah's audience included men who became pillars of Islam. ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb was on his way to kill the Prophet ﷺ when he was stopped by a page of the Qur'an.

The category is real. The membership list is not published, and reading anyone into it is a usurpation.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:97

> **though every sign should come to them, till they see the painful punishment.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Intensification**

*Wa-law jāʾathum kullu āyatin ḥattā yarawu al-ʿadhāba al-alīm.*

The previous verse stated that they will not believe. This verse forecloses the obvious objection: *what if they were shown more?*

**"Every Sign"**

*Kullu āyah.* Not many signs; *every* sign.

The universal quantifier closes the question completely. There is no evidence that has not been supplied, no demonstration that has been withheld, no further proof whose absence explains the refusal.

And the sūrah has just spent nineteen verses demonstrating this claim rather than asserting it. Pharaoh received the staff, the hand, the flood, the locusts, the lice, the frogs, the blood, and the sea itself opening in front of him. He believed when the water reached his throat.

**The Answer to the Demand of Verse 20**

This verse completes an arc that began early in the sūrah.

At v. 20 the objectors said: *"Why has a sign not been sent down unto him from his Lord?"* The reply given there was: *"The Unseen belongs only to God, so wait."*

Now the fuller answer arrives. The demand for a sign was never a request for evidence, because evidence had already been supplied and rejected. It was a delaying device — a way of appearing open to persuasion while remaining certain of refusing.

The Qur'an exposes the mechanism directly:

> *"And they swore by God their most solemn oaths that if a sign came to them they would surely believe in it. Say: Signs are only with God. And what will make you realise that even if they came, they would not believe? And We shall turn away their hearts and their eyes, just as they believed not in it the first time, and We shall leave them to wander blindly in their rebellion."* (Qur'an 6:109–110)

*Kamā lam yuʾminū bihi awwala marrah* — "just as they did not believe in it the first time." The evidence for the prediction is the record.

And the Qur'an gives the case that settles it:

> *"And even if We opened unto them a gate in Heaven, and they continued to ascend through it, they would say: Our eyes are merely spellbound. Nay, we are a people bewitched."* (Qur'an 15:14–15)

That is the argument in its final form. Even ascending physically into the sky, they would reach for the explanation that dissolves the experience — the same move Pharaoh's court made at v. 76 with the word *siḥr*.

**Why More Evidence Does Not Work**

The claim being made is worth examining on its merits, because it is easy to mistake it for special pleading.

Consider how belief actually changes in human beings. Evidence is not received by a neutral instrument. It is received by a person who already holds commitments, whose social standing depends on certain positions, whose identity is built around certain claims, and whose cognitive machinery is disposed to protect all of these.

When evidence arrives that threatens the structure, several defences are available, and they are deployed automatically:

*Reinterpretation.* The evidence is real but means something else. (*"This is manifest sorcery."*)

*Impugning the source.* The evidence may be sound but the one presenting it is untrustworthy or self-interested. (*"That the two of you might have greatness in the land."*)

*Raising the standard.* The evidence is insufficient; something more would be required. And when that arrives, something more again.

*Simple deferral.* The matter is complicated; one will look into it later; there are more pressing concerns.

None of these requires bad faith in the ordinary sense. They operate below the level of deliberate deception, which is why the people employing them experience themselves as reasonable.

The Qur'an's claim is not that such people are stupid. It is that the obstacle is not located where they say it is. And the proof is that raising the evidence does not move them.

**"Till They See the Painful Punishment"**

*Ḥattā yarawu al-ʿadhāba al-alīm.*

The clause is the same as the closing of Moses' prayer at v. 88, and it identifies the one thing that *will* produce belief in this category: the punishment itself, seen.

At which point, as vv. 90–91 have just demonstrated at length, it is worth nothing.

The Qur'an gives the future scene:

> *"And if only thou couldst see when they are made to stand before the Fire, and they say: Would that we were sent back! Then we would not deny the signs of our Lord, and we would be among the believers. Nay, but that which they used to hide before has appeared to them. And even were they sent back, they would surely return to that which they were forbidden; and truly they are liars."* (Qur'an 6:27–28)

*Wa-law ruddū la-ʿādū* — "even were they sent back, they would return to it." The disposition is not altered by the perception; only the circumstances are.

**The Function of "Painful"**

*Al-ʿadhāb al-alīm.* The adjective *alīm* is from *ʾ-l-m*, pain — an intensive form meaning acutely painful.

The Qur'an's descriptions of the punishment are physical and unsparing, and modern readers sometimes find this register uncomfortable. But its function is discernible.

Abstractions do not motivate. A person can assent to the proposition that a certain course leads to a bad end without being moved by it at all, because propositional assent and motivational force are different things. What moves human beings is concrete imagining.

So the Qur'an describes. And it balances the descriptions with equally concrete depictions of the Garden — the shade, the rivers, the fruit, the companions, and above all the *ziyādah* of v. 26.

Both registers serve the same purpose: to make real to the imagination what is already accepted by the intellect.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:98

> **Why has there not been any town that believed, such that its belief benefited it, save the people of Jonah? When they believed, We removed from them the punishment of disgrace in the life of this world, and We granted them enjoyment for a while.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exception**

*Fa-lawlā kānat qaryatun āmanat fa-nafaʿahā īmānuhā illā qawma Yūnus.*

Here, at last, the sūrah names the prophet whose name it bears. The single explicit mention of Jonah's people arrives in verse 98 of 109, and it arrives as an exception to a rule.

**The Construction: "Why Has There Not Been…?"**

*Fa-lawlā kānat qaryatun.*

*Lawlā* followed by a perfect verb functions in Arabic as an expression of reproach or regret over something that did not happen — *taḥḍīḍ* combined with *tawbīkh*. English "why has there not been" or "if only there had been" both capture parts of it.

The implied answer is: there has not been. Not one, in the whole record — except one.

**The Rule the Exception Proves**

The Qur'an's account of past communities is uniform. Warning, refusal, and destruction. Noah's people, ʿĀd, Thamūd, Lot's people, Midian, Pharaoh's Egypt. In each case belief came, if at all, only when the punishment was visible — and it was refused.

Against that record stands one town.

**Who Were the People of Jonah?**

Jonah — *Yūnus ibn Mattā* — was sent to Nineveh, the great Assyrian city on the Tigris. The Qur'an mentions the number of its people:

> *"And We sent him unto a hundred thousand or more."* (Qur'an 37:147)

The story is told across several sūrahs. Jonah preached, was rejected, and departed in anger before being given leave to do so:

> *"And Dhū al-Nūn, when he went off enraged and thought that We had no power over him. Then he cried out in the darkness: There is no god but Thee! Glory be to Thee! Truly I have been among the wrongdoers."* (Qur'an 21:87)

> *"So wait patiently for the Judgement of thy Lord, and be not like the companion of the fish, who cried out while choking with grief."* (Qur'an 68:48)

He was swallowed by the fish, made his supplication in the threefold darkness, and was delivered.
