Meanwhile — and this is the point of the present verse — the city he had abandoned repented.

**What They Did**

The Qur'an does not narrate the details here, but the commentators, drawing on early reports, describe it.

When Jonah departed and the signs of the promised punishment appeared — a darkness or cloud gathering over the city — the people recognised what was coming. And they did not wait.

Ibn ʿAbbās and others report that they went out into the open ground, the whole population together. They separated mothers from their infants and ewes from their lambs so that the crying of the young would join the crying of the people. They dressed in coarse garments, put dust on their heads, and cried out to God together.

Al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, and al-Qurṭubī all record versions of this account.

And the punishment was lifted.

**Why Theirs Was Accepted and Pharaoh's Was Not**

The comparison with vv. 90–91 is unavoidable and instructive, and the difference is precisely located.

*Pharaoh believed while drowning.* The punishment had arrived; the outcome was settled; no alternative remained.

*Nineveh believed when the sign appeared but before it struck.* The threat was visible on the horizon. The outcome was not yet settled. They could still, at that moment, have done what every other community did — argued, delayed, blamed the messenger, waited to see whether the cloud would pass.

They did not. They acted while action was still a choice.

This is the whole distinction, and it defines the boundary of accepted repentance. The door closes not when danger appears but when choice ends.

**"The Punishment of Disgrace in the Life of This World"**

*ʿAdhāba al-khizyi fī al-ḥayāti al-dunyā.*

*Khizy* is from *kh-z-y*, disgrace, humiliation, ignominy. The punishment threatened was not merely destructive but *shaming* — the fate that had overtaken ʿĀd and Thamūd, whose ruins the Arabs passed on their trade routes.

**"And We Granted Them Enjoyment for a While"**

*Wa-mattaʿnāhum ilā ḥīn.*

*Mattaʿnā* is the verb from *matāʿ*, the term examined at v. 70: provision for a limited term.

The phrase *ilā ḥīn* — "for a while," "until a time" — is characteristically Qur'anic in its refusal to specify. They were given more time. How much is not stated.

There is a sober realism in this closing clause. Nineveh was not granted permanence. It was granted a reprieve, within which the ordinary conditions of life resumed and the ordinary test continued. Historically, the Assyrian empire fell some generations later.

Repentance restores the opportunity. It does not confer immunity.

**The Sūrah's Name**

It is worth asking why this chapter is named for Jonah when he receives one verse.

Several answers converge.

*Because his people are the sūrah's counter-example.* The chapter has argued at length that people do not believe, that signs do not persuade, that hearts harden, that faith comes too late. And then it names itself after the one community that proved the pattern breakable.

*Because Jonah's own story embodies the sūrah's themes.* Impatience with the pace of divine judgement — the sūrah's constant subject from v. 11 onward — is precisely his failing. He wanted the outcome faster than it was given. And the closing verse of the sūrah (v. 109) commands the Prophet ﷺ: *"be patient till God renders judgement."*

*And because the sūrah is fundamentally about mercy arriving where it was not expected.* Its opening verses (vv. 2–3) established God as Creator and Governor; its middle argued His justice; and here, near its end, it records a hundred thousand people who were about to be destroyed and were not, because they cried out in time.

**The Verse as an Argument to Quraysh**

The application to the sūrah's first audience is unmistakable, and it is the reason the exception is mentioned at all.

Makkah was, at the time of this revelation, a town that had rejected its messenger. The sūrah has just recited what happens to such towns. And then it offers the single case in which it did not happen — and the reason it did not.

The message to Quraysh is therefore not a threat but an offer: *this is available to you.* A whole city turned, together, at the last moment, and was spared.

And in the event, something of the kind occurred. Makkah did not turn while the sūrah was being revealed. But at the conquest, some twenty years later, the city surrendered without a battle, and the man they had driven out declared: *"Go, for you are free"* (Ibn Hishām; and see al-Bayhaqī).

**The Application**

The verse's use for the individual reader is direct.

It establishes that the door is genuinely open until it closes, and that the closing is late. Whatever has accumulated — years of it, publicly, with witnesses — the case of Nineveh stands as evidence that a decisive turn is accepted.

The Qur'an's most expansive statement of the same principle:

> *"Say: O My servants who have been prodigal to their own harm! Despair not of God's Mercy. Truly God forgives all sins. Truly He is the Forgiving, the Merciful."* (Qur'an 39:53)

And the Prophet ﷺ:

> *"God is more delighted at the repentance of His servant than one of you would be if he lost his camel with his food and drink upon it in a desert land, and despaired of it, and then lay down in the shade of a tree in despair — and then found it standing beside him, and seized its halter and said, from the intensity of his joy: O God, Thou art my servant and I am Thy lord! — making a mistake from the intensity of his joy."* (Muslim 2747)

**Why Only One**

A final observation. It is worth asking why, in the entire record, only one community managed this.

The answer lies in what collective repentance requires. An individual can turn privately, at any moment, at no social cost. A *city* turning requires that its leadership abandon its position publicly, that its elite forfeit the arrangement that sustains them, and that everyone simultaneously admit having been wrong — with no one able to hold out and be proved right.

Nineveh did it. The commentators note that its people went out *together*, all of them, women and children and animals included. The unanimity was the achievement.

That is why it happened once.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:99

> **And had thy Lord willed, all those on earth would have believed all together. Wouldst thou compel people till they become believers?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Governing Principle**

*Wa-law shāʾa rabbuka la-āmana man fī al-arḍi kulluhum jamīʿan. A-fa-anta tukrihu al-nāsa ḥattā yakūnū muʾminīn?*

This verse and the two that follow constitute the sūrah's final statement on the nature of faith, and they are among the most consequential verses in the Qur'an for the question of religious coercion.

**"Had Thy Lord Willed"**

*Wa-law shāʾa rabbuk.*

The conditional *law* with the perfect indicates a condition contrary to fact: He did not will it, and therefore it did not happen.

The premise is God's omnipotence, which is not in dispute. If universal belief were what He willed, universal belief is what there would be. The Qur'an states this repeatedly:

> *"And had We willed, We would have given every soul its guidance."* (Qur'an 32:13)

> *"And had thy Lord willed, He would have made mankind one community. But they cease not to differ — save those upon whom thy Lord has mercy; and for that He created them."* (Qur'an 11:118–119)

That last verse is worth attending to: *wa-li-dhālika khalaqahum*, "and for that He created them." The diversity of response is not a failure of the design; it is the design.

**Why God Did Not Will It**

The reason is the entire structure of the human situation, and the Qur'an states it plainly:

> *"He who created death and life, that He may try you as to which of you is most virtuous in deed."* (Qur'an 67:2)

*Li-yabluwakum* — that He may *test* you. A test requires the genuine possibility of failing it.

Universal compelled belief would not be belief in the relevant sense at all. It would be the condition of the angels, of whom the Qur'an says: *"They disobey not God in what He commands them, and they do what they are commanded"* (66:6). The angels are not virtuous for obeying; they are constituted to obey.

What makes a human being's faith worth anything is that it was not compelled — that at every moment refusal was available and was not taken.

**"Wouldst Thou Compel People Till They Become Believers?"**

*A-fa-anta tukrihu al-nāsa ḥattā yakūnū muʾminīn?*

The question is rhetorical, and its force is a rebuke of the very idea.

*Tukrihu* is from *k-r-h*, to dislike, to find repugnant. Form IV *akraha* means to compel — to make someone do what he finds repugnant. The verbal noun *ikrāh* is the technical term in Islamic law for duress, which invalidates contracts, divorce, and legal declarations precisely because it removes consent.

The verse's argument is compressed and complete: *God, who could have compelled, did not. Would you?*

**The Verse in the Prophet's ﷺ Situation**

There is a pastoral dimension here that should not be missed.

The Prophet ﷺ wanted his people to believe — wanted it with an intensity the Qur'an describes as physically consuming:

> *"Perhaps thou wilt destroy thyself with grief, sorrowing after them, if they believe not in this discourse."* (Qur'an 18:6)

> *"Perhaps thou dost torment thyself that they are not believers."* (Qur'an 26:3)

> *"So let not thy soul be expended in regrets over them."* (Qur'an 35:8)

He watched his uncle Abū Ṭālib die refusing the declaration, having protected him for a decade at great cost (al-Bukhārī 1360). And the Qur'an addressed him afterwards:

> *"Truly thou dost not guide whomsoever thou lovest. Rather, God guides whomsoever He will."* (Qur'an 28:56)

This verse belongs to that consolation. It relieves him — and every subsequent caller — of a burden that was never his to carry.

**The Legal Doctrine: No Compulsion in Religion**

This verse stands alongside the Qur'an's most famous statement on the matter:

> *"There is no compulsion in religion. Sound judgement has become distinct from error."* (Qur'an 2:256)

*Lā ikrāha fī al-dīn* — using the same root as *tukrihu* here.

The reason given in that verse is significant: *qad tabayyana al-rushdu mina al-ghayy* — the truth has been made clear. Where clarity has been provided, compulsion is superfluous; and where it is applied, it produces nothing of value.

The *asbāb al-nuzūl* for 2:256 is instructive. Reports record that some of the Anṣār, before Islam, had given children to be raised among the Jewish tribes of Madinah. When Islam came, some wished to compel those children to convert. The verse was revealed forbidding it (Abū Dāwūd 2682, and others).

**Why Compulsion Cannot Produce Faith**

Beyond the prohibition, there is an argument about impossibility, and it is the deeper point.

Faith is an interior state — a conviction of the heart. External force can reach behaviour; it cannot reach conviction. The utmost coercion can achieve is a person who *says* he believes and does not.

The Qur'an has a word for the product: *munāfiq*, hypocrite. And it locates such people below the open disbelievers:

> *"Truly the hypocrites shall be in the lowest depth of the Fire."* (Qur'an 4:145)

So compulsion in religion is not merely forbidden and not merely ineffective. It is *counterproductive*: it manufactures precisely the category the Qur'an condemns most severely.

**The Prescribed Method**

If not compulsion, then what? The Qur'an is specific:

> *"Call unto the way of thy Lord with wisdom and beautiful exhortation, and dispute with them in the most beautiful manner."* (Qur'an 16:125)

*Ḥikmah*, *mawʿiẓah ḥasanah*, *jidāl bi-llatī hiya aḥsan.* Wisdom, good counsel, and argument conducted in the best way.

And the sūrah has just modelled it: Moses' patient questions at v. 77, his warning to the sorcerers before the contest (20:61), the gentle speech commanded even toward Pharaoh (20:44).

**A Necessary Clarification**

Since this verse is often raised in polemic, one clarification is warranted.

Historical Muslim polities, like all pre-modern states, fought wars, and Islamic law regulates warfare. But conversion by force was not the object, and the record is unambiguous: the Christian communities of Egypt, Syria, and Iraq persisted for centuries under Muslim rule and in several cases remained majorities long after the conquests. The Zoroastrians of Persia, the Jews of Spain and the Ottoman lands, and the Hindus of India persisted likewise. A policy of forced conversion would have produced a different demographic record.

The rules governing non-Muslim subjects — whatever their limitations by modern standards — presuppose their continued existence as non-Muslims, which is inconsistent with a doctrine of compelled belief.

**The Application Today**

The verse's practical force falls on anyone who cares about another's guidance — which is to say, on every parent, teacher, and friend of the wavering.

It permits everything except force: argument, example, patience, prayer, and the unwearying presentation of the case. And it forbids the one thing that anxiety most tempts a person toward, which is the attempt to *make* someone believe by pressure.

Because it does not work, and God, who could have done it, chose not to.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:100

> **It is not for a soul to believe, save by God's Leave. And He casts defilement upon those who understand not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Permission Required for Faith**

*Wa-mā kāna li-nafsin an tuʾmina illā bi-idhni Allāh.*

Verse 99 removed the messenger's power to compel. This verse removes the individual's power to believe unaided.

**"Save by God's Leave"**

*Illā bi-idhni Allāh.*

*Idhn* is from *ʾ-dh-n*, whose root sense concerns the ear — *udhun*, ear — and hence hearing, giving heed, and by extension permitting. *Idhn* is permission, leave, sanction.

The phrase has appeared already in this sūrah at v. 3, in the statement that no intercessor acts *illā min baʿdi idhnih* — except after His leave. The parallel is exact and deliberate: nothing consequential occurs in the moral universe except by divine permission, whether it is intercession on the Day of Judgement or the arrival of faith in a heart.

**The Doctrine of Tawfīq**

The theological term for what the verse describes is *tawfīq* — divine enabling, the granting of success. Its opposite is *khidhlān*, the withdrawal of that enabling.

The doctrine holds that a human being's capacity to perform any good act — including the interior act of assent — is itself created by God at the moment of the act. The person acts genuinely, and the capacity by which he acts is a gift.

This is the middle position between two errors that the early theologians were at pains to avoid.

*The Jabrī error* held that human beings do not act at all; God acts through them, and the appearance of choice is illusory. This makes reward and punishment arbitrary and contradicts the Qur'an's constant address to human agency.

*The Qadarī error* held that human beings create their own acts independently, which makes them creators alongside God and contradicts the Qur'an's statement that *"God created you and what you do"* (37:96).

The mainstream position — articulated by al-Ashʿarī as *kasb*, acquisition, and by al-Māturīdī in a related formulation — holds that God creates the act and the human being acquires it, bearing responsibility for it through his intention and choice.

The philosophical difficulties here are real and have never been fully dissolved, and the honest scholars said so. What the tradition insisted on was holding both ends of the rope: God's absolute sovereignty, and genuine human responsibility. Both are asserted by the Qur'an, and neither may be surrendered to make the other tidier.

**How This Verse Should Be Read Practically**

Whatever the metaphysics, the verse's practical import is clear and is not in dispute.

*It produces humility in the believer.* Anyone who has faith did not achieve it. Others no less intelligent, no less sincere, no less exposed to the evidence, do not have it. The appropriate response to possessing it is gratitude, not superiority.

The Prophet's ﷺ most frequent supplication reflected this:

> *"O Turner of hearts, make my heart firm upon Thy religion."* When ʿĀʾishah asked why he said it so often, he replied: *"There is no heart that is not between two of the fingers of the Merciful. When He wills, He makes it firm, and when He wills, He turns it away."* (al-Tirmidhī 2140)

A man guaranteed Paradise prayed constantly for the stability of his own heart.

*And it directs the seeker to ask.* If faith requires permission, the response is to request it. This is why the Qur'an teaches supplications for guidance, and why al-Fātiḥah — recited in every unit of every prayer — consists centrally of the petition *ihdinā al-ṣirāṭ al-mustaqīm*, "guide us upon the straight path." A Muslim asks for guidance seventeen times a day at minimum.

**"And He Casts Defilement Upon Those Who Understand Not"**

*Wa-yajʿalu al-rijsa ʿalā alladhīna lā yaʿqilūn.*

*Rijs* is from *r-j-s*, and denotes filth, impurity, abomination. The Qur'an uses it of physical impurity (6:145, of carrion and blood), of idols (22:30), of wine and gambling (5:90), and of a spiritual condition (9:125).

Here it describes a state cast upon those who do not use their reason. The commentators gloss it variously as punishment, as the wrath of God, as doubt and confusion, and as the state of being abandoned to one's own devices.

**"Those Who Do Not Use Reason"**

*Alladhīna lā yaʿqilūn.*

*ʿAqala* is to bind, to tie — the primary sense being the tethering of a camel (*ʿiqāl* is the cord used for it). The *ʿaql*, intellect, is so named because it *restrains*: it binds a person back from folly, ties impulses down, and holds thought together.

The Qur'an's conception of reason is therefore not primarily calculative. To have *ʿaql* is to be *restrained by understanding* — to have one's conduct tied to what one knows.

**The Connection Between the Two Halves**

At first glance the verse's two clauses sit oddly together. The first says faith requires divine permission; the second condemns those who do not reason. If faith is granted, why blame the unbeliever?

The resolution lies in what the second clause specifies. It does not say God casts defilement on those to whom He denied permission. It says He casts it on *those who do not use their reason*.

The failure named is a failure to *do* something — to employ a faculty that was given. And this is a matter within human control. Nobody's reason is exercised for him.

So the two halves describe a cooperation: the human being employs what he has been given, and God grants what only He can grant. The one who does not employ it has not met the condition — not because the condition earns the gift, but because the gift is given to those who seek it.

The Qur'an states the relation elsewhere:

> *"And those who strive for Us, We shall surely guide them upon Our ways."* (Qur'an 29:69)

*Jāhadū fīnā* — those who exert themselves. The striving comes first in the sentence; the guidance follows.

**The Qur'an's Insistence on Reason**

It is worth documenting how central this theme is, because the Qur'an's tone on the subject is unusual among scriptures.

Some forty-nine verses use forms of *ʿaqala*, almost always as reproach: *a-fa-lā taʿqilūn* — "will you not use reason?" Alongside these are the constant appeals to *tafakkur* (reflection), *tadabbur* (deliberation), *naẓar* (observation), and *tafaqquh* (understanding).

And the Qur'an describes those who refuse in terms that are deliberately shocking:

> *"Truly the worst of beasts in God's sight are the deaf and dumb, those who do not use reason."* (Qur'an 8:22)

> *"Or dost thou suppose that most of them hear or understand? They are but as cattle. Nay, they are further astray from the way."* (Qur'an 25:44)

The comparison to livestock is not casual. Cattle possess perception but not reflection; they respond to stimulus without evaluating it. A human being who does the same has forfeited the faculty that distinguishes him.

**What "Not Using Reason" Looks Like**

The sūrah has been documenting the failure throughout, and its instances can be gathered:

*Following conjecture as though it were knowledge* (v. 36).
*Guessing* — *in hum illā yakhruṣūn* (v. 66).
*Asserting without authority* — *sulṭān* (v. 68).
*Appealing to ancestral practice as a reason* (v. 78).
*Reassigning evidence to a familiar category without examining the fit* (v. 76).
*Demanding evidence one has already decided to reject* (v. 97).

None of these is a deficiency of intelligence. Every one of them is a refusal to apply intelligence where its conclusions would be inconvenient. That is what *lā yaʿqilūn* names.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:101

> **Say, "Behold that which is in the heavens and on the earth." But signs and warnings avail not a people who believe not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Look**

*Quli-nẓurū mādhā fī al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍ.*

Having said that reason must be used, the sūrah now says what to use it on.

**"Behold"**

*Unẓurū.* The imperative of *naẓara* — to look, but with the sense of looking *at* something in order to understand it. It is not *raʾā* (to see) or *baṣara* (to perceive); it is the deliberate direction of attention with a view to drawing a conclusion.

The same imperative has appeared at v. 39 and v. 73 of this sūrah, both times directing attention to the outcomes of history. Here it directs attention to the structure of the world.

**"That Which Is in the Heavens and on the Earth"**

*Mādhā fī al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍ.*

The scope is total. Not a particular sign, not a designated miracle — everything.

This is a distinctive feature of the Qur'an's argumentative method, and it is worth stating plainly. The Qur'an does not, in the main, ask people to believe on the basis of extraordinary events. It asks them to attend to *ordinary* ones.

The sūrah has done this from the beginning. Verse 3: the creation of the heavens and the earth in six days, and the governance of the affair. Verse 5: the sun as radiance and the moon as light, with its measured stations. Verse 6: the alternation of night and day. Verse 24: rain and the growth of plants. Verse 31: provision from heaven and earth, hearing and sight, the living from the dead. Verse 67: the night for rest and the day for seeing.

Every one of these is available to everyone, every day, without instruments, without travel, without special access.

**Why the Ordinary Is the Better Argument**

There is a reason the Qur'an prefers this route, and it connects directly to v. 97.

An extraordinary event can be disputed, explained away, or forgotten. It happens once, to a limited audience, and its evidential value depends on testimony.

The regularity of the cosmos cannot be disputed, because it is continuously present to everyone. It requires no testimony. It does not fade. And its evidential weight increases rather than diminishes with examination — the more closely the world is studied, the more structure is found in it.

The Qur'an also draws attention to what the demand for extraordinary signs actually reveals. A person who is not moved by the sun but says he would be moved by a portent has not made a statement about evidence. He has made a statement about *entertainment*.

**"But Signs and Warnings Avail Not a People Who Believe Not"**

*Wa-mā tughnī al-āyātu wa-l-nudhuru ʿan qawmin lā yuʾminūn.*

The verse ends by immediately qualifying the command it has just issued.

*Tughnī* is from *gh-n-y*, sufficiency, wealth — the root of *al-Ghanī*, the Self-Sufficient (v. 68). Form IV *aghnā* means to avail, to suffice, to be of use.

*Āyāt* — signs. *Nudhur* — warnings, the plural of *nadhīr*.

The two categories cover the whole apparatus of guidance: the evidence that persuades, and the alerts that motivate. Neither works on a people who have already decided.

**The Sūrah's Consistent Realism**

This is the third time in six verses that the sūrah has made this point — at v. 96, v. 97, and now here. The repetition is emphatic and it serves a purpose.

The purpose is to correct an expectation. A person who has recognised the truth naturally assumes that presenting it will produce recognition in others. When it does not, he concludes either that he presented it badly or that the other person is malicious.

The sūrah says the mechanism is different. Evidence operates on those who are open to it. Openness is not produced by evidence.

And there is no contradiction between commanding the look and stating that some will not benefit from it. The command is universal; the response varies. The caller's obligation attaches to the command, not to the response — which the sūrah will state directly at v. 108.

**The Verse and the Sciences**

A word is warranted on how Muslims have historically read this verse, because its influence has been considerable.

The instruction to *look at what is in the heavens and the earth*, issued as a divine command, was understood by the early Muslim scientific tradition as a religious warrant for the systematic study of nature.

The astronomers of Baghdad and Samarkand, the physicians of Cairo and Cordoba, the mathematicians and opticians and geographers of the classical Islamic centuries — many of them cited verses of this kind as the justification for their work. Al-Bīrūnī, in his book on India, wrote that the study of the cosmos is a religious obligation because it is the study of God's handiwork. Ibn al-Haytham, whose work on optics established the experimental method in that field, framed his enquiry in similar terms.

Whether that tradition can be revived is a separate question. But the textual warrant is not obscure. The Qur'an commands observation of the natural order, in the imperative, and states that the order is evidence.

**A Caution**

One qualification, because the enthusiasm for this reading has sometimes produced excess.

The verse does not authorise the practice — widespread in modern apologetics — of locating specific scientific findings in Qur'anic verses and treating them as miraculous predictions. That approach is methodologically unsound, since it reads the text through whatever the current state of science happens to be, and it hostages revelation to theories that may be revised.

What the verse commands is *looking* — the disposition of attention to the created order as a manifestation of divine wisdom. That is a stable instruction, and it does not depend on any particular scientific result.

**The Practical Discipline**

For the individual reader, the verse prescribes something simple and largely neglected.

Look. Deliberately, and with attention, at something in the world — the sky, a tree, a face, the mechanics of one's own hand — with the question the Qur'an intends: *what does this indicate?*

The obstacle is not the difficulty of the exercise but its unfamiliarity. Modern life is arranged to prevent it: attention is captured continuously, and the natural world is mediated through screens or excluded by architecture. Verse 67 noted the loss of the night; the same applies to the sky, which most urban dwellers now cannot see.

The Qur'an's remedy is the imperative *unẓurū* — a command to do something that costs nothing and that almost no one does.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:102

> **Do they await aught but the like of the days of those who passed away before them? Say, "Wait! Truly I am waiting along with you."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question of Expectation**

*Fa-hal yantaẓirūna illā mithla ayyāmi alladhīna khalaw min qablihim?*

The sūrah returns to the theme of waiting that it introduced at v. 20 and developed at vv. 48–52.

**"The Days of Those Who Passed Away Before Them"**

*Ayyāmi alladhīna khalaw min qablihim.*

The Arabic idiom *ayyām al-ʿarab* — "the days of the Arabs" — referred to the famous battles and calamities of pre-Islamic history. *Yawm*, "day," carried the sense of a decisive event, a day on which something conclusive happened.

So *ayyām alladhīna khalaw* means: the decisive days that befell earlier peoples. The flood. The wind that destroyed ʿĀd. The cry that took Thamūd. The overturning of Lot's towns. The sea that closed over Pharaoh.

The sūrah has just narrated two of these at length. The question now is whether the audience is waiting for its own.

**The Rhetoric of the Question**

*Fa-hal yantaẓirūna illā…* — "Do they await anything but…?"

The construction is exclusive: the only thing that can be awaited is this.

And the force of it is to expose what the audience's position amounts to. They have been told; they have refused; they have demanded that the threatened consequence appear. Very well — that is what remains. There is no third outcome.

**"Say: Wait! Truly I Am Waiting Along With You"**

*Quli-ntaẓirū innī maʿakum mina al-muntaẓirīn.*

The instruction to the Prophet ﷺ is the same as at v. 20: *fa-ntaẓirū innī maʿakum mina al-muntaẓirīn*. The formula is repeated verbatim, closing a frame that has held the whole sūrah.

**The Two Kinds of Waiting**

The verse's power lies in the fact that both parties are waiting, and they are waiting for opposite things.

The deniers wait to be proved right — for the threatened day not to come, or for the messenger to be discredited, or for the movement to collapse.

The believer waits to be proved right — for the promise to be fulfilled.

Neither can accelerate the outcome. Both are in the position of having stated a position and being unable to do anything further but let time run.

There is a curious equality in this. The Prophet ﷺ is not told to say "you will see"; he is told to say "I am waiting *with* you." The stance is not triumphant. It is the stance of a man who has staked everything on a claim and is prepared to be judged by the result.

**Why This Is a Strong Position**

Consider what it means for a claimant to invite the passage of time.

A fraud needs the question closed quickly, before scrutiny accumulates. Time is his enemy: inconsistencies emerge, promises come due, and the initial enthusiasm decays.

A true claim can afford to wait, because time supplies the evidence.

The Prophet ﷺ said this repeatedly and specifically during the Makkan years, when there was nothing to point to. The hadith of Khabbāb, quoted at v. 87, is the clearest instance: a tortured man asking for relief, and the answer being a description of ordinary safety on the road from Ṣanʿāʾ to Ḥaḍramawt — an outcome that could only be verified by waiting, and that was verified.

**The Sūrah's Structural Frame**

It is worth noting how the repetition at v. 20 and v. 102 functions.

Verse 20: they demand a sign; the reply is *"the Unseen belongs to God, so wait — I am waiting with you."*

Verse 102: they have received the argument, the natural signs, and two extended histories; the reply is the same.

Between them lies the whole body of the sūrah. And the identity of the two replies makes a point: nothing in the intervening material was designed to compel. The evidence was presented, the histories recited, the arguments made — and at the end, the position is the same. Wait and see.

This is consistent with the sūrah's entire epistemology. Evidence is offered; assent is free; verification is deferred; and the deferral is itself part of the test.

**The Days That Came**

The Makkan audience did not have to wait long, and the sūrah's implicit prediction was fulfilled within roughly two decades — though not in the form its first hearers would have expected.

There was no wind, no cry, no overturning. Instead there was Badr, where the leadership of Quraysh was destroyed in a morning: Abū Jahl, Umayyah ibn Khalaf, ʿUtbah and Shaybah, al-Walīd ibn ʿUtbah. The Prophet ﷺ stood at the well into which their bodies had been thrown and addressed them by name (al-Bukhārī 3976).

And then there was the conquest, when the city that had expelled him opened its gates, and he entered with his head bowed so low over his mount that his beard nearly touched the saddle.

The "day" came. It was not the destruction of the earlier peoples. It was, for most of them, the offer of pardon.

**The Application to the Present Reader**

The verse's use for a reader today is not primarily about awaiting a communal catastrophe.

It is about the fact that *everyone* is waiting, whether or not they conceive of themselves as doing so. Every person is proceeding toward a verification of the positions he has taken, on a timetable he does not control and cannot extend.

The Qur'an's word for the arrival is *al-yaqīn*, certainty:

> *"And worship thy Lord until certainty comes unto thee."* (Qur'an 15:99)

The commentators are unanimous that *al-yaqīn* here means death. It is called certainty because it is the point at which all questions are settled, for everyone, without exception.

So the instruction *intaẓirū* — wait — is not a threat directed at a particular ancient audience. It is a description of the condition of every living person, and the only question it leaves open is what one does during the waiting.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:103

> **Then We save Our messengers and those who believe. Thus is it incumbent upon Us to save the believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Promise**

*Thumma nunajjī rusulanā wa-alladhīna āmanū. Kadhālika ḥaqqan ʿalaynā nunji al-muʾminīn.*

After the warning of v. 102 comes its counterpart. The days that befell earlier peoples were not indiscriminate; they had a shape, and the shape included deliverance.

**"We Save Our Messengers and Those Who Believe"**

*Nunajjī rusulanā wa-alladhīna āmanū.*

The verb is *najjā* again — the fourth occurrence in this section of the sūrah (v. 73 of Noah, v. 86 in the believers' prayer, v. 92 of Pharaoh's body, and now here).

Its use here generalises what the narratives demonstrated. Noah was delivered; the believers of Egypt were delivered; and the verse states that this is not a series of coincidences but a rule.

Note the pairing: *Our messengers, and those who believed*. The deliverance extends beyond the prophet to those who accepted him. This was the pattern in both narratives — Noah *"and those with him in the ark"* (v. 73), Moses *and* the Children of Israel (v. 90).

**"Thus Is It Incumbent Upon Us"**

*Kadhālika ḥaqqan ʿalaynā.*

This is the verse's most remarkable phrase, and it must be handled carefully.

*Ḥaqqan ʿalaynā* — "a right upon Us," "incumbent upon Us," "a due binding on Us."

The construction attributes an obligation to God. And this raises an immediate theological question, since nothing external to God can impose a duty upon Him. He is *al-Ghanī*, the Self-Sufficient (v. 68), and the Qur'an states: *"He is not questioned about what He does, but they are questioned"* (21:23).

**How God Binds Himself**

The resolution, which the classical scholars are unanimous on, is that the obligation is *self-imposed*.

The sūrah's own earlier verse supplies the doctrine, and it was examined at v. 60:

> *"He has prescribed Mercy for Himself."* (Qur'an 6:12, repeated at 6:54)

*Kataba ʿalā nafsihi al-raḥmah* — He *wrote upon Himself* mercy. The verb *kataba* with *ʿalā* is the standard Qur'anic construction for the imposition of an obligation (compare *kutiba ʿalaykum al-ṣiyām*, "fasting is prescribed for you," 2:183).

So the duty is real, and its source is God's own commitment. He has bound Himself, and He does not break what He has bound.

The hadith qudsī states the same in the register of prohibition:

> *"O My servants, I have forbidden oppression to Myself and have made it forbidden among you, so do not oppress one another."* (Muslim 2577)

*Innī ḥarramtu al-ẓulma ʿalā nafsī* — He made injustice forbidden *to Himself*.

**Why This Is the Most Reassuring Thing in Scripture**

The significance of this construction is difficult to overstate, and it deserves to be dwelt on.

A promise from a party who might change His mind is a hope. A promise from a party who has bound Himself, and who is incapable of falsehood, is a certainty.

The Qur'an makes the point explicitly:

> *"The promise of God — God does not fail His promise, but most people know not."* (Qur'an 30:6)

> *"Who is truer in speech than God?"* (Qur'an 4:87)

For a persecuted believer — the sūrah's original audience, and many since — this verse states that deliverance is not a matter of divine goodwill that may or may not be extended. It is a debt God has entered into His own ledger.

**A Necessary Precision About What Is Promised**

Here a serious qualification is required, because this verse is easily misread into a promise it does not make.

It does not promise that no believer will suffer or die. The record forbids that reading, and the Qur'an itself supplies the counter-cases.

The believers among Pharaoh's people were persecuted (v. 83). Sumayyah bint Khayyāṭ was killed under torture at Makkah, the first martyr of Islam. Yāsir died likewise. Seventy companions were killed at Uḥud and seventy at Biʾr Maʿūnah. Ḥamzah, the Prophet's ﷺ uncle, was killed and mutilated.

And the Qur'an describes prophets themselves being killed:

> *"And they used to slay the prophets without right."* (Qur'an 2:61)

> *"How many a prophet has fought, and with him many devoted men? They faltered not for what befell them in the way of God, nor did they weaken, nor did they yield."* (Qur'an 3:146)

So *najāt* — deliverance — cannot mean the avoidance of harm. What does it mean?

**Three Levels of Deliverance**

*Deliverance of the cause.* The messengers' missions were not extinguished. Every prophet's message survived him, and the community he founded outlived those who tried to destroy it. Pharaoh is a corpse; the Torah is recited. Quraysh's leadership died at Badr; the Qur'an is memorised by millions.

*Deliverance from the punishment that fell on the deniers.* This is the immediate sense in the narratives. When the collective judgement came, the believers were not in it — the ark, the parted sea.

*And deliverance in the final reckoning.* This is the decisive sense, and it is the one the Qur'an ultimately means:

> *"Whosoever is kept from the Fire and made to enter the Garden has certainly triumphed."* (Qur'an 3:185)

That verse, cited at v. 64 of this sūrah as the definition of *al-fawz al-ʿaẓīm*, is the standard by which deliverance is measured. By that standard Sumayyah was delivered, and Pharaoh — who died in his own bed of the sea, having ruled an empire — was not.

**The Reversal of Appearances**

There is a hard consolation in this that the sūrah has been building toward since v. 24.

Judged by what is visible, the persecuted lose. They are the ones who suffer, and their oppressors are the ones with the palaces.

The sūrah's argument, made through the parable of the mown field (v. 24), the *matāʿ* of the wrongdoers (v. 70), and Pharaoh's preserved body (v. 92), is that the visible measure is the wrong one. It measures the *dunyā*, which is by definition the nearest and lowest, and which is scheduled to end.

Verse 103 states the true measure, and states that God has bound Himself to apply it.

**The Verse's Placement**

This verse concludes the sūrah's argumentative body. What follows (vv. 104–109) is a series of concluding declarations addressed to the Prophet ﷺ and, through him, to the reader.

Ending the argument here is fitting. The sūrah opened with a scoffing question about revelation (v. 2), moved through creation, judgement, human ingratitude, and two histories of destruction — and closes its case with a guarantee.

Not a threat. A guarantee, entered into by the One who does not fail.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:104

> **Say, "O mankind! If you are in doubt as to my religion, then I worship not those whom you worship apart from God. Rather, I worship God, Who takes your souls, and I am commanded to be among the believers."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Concluding Declarations Begin**

*Qul yā ayyuhā al-nāsu in kuntum fī shakkin min dīnī fa-lā aʿbudu alladhīna taʿbudūna min dūni Allāh.*

The sūrah's final section opens with the first of six imperatives to the Prophet ﷺ — *qul*, "say" — that will carry it to its close.

**"O Mankind"**

*Yā ayyuhā al-nās.*

The address is universal, as it was at v. 23 (*"O mankind! Your rebellion is only against yourselves"*), at v. 57 (*"O mankind! There has come unto you an admonition"*), and at v. 108.

The scope is deliberate. Whatever the immediate Makkan occasion, the declaration is framed for all human beings in all times. The sūrah's closing arguments are not addressed to Quraysh alone.

**The Structure of the Declaration**

The verse has a precise architecture, and it is a model of how a religious position should be stated.

*A concession about the audience's state:* if you are in doubt about my religion.
*A negation:* I do not worship what you worship.
*An affirmation:* I worship God.
*A description that grounds the affirmation:* Who takes your souls.
*And a statement of authority:* I am commanded.

Negation before affirmation, with a reason and a warrant. The pattern reproduces the structure of the *shahādah* itself: *lā ilāha* — there is no god — *illā Allāh* — except God. Clearing precedes building.

**"If You Are in Doubt as to My Religion"**

*In kuntum fī shakkin min dīnī.*

The conditional acknowledges the audience's uncertainty without arguing against it. There is no attempt here to remove the doubt; the argumentative work of the sūrah is finished. What remains is clarification of position.

This is an important distinction, and it governs the whole closing section. The Prophet ﷺ is not told to persuade further. He is told to make his own commitment unmistakable, so that no one can be confused about what is on offer.

*Dīn* is from *d-y-n*, a root with a wide field: debt (*dayn*), judgement (*yawm al-dīn*), obedience, and way of life. *Dīn* is accordingly not "religion" in the modern sense of a private conviction. It is the whole arrangement of obligation and requital within which a person lives.

**"I Worship Not Those Whom You Worship"**

*Fa-lā aʿbudu alladhīna taʿbudūna min dūni Allāh.*

The negation is total and it is stated in the present tense — not "I will not" but "I do not."

The closest parallel is Sūrat al-Kāfirūn, revealed in Makkah in response to an offer of compromise:

> *"Say: O disbelievers! I worship not what you worship, nor are you worshippers of what I worship. Nor am I a worshipper of what you have worshipped, nor are you worshippers of what I worship. Unto you your religion, and unto me my religion."* (Qur'an 109:1–6)

The occasion of that sūrah, recorded by Ibn Isḥāq and others, was a proposal from Quraysh: that the Prophet ﷺ worship their gods for a year and they would worship his for a year. The reply was six verses of absolute refusal.

**Why No Compromise Was Possible**

The refusal can appear rigid, particularly to a modern sensibility that values accommodation. It is worth explaining why it was necessary.

The Makkans were not asking for tolerance; they were offering it. Their religious system was already pluralistic — the Kaʿbah housed three hundred and sixty idols, and adding one more god cost them nothing. They were entirely willing to include Allah in the pantheon; indeed they already acknowledged Him as supreme, which is exactly what the sūrah recorded at v. 31.

What they could not accept was *exclusivity*. And exclusivity is the entire content of *tawḥīd*.

A claim that God is one and alone worthy of worship cannot be partially accepted. Adding Him to a collection refutes the claim in the act of appearing to honour it. This is why the negation must precede the affirmation, and why the *shahādah* begins with a denial.

**"Rather, I Worship God, Who Takes Your Souls"**

*Wa-lākin aʿbudu Allāha alladhī yatawaffākum.*

The description chosen to identify God is arresting, and its selection is deliberate.

Of all the attributes available — Creator, Sustainer, Merciful, Powerful — the verse identifies Him as *the One who takes your souls*.

*Yatawaffā* is form V of *w-f-y*, to be complete, to fulfil — the root of *wafāʾ*, faithfulness, and *awfā*, to render in full. *Tawaffā* means to take a thing in full, to receive it completely — hence to take a soul at death.

The etymology carries a striking implication. Death, in this vocabulary, is not a subtraction but a *collection in full*. A term has run; a deposit is reclaimed complete.

**Why This Attribute Here**

The rhetorical force is exact.

The Makkans' gods could do many things, in their view: intercede, protect, bring rain, grant children. What none of them could do was take a soul.

By identifying God as the one who performs *this* act, the verse points at the single unavoidable transaction in every human life — the one moment at which every person, without exception, is entirely in the hands of a power he does not control and cannot negotiate with.

The argument is therefore: *whatever you may think about the rest, this you cannot avoid, and the One who does it is the One I worship.*

The sūrah made the same move at v. 31: *"Who owns hearing and sight? And who brings forth the living from the dead?"* The identification of God by acts that no rival can plausibly claim.

**"And I Am Commanded to Be Among the Believers"**

*Wa-umirtu an akūna mina al-muʾminīn.*

The closing clause echoes Noah at v. 72 (*"I am commanded to be among those who submit"*) almost exactly, with *al-muʾminīn* in place of *al-muslimīn*.

The echo is the sūrah's method throughout: the earlier prophet's words are handed to the present one. Noah's speech in vv. 71–72 was, in effect, a rehearsal of the declaration the Prophet ﷺ is here commanded to make.

And the passive *umirtu* carries the same weight it did there. The Prophet ﷺ does not present his position as a conclusion he reached or a preference he holds. It is an instruction he received and is obeying — the same subordination established at v. 15, where he declared that he could not alter the Qur'an of his own accord.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:105

> **And, "Set thy face toward religion as a ḥanīf, and be thou not among the idolaters.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Imperative**

*Wa-an aqim wajhaka li-l-dīni ḥanīfan wa-lā takūnanna mina al-mushrikīn.*

The declaration of v. 104 is followed by the instruction that gives it its stance.

**"Set Thy Face"**

*Aqim wajhak.*

*Aqim* is the imperative of *aqāma*, form IV of *q-w-m* — to make stand, to set upright, to establish. It is the same verb used of prayer: *aqīmū al-ṣalāh* (v. 87).

*Wajh* is the face. But in Qur'anic usage it carries much more: it denotes the whole self as oriented, the direction of one's attention and purpose. To turn one's *wajh* toward something is to direct one's entire being toward it.

The Qur'an uses the idiom for total commitment:

> *"Nay, whosoever submits his face to God while being virtuous shall have his reward with his Lord."* (Qur'an 2:112)

> *"So set thy face toward the religion as a ḥanīf — the primordial nature from God upon which He originated mankind."* (Qur'an 30:30)

That last verse is the closest parallel and it supplies the key term.

**"As a Ḥanīf"**

*Ḥanīfan.*

*Ḥanīf* is from *ḥ-n-f*, whose root sense is to incline or turn away — specifically, to turn away from error toward what is right. The word describes one who has turned aside from the prevailing corruption toward pure monotheism.

The Qur'an uses it above all of Abraham:

> *"Abraham was not a Jew, nor a Christian, but he was a ḥanīf, a submitter, and he was not among the idolaters."* (Qur'an 3:67)

> *"Then We revealed unto thee: Follow the creed of Abraham, a ḥanīf; and he was not among the idolaters."* (Qur'an 16:123)

Note that in both verses *ḥanīf* is immediately paired with the denial of *shirk* — exactly as in the present verse. The pairing is formulaic and it defines the term: to be *ḥanīf* is to be turned away from association.

**The Historical Ḥunafāʾ**

The word had a currency in pre-Islamic Arabia. The sources record a small number of individuals in Makkah and elsewhere who had rejected the idols before the Prophet's ﷺ mission and were seeking the original religion of Abraham.

Ibn Isḥāq names several: Waraqah ibn Nawfal (Khadījah's cousin, who confirmed the first revelation), ʿUthmān ibn al-Ḥuwayrith, ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Jaḥsh, and Zayd ibn ʿAmr ibn Nufayl.

Zayd's case is the most striking. He refused to eat meat sacrificed to idols, forbade the burial of infant girls, and is reported to have stood against the Kaʿbah saying: *"O God, if I knew the most beloved way to You, I would worship You by it, but I do not know."* He died before the mission began. The Prophet ﷺ said of him:

> *"He will be raised as a community by himself on the Day of Resurrection."* (reported in al-Bukhārī 3826 in substance, and elsewhere)

The existence of the *ḥunafāʾ* is significant for the Qur'an's argument. It demonstrates that the idolatry of Makkah was not inevitable — that individuals, with no scripture and no prophet, had recognised its falsity by reflection alone. The excuse of ignorance was not available.

**The Concept of Fiṭrah**

Verse 30:30, quoted above, links *ḥanīf* to *fiṭrah*:

> *"So set thy face toward the religion as a ḥanīf — the fiṭrah of God upon which He originated mankind. There is no altering the creation of God. That is the upright religion, but most of mankind know not."*

*Fiṭrah* is from *f-ṭ-r*, to split open, to originate — the same root as *fāṭir*, Originator. It denotes the original constitution with which a thing is made.

The doctrine, established in this sūrah at vv. 12 and 22 (the crisis that strips away accretions and reveals native monotheism), holds that human beings are made oriented toward their Creator, and that idolatry is an overlay rather than a native condition.

The Prophet ﷺ stated it:

> *"Every child is born upon the fiṭrah. Then his parents make him a Jew, a Christian, or a Zoroastrian — just as an animal produces a whole offspring; do you find any mutilated among them?"* (al-Bukhārī 1358, Muslim 2658)

The analogy is careful. The animal is born intact; damage is inflicted afterwards. So too, on this doctrine, is religious deviation something done *to* a person rather than a natural growth.

To be *ḥanīf* is therefore to return to a condition, not to adopt a novelty. This is why Islam presents itself not as a new religion but as the restoration of the original one — the claim implicit in Noah's *"I am among those who submit"* at v. 72.

**"And Be Thou Not Among the Idolaters"**

*Wa-lā takūnanna mina al-mushrikīn.*

Again the emphatic doubled *nūn*, as at vv. 89, 94, and 95.

*Mushrik* is from *sh-r-k*, to share, to associate — the root of *sharikah*, a partnership. The *mushrik* is one who assigns a partner to God.

The address to the Prophet ﷺ raises the same question as vv. 94–95, and the answer is the same: the warning to the highest figure establishes that no one is exempt, and it protects the community against his own deification.

But there is an additional point here that bears stating, because *shirk* is not confined to the worship of statues.

**The Subtler Forms of Shirk**

The sūrah has already treated this at v. 18 in connection with intercession, and at v. 22 in connection with crisis-driven sincerity.

Classical scholars distinguish *al-shirk al-akbar* — major association, which removes a person from Islam — from *al-shirk al-aṣghar*, minor association, which does not but which is gravely dangerous.

The Prophet ﷺ identified the principal form of the latter:

> *"The thing I fear most for you is minor shirk."* They said: *"What is minor shirk, O Messenger of God?"* He said: *"Showing off (al-riyāʾ). God will say on the Day of Resurrection, when people are being recompensed for their deeds: Go to those you were showing off to in the world, and see whether you find any reward with them."* (Aḥmad 23630)

And in the hadith qudsī:

> *"I am the least in need of partners. Whoever performs a deed in which he associates another with Me, I leave him and his association."* (Muslim 2985)

The instruction *lā takūnanna mina al-mushrikīn* therefore has a live application to every believer, and it is not satisfied by the absence of idols. The question it puts is: for whom is this being done?

**Setting the Face: The Practical Sense**

There is a final observation worth making about the imagery.

A face points in one direction at a time. This is a physical fact and the metaphor depends on it.

The instruction *aqim wajhak* therefore excludes divided orientation — the attempt to face two ways, which produces facing neither. The Qur'an describes the condition:

> *"Wavering between this and that, neither to these nor to those."* (Qur'an 4:143)

*Mudhabdhabīna bayna dhālika.* The word suggests something swung back and forth.

The single-directed face is the outward image of the single-directed heart, and it is the condition the sūrah has been recommending since its opening: God alone as Governor (v. 3), Guardian (v. 62), Protector (v. 30), and object of reliance (v. 84).

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:106

> **And call not upon that which neither benefits thee nor harms thee, apart from God. For if thou dost, then thou wouldst surely be among the wrongdoers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Imperative**

*Wa-lā tadʿu min dūni Allāhi mā lā yanfaʿuka wa-lā yaḍurruka. Fa-in faʿalta fa-innaka idhan mina al-ẓālimīn.*

Having commanded orientation toward God, the sūrah now prohibits the alternative in the most practical terms.

**"Call Not Upon"**

*Lā tadʿu.*

The verb is *daʿā* — to call, to invoke, to summon. Its verbal noun *duʿāʾ* is the standard term for supplication.

The choice of verb rather than *taʿbud* (worship) is significant, because it targets the specific act at the heart of idolatry: *asking*.

The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Supplication is worship."* (Abū Dāwūd 1479, al-Tirmidhī 2969, who graded it ḥasan ṣaḥīḥ)

*Al-duʿāʾu huwa al-ʿibādah.* Not merely that supplication is *part of* worship, but that it *is* worship. The identification is total.

The reason is that supplication is the purest expression of the creature's relation to the Creator. To ask is to acknowledge one's need, the other's capacity, and one's dependence. Where the asking goes, the worship goes.

This is why the verse prohibits the calling rather than merely the bowing. A person may never prostrate to a stone and still direct his fundamental petitions elsewhere.

**"That Which Neither Benefits Thee Nor Harms Thee"**

*Mā lā yanfaʿuka wa-lā yaḍurruk.*

The description is the sūrah's second use of the formula. Verse 18 said of the idols: *"they worship, apart from God, that which neither harms them nor benefits them."*

The two terms cover the entire range of motives for religious appeal. People approach powers either to gain something (*nafʿ*) or to avert something (*ḍarr*) — hope and fear, the two engines of all petition.

The verse's claim is that these entities can do neither. Not that they are weak; that they are *irrelevant* — outside the causal chain entirely.

**The Argument From Impotence**

The Qur'an presses this argument repeatedly and with a certain relish, and it is worth noting how it does so.

It does not argue that the idols are demons, or evil, or rival powers. It argues that they *cannot do anything*, and it makes the point by attending to concrete details:

> *"Truly those upon whom you call apart from God are servants like yourselves. So call upon them, and let them respond to you, if you are truthful. Have they feet with which they walk? Or have they hands with which they grasp? Or have they eyes with which they see? Or have they ears with which they hear?"* (Qur'an 7:194–195)

> *"Truly those upon whom you call apart from God will never create a fly, even if they gathered together for that. And if the fly should rob them of aught, they could not rescue it from it. Feeble are the seeker and the sought!"* (Qur'an 22:73)

The image of the fly is deliberately undignified. It is not that the gods cannot create a world; they cannot recover a crumb from an insect.

And Abraham's argument to his father, in the Qur'an's account, has the same character:

> *"O my father, why dost thou worship that which neither hears nor sees, nor avails thee in aught?"* (Qur'an 19:42)

**"Then Thou Wouldst Surely Be Among the Wrongdoers"**

*Fa-innaka idhan mina al-ẓālimīn.*

The consequence is stated in the strongest available terms.

*Ẓālim* is from *ẓ-l-m*, whose root sense is darkness, and whose derived sense is the placing of a thing outside its proper place — *waḍʿ al-shayʾ fī ghayri maḥallih*, as the lexicographers define it.

*Ẓulm* is therefore, fundamentally, *misplacement*. To wrong someone is to give him other than his due; to wrong oneself is to put oneself where one does not belong.

**Why Shirk Is the Greatest Ẓulm**

On this definition, the reason *shirk* is called *ẓulm* becomes clear, and it is not merely rhetorical.

Worship directed at something other than the Creator is the placing of the highest act in the wrong location. It is a category error before it is a moral failure — like paying a debt to the wrong party, or addressing a petition to someone with no jurisdiction.

The Qur'an states it directly:

> *"Truly association is a tremendous wrong."* (Qur'an 31:13)

*Inna al-shirka la-ẓulmun ʿaẓīm.* Luqmān's counsel to his son, and the Qur'an's definitive statement on the matter.

And there is a hadith that turns on precisely this vocabulary. When 6:82 was revealed — *"Those who believe and do not obscure their faith with wrong (ẓulm), they shall have security"* — the companions were distressed, since who among them had committed no wrong? The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"It is not as you think. Have you not heard what the righteous servant said: 'Truly association is a tremendous wrong'?"* (al-Bukhārī 32, Muslim 124)

The *ẓulm* in that verse was *shirk*, not sin in general.

**Who Is Wronged?**

A final point, and it is the one that turns this verse from a prohibition into a mercy.

God is not diminished by *shirk*. The hadith qudsī states it:

> *"O My servants, were the first of you and the last of you, the human of you and the jinn of you, to be as pious as the most pious heart of any one of you, that would not increase My kingdom in anything. O My servants, were the first of you and the last of you, the human of you and the jinn of you, to be as wicked as the most wicked heart of any one of you, that would not decrease My kingdom in anything."* (Muslim 2577)

So the wrong is not done to God. It is done to the one who commits it — which is the doctrine the sūrah stated at v. 23 (*"your rebellion is only against yourselves"*) and at v. 44 (*"human beings wrong themselves"*).

The person who directs his petitions to the powerless has done himself an injury of a specific kind: he has spent the most valuable act he is capable of on something that cannot receive it. His need remains unmet, his hope is invested in nothing, and his one genuine recourse has been bypassed.

The prohibition is therefore not a restriction on his options. It is the removal of a dead end.

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:107

> **And if God should touch thee with affliction, none can remove it save He. And if He desires good for thee, none can repel His Bounty. He causes it to fall upon whomsoever He will among His servants, and He is the Forgiving, the Merciful."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Positive Counterpart**

*Wa-in yamsaska Allāhu bi-ḍurrin fa-lā kāshifa lahu illā huwa. Wa-in yuridka bi-khayrin fa-lā rādda li-faḍlihi.*

Verse 106 said the false objects of worship can neither harm nor benefit. This verse says who can.

The two verses form a single argument. The first empties the field; the second identifies the sole occupant.

**"If God Should Touch Thee With Affliction"**

*In yamsaska Allāhu bi-ḍurr.*

*Massa* — to touch. The verb is gentle, and its gentleness is theologically loaded. Affliction is described as a *touch*, not a blow.

The same verb is used at v. 12 of this sūrah: *wa-idhā massa al-insāna al-ḍurru daʿānā* — "when affliction touches man, he calls upon Us." The vocabulary is deliberately consistent across the sūrah.

*Ḍurr* is harm, distress, adversity — the counterpart to *nafʿ*, benefit, in the previous verse. The pairing *ḍurr/nafʿ* runs through both verses and ties them together.

**"None Can Remove It Save He"**

*Fa-lā kāshifa lahu illā huwa.*

*Kāshif* is the active participle of *kashafa*, to uncover, to remove a covering — the root of *kashf*, unveiling.

The image is precise. Affliction is conceived as something laid *over* a person, and its removal is an uncovering. The one who placed it is the one who lifts it.

The Qur'an repeats the formula at 6:17 with both halves:

> *"And if God should touch thee with affliction, none can remove it save He. And if He should touch thee with good — He is Powerful over all things."*

**"If He Desires Good for Thee, None Can Repel His Bounty"**

*Wa-in yuridka bi-khayrin fa-lā rādda li-faḍlih.*

*Rādd* is the active participle of *radda*, to turn back, to repel, to return.

*Faḍl* is the term examined at v. 60: surplus, overflow, grace beyond what is owed. Its use here rather than *khayr* in the second clause is worth noting — what cannot be repelled is specifically His *bounty*, the excess that is given without being earned.

**The Complete Doctrine of Tawḥīd al-Rubūbiyyah**

Together the two clauses state the sūrah's doctrine of divine sovereignty in its practical form.

Nothing reaches a person except through Him, and nothing He sends can be blocked. The causal chain has a single origin and no independent branches.

The Prophet ﷺ gave this to Ibn ʿAbbās as a boy, riding behind him, in what is among the most complete counsels in the Sunnah:

> *"O young man, I shall teach you some words. Be mindful of God, and He will protect you. Be mindful of God, and you will find Him before you. When you ask, ask of God; when you seek help, seek help from God. And know that if the whole community were to gather to benefit you with something, they would not benefit you except with what God had already written for you. And if they were to gather to harm you with something, they would not harm you except with what God had already written against you. The pens have been lifted and the pages have dried."* (al-Tirmidhī 2516)

The correspondence with this verse is exact, down to the pairing of benefit and harm.

**What This Doctrine Does to a Person**

The practical effect of holding this belief seriously is worth setting out, because it is transformative and it is the reason the sūrah places it here, near its close.

*It removes the fear of people.* If no one can harm you except by what has been decreed, then the calculation of whom to appease collapses. This is precisely the liberation the sorcerers of Egypt discovered at v. 82, and the believers of v. 84 were being offered.

*It removes the servility of hope.* If no one can benefit you except by what has been decreed, then the exhausting cultivation of the powerful becomes pointless. Much of ordinary social life consists of managing people who might be useful; this doctrine dissolves the necessity.

*It relocates gratitude.* Benefits still arrive through people, and gratitude to them remains obligatory — the Prophet ﷺ said *"whoever does not thank people has not thanked God"* (Abū Dāwūd 4811). But the thanks are given to a conduit, not to a source.

*And it produces equanimity in both conditions.* The Prophet ﷺ described this:

> *"Wondrous is the affair of the believer! All of it is good for him, and that is for no one but the believer. If prosperity befalls him, he gives thanks, and that is good for him. And if adversity befalls him, he is patient, and that is good for him."* (Muslim 2999)

**Does This Doctrine Discourage Effort?**

The objection is predictable and it must be met, because it has been raised in every generation.

If nothing can be changed, why act?

The answer is the doctrine of means, established at v. 71 and v. 84. God has established a causal order and commanded us to operate within it. Effort is *itself* decreed; the outcome is *also* decreed; and no one knows the second in advance.

The Prophet ﷺ was asked precisely this question:

> They said: *"O Messenger of God, do the medicines with which we treat ourselves, the incantations we use, and the precautions we take avert anything of God's decree?"* He said: *"They are part of God's decree."* (al-Tirmidhī 2065, Ibn Mājah 3437)

*Hiya min qadari Llāh.* The remedy belongs to the decree. Taking it is not an attempt to escape destiny; it is the enactment of destiny.

And ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, turning back from plague-stricken Syria and challenged with *"Are you fleeing from God's decree?"*, gave the classic reply: *"We are fleeing from God's decree to God's decree"* (al-Bukhārī 5729).

**"He Causes It to Fall Upon Whomsoever He Will Among His Servants"**

*Yuṣību bihi man yashāʾu min ʿibādih.*

*Yuṣību* is from *ṣ-w-b*, to hit a target — the root of *muṣībah*, calamity, and of *aṣāba*, to strike or to hit correctly.

The verb has a striking double resonance. What reaches a person is described as *hitting its mark*. Nothing arrives at random or by misdirection; each thing that comes was aimed.

**"And He Is the Forgiving, the Merciful"**

*Wa-huwa al-Ghafūru al-Raḥīm.*

The verse closes with two of the most frequent divine names, and their placement here is not conventional.

*Al-Ghafūr* is from *gh-f-r*, to cover, to conceal — the root of *mighfar*, a helmet. Forgiveness in Arabic is a *covering* of the fault. The intensive form *ghafūr* indicates one who forgives much and repeatedly.

*Al-Raḥīm* is from *r-ḥ-m*, the root of *raḥim*, the womb. Divine mercy is named after the organ that shelters, feeds, and protects what is entirely helpless and has done nothing to deserve it.

**Why These Names Close This Verse**

The verse has just asserted absolute divine control over benefit and harm. That assertion, standing alone, could produce fear — the sense of being at the disposal of an unaccountable power.

The names correct the impression. The One in whose hand all of this rests is *the Forgiving, the Merciful*. His absolute power is exercised by a character that has been disclosed, and the disclosure is of mercy.

The hadith qudsī, engraved above the Throne:

> *"Truly My Mercy prevails over My Wrath."* (al-Bukhārī 3194, Muslim 2751)

And the Prophet's ﷺ image of the extent of it:

> He saw a woman among the captives searching frantically, and when she found her infant she clutched him to her breast and nursed him. He said: *"Do you think this woman would throw her child into the fire?"* We said: *"No, by God, not if she is able to prevent it."* He said: *"God is more merciful to His servants than this woman is to her child."* (al-Bukhārī 5999, Muslim 2754)

The verse therefore ends the sūrah's theological argument where the sūrah began: with a God who governs everything (v. 3) and whose governance is characterised by mercy (v. 58).

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:108

> **Say, "O mankind! The truth has come unto you from your Lord. Whosoever is guided is guided only for the good of his own soul, and whosoever is astray is astray only to its detriment. And I am not a guardian over you."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Final Address to Humanity**

*Qul yā ayyuhā al-nāsu qad jāʾakumu al-ḥaqqu min rabbikum.*

The sūrah's penultimate verse gathers its argument into a single universal declaration. It is the fourth and last of the chapter's addresses to *al-nās*, mankind at large.

**"The Truth Has Come Unto You From Your Lord"**

*Qad jāʾakumu al-ḥaqqu min rabbikum.*

The particle *qad* with the perfect verb marks completion: the arrival has taken place. This is not an announcement of something forthcoming but the registration of a fact.

*Al-ḥaqq* — the term whose density was examined at v. 82: truth, reality, right, that which is due. And *min rabbikum*, "from *your* Lord" — with the possessive attached to the addressees, not to the speaker. The One who sent it is their own Lord, the One who created and sustains them, not a foreign deity being introduced.

The formula appeared at v. 94 addressed to the Prophet ﷺ: *la-qad jāʾaka al-ḥaqqu min rabbik*. Here it is turned outward to everyone.

**The Legal Effect of Arrival**

There is a juridical dimension to this statement that should not be missed.

Once a message has *arrived*, the condition of ignorance no longer obtains, and with it goes the excuse. The Qur'an is explicit that responsibility attaches to reception:

> *"And We never punish until We have sent a messenger."* (Qur'an 17:15)

> *"Messengers as bearers of glad tidings and as warners, that mankind might have no argument against God after the messengers."* (Qur'an 4:165)

*Li-allā yakūna li-l-nāsi ʿalā Allāhi ḥujjatun baʿda al-rusul* — that people might have no *ḥujjah*, no argument or plea, after the messengers.

The verse therefore closes a legal file. The evidence has been served; whatever follows is on the recipient.

The corollary, treated at v. 47, is that those whom the message never reached in an intelligible form — *ahl al-fatrah* — are in a different position, and their case belongs to God.

**"Whosoever Is Guided Is Guided Only for the Good of His Own Soul"**

*Fa-mani-htadā fa-innamā yahtadī li-nafsih.*

*Ihtadā* is form VIII of *h-d-y*, to guide: to accept guidance, to take the right way. The reflexive form is significant — it denotes the person's own reception, not merely his being directed.

*Li-nafsih* — "for his own soul." The lām is the lām of benefit: the advantage accrues to him.

**"And Whosoever Is Astray Is Astray Only to Its Detriment"**

*Wa-man ḍalla fa-innamā yaḍillu ʿalayhā.*

The parallel construction inverts the preposition: *li-nafsih* becomes *ʿalayhā*. The lām of benefit becomes *ʿalā*, the preposition of burden.

Arabic uses this pair — *lahu* / *ʿalayhi* — as the standard idiom for credit and debit. The Qur'an uses it in the great verse of accountability:

> *"It has what it has earned, and against it is what it has merited."* (Qur'an 2:286)

*Lahā mā kasabat wa-ʿalayhā mā-ktasabat.*

So the verse states a ledger. Guidance is entered on the credit side of the guided; misguidance is entered against the account of the misguided. Neither transaction touches anyone else.

**The Restrictive Particle**

Note *innamā* in both clauses — the particle of restriction, meaning "only," "nothing but."

Its force is to exclude any other beneficiary or victim. The one who accepts guidance does not thereby do God a favour; the one who refuses does not thereby injure God.

The hadith qudsī quoted at v. 106 states this exactly: neither the piety nor the wickedness of all creation combined would increase or decrease the divine dominion by anything (Muslim 2577).

And the sūrah has stated it in its own voice at v. 23 (*"your rebellion is only against yourselves"*) and v. 44 (*"human beings wrong themselves"*).

**Why This Matters for the Caller**

This verse, together with v. 99, forms the sūrah's complete instruction to anyone who invites others to faith.

Verse 99: you cannot compel.
Verse 108: and it is not your account that is affected.

Both statements relieve a burden. But the second is subtler, because it addresses a motive that can masquerade as concern.

There is a form of anxiety about others' guidance that is not really about them. It is about the caller's need to succeed, to be validated, to see his effort produce results. This verse dissolves that motive by pointing out that the transaction is not his.

The caller's obligation is to convey. The Prophet ﷺ was told this in the plainest terms:

> *"Thou art only a reminder; thou art not a warder over them."* (Qur'an 88:21–22)

> *"Naught is incumbent upon the Messenger but to convey."* (Qur'an 5:99)

**"And I Am Not a Guardian Over You"**

*Wa-mā anā ʿalaykum bi-wakīl.*

*Wakīl* is from *w-k-l* — the root of *tawakkul*, examined at v. 84. A *wakīl* is an agent, a trustee, one who has charge of another's affairs.

The declaration disclaims a specific role: the Prophet ﷺ is not in *charge* of these people. He is not responsible for their outcomes, does not manage their destinies, and will not be answerable for their choices.

The negation is grammatically emphatic: *mā anā* with the pronoun, *ʿalaykum* fronted, and the redundant *bi-* on the predicate — the same three devices used by Pharaoh's court at v. 78.

**The Theological Weight of This Disclaimer**

The statement performs a function beyond the immediate context, and it is one of the Qur'an's persistent concerns.

It defines the limit of prophetic office. A messenger conveys; he does not own, control, or dispose. The people are God's servants, not his.

This forecloses a development that has occurred in many religious histories: the transformation of a messenger into a mediator, and then into a proprietor of salvation. The Qur'an blocks it at the root by having the Prophet ﷺ himself declare that he holds no such position.

He was made to say it about his own family:

> When 26:214 was revealed — *"And warn thy tribe, thy nearest kin"* — he called them and said: *"O Fāṭimah, daughter of Muhammad, ask me of my wealth what you will, but I can avail you nothing against God."* (al-Bukhārī 2753, Muslim 206)

The most beloved person to him, told directly that his relationship to her would not serve her on that Day.

**The Verse and the Sūrah's Structure**

It is worth observing where this verse sits.

The sūrah opened at v. 2 with the objection: *"Is it a marvel unto mankind that We revealed unto a man from among them?"* The whole chapter has been a response to the doubt about a human messenger.

And it closes by defining precisely what that human messenger is and is not. He is one who conveys the truth from their Lord. He is not their guardian, and their fates are their own.

The answer to *"is it a marvel that God sent a man?"* turns out to be: no, because a man is exactly what is required. Not a guardian, not a controller, not a god — a conveyor, whose humanity is the point (v. 16: *"I dwelt among you a lifetime before it"*).

---

## Sūrah Yūnus 10:109

> **And follow that which is revealed unto thee, and be patient till God renders judgement. And He is the best of judges.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Final Verse**

*Wa-ttabiʿ mā yūḥā ilayka wa-ṣbir ḥattā yaḥkuma Allāh. Wa-huwa khayru al-ḥākimīn.*

One hundred and eight verses of argument, narrative, warning, and consolation conclude in two commands and a divine name.

**"Follow That Which Is Revealed Unto Thee"**

*Wa-ttabiʿ mā yūḥā ilayk.*

*Ittabiʿ* is the imperative of form VIII of *t-b-ʿ*, to follow — to walk behind, to take as one's guide. The form conveys deliberate, sustained following.

*Mā yūḥā ilayk* — "what is revealed to you," in the passive and the imperfect: what *is being* revealed, continuously.

The object of following is specified exactly: the revelation, and nothing else. Not the demands of the audience, not the strategic calculation of what will work, not the accumulated wisdom of the messenger's own judgement. The text.

This closes an arc opened at v. 15, where the objectors asked for a different Qur'an and the reply was: *"It is not for me to alter it of my own accord. I follow naught but what is revealed unto me."* The sūrah's first statement of the Prophet's ﷺ relation to the text and its last are the same statement.

**Why "Follow" Rather Than "Convey"**

Note that the command is not *balligh*, convey, but *ittabiʿ*, follow.

The distinction matters. A messenger could in principle transmit a message he did not himself observe. The Qur'an rules this out for its Prophet ﷺ at every turn, and here it does so in the sūrah's closing instruction: before he is a transmitter, he is a follower.

ʿĀʾishah, asked about his character, gave the answer that has become the tradition's summary of him:

> *"His character was the Qur'an."* (Muslim 746, Aḥmad 24601)

*Kāna khuluquhu al-Qurʾān.* Not that he taught it, but that he *was* it — the text made into a life.

**"And Be Patient"**

*Wa-ṣbir.*

*Ṣabara* — to bind, to restrain, to hold fast. *Ṣabr* is not passive endurance but the active holding of a position under pressure. The Arabs used the verb of tethering an animal and of confining a person in one place.

The scholars distinguish three domains of *ṣabr*:

*Ṣabr ʿalā al-ṭāʿah* — patience in maintaining obedience, which is difficult because obedience is continuous and unglamorous.

*Ṣabr ʿan al-maʿṣiyah* — patience in restraining from disobedience, which is difficult because the pull is immediate and the cost deferred.

*Ṣabr ʿalā al-qadar* — patience under what is decreed, which is difficult because it cannot be argued with.

The command here encompasses all three, but its immediate reference is the third: the Prophet ﷺ is to endure the pace at which God is proceeding.

**"Till God Renders Judgement"**

*Ḥattā yaḥkuma Allāh.*

The patience is given a terminus, and the terminus is not specified in time. It is specified by an event: the divine judgement.

*Yaḥkum* is from *ḥ-k-m*, the root treated at v. 1 of this sūrah — the root of *ḥikmah* (wisdom), *ḥukm* (judgement, rule), *ḥakīm* (wise), and *ḥakam* (arbiter). Its primary sense is to restrain, as a bridle restrains a horse; from this comes the sense of judging, since a judgement restrains a dispute from continuing.

The sūrah opened with *al-kitāb al-ḥakīm*, the Wise Book. It closes with *khayru al-ḥākimīn*, the best of judges. The same root frames the entire chapter.

**The Sūrah's Governing Theme, Stated Last**

Here at the end, the sūrah's most persistent concern receives its final formulation.

Look at what the chapter has been arguing from its earliest verses.

Verse 11: *"And if God were to hasten for people the evil they seek to hasten, their term would have already been decreed for them."* God does not hasten.

Verse 20: *"Say: The Unseen belongs only to God. So wait; truly I am with you among those who wait."*

Verse 46: *"Whether We show thee a part of that which We promise them, or We take thee unto Us — unto Us is their return."*

Verse 48: *"They say: When will this promise come to pass?"*

Verse 50: *"Why do the guilty seek to hasten it?"*

Verse 102: *"Say: Wait! Truly I am waiting along with you."*

And now: *be patient till God renders judgement.*

The sūrah is, at its core, about *timing* — about the gap between the truth of a claim and its visible vindication, and about what a person does in that gap.

Its answer is: follow what you have been given, and wait. That is the whole practical instruction, and it is placed last.

**Jonah and the Sūrah's Name**

This is where the chapter's title becomes fully intelligible.

Jonah's failing, as the Qur'an describes it, was precisely impatience:

> *"So be patient with the Judgement of thy Lord, and be not like the companion of the fish, who cried out while choking with grief."* (Qur'an 68:48)

*Wa-lā takun ka-ṣāḥibi al-ḥūt.* The instruction is explicitly a warning against Jonah's example — a prophet who left before he was told to leave, because the outcome was not arriving fast enough.

And the irony, recorded at v. 98 of this sūrah, is that his people repented after he left. The judgement he had despaired of was coming; he was simply not present for it.

So the sūrah named for Jonah closes with the command that Jonah found hardest: *iṣbir*.

**"And He Is the Best of Judges"**

*Wa-huwa khayru al-ḥākimīn.*

The final clause supplies the ground of the patience.

Waiting is bearable in proportion to one's confidence in the outcome. A person waiting on a verdict from a corrupt or incompetent court has every reason to be agitated. A person waiting on a judge who is perfectly informed, perfectly impartial, and perfectly capable has no reason to be.

The name *ḥākim* here carries the sense of both judge and ruler. God is not merely the one who will assess; He is the one who is currently governing — the *yudabbiru al-amr* of v. 3.

The Qur'an uses the superlative elsewhere in the same register: *aḥkam al-ḥākimīn*, "the most just of judges" (11:45; 95:8), and *khayr al-fāṣilīn*, "the best of deciders" (6:57).

**The Verse's Application to Every Reader**

The closing instruction is not exhausted by its original addressee.

Every believer occupies the same structure: in possession of a claim about how things are, unable to compel others to see it, unable to accelerate its vindication, and required to keep going in the interval.

The two commands are the complete answer.

*Follow what has been revealed.* Not what is expedient, not what is popular, not what one has reasoned out independently. The instruction defines the content of the life.

*And be patient until God judges.* Not until results appear, not until the opposition relents, not until the situation improves. Until God judges — which He will, on His schedule.

The Qur'an's most compressed statement of the same thing is Sūrat al-ʿAṣr, which al-Shāfiʿī said would suffice mankind if they reflected on it:

> *"By time! Truly mankind is in loss — save those who believe, and perform righteous deeds, and exhort one another to truth, and exhort one another to patience."* (Qur'an 103:1–3)

Belief, action, truth, patience. The same four, in the same order, as the sūrah's own conclusion.

**A Closing Reflection on the Sūrah as a Whole**

Sūrah Yūnus begins with a book called wise and a scoffing question about a human messenger. It ends with two commands and the name of the best of judges.

Between those points it has argued that the cosmos is ordered and its order is evidence (vv. 3–6); that human beings are ungrateful by default and sincere only in crisis (vv. 12, 22–23); that this life is a field that will be mown while it is at its most beautiful (v. 24); that God has invited everyone to the abode of peace (v. 25); that the deniers' certainty rests on conjecture (v. 36); that God does not wrong anyone (v. 44); that the friends of God have no fear (v. 62); that Noah's people drowned and Pharaoh's body was preserved as a sign (vv. 73, 92); that one city, once, repented in time (v. 98); that faith cannot be compelled (v. 99); and that the believers will be delivered because God has made it a debt upon Himself (v. 103).

And having said all of it, it does not end in triumph or in threat. It ends with a man being told to keep following what he has been given, and to wait.

> *"And He is the best of judges."*
